# Design how your system behaves, together

> Cratis Studio is the shared board where product owners, domain experts, designers and developers model events, screens and examples - and carry them on to working software. These guides show you how.

# The event model board

> The event model board is where a team draws how a system behaves, left to right, one slice at a time. Start here to find the guide for each thing you do on it.

The event model board is where you and your team draw how a system behaves. It reads left to right, like time: a person does something, a fact is recorded, and later someone looks at the result. app.cratis.studio ![The Storefront event model: columns of slices with their commands, events and read models, and the toolbar on the left edge](/_astro/storefront-board.Cocj6HLG_AQgEy.webp) The Storefront board of Acme Commerce. Each column is one slice: one behavior of the system. Everything on the board is one of three things. A [command](/reference/glossary/#command) is a request that can be refused. An [event](/reference/glossary/#event) is a fact that happened and is never taken back. A [read model](/reference/glossary/#read-model) is what someone looks at afterwards. Boards group them in [modules](/reference/glossary/#module), [features](/reference/glossary/#feature) and [slices](/reference/glossary/#slice). If these words are new, read [Commands, events and read models](/event-modeling/commands-events-and-read-models/) first. This section is about doing the work. ## What you can do here [Section titled “What you can do here”](#what-you-can-do-here) [Find your way around ](/board/board-basics/)Pan, zoom, the minimap, detail levels and the View menu. [Build the structure ](/board/modules-and-features/)Add modules and features, and connect the areas of your business. [Add behavior ](/board/slices/)Add a slice, pick its type and keep track of its status. [Describe a command ](/board/commands/)Give a command its fields, its rules and who may run it. [Describe an event ](/board/events/)Name the facts, make one public and give it tags. [Shape a read model ](/board/read-models/)Map events onto a read model and add queries. [Let the system act ](/board/automations/)Add automations that react to events or to the clock. [Cross a border ](/board/translations/)Publish public events and translate events from other areas. [Say who does what ](/board/actors-and-personas/)Add actors and choose a persona for each row. [Talk about the model ](/board/notes-comments-and-topics/)Sticky notes, comments and conversations on the board. [Look back and undo ](/board/history-and-restore/)See what changed and restore an earlier version. [Check your work ](/board/check-the-model/)Findings, storylines, see as a caller and where a value comes from. [Copy and reuse ](/board/copy-and-paste/)Copy, paste, duplicate and linked copies. [Work together ](/board/collaborate/)See who else is on the board, live. ## Where to go from the board [Section titled “Where to go from the board”](#where-to-go-from-the-board) The board is the center of a project, and most other tasks start from it. * To capture ideas before you have a model, use the [brainstorming board](/discover/). * To design what people see, add [screens](/screens/) to your slices. * To prove a slice behaves as agreed, add specifications in [Check behavior](/check/). * To change the board by talking to it, see [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/). * To turn the model into working software, see [Build and deliver](/deliver/).

# Add actors and choose personas

> Give every row of a module an actor, choose a persona for UI rows, and know when to use a system or chat actor.

Every row of a module belongs to whoever does the work in it. That is its **actor**, and the board always shows who does what. Here you add actors and give them personas. For the idea, see [Screens and personas](/event-modeling/screens-and-personas/). ## The three kinds of actor [Section titled “The three kinds of actor”](#the-three-kinds-of-actor) | Actor | What it is | | ---------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **UI Actor** | A person using a screen. Give each persona that owns screens its own row. | | **System Actor** | The system itself, for [automations](/board/automations/). A module has exactly one. | | **Chat Actor** | An AI conversation. Unlike the others it has a system prompt, which you edit with **Edit system prompt**. | ## Add an actor [Section titled “Add an actor”](#add-an-actor) 1. On the row labels at the left edge of a module, select **Add Actor**. 2. Choose **UI Actor**, **System Actor** or **Chat Actor**. app.cratis.studio ![The Add Actor menu with UI Actor, System Actor and Chat Actor](/_astro/add-actor-menu.BCyx5K3n_ivJRC.webp) The Add Actor menu of a module. ## Choose a persona for a row [Section titled “Choose a persona for a row”](#choose-a-persona-for-a-row) A [persona](/reference/glossary/#persona) says who the person on a UI row is, such as **Shopper**, **Merchandiser**, **SalesRep**, **SalesManager**, **Buyer** or **WarehouseClerk**. Personas come from the domain’s personas canvas. 1. On the row label, open **Choose persona**. 2. Pick a persona from the list, for example **Shopper**. To use a name that is not on the canvas, choose **Custom…**. 3. To change the description or, for a chat actor, the system prompt, use **Edit persona description** or **Edit system prompt**. app.cratis.studio ![The Choose persona list on the Shopper row, with Shopper, Custom and Delete actor](/_astro/choose-persona.GtxWfSiv_Zg0YjU.webp) Choosing the persona of a row. ## Remove an actor [Section titled “Remove an actor”](#remove-an-actor) Open **Choose persona** on the row label and choose **Delete actor**. If the actor has screen content on any slice, Studio warns you that deleting it removes that content too. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The row label shows the persona’s name, such as **Shopper**, and the slices on that row are the ones that person uses. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Screens and personas](/event-modeling/screens-and-personas/): the idea behind actors. * [Check the model](/board/check-the-model/): see what each role is offered. * [Personas](/discover/personas/): where personas are defined. * [Screens on slices](/screens/screens-on-slices/): the screens a UI row owns.

# Add an automation

> Add a slice where the system reacts on its own, choose what triggers it and say what to do when a command it issues is refused.

An [automation](/reference/glossary/#automation) is the system reacting to a fact and acting on it, with nobody pressing a button. In Acme Commerce, **EmptyBasketAfterCheckout** empties the basket once checkout has completed. For the idea, read [Automations and translations](/event-modeling/automations-and-translations/). ## Add an automation [Section titled “Add an automation”](#add-an-automation) 1. In the header of a feature, select **Add**, then **Add Slice**. 2. Choose **Automation**: *System reacts to events and issues commands*. 3. Name the slice, for example **EmptyBasketAfterCheckout**. app.cratis.studio ![The AutoApproveSmallOrders automation on the Sales board: the system reacts to an event and issues a command](/_astro/automation-slice-sales.CMvvOfMh_ZLf7qx.webp) An automation on the Sales board: AutoApproveSmallOrders runs on its own when an order arrives. An automation has no screen and no person behind it. Its row belongs to the module’s **System Actor**, drawn with a cogwheel. A module has exactly one system actor, and every automation in the module shares that row. See [Actors and personas](/board/actors-and-personas/). ## Choose what triggers it [Section titled “Choose what triggers it”](#choose-what-triggers-it) An automation needs to know when to run. The small lightning bolt in a circle, in the lower corner of its row, is the trigger. In the picture, **TakeStockWhenDispatched** sits on the Warehouse board between **DispatchShipment** and **TakeDispatchedStock**: the cogwheel is the system actor, **TakeDispatchedStock** is the command it issues, and **Add refusal handling** is the link you use below. app.cratis.studio ![The TakeStockWhenDispatched automation on the Warehouse board: a cogwheel with the command TakeDispatchedStock and the Add refusal handling link, the trigger bolt in a circle at the lower right, between the DispatchShipment and TakeDispatchedStock slices](/_astro/automation-slice-warehouse.DGiIAezh_1Cdvrq.webp) Look for the cogwheel, the command name under it, the Add refusal handling link and the lightning bolt in a circle: that bolt is the trigger. 1. Select **Add trigger**. 2. Choose a kind: * **Event**: when a specific event happens, such as **CheckoutCompleted**. * **Timer**: on a fixed schedule, such as a nightly clean-up. Pick a schedule template or describe your own in plain words. * **Custom**: describe the behavior in your own words and let generation use the description. 3. To change it later, use **Change trigger type**, **Edit trigger** or **Remove trigger**. For an event trigger, **Edit trigger** opens **Event trigger**: choose the event under **Select trigger event**, then select **Save**. **Delete trigger** takes the trigger away, and **Cancel** leaves it as it was. app.cratis.studio ![The Event trigger dialog with ShipmentHandedToCarrier chosen under Select trigger event, and the Delete trigger, Cancel and Save buttons](/_astro/automation-event-trigger.Cze9Cbr__1P608T.webp) The automation runs when the event in Select trigger event happens: here ShipmentHandedToCarrier. Most of the time an event is enough: something happened, so something else should happen. ## Say what happens when a command is refused [Section titled “Say what happens when a command is refused”](#say-what-happens-when-a-command-is-refused) An automation usually issues a command, and a command can be refused. For each one you can say what the automation does about it. 1. Select **Add refusal handling** under the command the automation issues. 2. In **Refusal branches**, pick the kind of refusal: **Any refusal**, **Validation**, **Constraint** or **Authorization**. 3. Choose **Acknowledge the refusal**, or add an event with **Add event** and give the values it carries. **Add branch** adds a branch for another kind of refusal. 4. Select **Save**. With no branches, a refusal of the command is not answered. app.cratis.studio ![The Refusal branches dialog after Add branch: one branch set to Any refusal with Acknowledge the refusal ticked, and the Move up, Move down, Remove branch, Add branch, Cancel and Save buttons](/_astro/automation-refusal-branches.Bhxw8kaM_Z2polpv.webp) Each branch picks a kind of refusal (here Any refusal) and what to do about it. Use Move up and Move down to order the branches, and Remove branch to drop one. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The slice shows the cogwheel row of the system actor, its trigger and, below, the event it produces. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Commands and rules](/board/commands/): the commands an automation issues. * [Translations and public events](/board/translations/): when the fact comes from another area. * [Check behavior](/check/): prove the automation reacts as agreed. * [Slices](/board/slices/): the four slice types.

# Find your way around a board

> Pan and zoom the event model board, turn on the minimap, choose how much detail you see and use the View menu.

A board can grow to dozens of slices. Here you move around it, choose how much of it to look at and find the part you need. ## Open a board [Section titled “Open a board”](#open-a-board) In the project drawer, open the application and its domain, then **Event Models**, and choose a board. To start a new one, use **Add Event Model**. The drawer is described in [Find your way around Studio](/get-started/find-your-way-around/). ## Pan and zoom [Section titled “Pan and zoom”](#pan-and-zoom) Drag the empty canvas to pan. To zoom, hold Cmd (Mac) or Ctrl (Windows and Linux) and scroll, or pinch on a trackpad. The controls in the bottom right corner do the same: * **Zoom In** and **Zoom Out** step the zoom. * **Reset Zoom** returns to 100%. * **Toggle minimap** shows or hides the minimap, a small map of the whole board. app.cratis.studio ![The Storefront board with the minimap switched on and the zoom controls in the bottom right corner](/_astro/board-minimap-and-zoom.BccHb5mN_1oUDv6.webp) The zoom controls and the minimap sit in the bottom right corner. ## Choose how much detail you see [Section titled “Choose how much detail you see”](#choose-how-much-detail-you-see) Use the **View** menu in the top bar. The choices are remembered in your browser. 1. Select **View** in the top bar. 2. Under **Detail level**, choose **Full** to see every property on every card, or **Overview** to see only names. Overview is best for looking at the shape of a whole board. 3. Under **Visualization**, choose **Arrows** or **Lines** for how events and read models are connected. 4. Under **Text size**, choose **Small**, **Medium** or **Large**. 5. Under **Chat**, choose **Inline** or **Sidebar** for where the board chat sits. app.cratis.studio ![The View menu with Full and Overview, Properties, Arrows and Lines, three text sizes, and Inline and Sidebar](/_astro/view-menu.BA3yIHzR_11DGX4.webp) The View menu holds every display option of the board. **Properties** in the same menu shows or hides the properties on all cards at once. To change it for one slice, select **Show details** or **Hide details** in that slice’s header. app.cratis.studio ![The Basket feature at Full detail, with every command and event showing its properties](/_astro/detail-full.UfKDw9mn_1DnTHC.webp) Full detail: the Basket feature with its slices. app.cratis.studio ![The same board at Overview detail, zoomed out so much more of it fits](/_astro/detail-overview.C2-C-lUG_Z1dLTPH.webp) Overview detail: the same board, much more of it on screen. ## Collapse a row [Section titled “Collapse a row”](#collapse-a-row) Each row of a module belongs to an actor. To save space, select **Collapse row** in a feature’s header, and **Expand row** to bring it back. A collapsed row says how many slices are hidden, with a **Show** button. ## Focus on one feature [Section titled “Focus on one feature”](#focus-on-one-feature) When a board is big, show one feature and nothing else. 1. Select **Focus on a feature** in the toolbar on the left edge, and pick the feature. You can also open a feature’s options menu and choose **Focus on this feature**. 2. Studio draws only that feature and says **Focused on** and its name. 3. Select **Show the whole board** to leave. app.cratis.studio ![The board toolbar with Focus on a feature chosen, showing the features of the board to pick from](/_astro/focus-on-a-feature.CC7SRqNY_s8EGY.webp) Pick the feature to focus on. ## Find something on the board [Section titled “Find something on the board”](#find-something-on-the-board) To find a particular thing, use one of these: * **Focus on a feature** to show only its slices. * **Storylines**, which jump to each step of a path through the board. See [Check the model](/board/check-the-model/). * The findings list from [Check the model](/board/check-the-model/), which names the slice each problem is about. * The board chat, which can answer questions about the model. See [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/). * When you add an existing event to a slice, a search box (**Search events…**) finds events from the whole application. ## Get help on the board [Section titled “Get help on the board”](#get-help-on-the-board) The help button in the bottom right corner (tooltip **Explain how this event model works**) asks an agent to explain the board. It appears only when an AI agent is available for your organization. To take a guided tour of the board, use **Take the guided tour** at the bottom of the left toolbar. See [Guided tours](/get-started/guided-tours/). ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Modules and features](/board/modules-and-features/) to build the structure of the board. * [Slices](/board/slices/) to add behavior. * [Statuses and colors](/reference/statuses-and-colors/) for what the colors on the board mean. * [Copy, paste and duplicate](/board/copy-and-paste/) to select several things and move them at once.

# Check the model

> Let Studio find gaps in the model, walk through it as a storyline, see what each caller is offered and trace where a value comes from.

A model can look right and still have holes. Four tools in the board’s left toolbar help you find them before anything is built: findings, storylines, see as a caller and trace a value. To prove behavior with examples, use [Check behavior](/check/). ## Read the findings [Section titled “Read the findings”](#read-the-findings) Studio checks the model as you work and lists what it found. 1. Select **Show findings on this board** (the warning triangle) in the toolbar. The number in brackets is how many there are. The button is there only while the model has findings. 2. Read **Findings on this board**. Each has a severity and a sentence about the problem. 3. Each finding says where it is, **Why it matters** and a **Suggested fix**. Select it to go to the slice it is about. Examples of what is found: a read model with no identifier, a slice with no specifications, a field nothing fills, an event nothing reads, a view waiting for an event no slice produces, a command value no event records, a command that refers to something no earlier slice creates, a screen field missing from its read model, and a command that reads recorded state while nothing authorizes it. See [finding](/reference/glossary/#finding) in the glossary. ## Walk through a storyline [Section titled “Walk through a storyline”](#walk-through-a-storyline) A [storyline](/reference/glossary/#storyline) names a path through the board, such as a user journey. It is a good way to tell the model to someone else, step by step. 1. Select **Storylines** in the toolbar. The panel is called **Storylines on this board**. 2. Select **New storyline**. Fill in a name, a title and a description. 3. Select **Add step**, then **Pick an element of the board…**, and select an element. Write a note for the step. Repeat for every step. 4. Select **Show on the board** to see the path drawn. 5. Select **Play** to walk through it. Use **Previous step** to go back and **Stop** to end. app.cratis.studio ![The Storylines on this board panel with a New storyline button](/_astro/storylines-panel.BNrKgnUR_ZorQU8.webp) Storylines are listed here; New storyline starts one. ## See the board as a caller [Section titled “See the board as a caller”](#see-the-board-as-a-caller) This shows what each screen offers a particular kind of caller. Use it to check that the right people can do the right things. 1. Select **See as a caller** in the toolbar. The panel is called **What a caller is offered**. 2. Tick **Signed in** if the caller is signed in. 3. Under **Roles**, enter the caller’s roles, separated by commas. 4. Under **Data on the screen**, enter one name=value per line, for the thing the screen is showing. 5. Under **Recorded state**, enter one read model per line followed by its properties. What is not recorded here is treated as not recorded at all, and it denies. 6. Read the result: which actions and which data each screen offers this caller. **Who may do what** lists the policies of the application. app.cratis.studio ![The What a caller is offered panel with Signed in, Roles, Data on the screen and Recorded state, and the policies listed below](/_astro/see-as-caller.RzQ8sL-G_Z2qUH0O.webp) Describe a caller and see what each screen offers them. Caution This is a preview of the model. The server still decides when anything runs. ## Trace where a value comes from [Section titled “Trace where a value comes from”](#trace-where-a-value-comes-from) When you are unsure why a field has the value it has, ask the board. 1. Select **Trace a value** in the toolbar. 2. Under **Element**, pick a command, event, read model or screen. 3. Studio answers **Where does this value come from?** by tracing it back through read models, events and commands. This is the value’s [lineage](/reference/glossary/#lineage). app.cratis.studio ![The Where does this value come from panel with an Element picker](/_astro/trace-a-value.DADPaSiP_1LOwph.webp) Pick an element to trace its values back to their source. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) Findings are down to the ones you accept, a storyline plays through from start to end, and each caller sees only what they should. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Commands and rules](/board/commands/): the checks bar of a command. * [Check behavior](/check/): specifications and Play. * [Notes, comments and topics](/board/notes-comments-and-topics/): note down what you decide. * [Policies and roles](/discover/policies-and-roles/): define who may do what.

# Collaborate on a board

> See who else is on the board, follow their live cursors and edit together in real time.

A board is meant to be worked on by several people at once: the product owner, the domain expert and the developer, in the same model at the same time. Studio shows who is there and what they are doing, and keeps everyone’s view up to date. ## See who is on the board [Section titled “See who is on the board”](#see-who-is-on-the-board) 1. Select **Active collaborators** in the top bar. When you are alone, it says **Only you**. 2. Read the list. Each person is shown with the area they have open, such as **Event Model**, **Brainstorming**, **Concepts** or **Personas**. 3. A person who has stepped away is marked **(idle)**. app.cratis.studio ![The top bar of the board with the Active collaborators button](/_astro/active-collaborators.IkmxSqtU_ZuGzwG.webp) Active collaborators in the top bar tells you who else is here. If you have the same board open in more than one tab, the list says *You are also open in N other tabs*. ## Work at the same time [Section titled “Work at the same time”](#work-at-the-same-time) People who are on the same board see each other’s live cursors, and changes show up as they are made. There is no need to refresh the page or to take turns. Typing indicators show who is writing a comment. See [Notes, comments and topics](/board/notes-comments-and-topics/). If someone changes something by mistake, you can bring it back from the board’s history. See [History and restore](/board/history-and-restore/). ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [History and restore](/board/history-and-restore/): see what changed while you were away. * [Notes, comments and topics](/board/notes-comments-and-topics/): talk about the model. * [Share and export](/share/): show a board to people who are not in the project.

# Describe a command and its rules

> Open the command editor to name a command, give it fields, add validation rules, say who may run it and see which events it produces.

A [command](/reference/glossary/#command) is a request that can be refused. The command editor is where you say what it asks for, what must hold before it is accepted, and which events it leaves behind. Commands live on **State Change** slices. See [Slices](/board/slices/) to add one, and [Commands, events and read models](/event-modeling/commands-events-and-read-models/) for the idea. ## Open the command editor [Section titled “Open the command editor”](#open-the-command-editor) Select the command card on a State Change slice, for example **AddToBasket**. The editor opens as a dialog with the steps of the command down the left, tabs along the top (**Design**, **Describe** and **Screenplay**, plus **C#** once a handler is written) and a checks bar at the bottom. app.cratis.studio ![The command editor of AddToBasket with its steps on the left, the command card with three properties in the middle and the property inspector on the right](/_astro/command-editor.CDuIhQWV_2jyS4M.webp) The Design tab draws the command from top to bottom, in the order it runs. If the slice is done, or the board is read only, the editor still opens, and its title ends in *(Read only)*. ## Say what the command is for [Section titled “Say what the command is for”](#say-what-the-command-is-for) Under the command’s name, write a sentence in the domain’s words, such as what a shopper asks for when they add something to the basket. Beside it, **Does its work by** chooses whether the command works by **Producing events** or by **A C# handler**. Most commands produce events. ## Add the fields the sender gives [Section titled “Add the fields the sender gives”](#add-the-fields-the-sender-gives) 1. In the **Design** tab, select **+ Add property** on the command card. 2. Name the property and choose its type with **Change the type**. The type menu offers the domain’s concepts, such as **Sku** or **Quantity**, as well as plain types. 3. Tick what applies in the inspector on the right: * **Required**: the sender must give it. * **Identifier**: names what changes. A command has one identifier. * **Generated**: the command makes the value itself. 4. To remove a property, select **Remove the property**. ## Add a rule [Section titled “Add a rule”](#add-a-rule) A command that breaks a rule never becomes an event. Nothing is recorded and the caller is told why. 1. Select the property in the command card. 2. In the inspector, under the rules of that property, select **Add a rule**. 3. Compare the property to a value you type, or to another property. For example, **quantity** must be greater than zero. 4. The rule shows as a chip on the property. Some rules need history. A basket cannot be checked out when it is empty, and only earlier events know what the basket holds. To rule against recorded history, select **+ Define state**. The state is built from earlier events the same way a read model is, in the projection designer described in [Read models, projections and queries](/board/read-models/). The **Requirements** step then compares the command’s properties with that state, and a failed requirement leads to **Rejected**. ## Say who may run the command [Section titled “Say who may run the command”](#say-who-may-run-the-command) Who may run a command is decided by the application’s policies. 1. Select **Authorization** in the steps on the left. 2. Under **Only if the caller**, tick the policies that must hold. Only callers admitted by all the selected policies may run the command. With nothing ticked, the step says **No policy required**. 3. If a policy takes values from the command it guards, the **Policy inputs** section lets you say which property fills each one. An input you leave alone is filled from the property of the same name. If the application has no policies yet, the step says so. Policies are defined for the whole application, not in the command editor. See [Policies and roles](/discover/policies-and-roles/). A command that reads recorded state, with nothing that authorizes it, is open to every caller. The board’s findings point this out. See [Check the model](/board/check-the-model/). ## The rest of the flow [Section titled “The rest of the flow”](#the-rest-of-the-flow) The steps down the left side follow the order in which the command runs. | Step | What you do there | | ----------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Properties** | The fields and their rules. | | **Authorization** | Choose the policies a caller must satisfy to run the command. | | **State** | Define state projected from earlier events. | | **Requirements** | Compare the command with that state. | | **Generate** | See the values the command generates. | | **Produces** | See the events the slice records. They are the events on the board. | | **Concurrency** | Scope the append so two racing commands cannot both win. A command that loses leads to **Lost the race**. | | **Returns** | Choose what the caller gets back: nothing, one value or a record. Then the command is **Accepted**. | If you chose **A C# handler**, a **Handler** step takes the place of **Produces**. Select **+ Add C#** to write one from the events the command produces. Code is only for what the visual design cannot say. ## Describe and Screenplay [Section titled “Describe and Screenplay”](#describe-and-screenplay) The **Describe** tab tells the command in words, a paragraph for each part: what it is for, what goes in, which state it uses, its checks, what it generates, what it records and what it gives back. app.cratis.studio ![The Describe tab of the AddToBasket command, with the Does its work by choice and the concurrency guard](/_astro/command-editor-describe.uYwISUh__KkA0V.webp) The Describe tab reads the design back to you as sentences. The **Screenplay** tab shows the command, its state and the slice’s specifications as a [Screenplay](/reference/glossary/#screenplay) file would hold them. It only shows; you change the design on the other tabs. ## Read the checks bar [Section titled “Read the checks bar”](#read-the-checks-bar) The bar at the bottom says how many problems and warnings there are, with the first of them. Select it to list them all. Select one to go to the step it is about. A command that records nothing, or a value an event needs that no command property provides, shows up here. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The checks bar shows no errors, and the command card on the board lists the fields you added. What you edit in the dialog is saved when you close it. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Events](/board/events/): describe what the command leaves behind. * [Check the model](/board/check-the-model/): findings on the whole board. * [Check behavior](/check/): examples that prove the rules work. * [Screens on slices](/screens/screens-on-slices/): design the screen that sends the command.

# Copy, paste and duplicate

> Copy slices, features, modules and events, paste them somewhere else, duplicate in place and paste an event as a linked copy.

Reuse beats redrawing. You can copy a slice, a feature, a module or an event, paste it where you need it, or duplicate it in place. The tools are in the left toolbar, and each has a keyboard shortcut. ## Copy and paste [Section titled “Copy and paste”](#copy-and-paste) 1. Select what to copy: a slice, a feature, a module or an event. To select more than one, hold Shift, Ctrl or Cmd while you select. 2. Select **Copy** in the toolbar, or press Cmd + C (Mac) or Ctrl + C. Studio confirms with a message such as *Slice copied*. Hover the button to see its tooltip, **Copy** with its shortcut. 3. Select where it goes (see the table), then select **Paste**, or press Cmd + V or Ctrl + V. app.cratis.studio ![The OrderReceived event selected on the Sales board with the mouse on the Copy button of the left toolbar, whose tooltip reads Copy with its shortcut](/_astro/copy-paste-toolbar.BVv5DasV_Z2nuKsw.webp) Select the event first (OrderReceived has the highlighted outline), then point at Copy in the left toolbar to see its tooltip. | You copied | Paste it into | | ---------- | --------------------- | | Slices | A slice or a feature | | Features | A module or a feature | | An event | A slice | If nothing is selected, Studio asks you to select a slice, feature, module or event to copy. If it cannot tell where to paste, it says where each kind can go. ## Duplicate [Section titled “Duplicate”](#duplicate) Select what you want and choose **Duplicate** in the toolbar, or press Cmd + D or Ctrl + D. To duplicate a single slice you can also choose **Duplicate slice** in its options menu. ## Paste an event as a linked copy [Section titled “Paste an event as a linked copy”](#paste-an-event-as-a-linked-copy) A linked copy shows the same fact in another place. It is not a second event; it refers to the first. 1. Select the event and copy it. 2. Select the slice that should show it. 3. Select **Paste as linked copy**, or press Cmd + Shift + V or Ctrl + Shift + V. Only an event can be pasted as a linked copy. On a linked copy, **Go to source** takes you to the slice that produces the event. Deleting an event that other slices refer to warns you first. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The pasted element is on the board where you put it. A linked copy shows the same event as the original. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Slices](/board/slices/): move a slice to another feature instead of copying it. * [Events](/board/events/): add an existing event from another slice. * [Modules and features](/board/modules-and-features/): where features and modules live. * [Keyboard shortcuts](/reference/keyboard-shortcuts/): every shortcut in one place.

# Describe an event

> Add an event to a slice, name it in the past tense, give it properties, make it public and take it into a constraint.

An [event](/reference/glossary/#event) is a fact that happened and is never taken back, so it is named in the past tense: **BasketLineAdded**, not *AddBasketLine*. Here you add events and describe them. For the idea behind them, read [Commands, events and read models](/event-modeling/commands-events-and-read-models/). ## Add an event to a slice [Section titled “Add an event to a slice”](#add-an-event-to-a-slice) 1. On a slice, select **Add Event** to create a new event. 2. Name it in the past tense. Double-click the name to rename it later. 3. To use an event another slice already records, choose **Add existing event** and search with **Search events…** instead. The slice then shows a reference to the event, and **Go to source** takes you to the slice that produces it. Studio checks the order for you. A slice that reads an event must sit to the right of the slice that records it. ## Open the event editor [Section titled “Open the event editor”](#open-the-event-editor) Select the event card. The event editor is where an event is changed, and its layout matches the [command editor](/board/commands/). Its sidebar lists **Properties** with how many there are, **Constraints** and **Tags**. If the slice is done, or the board is read only, the editor opens read only. app.cratis.studio ![The event editor of ShipmentHandedToCarrier: the sidebar with Properties, Constraints and Tags, the event card with seven properties each showing its type and Required, an Add property row, and the Visibility choice Private or Public](/_astro/event-editor.BPgAMvSL_p8TeN.webp) The sidebar on the left lists Properties (with the count, 7 here), Constraints and Tags. The card in the middle is the event with its properties, and Visibility is at the top right. ## Describe its properties [Section titled “Describe its properties”](#describe-its-properties) Each property has a name, a type or a domain concept, and whether it is required. Pick a property to rename it, change its type or make it required in the inspector beside the card. In the picture above, each row of the card is one property of **ShipmentHandedToCarrier**, with its type on the right and a **Required** tick. Select **Add property** under the last row to add another. An event never decides a property’s value itself. The command decides it, and the event copies it. If a command value has no matching event property, or an event property has no command value to fill it, the checks bar tells you. See [Commands and rules](/board/commands/). ## Make an event public [Section titled “Make an event public”](#make-an-event-public) A private event is a fact the application keeps to itself. A public event is a contract that other applications can use. 1. In the event editor, find **Visibility**. 2. Choose **Private** or **Public**. The help next to the choice explains each. Only an output translation publishes a public event, so a public event that no output translation produces is flagged. See [Translations and public events](/board/translations/). ## Add tags [Section titled “Add tags”](#add-tags) In the event editor, open **Tags**, then add or remove tags. Tags are saved as you change them. The **Events** page of the application lists its events. ## Take an event into a constraint [Section titled “Take an event into a constraint”](#take-an-event-into-a-constraint) Some facts must never happen twice, such as an order number. A constraint is enforced when the event is written, wherever it is written from. 1. Open the event editor and select **Constraints** in its sidebar, or hover the event card and select the small lock that appears in a corner of it. 2. Studio lists the constraints of the domain, each with a name and a kind: **Unique event type** or **Unique by properties**. 3. Tick the constraints the event takes part in. For a unique-by-properties constraint, tick the properties under it. app.cratis.studio ![The Constraints page of the event editor for ShipmentHandedToCarrier: OneDispatchPerShipment, a Unique event type constraint, ticked, and UniqueLocationCode, a Unique by properties constraint, not ticked, with an Open the constraint board link](/_astro/event-editor-constraints.COoP3KLP_Z14bXBf.webp) Each constraint shows its kind on the right. OneDispatchPerShipment is ticked, so this event takes part in it; UniqueLocationCode is not. Only a private event can take part in a constraint. Constraints are defined on the domain’s constraints board; select **Open the constraint board** in the dialog to go there. ## Delete an event [Section titled “Delete an event”](#delete-an-event) Delete an event from its card. When other slices refer to it, Studio warns you before it removes it. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The event card shows its properties, the lock shows the constraints it takes part in, and the checks bar of the command that produces it shows no problems. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Read models, projections and queries](/board/read-models/): what the events become. * [Copy, paste and duplicate](/board/copy-and-paste/): show the same event somewhere else as a linked copy. * [Constraints](/discover/constraints/): define the rules that events take part in. * [Brainstorming](/discover/brainstorming/): start with events before you have a model.

# See what changed and restore an earlier version

> Open the History panel to see what happened on a board since you were last there, and restore the whole board or a single item.

Every change to a board is remembered. Use the History panel to catch up on what your team did, and to put things back when a change was a mistake. ## Open the History panel [Section titled “Open the History panel”](#open-the-history-panel) Select **See what has happened on this board** in the top bar. The panel opens on the right with two tabs: * **Since I was here**: what changed since you last looked. When nothing did, it says *Nothing has changed since you were last here.* * **All time**: everything that ever happened on the board. app.cratis.studio ![The History panel open on the right of the Storefront board with the Since I was here and All time tabs](/_astro/history-panel.QI2ZO7li_Z29rlaw.webp) The History panel. Here nothing has changed since the last visit. Each entry says what someone did: added, renamed, removed, moved, updated, changed the status of, commented on or restored a board, module, feature, slice, command, event, read model, actor, specification, sticky note, comment, chat or region. ## Restore an earlier version [Section titled “Restore an earlier version”](#restore-an-earlier-version) 1. In the History panel, find the entry for the point you want to go back to. 2. Choose **Restore to this point**. Studio opens **Restore an earlier version**. 3. Under **Restore**, choose **The whole board**, or choose the single item you want back (the second choice names it, for example *Only slice “AddToBasket”*). 4. Read **As it was then** and **What restoring does**. It lists what will be removed, brought back, put back, renamed back or moved back. 5. Select **Restore**. Restoring puts that part of the board back as it was just after the change you picked. It is recorded as new changes, so nothing in the history is lost and a restore can itself be restored. If the board is already as it was then, the dialog says **Nothing to restore**. Caution Comments and follow-up work are not restored. Changes to parts of the board that a restore does not cover yet, such as sticky notes, stay as they are, and the dialog tells you how many there are. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The board shows the earlier version, and **All time** has an entry saying it was restored. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Collaborate on a board](/board/collaborate/): see who is making the changes right now. * [Find your way around a board](/board/board-basics/): the rest of the top bar. * [Sticky notes, comments and topics](/board/notes-comments-and-topics/): the conversation behind the changes.

# Add modules and features

> Add a module for each area of your business, group related behavior in features, connect modules and set a feature's status.

A [module](/reference/glossary/#module) is one area of your business, and a [feature](/reference/glossary/#feature) groups related behavior inside it. In Acme Commerce, the Storefront has features such as **Basket**, **Browse**, **Checkout** and **MyOrders**. This page shows how to build that structure. For why the board is shaped this way, read [Modules and features](/event-modeling/modules-and-features/). ## Add a module [Section titled “Add a module”](#add-a-module) 1. Select **Add Module** (the sitemap icon) in the toolbar on the left edge of the board. A new, empty board points you to it. 2. Studio places the module near the middle of your view. Double-click its name to rename it. 3. To drag a whole collection of modules somewhere else, use its handle, labeled **Drag to move collection**. To add another module next to an existing one, open the **Add** menu in the module header and choose **Add Module**. ## Add a feature [Section titled “Add a feature”](#add-a-feature) 1. Open the **Add** menu in the header of a module. 2. Choose **Add Feature**. 3. Double-click the new feature’s name to rename it. Features can nest. Open the **Add** menu in a feature’s header and choose **Add Sub-feature** to put a smaller group of behavior inside it. app.cratis.studio ![The options menu of a module with Explain how this module works, Export module to Mermaid and Delete module](/_astro/module-options-menu.Dw00-vD0_stwwv.webp) A module's options menu. ## Work with a feature [Section titled “Work with a feature”](#work-with-a-feature) A feature’s options menu holds the actions for the whole group. app.cratis.studio ![The options menu of a feature listing Generate, Assign feature, Preview code, Generation runs, Focus on this feature, Explain, Export, Context for an agent, Disable feature and Delete feature](/_astro/feature-options-menu.Cq0F3SM9_ZGTG9Q.webp) A feature's options menu. | Choose | To | | ---------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | **Focus on this feature** | Draw only this feature. See [Find your way around a board](/board/board-basics/#focus-on-one-feature). | | **Assign feature** | Give the whole feature to a team member. | | **Generate** | Ask AI to fill the feature with slices. See [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/). | | **Preview code** and **Generation runs** | Look at generated code and earlier generation runs. See [Build and deliver](/deliver/). | | **Explain how this feature works** | Ask an agent for an explanation. It appears only when AI is available. | | **Export feature to Mermaid** | Download the feature as a diagram. | | **Context for an agent** | Copy or download the feature’s description for an external AI tool. | | **Disable feature** | Turn the feature off without deleting it; **Enable feature** turns it back on. | | **Delete feature** | Remove the feature. | A module’s menu has **Explain how this module works**, **Export module to Mermaid** and **Delete module**. ## Set the status of a feature [Section titled “Set the status of a feature”](#set-the-status-of-a-feature) A feature can be raised to **Draft**, **Analysis** or **Ready for implementation**. The working statuses, such as **In progress** and **Done**, are set on each [slice](/board/slices/#change-a-slices-status). All statuses are listed in [Statuses and colors](/reference/statuses-and-colors/). ## Connect two modules [Section titled “Connect two modules”](#connect-two-modules) Modules do not share a database. They only share facts. Draw the connection first, then name the fact. 1. Find the **connect dot** on the edge of the module that produces the fact. 2. Drag from the dot to the other module. A line follows the pointer. Drop it on the module that needs the fact, and the flow is drawn. 3. On the slice in the receiving module, select the **+** on its row and search for the event. Pick it. The slice now refers to the other module’s event. 4. Hover the reference. The rest of the board dims, leaving the event and the slice that produces it. For facts that cross between applications, see [Translations and public events](/board/translations/). ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) Your module shows up on the board with its features beneath it, and a connection draws a line between the two modules. Open a feature’s header to see its **Add** menu, comments and status. app.cratis.studio ![The Catalog board zoomed out: the Merchandising module with its Pricing, Products and Publication features, each holding slices](/_astro/catalog-board-overview.3NDoqvRD_1Yy7d1.webp) A finished structure: the Merchandising module of Acme Catalog, with the features Pricing, Products and Publication side by side. Caution Use **Disable feature** when you only want a feature out of the way for a while. If you delete something by mistake, you can bring it back by restoring an earlier version of the board: see [History and restore](/board/history-and-restore/). ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Slices](/board/slices/): add behavior to a feature. * [Find your way around a board](/board/board-basics/): focus on one feature when the board gets big. * [Copy, paste and duplicate](/board/copy-and-paste/): copy a feature into another module. * [Modules and features](/event-modeling/modules-and-features/): the idea behind the structure.

# Use sticky notes, comments and topics

> Write questions, risks and decisions on sticky notes, comment on a slice and start a conversation about the board.

A model is never only boxes. Teams also need a place for the question nobody has answered, the risk someone spotted and the decision that was made. Studio has three: sticky notes next to the model, comments on a slice, feature or module, and topics for conversations about the whole board. ## Add a sticky note [Section titled “Add a sticky note”](#add-a-sticky-note) 1. Select **Add Sticky Note** in the toolbar on the left edge. The note appears on the board. 2. Type your text in it. 3. Drag the note next to the part of the model it is about. Resize it if you need more room. 4. To delete a note, select it and press Delete or Backspace. Sticky notes work the same on the brainstorming board and the other canvases of a domain. ### Choose a color [Section titled “Choose a color”](#choose-a-color) A note is yellow until you change it. Select a note and a palette of colors appears above it. Pick one. | Color | Meaning | | ----------------- | ------------------------------------ | | Yellow | A **question**. This is the default. | | Pink | A **risk**. | | Green | A **decision**. | | Blue | An **assumption**. | | Orange and purple | Free to use as your team likes. | app.cratis.studio ![Sticky notes on the Sales board: a question, an assumption and a decision next to the model](/_astro/sticky-notes-sales.GHxQTrMq_ZMgKTy.webp) Colors let the team scan a board for questions, risks and decisions. Make a decision visible When a question has an answer, turn the note green and write the answer in it. The next person sees what was decided. The colors are the same on every board. See [Statuses and colors](/reference/statuses-and-colors/). ## Comment on a slice, a feature or a module [Section titled “Comment on a slice, a feature or a module”](#comment-on-a-slice-a-feature-or-a-module) Slices, features and modules each have a comments button in their header: **Open slice comments**, **Open feature comments** and **Open module comments**. 1. Select the comments button in the header. 2. Write in **Write a comment… (Enter to send)** and press Enter. 3. To react quickly, use **Add a quick reaction** on a comment. **Insert emoji** adds an emoji while you write. app.cratis.studio ![The comments panel of the AddToBasket slice with the emoji and send buttons](/_astro/slice-comments.z7p_9KSn_B9qfw.webp) Comments are kept with the slice they are about. Until someone writes the first one, the panel says *No comments yet. Be the first!* ## Start a topic [Section titled “Start a topic”](#start-a-topic) A topic is a conversation about the board as a whole, or about something on it. 1. Select **Topics on this event model** in the top bar. 2. Select **New topic** and say what the topic is about. 3. Write your comments in it. The topic says who started it. Use **Show what this conversation is about** to see what it points at on the board. app.cratis.studio ![The Topics panel listing one topic with its starter and when it started, and a New topic button](/_astro/topics-panel.DNFXyTsN_1RxJem.webp) Topics on the Storefront board. ### Mention an agent [Section titled “Mention an agent”](#mention-an-agent) Mention an AI agent in a topic and it answers. If nothing has come back after three minutes, the topic says **No answer yet**; the answer may still arrive, and if it does not, you can report it. Agents are set up for your organization. See [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/). ### Coding agents on the board [Section titled “Coding agents on the board”](#coding-agents-on-the-board) A coding agent you connect can propose changes to the board. The board lists **Coding agents connected to this board**, and a proposed change waits for a yes before the board changes. Choose **Go ahead** or **Don’t**. See [For developers](/developers/). ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Check the model](/board/check-the-model/): the board’s own findings. * [Collaborate on a board](/board/collaborate/): see who else is there. * [Brainstorming](/discover/brainstorming/): start with sticky notes and events. * [Talk to your board](/working-with-ai/talk-to-your-board/): ask an agent about the model.

# Shape a read model and its queries

> Open the read model editor to map events onto a read model, choose how it is kept and add the queries that read it.

A [read model](/reference/glossary/#read-model) is what someone looks at after things have happened: the basket as it stands right now. It is built from events by a [projection](/reference/glossary/#projection), and read through [queries](/reference/glossary/#query). Here you design all three on a **State View** slice. A read model is derived state, never a second source of truth. You can change how it is built, rebuild it from the same events, and no facts are lost. ## Open the read model editor [Section titled “Open the read model editor”](#open-the-read-model-editor) Select the read model card on a State View slice. The sidebar lists **Definition & projection** with the number of properties, and below it the queries of the read model. app.cratis.studio ![The read model editor of BasketContents: events on the left, the read model on the right with lines between them, and a preview of one instance](/_astro/read-model-editor.BZ0jnvlv_Zn3bJ9.webp) Events and their properties on the left, the read model on the right, and the lines that map one onto the other. ## Choose the events a read model is built from [Section titled “Choose the events a read model is built from”](#choose-the-events-a-read-model-is-built-from) A read model starts by choosing its events. Add the events the slice should listen to, for example **BasketLineAdded**, **BasketLineRemoved**, **BasketCleared** and **BasketLineQuantityChanged** for **BasketContents**. They appear as cards on the slice, each a reference to the slice that records it. See [Events](/board/events/). In the editor, **Other events** lists the events that are not sources yet. Drag one onto the read model header to add it as a source. ## Map events onto the read model [Section titled “Map events onto the read model”](#map-events-onto-the-read-model) 1. In **Definition & projection**, find the event on the left and the read model card on the right. 2. Drag from a property of the event onto a property of the read model. That property is now set from the event whenever it happens. 3. To say which instance an event updates, click the event. **Finds its BasketContents by** is the event’s own event source by default. Choose one of its properties to look the instance up by that instead. 4. To count, add or record something, select the line. Under **Operation** choose what happens each time the event does, such as **Counts it**, **Adds one**, **Takes one away**, **Adds the amount**, **Subtracts the amount**, **Takes when it happened** or **Takes who caused it**. 5. To remove an instance when an event happens, such as **BasketCleared**, click that event and turn on **Removes the BasketContents when it happens**. 6. To take a line out, select it and press Delete or Backspace. Properties whose names match are mapped for you (“By convention”). Select that line and choose **Pin it as an explicit mapping** to keep it whatever else changes. Select **+ Add Property** to add a property to the read model and **Change the type** to retype it. Use **Preview** to see one instance with an example value in every property, and **Structure** to see the projection written out as text. ## Choose how it is kept [Section titled “Choose how it is kept”](#choose-how-it-is-kept) Under **Kept**, choose: * **Materialized**: the read model is stored and kept up to date as events happen, so asking for it is quick. * **Passive**: it is not stored. It is built from its events each time it is asked for, so it is always exact but costs more. The help next to **Kept** explains the choice. ## Add a query [Section titled “Add a query”](#add-a-query) A query is how the read model is asked for. A read model can have several. In the sidebar, select **+ Add query**. A query is marked **live** when it updates while you look at it. 1. Name the query. Under **Gives back**, choose **One item** or **A list**. Turn on **May find nothing** when an empty answer is fine. 2. Under **Find by**, choose the property that identifies the item. For a list, **Narrow by** adds filters that keep only items whose property equals the value given; several filters must all match. 3. Under **Whose data**, choose the scope: **Everyone in the current tenant**, **Only the person asking** or **All tenants**. Under **Who can ask**, choose the policies. Any one of them is enough. 4. Turn on **Reactive** if screens should receive changes on their own, without a refresh. Visual design covers finding one item by a key, narrowing a list by matching values and reactive delivery. A query that needs sorting, comparisons, text search or totals needs code. The **Describe** tab can propose a query from a sentence, and the **Screenplay** tab shows the query as an export would write it. Both need nothing more from you than reading: nothing changes until you apply the proposal. ## Read the checks bar [Section titled “Read the checks bar”](#read-the-checks-bar) The bar at the bottom of the editor says what cannot work as drawn, such as a read model property set from an event property of another type, or a count kept in something that is not a number. When all is well it says *No problems found.* ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The checks bar says *No problems found.*, the preview shows the shape you expect, and the screen on the slice shows the data. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Commands and rules](/board/commands/): the state a command checks uses the same designer. * [Slices](/board/slices/): the State View type. * [Screens on slices](/screens/screens-on-slices/): what people see of a read model. * [Events](/board/events/): the facts a read model is built from.

# Add and manage slices

> Add a slice to a feature, choose its type, and use the slice options and statuses to keep track of the work.

A [slice](/reference/glossary/#slice) is one behavior of the system, drawn as one column. Here you add one, learn what the four types are for, and move it, hand it to someone and mark how far along it is. The idea behind slices is in [Slices](/event-modeling/slices/). ## Add a slice [Section titled “Add a slice”](#add-a-slice) 1. In the header of the feature, select **Add** (the **+** button). 2. Choose **Add Slice**. Studio asks you to *Choose a vertical slice type*. 3. Pick the type that fits the behavior (see the table below). The new slice appears in the feature. 4. Double-click the slice’s name to rename it. app.cratis.studio ![The Add Slice chooser listing State Change, State View, Automation and Translator](/_astro/add-slice-chooser.CD0lTpGG_sLQlO.webp) The chooser asks which kind of behavior the slice is. ## Choose a slice type [Section titled “Choose a slice type”](#choose-a-slice-type) | Type | Studio describes it as | Choose it when | Acme Commerce example | | ---------------- | ------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------- | | **State Change** | UI drives a command that produces events | A person asks for something and facts are recorded | **AddToBasket** | | **State View** | UI reads a projection built from events | A person looks at something built from earlier facts | **BasketView** | | **Automation** | System reacts to events and issues commands | The system should act on its own | **EmptyBasketAfterCheckout** | | **Translator** | Translates external events into internal commands | Facts cross the border of an area or an application | **ListProduct** | What goes inside each type is in [Commands and rules](/board/commands/), [Read models, projections and queries](/board/read-models/), [Automations](/board/automations/) and [Translations and public events](/board/translations/). ## Put slices in order [Section titled “Put slices in order”](#put-slices-in-order) Drag a slice to reorder it. Because the board reads left to right as time, Studio refuses a drop that would put a view before the events it reads. It tells you *These events are not produced before this position*, and lists them. ## Use the slice options [Section titled “Use the slice options”](#use-the-slice-options) Select **Slice options** in the slice header. app.cratis.studio ![The slice options menu: Description, Generate, Preview generated code, Link the slice to an issue, Context for an agent, Duplicate slice, Move to feature, Assign to, Builder and Delete slice](/_astro/slice-options-menu.BNMjn6sA_1qJCcD.webp) The options of the AddToBasket slice. | Choose | To | | ------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Description** | Write what the slice is for. | | **Generate** | Let AI fill in the slice. See [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/). | | **Preview generated code** | See the code Studio would generate for this slice. See [Build and deliver](/deliver/). | | **Link the slice to an issue** | Connect the slice to an issue in your tracker. See [Link slices to issues](/deliver/issue-links/). | | **Context for an agent** | Copy or download the slice as Markdown, JSON, YAML or TOON, to give to an external AI tool. | | **Duplicate slice** | Make a copy next to it. See [Copy, paste and duplicate](/board/copy-and-paste/). | | **Move to feature** | Move the slice to another feature of the board. | | **Assign to** | Give the slice to a team member. | | **Builder** | Choose which builder builds the slice. | | **Delete slice** | Remove the slice. | ### Move a slice to another feature [Section titled “Move a slice to another feature”](#move-a-slice-to-another-feature) 1. Open **Slice options** and choose **Move to feature**. 2. Pick the feature from the list. app.cratis.studio ![The Move to feature list showing the features of the board](/_astro/move-to-feature.NiddFhWy_ZunMwp.webp) Pick the feature the slice moves to. ### Assign a slice [Section titled “Assign a slice”](#assign-a-slice) Choose **Assign to**, then pick a team member. The slice menu offers **Reassign** and **Remove assignee** when someone is already assigned. A slice can also be assigned to an AI agent or to the System member. Studio manages the status while a slice is assigned to either. Assigning slices to AI agents is off until AI is turned on for the application: see [Project and application settings](/organization/project-and-application-settings/). ## Change a slice’s status [Section titled “Change a slice’s status”](#change-a-slices-status) Every slice has a status that says how far along it is. Select **Change status** in the slice header and pick one: **Not started**, **Draft**, **Analysis**, **Ready for implementation**, **In progress**, **Ready for review**, **Done**, **Blocked** and **Waiting for user interface**. What each one means is on the [Statuses and colors](/reference/statuses-and-colors/) page. ### Block a slice [Section titled “Block a slice”](#block-a-slice) Choose **Blocked**. Studio opens **Block slice** and asks: * **Why can it not be built?** * **Questions that need an answer, one per line** Fill them in and select **Block**. The reason and the questions stay with the slice, and the Progress board lists them. See [Track progress](/deliver/track-progress/). ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The new slice shows as a column in its feature, with its name, its status and its options in the header. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Commands and rules](/board/commands/) for the first thing to add to a State Change slice. * [Notes, comments and topics](/board/notes-comments-and-topics/) to discuss a slice. * [Slices](/event-modeling/slices/) for the idea behind the four types. * [Track progress](/deliver/track-progress/) to see every slice by status across the project.

# Translate events between modules and applications

> Publish a public event from one area, bring events from another area in with an input translation, and capture outside data as events.

A [translation](/reference/glossary/#translation) lets one area react to another without either reaching into the other. They share only a fact. In Acme Commerce, Catalog publishes **ProductPriceChanged** as a [public event](/reference/glossary/#public-event), and Storefront and Sales bring it in. For why modules and applications stay separate, read [Automations and translations](/event-modeling/automations-and-translations/). A translation is a **Translator** slice. It goes one of two ways: * An **output translation** sends events out to others, by publishing a public event. * An **input translation** brings events in from outside. You can change the direction later with **Make this an input translation** or **Make this an output translation**. Studio warns you about the configuration the change invalidates. ## Add a translation [Section titled “Add a translation”](#add-a-translation) 1. In a feature header, select **Add**, then **Add Slice**. 2. Choose **Translator**: *Translates external events into internal commands*. 3. Name the slice and choose its direction. ## Publish a public event (output translation) [Section titled “Publish a public event (output translation)”](#publish-a-public-event-output-translation) 1. On the output translation, add the private event the application records and wants to publish. 2. Select **Add projection**. It maps the private event onto the public contract. 3. Choose **Publish as this translation’s public event**. In Acme Catalog this is how **ProductPriceChanged** is published. The event is now **Public**: the contract this application publishes. A public event is a promise to others, so keep its properties stable. Only an output translation publishes. You can see an event’s visibility in the [event editor](/board/events/#make-an-event-public). app.cratis.studio ![The PublishPriceChange slice on the Catalog board: the events ProductDrafted and ProductPriceSet on the left, the PriceChangePublisher projection of 2 events beside them, and the ProductPriceChanged event marked PUBLIC below; the PublishRetirement slice sits to its right](/_astro/output-translation-catalog.CCauKV88_Z2wS9Mu.webp) In Acme Catalog, the projection card PriceChangePublisher (a projection of 2 events) turns ProductDrafted and ProductPriceSet into ProductPriceChanged. The PUBLIC tag under the event name shows it is published. PublishRetirement next to it does the same for retirements. app.cratis.studio ![The Warehouse board zoomed out, with the PublishDispatch output translation in the middle publishing ShipmentDispatched](/_astro/warehouse-board-overview.CN8nnUwJ_244pGv.webp) Acme Warehouse publishes ShipmentDispatched with an output translation, PublishDispatch, in the Dispatch feature. Its event is tagged public. ## Bring events in (input translation) [Section titled “Bring events in (input translation)”](#bring-events-in-input-translation) 1. On the input translation, select **Add external event to translate from**. 2. Search with **Search external events…** and pick the public event, such as **ProductPriceChanged** from Acme Catalog. 3. Say what to do with it: * **Add command**: a reactor carries out a command in your area. Read about commands in [Commands and rules](/board/commands/). * **Add CDC**: capture outside data as your own facts. ## Capture outside data as your own events [Section titled “Capture outside data as your own events”](#capture-outside-data-as-your-own-events) Besides another application’s public events, an input translation can capture records from outside, such as an **API** polled on a schedule, a **Webhook** or a message **Topic**. The translation editor opens from the capture or projection card. It has three columns: * **Outside**: what the source delivers, in its own words. * **Captured record**: your shape of it, keyed by the field that tells one record from another. Steps rename fields, build values from templates, translate values through a table or split a value. * **Our events**: what the capture records, each with a *when* (the first time a key is seen, when fields change, and so on). app.cratis.studio ![The UpdateAvailability slice on the Storefront board: the outside event StockLevelChangedAtStorefront on the left, the AvailabilityFeed card marked CDC with 1 event, and the AvailabilityChanged event it records below; the RetirementFeed capture is to the left of it](/_astro/capture-cdc-shop.7R7mmXbr_16OQ3n.webp) A capture appears as a card marked CDC with the number of events it captures. Here AvailabilityFeed takes the outside event StockLevelChangedAtStorefront and records AvailabilityChanged. Drag an outside field onto a field of the record, and a field of the record onto an event or onto its *when*. **Try it** runs the capture on the canvas with example input, and **Save as specification** keeps the run on the slice, to be run again whenever the design changes. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The output translation shows its public event. The input translation shows the external event and what it leads to. Open the public event in the [event editor](/board/events/): its visibility says **Public**. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Automations](/board/automations/): when the system reacts inside one area. * [Modules and features](/board/modules-and-features/): connect two modules on the board. * [Events](/board/events/): visibility, tags and constraints of an event. * [Check behavior](/check/): specifications for a translation.

# Check behavior

> Prove that your event model does what you mean, with examples written on each slice and by running the whole model as an application.

Check behavior ## Know the model works before anything is built A drawing that nobody can check is only a drawing. Studio lets you check a model two ways: small examples on each slice, and the whole model running as an application. Both ways use the model you already drew, so there is nothing to set up. A **specification** is one example, written as what has already happened, what is done, and what must follow. **Play** starts the model as a working application that you can click through. Use specifications to pin down the rules, and Play to see how the whole thing feels. app.cratis.studio ![Specifications under the Sales slices, each with a Caller, Given, When and Then row](/_astro/specifications-sales.Bj7mAU7S_1E0glY.webp) Specifications sit under the slice they check, so the examples and the model change together. ## What you can do here [Section titled “What you can do here”](#what-you-can-do-here) [Write specifications ](/check/specifications/)Describe a slice's behavior as Given, When, Then examples, and run them. [Generate specifications with AI ](/check/generate-specifications/)Let an agent draft the first scenarios from what the slice already says. [Play your model ](/check/play/)Run the model as an application, try a command and look at what it produced. ## Where to go next [Section titled “Where to go next”](#where-to-go-next) * [Specifications and Play](/event-modeling/specifications-and-play/) explains why the examples read the way they do, with the Acme Commerce shop as the example. * [Check the model](/board/check-the-model/) finds gaps on the board itself, before you write any example. * [Generate code](/deliver/generate-code/) turns a checked model into an application.

# Generate specifications with AI

> Let an AI agent draft Given, When, Then specifications for a slice from what the slice already models, then review and run them.

Writing the first few specifications for a slice is mostly typing out what the slice already says. Studio can do that for you: an AI agent reads the slice and drafts scenarios, which you then review like any other specification. What you'll learn * generate specifications for a slice * know what the agent reads, and what it leaves out * review, edit and run what it wrote ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * Your organization has connected an AI provider and an agent can use it. Generation is the agent’s job, so without one the action is not there at all. A maintainer sets this up under [Your organization](/organization/). * The slice has a command or events to write scenarios from. A slice with neither is refused with *The slice has no command and no events to write specifications from.* * You are on an editable board. ## Generate [Section titled “Generate”](#generate) 1. Open the specifications area under the slice. 2. Select **Generate specifications with AI**. It is the ✦ action beside the plus, **Add Specification**, under the specification. Point at it to see its tooltip. While it works, it reads **Generating specifications…**. 3. Wait for the new specifications to appear under the slice, the normal case first. 4. Read each one. Check the values, and that the **Then** row says what you want. 5. Select **Run this slice’s specifications**. app.cratis.studio ![The specification of the BasketView slice with its WHEN row of events and its THEN row of read models, and below it the plus for Add Specification and the star action whose tooltip reads Generate specifications with AI](/_astro/generate-specifications-button.DihMlCTK_1hddIC.webp) Under the slice's specification, the plus adds one by hand and the star next to it, with the tooltip Generate specifications with AI, drafts them. There is nothing to type. You do not describe the slice to the agent, because the slice is the description: its command and rules, its events and their fields are already on the board, and a description typed again could only disagree with them. If it fails, the action says **Could not generate specifications. Make sure an AI provider is configured in the AI settings.** Check the provider, and try again. ## What you get [Section titled “What you get”](#what-you-get) Each generated specification has a short name for its scenario, the events that already happened, the values the command is called with, and the events or validation messages that must follow. They are ordinary specifications: edit them, rename them, delete the ones you do not want, and add your own. * Names that clash with existing specifications, or with each other, are skipped. See [names](/check/specifications/#names). * Only the scenarios that Studio can match to the slice are recorded, so a draft can contain fewer than the agent wrote. * Values are converted to the types of the fields, so a number stays a number. Caution A generated specification is a draft by a language model. Run it, and read it: it can describe a scenario you did not mean, or agree with the model where the model is the thing that is wrong. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The new specifications are on the slice, each with a result after you run them. A failing one is useful: either the model or the example needs a fix. See [Write specifications](/check/specifications/) for what each row can hold. Recap You generated specifications for a slice, read them and ran them. Next, [play the whole model](/check/play/) to see it work as an application. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Write specifications](/check/specifications/): every row a specification can hold. * [AI providers](/organization/ai-providers/): connect the provider that generation needs. * [Explain and generate with AI](/working-with-ai/explain-and-generate/): other things an agent can draft for you.

# Play your model

> Run an event model as a working application in an isolated session, try a command, and look at the events and read models it produced.

Play runs the event model you have open as a real application, so you can use it before anything is built: fill in a form, run a command, and see the events and read-model state it produces. It is the quickest way to find out whether the model does what you meant. What you'll learn * start Play for the open event model, and stop it * run a modeled command and check what it produced * move between the application, its API, its events and its read models * recover when Play does not start Play is a sandbox Play explores modeled behavior. It is not a deployment, and a good session is not proof that the generated code is ready to ship. Everything you create in a session lives only as long as the session. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * Open an event model on its board. Play starts this event model, not every one in the application. * Pick a small path through the model: one command, the event it produces, and a read model that shows that event as state. The command and event belong in a state-change slice, the read model in a state-view slice. * Decide what state you expect after the command. That gives you something to check. * Give the command exactly one **Key** property, in the command’s property editor. It must be a single value, not an object or a list. The key says which thing the command acts on. * Supply the values that the command’s events require, and resolve the board’s warnings about missing event values. Play checks the model before it starts, and refuses a model that has them. [Generate code](/deliver/generate-code/) explains those warnings. ## Start, try and inspect [Section titled “Start, try and inspect”](#start-try-and-inspect) 1. On the board, select the play icon **Run Solution** in the top bar. 2. Wait while it reads **Starting stage…**. The application’s frontend then appears in place of the board. 3. In the application, run the command you modeled, with its required values. 4. Select **Read models** in the Play toolbar and open the read model that shows the event. Compare its current instances with the state you expected. 5. Select **Chronicle Workbench** to see the events the session recorded. 6. Select the stop icon **Stop Solution** when you are finished. Studio returns to the board and stops the session in the background. app.cratis.studio ![The run menu in the top bar of the board, open, with Run Solution above Run permanently or for a period](/_astro/run-options.VJxmi9vy_Z19BJpg.webp) Open the run menu in the top bar. Run Solution starts a Play session; the entry below it publishes the application instead. What you will see: the board is replaced by a full-size view of your application, with a small toolbar at its top right. **Stop Solution** comes first in that toolbar, followed by the four surfaces below. ## The four surfaces [Section titled “The four surfaces”](#the-four-surfaces) Play shows one surface at a time. Use the toolbar to move between them. | Surface | What it shows | | ----------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- | | **Frontend** | The application’s screens, drawn from your model. | | **Backend API** | The reference of the API the session hosts. | | **Chronicle Workbench** | The events the session recorded, in a browser of its own. | | **Read models** | The read models of the session and their current instances. | Switching surface reloads the view while a loading indicator shows. The session, and what you created in it, stays. The **Runtime engine** is shown with its status. Refusals while you try things are named: **Denied (403)** when the caller is not allowed, **Validation refused (400)** when a rule refused the input, and **Not admitted (501)** when the session does not run that part of the model. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) If the frontend loaded, the session started. The read model shows what your command did. If it differs from what you meant, return to the board and write a [specification](/check/specifications/) that states the behavior you expect, then run it until it passes. ## If Play does not start [Section titled “If Play does not start”](#if-play-does-not-start) * **Studio refuses the model.** It returns you to the board with a reason, such as a duplicate slice name inside a feature or a missing key. Fix what it names and start again; starting with the same problem fails the same way. * **The stage did not start.
