Stop and try again.** Select **Stop Solution**, then **Run Solution** again, which creates a new session. Switching surface only changes the view of the session you have, so it does not help. If a new session also fails, ask your Studio operator to look into it. Recap You started a Play session, ran a command, and checked the events and read models it produced. Next, put the behavior you saw into [specifications](/check/specifications/) so it stays checked. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Specifications and Play](/event-modeling/specifications-and-play/): why Play and specifications complement each other. * [Generate code](/deliver/generate-code/): the board warnings Play refuses to start with. * [Publish and collect feedback](/deliver/publish-and-feedback/): keep an application running at its own address.

# Write specifications

> Describe what a slice must do as Given, When, Then examples on the board, run them, and see which ones pass.

A specification is an example that a slice has to live up to. It reads as a short story in three rows: **Given** what has already happened, **When** something happens, **Then** what the slice does about it. You write it on the slice itself, and Studio runs it for you, so the model is checked before anything is built. For the idea behind specifications, read [Specifications and Play](/event-modeling/specifications-and-play/). What you'll learn * add a specification to a slice and fill in its Given, When and Then rows * say who is calling, and what a refusal looks like * run one specification, a slice’s specifications or every slice’s * express that nothing happens, that a fact arrives twice, or that a query returns a result ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * Open an event model on an editable board. A read-only board shows specifications but no run results and no notices. * Have a slice with something to check: a command and the events it produces, an automation, a translator or a read model with a query. A slice with nothing in it cannot be specified. ## Add a specification [Section titled “Add a specification”](#add-a-specification) The specifications of a slice sit below it on the board. app.cratis.studio ![Two specifications under the Sales slices. The first, A small order approves itself, shows a Caller, a Given event OrderReceived, a When command and a Then row with four events](/_astro/specifications-sales.Bj7mAU7S_1E0glY.webp) A specification for the Sales slice: the caller, what already happened, what is done, and the events that must follow. 1. Select **Add Specification** under the slice. The new specification gets the first unused numbered name in that slice. 2. Rename it to describe the scenario, such as *A small order approves itself*. 3. In the **Given** row, select **Add item** to add the events that have already happened, and give them their values. Select **Use given context** to start from a named starting situation your team has already described (see [Given contexts and seed data](/discover/given-contexts-and-seed-data/)). 4. In the **When** row, fill in the command with the values it is called with. An automation or a translator has an event here instead, and a state view has a query. 5. In the **Then** row, add what must follow: events, read models or one error. 6. Select **Run specification**. The add button in each row offers only what that row can hold for this slice, so you cannot put the wrong kind of thing in the wrong place. ## Say who is calling [Section titled “Say who is calling”](#say-who-is-calling) Open the **Caller** of a specification to say who makes the call: | Choice | Meaning | | ----------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **None** | The specification does not say. | | **Anonymous** | Nobody is signed in. | | **Authenticated** | Anybody who is signed in. | | **Persona** | One of your [personas](/discover/personas/). Choose which in **Choose a persona**. | | **Roles** | One or more roles. Add them with **Add a role**. | To check that a protected command refuses someone, choose the caller and then **Expect the command to be denied**. A query has the same choice: **Expect the query to be denied**. A specification that expects a denial holds no results. ## Say what must follow [Section titled “Say what must follow”](#say-what-must-follow) A specification states either a success or a refusal, never both. Mixing them is refused, because it would not be clear what you are checking. * **Events** that the slice appends. If the order does not matter, choose **Events in any order**. * **Read models**, to check the state a read model ends up in. * **Add error** for a validation message the command is refused with, or **Add rule or constraint error** for a rule or constraint you modeled. If the rule is later removed from the model, the specification says **Rule no longer defined**. * **No events**, to say that nothing happens. See [When nothing should happen](#when-nothing-should-happen). ## Run specifications [Section titled “Run specifications”](#run-specifications) You can run specifications at three levels: | Select | What runs | | ----------------------------------------- | --------------------------------- | | **Run specification** | The one specification. | | **Run this slice’s specifications** | Every specification of the slice. | | **Run the specifications of every slice** | Every specification on the board. | Studio runs them in a sandbox and shows a result on each specification: **Not run**, **Running…**, **Last run succeeded**, **Last run failed**, **Last run was inconclusive** or **Could not run**. Select **Cancel run** to stop one that is going. Open a result with **Show the whole result** to read the detail, and **Copy the result** to paste it somewhere. When Studio could not read the model at all, the result gives the line and offers **Report this**. A passing specification says the slice behaves as the example expects, in that scenario. It does not say every other scenario works, and it is not a running application. To try the whole model as an application, [play it](/check/play/). ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) Each specification shows **Last run succeeded**. If one fails, open the result: it tells you which expectation was not met. Fix the model or the example, and run it again. ## What each part checks [Section titled “What each part checks”](#what-each-part-checks) The sections below are the reference for the less obvious parts of a specification. ### Names [Section titled “Names”](#names) Give each specification in a slice a name that stays distinct once the model is generated. Studio ignores punctuation, spaces and capitalization when it compares names, so *register author*, *Register Author* and *registerAuthor* clash. A blank name, or one with only punctuation, becomes *Unnamed*. The board shows **Clashes with … when generated; rename one.** beneath each clashing name, even when the specification is collapsed. Rename one to describe a different scenario. Studio never renames them for you, and an export is refused while two names clash. When AI [generates specifications](/check/generate-specifications/), it skips names that clash with existing ones. ### Missing required values [Section titled “Missing required values”](#missing-required-values) Making a command or event property required can leave older specifications incomplete. The board then shows **Missing required values** beneath the name of each affected specification, and outlines the step that lacks a value. Open that step and enter the value. Nested properties are shown as paths. The notice follows your changes after a short pause. A missing notice is not proof that a specification is complete: while the check is pending or unavailable nothing is shown, and given contexts, text-only steps and expectations on read models and queries are not marked. Running the specification or [previewing](/board/) the model still reports those. ### Query specifications [Section titled “Query specifications”](#query-specifications) A state view can be specified through one of its queries instead of its projection. In the **When** row, select **Add query** and choose the query. The specification then reads: **Given** these read models, **When** this query is run with these arguments, **Then** the result is these read models. * **When** shows the query as its own card. Open it to set one argument for each parameter. A query without parameters has nothing to open. * **Given** holds read models only, and you can add several. Select **Define read model as given state** and fill in their values. * **Then** holds the results. **Add result** adds one. A query that returns one item takes exactly one result. A query that returns a list takes as many as you add, in the order they must come back. A specification holds a query or a command, never both. Choosing a query is refused while the specification already holds a command, events, a returned value or a redelivery. ### Generated identifiers and values [Section titled “Generated identifiers and values”](#generated-identifiers-and-values) Some values are not typed in by whoever runs a command: a receipt number, a reference, an identifier the command creates. Mark the property as **Generated** in the command’s schema editor, and the generated application creates it. A required identifier can also be marked **Key** and **Generated**: the key is what the command acts on, and a specification does not have to supply it. A specification cannot predict a generated value, so it pins one. Open the command in the **When** row and enter the value for the generated property. The run uses it in place of the one the command would create, so the **Then** row can state the exact value. The command’s key is never pinned: the value you enter for it stays an entered value. When a property stops being generated, its pinned value is cleared on the next save. Only some properties can be generated: a unique identifier, or a text [concept](/discover/concepts/) with a generation strategy. A plain number, date or text without a strategy cannot, and saving the schema refuses it. ### When nothing should happen [Section titled “When nothing should happen”](#when-nothing-should-happen) **No events** in the **Then** row says the slice must append nothing, and the run fails if it appends anything. An empty **Then** row does not say this. It means you have not yet written the outcome, so Studio never reads an empty row as “nothing happens”. Use it when: * a command is a retry or does nothing new, such as confirming an order that is already confirmed; * an automation ignores an event it should ignore, such as a reaction that only acts above a threshold. It is offered on state change, automation and translator slices, and only while **Then** is empty. It cannot be combined with events or errors: remove those first, or remove **No events** before adding them. A state view never offers it. Remove the card with its remove button to go back to an empty row. ### When a fact arrives twice [Section titled “When a fact arrives twice”](#when-a-fact-arrives-twice) After a failure, an event can reach an automation or a translator more than once. **Redeliver a given event** in the **When** row tells the slice’s reaction about one of the specification’s own given events again. The row reads *when Event is redelivered to Slice*. Pair it with **Then** to say what happens the second time: the same follow-up again, or, with **No events**, nothing. Studio offers it only when all of these hold: * the slice is an automation or a translator; * the specification has at least one given event, and the redelivered event is one of them; * the reaction observes that event. A reaction on a timer, or with a custom trigger and no events read, observes none; change the slice’s trigger first; * the **When** row holds no command with values. A redelivery is the whole **When**, and a command with values must be removed first. Remove the redelivery with its remove button to choose again. Removing the given event it points at also removes the redelivery. An [exported Screenplay file](/share/export/) states the redelivery only when it can pick out exactly one given occurrence. A specification that gives the same event twice with identical values leaves nothing to pick out, so the export leaves the redelivery out and says so in its warnings. Give the occurrences different values to keep it. Example: an automation sends a receipt when an order is placed, and a recovery redelivery must not send a second one. 1. Add a specification and give it the **Order placed** event in **Given**. 2. In **When**, choose **Redeliver a given event** and pick **Order placed**. 3. In **Then**, choose **No events**. 4. Select **Run specification**. Recap You added a specification to a slice, said who is calling, wrote what must follow, and ran it. Next, let AI [draft specifications](/check/generate-specifications/) for you, or [play the whole model](/check/play/) as an application. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Specifications and Play](/event-modeling/specifications-and-play/): the idea behind the examples. * [Given contexts and seed data](/discover/given-contexts-and-seed-data/): reusable starting situations. * [Export a model](/share/export/): specifications travel with the Screenplay file.

# Build and deliver

> Turn an event model into working software. Keep the model as the deliverable, generate code from it, or let a builder or coding agent build it, and follow the progress on a board.

An event model describes what a system does. This section is about what comes after: turning that description into software, and keeping track of how far along each part is. app.cratis.studio ![The Progress board of Acme Commerce with one column per slice status and a card for every slice](/_astro/progress-board.BabxSoyZ_Z1K1zKw.webp) The Progress board shows every slice of a project by status, so the whole team sees how far the build has come. ## Three ways to get from model to software [Section titled “Three ways to get from model to software”](#three-ways-to-get-from-model-to-software) You choose how a [slice](/reference/glossary/#slice) becomes software, per application, in the application’s settings. Nothing about the model changes when you choose differently. * **Keep the model as the deliverable.** The model is complete and exact enough to hand to a team, to export, or to keep next to code that people write by hand. Studio writes no code. * **Generate code from the model.** Studio renders the code for commands, events, read models, rules and constraints from the model, the same way every time. You preview it, generate it, and review the result before it is merged. You can also let an AI agent add the parts that cannot be rendered. * **Let a builder or a coding agent build it.** A builder is a program that registers with your organization and builds slices on a machine you control, for a stack you choose. Developers can also connect their own coding agent to a board, so the agent models and builds alongside the team. The first two are covered here. The third is described for developers in [For developers](/developers/); start with [Builders](/developers/builders/). ## What you can do here [Section titled “What you can do here”](#what-you-can-do-here) [Choose how slices are built ](/deliver/implementation-modes/)Pick Screenplay only, Static code, Static code + AI or AI only for an application. [Track progress ](/deliver/track-progress/)Change a slice's status, assign it, and see every slice on the Progress board. [Generate and review code ](/deliver/generate-code/)Preview what would be written, generate it, follow the run and review the work. [Connect a repository ](/deliver/connect-a-repository/)Give a project the repository that generated code is written to. [Keep a board in sync with Git ](/deliver/git-sync/)Commit the board's Screenplay files to a folder of the repository, and bring changes back. [Link slices to issues ](/deliver/issue-links/)Link a slice to an issue in GitHub, Jira, Linear or another tracker, and keep their status in step. [Publish and collect feedback ](/deliver/publish-and-feedback/)Publish an application and collect feedback from the people who use it. Experimental. ## Where to go next [Section titled “Where to go next”](#where-to-go-next) * To share the model without building anything, see [Share and export](/share/). * To connect your own coding agent or builder, see [For developers](/developers/). * To set up the people, AI providers and integrations that building depends on, see [Your organization](/organization/).

# Connect a repository

> Give a project the repository that generated code, pull requests and Git sync use, or work with a local repository only.

Every project has a repository: the place where Studio commits generated code, and where a board can keep a copy of its model. By default that is a local repository kept by Studio. Connect a GitHub repository to get branches and pull requests, to keep a board in sync with Git, and to create issues. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * A maintainer of your organization has connected GitHub once, under **Settings** > **Integrations** > **Source Control**, with **Connect GitHub**. That installs the Studio GitHub App on the GitHub accounts your code lives in. Studio keeps no personal token. See [Connect GitHub](/organization/github/) for the organization side. * You are allowed to change the project’s settings. * The GitHub App installation can reach the repository you want. If **The installation cannot reach any repository** shows up, choose which repositories the installation can reach on GitHub. ## Connect a GitHub repository [Section titled “Connect a GitHub repository”](#connect-a-github-repository) 1. Open **Project Settings** and select the **Source control** tab. 2. Under **Source control provider**, choose **GitHub**. 3. If your organization has more than one GitHub installation, choose one under **Installation**. 4. Under **Repository**, choose the repository from the list, then select **Connect repository**. The tab then shows the **Connected repository** and tells you where it stands: * **Ready to publish generated branches.** Everything works. * **This installation needs to be reapproved on GitHub before Studio can publish generated branches.** An organization admin on GitHub must grant the widened permissions. **Review on GitHub** takes you there. * **This installation is no longer available on GitHub. Reconnect the repository to continue.** * **Studio cannot read this installation’s settings on GitHub**, so it cannot tell whether it grants what code generation needs. If your organization has not connected GitHub yet, the tab says so and points to **Organization Settings**, **Integrations**. ## Use a different repository for one application [Section titled “Use a different repository for one application”](#use-a-different-repository-for-one-application) A project’s applications use the project’s repository unless you say otherwise. To give one application its own repository, open its **Application Settings**, select the **Source control** tab and choose **Use a separate repository**. Choose **Use the project’s repository** to go back. Studio refuses the change while the application has active work sessions: complete or abandon them first. app.cratis.studio ![The Source control tab of the Acme Sales application settings with the choice between the project's repository and a separate one](/_astro/application-settings-source-control.DoGIj8Ch_Z25XQx2.webp) An application can use the project's repository or one of its own. The **Repository** tab of the same settings holds the **Base path**: the folder inside the repository where the application lives. app.cratis.studio ![The Repository tab of the Acme Sales application settings with the Base path field and the resolved path](/_astro/application-settings-repository.BG_Jcx9j_ZtxMxP.webp) The base path says where inside the repository the application lives. ## Work with a local repository only [Section titled “Work with a local repository only”](#work-with-a-local-repository-only) Choose **None (local repository only)** under **Source control provider**. Studio then works in a local repository and cannot publish branches or open pull requests. [Generating code](/deliver/generate-code/) works this way too: the work is committed to a work-session branch and merged into the project’s default branch when you accept it. ## GitLab and Azure DevOps [Section titled “GitLab and Azure DevOps”](#gitlab-and-azure-devops) You can name a project’s repository in GitLab or Azure DevOps, from a connection your organization made under **Integrations**: choose the **Connection**, then the repository, and select **Use this repository**. These repositories are not fully supported yet. Studio cannot yet push generated code, open merge requests, receive webhooks, sync boards or run builds with them. See [Organization integrations](/organization/integrations/). ## Disconnect a repository [Section titled “Disconnect a repository”](#disconnect-a-repository) Select **Disconnect** on the **Source control** tab and confirm. Studio returns to the project’s local repository as it was before connecting, and keeps any history the repository already had. Changes already in GitHub stay there. ## Choose where issues go [Section titled “Choose where issues go”](#choose-where-issues-go) The same tab has an **Issues** section. By default a project’s issues go where its source control is. You can send them to a repository of its own. See [Link slices to issues](/deliver/issue-links/). ## What happens next [Section titled “What happens next”](#what-happens-next) * [Generate code](/deliver/generate-code/) and review the pull request it opens. * [Keep a board in sync with Git](/deliver/git-sync/) to store the model next to the code. * [Link slices to issues](/deliver/issue-links/) and choose where issues go. * [Connect integrations](/organization/integrations/), the organization side of the connection.

# Generate and review code

> Preview what Studio would write for a slice or feature, generate it, follow the run, and review the work before it is merged.

When a slice or feature is ready to implement, Studio can write the code for it from the model, and write it again after the model changes. You see the plan first, follow the run while it works, and review the result before it lands. This page is for the whole team: a product owner can start a run and read the review, and a developer can look closer at the files and the diff. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * Choose a code-generating [implementation mode](/deliver/implementation-modes/) for the application: **Static code**, **Static code + AI** or **AI only**. In **Screenplay only**, Studio generates no code and **Generation runs** tells you so. * The two AI modes need AI to be enabled and a code-generation agent to be configured. * Check the model first: [findings and storylines](/board/check-the-model/) catch the problems that would otherwise stop a run. * You do not need a GitHub connection. Without one, Studio commits to the project’s local repository. See [Connect a repository](/deliver/connect-a-repository/). Generating needs no assignee and no particular status, so you can look at the output before you hand the work to anyone. ## Preview a slice [Section titled “Preview a slice”](#preview-a-slice) Preview shows what a run would write. It starts no job and writes nothing. 1. Open the slice’s menu with **Slice options**. 2. Choose **Preview generated code**. Studio opens **Generated code for** the slice and says *Read-only preview. Nothing is written.* 3. Select a file in the list to open it in a read-only tab. You can keep several tabs open and switch between them. app.cratis.studio ![The options menu of the AddToBasket slice with Generate, Preview generated code and Link the slice to an issue](/_astro/slice-options-menu.BNMjn6sA_1qJCcD.webp) Generate and Preview generated code are in the slice's menu. The preview lists the files a run would commit for the current version of the model: the Screenplay baseline, metadata and deletions included. The **Base commit** is the commit on the default branch that a new run would start from. Changes you have not committed are not part of it, so open the preview again after you change the model or the repository. If the application has no scaffold yet, the preview also lists the files labeled **Created by scaffolding the application first**. A separate job creates the scaffold before the feature’s run starts. What you can inspect depends on the mode: * **Static code**: the Screenplay baseline and the planned code files. * **Static code + AI**: the static plan. What the AI step adds is produced when it runs, so it cannot be listed file by file. * **AI only**: the Screenplay baseline. The code is written by an agent, so it cannot be predicted. * **Screenplay only**: the baseline only. When the model cannot be rendered, the preview says why: it names the slice, concept or file that prevents it, under headings such as **The model does not compile**, **Diagnostics** and **Gaps**. A render marked **Degraded** has placeholders, so review every gap before you rely on it. The feature’s menu has **Preview generated code** too, for all its slices at once. ## Generate code for a slice or feature [Section titled “Generate code for a slice or feature”](#generate-code-for-a-slice-or-feature) 1. Open the slice’s or the feature’s menu and choose **Generate**. A slice generates only itself. A feature generates all its slices, including those in sub-features. 2. Read **Generate code?** and confirm. If a selected slice is **Done** or **Ready for review**, Studio warns that it will move to **Ready for review** when the generated code is committed. 3. Studio says **Generation requested**. Follow the run, as described next. app.cratis.studio ![The options menu of a feature with assign, generate, preview generated code, focus, explain, export and context for an agent](/_astro/feature-options-menu.Cq0F3SM9_ZGTG9Q.webp) A feature's menu generates all its slices in one run. If Studio refuses, it says **Code generation was refused** and gives the reason. Slices that already have a generation claim or are **In progress** cannot start another run. You can also let Studio start the run. Assigning a slice that is ready for implementation to the **System** member starts static code generation. Assigning it to a code-generation agent starts AI implementation. A person as assignee starts nothing, and slices that someone already claimed are not started again. With **Run autonomously** turned on in the project’s AI settings, Studio runs the whole flow by itself: a slice whose analysis comes back sound is assigned to the code-generation agent and made ready for implementation. ### Status changes during a run [Section titled “Status changes during a run”](#status-changes-during-a-run) During generation, the requested slices move to **In progress**. When the generated code is committed, they move to **Ready for review**, even if they were **Done** before. If the run fails or changes nothing, Studio puts each slice back to the status it had, **Not started** included. While a slice is being generated, its status cannot be changed by hand. ## Follow a run [Section titled “Follow a run”](#follow-a-run) Open **Generation runs** from the feature’s menu. It lists the runs of the feature, and for each one its status, when it started, how long it took, the outcome, the pull request if there is one, and the reason when it failed. A run goes through four stages: **Prepare**, **Generate**, **Verify** and **Publish**. Verification builds the result and runs its checks, listed under **Verification gates**. A run can end as **Published**, **Failed**, **Cancelled**, **Nothing to generate** or **Waiting for application scaffold**. On the slice itself, **Show implementation output** opens the **Implementation** panel with the live output of the agent while it works. A slice that depends on another slice waits until that one is generated and merged, and starts by itself afterwards. When a file you edited and the new render both claim the same path, Studio lists it under **File conflicts** with the three versions (**Mine**, **Generated** and **Baseline**). Choose **Keep mine** or **Take generated**. The choice is recorded for the next run. It does not change the repository now. ## Review the work [Section titled “Review the work”](#review-the-work) When a run has produced code, the slice is **Ready for review** and shows a **Review** button in its header. Select it to open the review of the work session that generated the slice. 1. Read the **Description** tab. It holds the **Release notes** Studio wrote for this work. They become the pull request description, and any edit is published to the pull request before it is merged. 2. Open the **Review** tab. **Changes** lists the changed files as added, modified, deleted or renamed. Select a file to see what changed, **Inline** or **Side by side**. 3. Read the **Independent review**. When the work contains code written by AI, a different AI provider reviews it. The review says either *The independent review found nothing to change.*, how many things it found, or that it has not concluded yet. 4. Select **Publish** to put the work on the pull request, or **Accept and merge** to merge it. When the merge succeeds, the slices are **Done**. With a connected repository the work lands as a pull request on a branch Studio created. Without one, the release notes stay with Studio and nothing is published; accepting merges the work into the project’s local default branch. ## Which files can change [Section titled “Which files can change”](#which-files-can-change) Static modes write only files that belong to the requested slices, and can create missing files that the whole application shares. A shared file that differs from what Studio would render is not overwritten: the run refuses instead. When you generate again, Studio also looks at files the model no longer produces, for example after you delete a command. It removes such a file only when it recorded that it rendered the file, the file is still exactly as rendered, and nothing else in the application owns it. A file you edited, a file an agent wrote and a file from before Studio recorded provenance are kept and reported under **Generation runs**. Delete them yourself when you no longer need them. In **Static code + AI**, Studio commits the static output first and then asks the agent to add implementation from the slice descriptions. The agent cannot edit or delete files that the static render plans, nor model or scaffold files. If the AI step fails, times out or is refused, the static result is kept and the failure is shown in **Generation runs**. The static result must pass verification before it is published. In AI only mode, slice selection does not limit what is written The agent is asked to implement only the requested slices, but it may write anywhere in the domain. Regenerating a feature that already has an accepted AI patch does not replay the patch. Studio carries accepted work forward, and reports a conflict instead of silently recreating a file the patch deleted. In **AI only** mode, Studio refuses to regenerate such a feature; **Static code + AI** skips the AI step and says why in **Generation runs**. ## Prepare the model for generation [Section titled “Prepare the model for generation”](#prepare-the-model-for-generation) ### Give state-change commands a key [Section titled “Give state-change commands a key”](#give-state-change-commands-a-key) A command that changes state needs a key for static code: the property that identifies what the command acts on. On the board, open the command’s property editor and turn on **Key** for exactly one scalar property. A Screenplay import keeps the command’s identifier as its key, so you do not mark it again. ### Resolve required event value warnings [Section titled “Resolve required event value warnings”](#resolve-required-event-value-warnings) A slice’s header shows warnings when an event has a required value that nothing supplies. Expand the indicator, or open the command’s property editor, to see the command, the event and the property. Supply a required command property with the same name, rename one to match, or make the event property optional if the value really is optional. The warnings update as you edit. ## Application merge policy [Section titled “Application merge policy”](#application-merge-policy) On the **Implementation** tab of Application Settings, **Merge policy** says who merges a generation’s pull request: * **Studio merges verified pull requests automatically** is the default. * **A person merges every pull request** keeps merging in your hands, even when verification and the independent review pass. app.cratis.studio ![The Implementation tab of Application Settings with the implementation mode and the merge policy](/_astro/application-settings-implementation.Cd_HhlQ3_Z1oIEq5.webp) The merge policy sits next to the implementation mode. Studio does not merge pull requests by itself yet. Until it does, a person merges with **Accept and merge**, whichever policy is chosen. Choosing the automatic policy is recorded and shows as suspended. ## Local work sessions [Section titled “Local work sessions”](#local-work-sessions) Without a remote repository, completing a work session merges its files into the project’s default branch and saves a durable copy before the session is marked completed. New sessions and the **Files** page use that saved history, even after a restart. If saving fails, the session stays finished and keeps its working copy so you can try completing it again. A local default branch that has diverged or has uncommitted changes is refused rather than overwritten. ## Abandoning a generation session [Section titled “Abandoning a generation session”](#abandoning-a-generation-session) A feature’s generation session stays open until its pull request is merged or you abandon it. If it is stuck, or you no longer want its result, open **Generation runs** from the feature’s menu and choose **Abandon session**. The button shows only while the feature has an open session. Studio asks **Abandon this generation session?** and shows what happens: * The uncommitted work in the session’s working copy is deleted. * The pull request on the source control host is closed, and Studio comments on it that the session was abandoned. If the comment cannot be posted, the session is still abandoned. * The branch itself stays on the host. Delete it there when you no longer need it. Choose **Abandon** to confirm, or **Keep session**. Abandoning is refused when the session is no longer the feature’s open one, for example because it was completed elsewhere. Generate again to start a new session. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Choose how slices are built](/deliver/implementation-modes/) * [Track progress](/deliver/track-progress/) * [Connect a repository](/deliver/connect-a-repository/) * [Builders](/developers/builders/), for builders and coding agents

# Keep a board in sync with Git

> Connect a board to a branch and folder of the project's repository, so the model is committed next to the code and every change has a history.

A board can be connected to a branch and a folder of its repository. Studio then commits the board’s [Screenplay](/reference/glossary/#screenplay) files there. The model lives next to the code it describes, and every change to it has a history. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * The project has a repository connected. See [Connect a repository](/deliver/connect-a-repository/). * Git sync works through the GitHub App installation of your organization (see [Connect GitHub](/organization/github/)). Studio stores no Git credentials for it. ## Connect a board [Section titled “Connect a board”](#connect-a-board) app.cratis.studio ![The Export submenu of Project options listing Screenplay file, Screenplay folder, Mermaid, Event Modeling JSON, Context for an agent, Share snapshot link, Keep in sync with Git and Draw on Miro](/_astro/export-menu.Cr3REasF_Z1Hxy7E.webp) Keep in sync with Git is in the Export submenu. 1. On the event model board, open **Project options**, then **Export**, and choose **Keep in sync with Git**. 2. In **Keep the board in sync with Git**, choose the **Branch**. 3. Enter the **Folder** inside the repository, such as `model`. The contents of the folder are replaced by the board’s files, so use a folder that holds nothing else. 4. Turn on **Offer changes pushed to these files for import into the board** if people may also edit the files in the repository and you want those edits offered back to the board. 5. Select **Keep in sync**. app.cratis.studio ![The Keep the board in sync with Git dialog with Branch set to main, Folder set to model, the option Offer changes pushed to these files for import into the board ticked, and the Keep in sync and Close buttons](/_astro/git-sync-dialog.BPUmpMSK_Z2sRf69.webp) Fill in Branch and Folder, decide on the import option, then select Keep in sync. The dialog warns that the contents of the folder are replaced by the board's files. The dialog then shows **Synced to** the folder and branch, whether repository changes are offered for import, and the time and commit of the last sync. ## What is committed [Section titled “What is committed”](#what-is-committed) The folder holds what the Screenplay export gives you: the `.play` documents, the screens’ sketches, mockups and images, and a `.screenplay/notes.json` file with the slices’ [issue links](/deliver/issue-links/). Images are committed byte for byte. Each commit message names the board and lists the files added, changed and removed. ## When it is committed [Section titled “When it is committed”](#when-it-is-committed) Studio waits until the board has been quiet for a while before it commits, so a modeling session becomes a few commits instead of one per change. By default that is about a minute without changes. A board that keeps changing is committed anyway after at most ten minutes. ## Read the status [Section titled “Read the status”](#read-the-status) The dialog says where the sync stands: | Status | What it means | | ----------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- | | **In sync with the repository at** a commit | The repository holds the board as it is. | | **Waiting for the board to settle before the next commit.** | The board changed recently. Studio commits when it goes quiet. | | **Someone changed the board’s files in the repository.** | Studio has not committed since. See the next section. | | **Both the board and its files in the repository changed.** | Studio has not overwritten either. | | **The last sync failed** with a reason | Fix what the reason says, and Studio tries again. | ## When the repository changed [Section titled “When the repository changed”](#when-the-repository-changed) Studio never overwrites a change it has not seen. When the folder changed in the repository since Studio’s last commit, Studio stops committing and the dialog lists **Files that differ**. Choose one of two ways forward: * **Import the repository’s version.** It goes through the usual import preview, and brings the sketches, mockups, images and issue links in the folder back with it. * **Commit Studio’s version on top.** The repository’s change stays in the history, but the files in the folder become the board’s. Studio asks you to confirm first. When **Offer changes pushed to these files for import into the board** is on, a repository change that has no matching board change is offered for import. When both changed, the conflict is reported and neither side is overwritten. ## Stop syncing [Section titled “Stop syncing”](#stop-syncing) Select **Stop syncing** in the dialog and confirm. Studio stops committing the board. Nothing already committed is removed. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Connect a repository](/deliver/connect-a-repository/) * [Link slices to issues](/deliver/issue-links/), whose links travel with the files * [Export the model](/share/export/)

# Choose how slices are built

> Set an application's implementation mode to keep the model as the deliverable, render code from it, add an AI agent, or let an agent implement from the model alone.

The **implementation mode** says how an application’s modeled behavior is turned into code once a slice is ready for implementation. Each application has its own, so one application can stay a pure model while another generates code. ## The four modes [Section titled “The four modes”](#the-four-modes) | Mode | What Studio does | Needs AI | | -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------- | | **Screenplay only** | The Screenplay is the deliverable. Studio exports and maintains it and generates no code. | No | | **Static code** | Stage, Cratis’s application renderer, renders the deterministic code: commands, events, read models, rules and constraints. No AI agent takes part, and a gap that needs an implementation blocks the run instead of being filled in. | No | | **Static code + AI** | Stage renders the code first, then the assigned AI agent refines it within the rendered shapes. | Yes | | **AI only** | The assigned AI agent implements from the Screenplay and the model’s context alone, without rendered files as a baseline. The result is not repeatable: two runs can differ. | Yes | The two AI modes need AI to be enabled for the application and an AI provider and a code-generation agent configured for the organization. If neither is set up, Studio says so under the mode: **Configure an AI provider and an agent in Settings to choose a mode that involves AI.** See [Your organization](/organization/) for setting up providers and agents. If AI is switched off for an application that uses an AI mode, no AI agent takes part, and slices are implemented as **Static code** until AI is enabled again. ## Choose a mode [Section titled “Choose a mode”](#choose-a-mode) 1. Open the application’s settings: select **Application Settings** in the project drawer. 2. Open the **Implementation** tab. 3. Under **Implementation mode**, choose one of the four modes. Studio saves your choice at once. If the choice is greyed out, you are not allowed to change the application’s settings. app.cratis.studio ![The Implementation tab of the Acme Sales application settings with the mode Static code and the merge policy](/_astro/application-settings-implementation.Cd_HhlQ3_Z1oIEq5.webp) The Implementation tab of Application Settings. The mode is chosen per application. An application that has not chosen a mode yet runs in the mode Studio derives from the earlier settings of the project and application. The tab tells you when that is the case, until you choose one. The mode is captured when a run starts. Changing it later never changes a run that is already under way. ## What else is on the tab [Section titled “What else is on the tab”](#what-else-is-on-the-tab) * **Merge policy** says who merges the pull request a generation produces. See [Generate and review code](/deliver/generate-code/#application-merge-policy). * **When a built slice changes** says what happens to a slice that has been built when its model changes, is renamed or is deleted: **Mark it out of date** (the slice is marked out of date with what changed, and is rebuilt when someone makes it ready for implementation) or **Rebuild it automatically** (the slice is made ready for implementation again, so its builder updates what it built). ## What happens next [Section titled “What happens next”](#what-happens-next) When a slice is ready for implementation, you can [generate code](/deliver/generate-code/) for it, or assign it and let Studio start. Whichever mode you chose, you can [track progress](/deliver/track-progress/) on the Progress board. To build with a builder on a machine you control, or with your own coding agent, see [Builders](/developers/builders/).

# Link slices to issues

> Link a slice to an issue in GitHub, Jira, Linear or another tracker, create issues from slices, and keep slice and issue status in step.

A slice can be linked to one or more issues, so the work for the slice and the ticket for it are one click apart. Studio shows whether a linked issue is open or closed, and can keep the slice’s status and the issue’s in step. ## Link a slice to an existing issue [Section titled “Link a slice to an existing issue”](#link-a-slice-to-an-existing-issue) 1. Open the slice’s menu with **Slice options** and choose **Link the slice to an issue**. The **Issues for** dialog opens. It lists the slice’s issues, or says *The slice is not linked to any issue.* 2. In **Issue**, paste the issue’s address or key. Studio shows what it understood, for example *GitHub issue owner/repository#12*. 3. If Studio asks which **Tracker** the issue is in, choose it. 4. Select **Link**. app.cratis.studio ![The Issues for AddToBasket dialog with a field for an issue address and a Create issue section for GitHub](/_astro/link-slice-to-issue.CWFbOVe2_Z2oosCA.webp) Link an existing issue, or create one in a connected tracker. What you can paste: * A GitHub issue as `owner/repository#12`. * A Jira issue as its key, such as `SHOP-77`. * A Linear issue as its identifier, such as `ENG-4`. * Any other tracker’s issue by its address. The trackers Studio knows are **GitHub**, **Jira**, **Linear**, **GitLab**, **Azure DevOps** and **Other**. Once a slice has links, its header shows a **Linked issues** chip with the number of issues. The dialog lists every issue with its status: **Open**, **Closed** or **Unknown**. When the organization is not connected to the tracker, Studio shows the state of a public GitHub issue and otherwise shows the issue by its key alone. Select **Unlink** to remove a link. That does not change the issue. ## Create an issue from a slice [Section titled “Create an issue from a slice”](#create-an-issue-from-a-slice) In the **Issues for** dialog, use **Create an issue**. 1. Under **Where**, enter where the issue goes: `owner/repository` for GitHub, a project key such as `SHOP` for Jira, or a team key such as `ENG` for Linear. 2. Select **Create issue**. The issue gets the slice’s name as its title, with the slice’s description and specifications and a link back to the board. The slice is linked to the new issue. If no tracker is connected yet, the dialog says to connect GitHub under **Settings** > **Integrations**. ## Connect your trackers [Section titled “Connect your trackers”](#connect-your-trackers) Issues go through your organization’s integrations, which a maintainer sets up once under **Settings** > **Integrations**: GitHub, Jira and Linear each have an app to connect. See [Connect GitHub](/organization/github/) and [Organization integrations](/organization/integrations/). By default a project’s issues go where its source control is. A project can send them to a repository of its own on the **Source control** tab of **Project Settings**, in the **Issues** section. See [Connect a repository](/deliver/connect-a-repository/). ## Keep slices and issues in step [Section titled “Keep slices and issues in step”](#keep-slices-and-issues-in-step) An application can keep a slice’s status and its issues’ state in step. It is off until you turn it on, because it changes issues in another system. 1. Open the application’s **Application Settings** and select the **Issue status sync** tab. 2. Turn on **Keep slices and their issues in step**. app.cratis.studio ![The Issue status sync tab of the Acme Sales application settings with the switch to keep slices and their issues in step](/_astro/application-settings-issue-status-sync.C5keHIOs_Z1pMR4M.webp) Issue status sync is a switch per application. Every few minutes: * A slice that is done closes the issues it is linked to, and a closed issue marks its slice done. * Reopening either one reopens the other. * When both changed since the last round and disagree, the slice wins, because the model is what Studio keeps. Turning it on changes nothing by itself: the first round only records where the slice and its issue stand. Only issues in trackers your organization has connected are kept in step. Through the GitHub App, only a person’s own request can change an issue, so an issue reached only through the app is followed but not changed, and the slice records why. When a tracker refuses a change, for example because a token has expired or a Jira workflow has no way to finish the issue, Studio records it on the link and tries again in the next round. ## Issue links in the model [Section titled “Issue links in the model”](#issue-links-in-the-model) Issue links travel with the model. They are exported with the Screenplay files, so [Git sync](/deliver/git-sync/) commits them, and they are linked again when the model is imported, even into another application. Importing the same model twice does not link an issue twice. An issue link whose slice is not in the imported model is reported. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Track progress](/deliver/track-progress/) * [Connect integrations](/organization/integrations/) * [Connect a repository](/deliver/connect-a-repository/#choose-where-issues-go)

# Publish and collect feedback

> Publish an application under its own address, choose how long it runs, and collect feedback from the people who use it. This feature is experimental.

Publishing runs an application for real, under an address of its own, so that people can use it and tell you what they think. The feedback they give shows up on the board, next to the slices it is about. Experimental Publishing and feedback are new and may change. Expect rough edges, and check with your Studio team that publishing is available for your organization before you plan around it. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * The application has a model that compiles. Publishing refuses to start otherwise and says what to fix in the event model. * Sign-in for the published application is set up under **Settings** > **Authentication** in the application’s settings. Publishing refuses to start until it is. ## Publish an application [Section titled “Publish an application”](#publish-an-application) 1. On the event model board, open **Run options**, the arrow next to **Run Solution** in the top bar, and choose **Run permanently or for a period…**. 2. Enter a **Published name**. Use 3 to 40 lowercase letters, digits and dashes. Studio tells you whether *This name is free.* or whether it is reserved, taken or not valid. 3. Select **Claim name**. The name becomes the application’s address and the basis of its sign-in setup, and it is *chosen once and never changed*, so pick it with care. 4. In **Publish the application**, choose **How long should it run?**: **Permanently**, or **For a period**, as **A duration** in days, weeks or months, or **Until a date**. A period ends at the end of the chosen day, in UTC. 5. Under **What happens with feedback from its users?** choose **Don’t collect feedback**, **Collect feedback for me to review**, or **Collect feedback and improve the application automatically**. 6. Select **Publish**. app.cratis.studio ![The run options menu next to Run Solution in the top bar with Run permanently or for a period](/_astro/run-options.VJxmi9vy_Z19BJpg.webp) Publishing starts from the run options next to Run Solution. If publishing is not possible yet, the dialog says **Publishing is not possible yet** and lists what is missing, with where to fix it: sign-in under **Settings** > **Authentication**, or a problem in the event model. ## Manage a publication [Section titled “Manage a publication”](#manage-a-publication) Once an application is published, manage it under **Settings** > **Publication** in the application’s settings. It shows the status (**Rolling out**, **Running**, **The rollout failed**, **Expired** or **Unpublished**), the **Address**, the **Running version**, when it **Expires**, and the feedback handling. You can: * **Extend** the period to a new end date, or **Make permanent**. * **Change feedback handling**. * **Publish new version** to roll out the current model. * **Unpublish**. The application stops and can no longer be reached. Its data is not deleted, so publishing it again picks up where it was. The page also lists the versions that were rolled out and what the application used each day. app.cratis.studio ![The Publication tab of the Acme Sales application settings](/_astro/application-settings-publication.BS8ta6v9_1R1sYA.webp) The Publication tab of Application Settings. ## Read the feedback [Section titled “Read the feedback”](#read-the-feedback) When feedback is collected, people using the published application can send you a **Problem**, a **Suggestion**, a **Question** or **Praise**. Each one carries what was on their screen when they wrote it. 1. Select **Feedback from the published application** in the top bar. The **Feedback** panel opens. 2. Narrow the list with the filters **Kind**, **Status** and **On** (a module, a feature or a slice). 3. Open an entry. It shows **What was on screen** and **Where it shows**, the slices it concerns. Each entry has a status: **Open**, **Accepted**, **Declined**, **Addressed** or **Withdrawn**. Choose what to do with it: * **Accept** it, to say you will act on it. * **Decline** it. Studio asks *Why will you not act on this?*, and the person who gave the feedback reads your answer. * Mark it **Addressed** once it is fixed, and choose the version that addresses it. A suggested change to a screen shows its **Property**, **From** and **To**. Change the property in the screen editor, then choose **Mark as applied**. ## Improve the application automatically [Section titled “Improve the application automatically”](#improve-the-application-automatically) If you chose **Collect feedback and improve the application automatically**, Studio turns feedback into changes to the event model, checks them against every specification, and rolls them out as a new version. Anything that touches sign-in, roles, policies, personal data or what the model contains is left to you. The **Publication** tab lists each improvement and what became of it, and lets you **Pause** and **Resume** the improving, or **Revert** a change. A reverted change rolls the application back as a new version. Because it changes what runs, give your slices [specifications](/check/) before you turn this on. Studio only rolls out a change that passes all of them. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Track progress](/deliver/track-progress/) * [Check behavior](/check/) * [Share a read-only snapshot link](/share/snapshot-link/), for showing the model without publishing it * [Project and application settings](/organization/project-and-application-settings/)

# Track progress

> Set a slice's status, assign it to a person, an agent or Studio, and see every slice of a project by status on the Progress board.

Every slice carries a status that says how far along it is. Together, the statuses tell the team what is settled, what is being built and what is stuck, without anyone having to ask. ## Change a slice’s status [Section titled “Change a slice’s status”](#change-a-slices-status) 1. On the event model board, find the slice. Its status shows in its header. 2. Select **Change status** in the slice header. 3. Choose the new status. app.cratis.studio ![The status menu of the AddToBasket slice listing Not started, Draft, Analysis, Ready for implementation, In progress, Ready for review, Done, Blocked and Waiting for user interface](/_astro/change-slice-status.Bl7-EjBr_ZSlTtb.webp) A slice moves through the statuses from Not started to Done. Blocked and Waiting for user interface are for work that cannot go on yet. The statuses are **Not started**, **Draft**, **Analysis**, **Ready for implementation**, **In progress**, **Ready for review**, **Done**, **Blocked** and **Waiting for user interface**. [Statuses and colors](/reference/statuses-and-colors/) explains what each one means. ### Block a slice [Section titled “Block a slice”](#block-a-slice) Choosing **Blocked** opens **Block slice** with two questions: **Why can it not be built?** and **Questions that need an answer, one per line**. Write the reason and the questions the team has to answer before work can go on. The reason and the questions show on the Progress board. ### When Studio sets the status for you [Section titled “When Studio sets the status for you”](#when-studio-sets-the-status-for-you) * While a slice is assigned to the **System** member or to an AI agent, Studio moves the status along and says *Studio manages this status while the slice is assigned to the System member or an agent*. * While Studio is generating a slice, its status cannot be changed until the generation has ended. ## Assign a slice [Section titled “Assign a slice”](#assign-a-slice) Assigning says who is responsible for a slice. 1. Open the slice’s menu with **Slice options**. 2. Choose **Assign to**, then pick who gets the slice. app.cratis.studio ![The Assign to submenu of a slice listing the System member, the agents and the people of the organization](/_astro/slice-assign-to-menu.BSh6_rBA_Z2aSsdD.webp) A slice can go to the System, to an AI agent or to a person. People's names are blurred here. The list starts with the **System** member, then the AI agents that can generate code, then the people of your organization. Once a slice has an assignee, its menu offers **Reassign** and **Remove assignee** instead. Assigning a ready slice to the **System** starts static code generation, and assigning it to a code-generation agent starts AI implementation. Assigning it to a person starts nothing. [Generate and review code](/deliver/generate-code/) explains the rest. ## See every slice on the Progress board [Section titled “See every slice on the Progress board”](#see-every-slice-on-the-progress-board) The Progress board shows all slices of a project in one place, one column per status. 1. Select **Project options** next to the project name in the top bar. 2. Choose **Progress board**. app.cratis.studio ![The Project options menu open next to the project name in the top bar](/_astro/project-options-menu.DJhB1qFE_Zl6zzf.webp) The Progress board is in the Project options menu. Every slice is a card with its name and where it lives: its event model, module and feature. A column header shows how many slices are in it. A column with no slices says *No slices*. A blocked slice shows its reason and its questions under the heading *Questions that need an answer*. app.cratis.studio ![The Progress board of Acme Commerce with a column for each status and the slices of all five applications as cards](/_astro/progress-board.BabxSoyZ_Z1K1zKw.webp) The Progress board of Acme Commerce. Use the three filters at the top to narrow it down. Use the filters at the top, **All event models**, **All modules** and **All features**, to look at one part of the project. The board is for reading: change a slice’s status on the event model board. ## Keep issues in step [Section titled “Keep issues in step”](#keep-issues-in-step) If your team tracks work in GitHub, Jira or Linear, you can [link a slice to an issue](/deliver/issue-links/) and have the slice and the issue follow each other’s status. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Statuses and colors](/reference/statuses-and-colors/) * [Slices](/board/slices/) * [Generate and review code](/deliver/generate-code/)

# For developers

> Connect AI agents and builders to Cratis Studio, keep the model in Screenplay files, and react to board changes with webhooks.

Studio is a place where a team models a system. This section is for the people who connect that model to the rest of their tooling: coding agents, build machines, repositories and other systems. You do not need any of it to model in Studio. Start with [Get started](/get-started/) if you have not used Studio yet, and see [Your organization](/organization/) for the settings pages that several of these pages send you to. ## Ways to extend Studio [Section titled “Ways to extend Studio”](#ways-to-extend-studio) | You want to | Use | Who sets it up | | --------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- | | Let an AI agent read and change your models | [MCP](/developers/mcp/) | A maintainer issues the credential | | Hand part of a model to a coding agent | [Agent context](/developers/agent-context/) | Anyone who can see the board | | Model on a board with the coding agent you run yourself | [Live modeling](/developers/live-modeling/) | Anyone with the organization’s MCP credential | | Turn slices into code on your own machine or build server | [Builders](/developers/builders/) | A maintainer approves each builder | | Keep the model next to your code | [Screenplay files](/developers/screenplay-files/) | Anyone who can export; a maintainer connects the repository | | Tell another system when a board changes | [Webhooks](/developers/webhooks/) | A maintainer | Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram What connects to Studio. A double arrow goes both ways; a single arrow only leaves Studio. [Connect an AI agent over MCP ](/developers/mcp/)The endpoint, the credential and a client configuration that works. [Builders ](/developers/builders/)Connect a builder that turns ready slices into code, and choose who builds what. [Build a builder ](/developers/builders/build/)The Cratis CLI plus a coding agent, a generator of your own in any language, or both. Samples for each. [Screenplay files ](/developers/screenplay-files/)The .play model as source: export, import and Git sync. [Webhooks ](/developers/webhooks/)The payload, the signature and the retries. [Live modeling ](/developers/live-modeling/)Model on a board with the agent you already run. ## What is public and what is not [Section titled “What is public and what is not”](#what-is-public-and-what-is-not) Everything here is a surface Studio publishes on purpose: the MCP endpoint, the builder connection address, the JSON schemas and the webhook deliveries. Each one works inside one organization and only for the people or machines that organization has allowed. Studio’s source code is not public, and nothing on these pages needs it. Where a page describes behavior, it describes what you can observe from outside. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [The Screenplay language](https://www.cratis.io/screenplay/) at cratis.io describes the `.play` format itself. * [Glossary](/reference/glossary/) explains the event-modeling words these pages use. * [Build and deliver](/deliver/) covers the same journey from the board’s side: implementation modes, progress and pull requests.

# Give a coding agent the context of a slice

> Copy or download a slice, feature, event model or domain from Cratis Studio as Markdown, JSON, YAML or TOON, with its specifications, its Screenplay and the guidance that teaches an agent Screenplay.

When you hand part of a model to an AI assistant or a review pipeline, it needs the slices, what they take, record and show, the specifications they must pass, and the Screenplay they are written in. Studio exports exactly that part of the model in a form made for agents, so you do not paste whole models or verbose JSON. ## What the context holds [Section titled “What the context holds”](#what-the-context-holds) * Each slice in scope: its type, where it sits (event model, module and feature), its description, its command, events, read models and queries with their properties, and its trigger. * Every specification of each slice as Given, When and Then lines, including the rejections and the “no events” expectations. A slice without specifications says so. * The Screenplay documents the scope covers: the slice’s own document, or everything under the feature. * The Screenplay session guidance, unless you leave it out. It teaches an agent how to read Screenplay and build a slice from it, so the agent needs nothing installed. Studio checks the guidance against its published checksum before using it. ## Choose a format [Section titled “Choose a format”](#choose-a-format) | Format | Use it for | | -------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Markdown | Pasting into an assistant. A compact summary of each slice that always lists the specifications, then the Screenplay and the guidance | | JSON | Tools and pipelines that parse the context | | YAML | Reading the context by eye | | TOON | Fitting a large context into a token budget. [Token-Oriented Object Notation](https://github.com/toon-format/spec) writes lists such as properties as one header and a row per item | ## Copy or download from the board [Section titled “Copy or download from the board”](#copy-or-download-from-the-board) * On a slice header, **Copy for an agent** copies the slice’s context as Markdown. * In a feature’s menu, **Context for an agent** copies the feature’s context or downloads it in any format. * In the project menu’s **Export** submenu, **Context for an agent** does the same for the open event model. ## Fetch it from an agent or a pipeline [Section titled “Fetch it from an agent or a pipeline”](#fetch-it-from-an-agent-or-a-pipeline) * Over [MCP](/developers/mcp/), call `studio_export_context` with a `scope` (`slice`, `feature`, `eventModel` or `domain`), the `id`, a `format` (`markdown`, `json`, `yaml` or `toon`) and, to save tokens, `includeGuidance: false`. * From a terminal or CI, `cratis studio fetch` uses the same tool with your organization’s MCP credential. See [the Cratis CLI](https://www.cratis.io/cli/). ## What Studio’s own chat agents receive [Section titled “What Studio’s own chat agents receive”](#what-studios-own-chat-agents-receive) Agents you talk to in Studio’s board chat get their context without being asked. Every message to every agent carries: 1. **The subject.** The event model or brainstorming board the conversation is about, with an outline of every module, feature and slice. A model too large for a prompt drops slice detail and says so in the outline. 2. **The surroundings.** The project, application and domain, and the names of the domain’s concepts. 3. **Where you are.** The page you are on, what is selected or being edited, who you are and which organization you are in. An agent that has the model open can read any part of it in full. Reading is never counted as work done: a reply that only read the model is an answer, not an action Studio carried out. If Studio cannot load the subject, the agent says exactly that instead of asking you to paste the model. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Connect an AI agent over MCP](/developers/mcp/) * [Live modeling with your own agent](/developers/live-modeling/) * [Screenplay files](/developers/screenplay-files/)

# Builders

> What a builder is, how Studio hands it ready slices, and how to build one with the Cratis CLI - with a coding agent, a generator, or both.

Builders ## Turn ready slices into code in your repository A builder takes the slices you mark **Ready for implementation** on the board and turns them into code, then tells Studio what came of it. Studio keeps the board and the process around it in order. A **builder** does the building: it takes one slice at a time, builds it with whatever tooling it likes, and reports one outcome. You review what it built on the slice, including the specifications it ran and the assumptions it made. ## Three ways to build [Section titled “Three ways to build”](#three-ways-to-build) You do not write a builder from scratch. A builder is **`cratis build` from the [Cratis CLI](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/), plus whatever writes the code**, and there are three ways to write it: * **With AI.** A coding agent, such as Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, Copilot CLI or OpenCode, builds the slice inside the files, commands and checks of a build kit. The most code for the least effort. See [Build with AI](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/). * **Deterministic.** A generator of your own, in any language, turns the order into code the same way every time. See [Build with a generator](/developers/builders/build/deterministic/). * **Deterministic with AI on top.** Your generator writes the structure, and the agent fills in the parts that need judgment. See [Deterministic with AI on top](/developers/builders/build/deterministic-and-ai/). Any stack works: the build order describes the slice in Screenplay, with its specifications as acceptance criteria, and what you turn that into is up to you. [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/) helps you choose. The CLI does the rest: it connects to Studio, waits for approval, asks for work, keeps the claim on a slice alive, keeps an agent within its permissions, verifies, commits, pushes and reports the outcome. It runs anywhere that can reach Studio over HTTPS, and needs no inbound port. ## Choose your path [Section titled “Choose your path”](#choose-your-path) [Configure a builder ](/developers/builders/configure/connect-and-approve/)Connect a builder, have a maintainer approve it with its code, and choose who builds the application, its screens or a single slice. [Build with AI ](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/)Let a coding agent build your slices inside a build kit, from a registered kit or one you write. [Build a builder ](/developers/builders/build/)Choose between AI, a generator of your own, or a generator with an agent on top, with samples for each. [Talk to Studio directly ](/developers/builders/protocol/)For a builder that cannot run the CLI: the HTTPS conversation with Studio, request by request. ## Studio’s own generation, or a builder you connect [Section titled “Studio’s own generation, or a builder you connect”](#studios-own-generation-or-a-builder-you-connect) Every application has a builder from the start: **Studio’s own generation**, as static code, static code refined by AI, or AI only (the two that involve AI need an AI provider configured). Until you choose another, that is who builds. A **connected builder** runs on a machine you control: a laptop, a build server, a container in another cloud. It dials out to Studio, so it needs no inbound port. A maintainer approves it once, and from then on you can choose it for an application, for the backend or the screens of an application, or for a single slice. Both go through the same loop and the same statuses, so the built-in path and a builder you connect cannot drift apart. Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram Studio runs the loop. A builder only claims, builds and reports. ## The pieces [Section titled “The pieces”](#the-pieces) | Piece | What it is | | ------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | **Builder** | Anything that connects to Studio, takes a slice to build, builds it with whatever tooling it likes, and reports what came of it. Studio’s own generation is a builder. So is a coding agent driven by the Cratis CLI, and so is a program a team wrote for its own stack | | **Build order** | The JSON document Studio hands a builder: what to build, written in Screenplay, with the specifications as acceptance criteria and the context the builder needs. See [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/) | | **Build outcome** | The JSON document a builder answers with: built, nothing to do, blocked or failed, with the commit it made, the results of the specifications it ran and the assumptions it made | | **Build loop** | Studio’s side of the exchange: offering work, claiming it, holding the claim under a lease, retrying and settling the slice’s status. See [The build loop](/developers/builders/protocol/build-loop/) | | **`cratis build`** | The builder in the Cratis CLI. It connects, claims, keeps the lease alive, runs your agent or generator, verifies, commits, pushes and reports. See [Use the reference builder](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/) | | **Coding agent** | Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, Copilot CLI, OpenCode or any Agent Client Protocol agent, run by `cratis build` with `--agent`. See [Build with AI](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/) | | **Generator** | Any program that reads a build order and writes code and an outcome, run by `cratis build` with `--exec`. See [Build with a generator](/developers/builders/build/deterministic/) | | **Build kit** | A description of a stack for `cratis build`: a manifest, a scaffold, instructions, permissions and verification commands. What an agent builds with. See [Build kits](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/#build-kits) | ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Build and deliver](/deliver/) shows a build from the board’s side: implementation modes, progress and pull requests. * [Approve builders](/organization/builders/) is the maintainer’s page in the organization settings. * [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/) is the JSON contract between Studio and every builder.

# Build a builder

> Three ways to build slices with the Cratis CLI - a coding agent, a generator of your own, or a generator with an agent on top - and how to pick one.

A builder takes slices that are ready to build and turns them into code. You do not write one from scratch: **a builder is `cratis build` from the Cratis CLI, plus whatever writes the code**. The CLI talks to Studio, keeps the work inside the limits you set, runs your checks and commits. What writes the code is your choice, and there are three good ways to do it. What you'll learn * the three ways to build, and when to choose each * what the CLI does for you, whichever way you choose * how to try a build offline, run it as a builder, and keep it running ## Three ways to build [Section titled “Three ways to build”](#three-ways-to-build) Whatever writes the code, the same checks decide whether it is committed. With AI A coding agent such as Claude Code, Codex or Copilot CLI writes the slice from the order, inside the files and commands you permit. **Choose it** to get the most code for the least effort, on any stack. It needs good instructions and checks that catch what the agent gets wrong. Deterministic A generator of your own, in any language, turns the order into code. The same order always gives the same code. **Choose it** when your conventions are fixed and you want builds that are fast, cheap and repeatable. It writes only what you taught it. Deterministic with AI on top Your generator writes the predictable part: files, names, types, wiring and test skeletons. The agent fills in what needs judgment, such as business rules and test data. **Choose it** for the best of both: your structure every time, and the agent’s effort where it counts. [Build with AI ](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/)Install a kit and build with your agent, then write your own kit for a TypeScript stack. [Build with a generator ](/developers/builders/build/deterministic/)The generator contract, with samples in Bash, TypeScript and C#. [Deterministic with AI on top ](/developers/builders/build/deterministic-and-ai/)A kit whose generator writes the structure and whose agent completes it. Not sure? Start **with AI** and a [registered kit](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/#quick-start-with-a-registered-kit): it is the quickest way to see a slice built. When you notice the agent writing the same structure on every slice, move that structure into a generator and keep the agent for the rest. ## Who does what [Section titled “Who does what”](#who-does-what) Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram The CLI holds the conversation with Studio. What writes the code only sees the order, the project and the outcome. | The Cratis CLI | Your agent or generator | | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- | | Registers the machine and waits for a maintainer to approve it | Reads the build order | | Asks Studio for work, and keeps the claim on a slice alive while you build | Writes code into the project | | Writes the order to a file, then starts the agent or runs the generator in the project | Writes the outcome: built, nothing to do, blocked or failed | | Keeps an agent to the files and commands you permit | | | Runs the verification commands you give it | | | Commits the files you report, pushes the branch and reports the outcome to Studio | | The agent or generator never talks to Studio, never sees a credential and never runs Git. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * **The Cratis CLI.** Install it with `brew tap cratis/cratis && brew install cratis` on macOS or Linux, or `dotnet tool install -g Cratis.Cli` anywhere .NET runs. See [Getting started](https://www.cratis.io/cli/getting-started/) in the CLI documentation. * **A clone of the application’s repository**, with Git credentials that can push to it. Studio never hands a builder a repository credential. * **A maintainer of the organization**, to approve the builder once. See [Connect a builder and approve it](/developers/builders/configure/connect-and-approve/). * **For AI, a coding agent** installed and signed in on the machine: Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, Copilot CLI, OpenCode, or any agent that speaks the Agent Client Protocol. ## What every build has in common [Section titled “What every build has in common”](#what-every-build-has-in-common) The order is Studio’s [build order](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-order): the slices to build with their type, the Screenplay model they live in, each slice’s specifications as acceptance criteria, and the hints and feedback people left on the slices. The outcome says what came of it: `built` with the files written, the result of each specification and the assumptions made; `nothingToDo`; `blocked` with questions for a person; or `failed`. Whichever way you build, the CLI treats the result the same: * **After `built` or `nothingToDo`, verification runs.** Every `--verify` command, and every `verify` command of a [kit](#add-a-kit), runs in the project, in order. A command that exits with anything but 0 turns the outcome into `failed`, whatever the agent or generator said. * **After `built`, the CLI commits.** It commits the files that were reported and changed, on the branch Studio names or the branch you are on, and pushes it with your own Git credentials.
