With a kit, it commits only files inside the kit’s permitted paths. * **Nothing is lost.** Anything left uncommitted after a build is put away in a Git stash named after the order, and the CLI goes back to the branch it started on. * **The project must be clean.** `cratis build run` refuses to start in a repository with uncommitted changes, so a build commits only its own work. ## Add a kit [Section titled “Add a kit”](#add-a-kit) A [build kit](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/#build-kits) describes a stack once: the instructions and prompt an agent gets, the files it may write and the commands it may run, the checks that must pass, and the files of a new project. A kit is what makes building with AI repeatable, and it works with a generator too. Without a kit, a build covers the backend of every slice type, may write anywhere in the project, and reads version 1 of the build contract. Install a kit into the project with `cratis build init --kit <name|git url|folder>`, or pass it to a single build with `--kit <folder>`. [Build with AI](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/) shows how to write one. ## Try it offline [Section titled “Try it offline”](#try-it-offline) `cratis build once` builds one order from a file, without connecting to Studio. It runs your agent or generator, the verification and the commit exactly as a real build does, but does not push. 1. Save the [example backend order](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-order) as `order.json`, outside the project. 2. In a clean clone of the repository, build it once with an agent or with a generator: 
   ```bash
   cratis build once --order ../order.json --agent claude --outcome ../outcome.json
   cratis build once --order ../order.json --exec "<your command>" --verify "<your test command>" --outcome ../outcome.json
   ```
 3. Read `outcome.json`, and check the commit with `git log`. `once` exits with `5` when the outcome is `failed`, so it also fits in a CI job. ## Run it as a builder [Section titled “Run it as a builder”](#run-it-as-a-builder) 1. Register the machine with your organization, once: 
   ```bash
   cratis build connect --studio --organization <your organization>
   ```
 It prints a code and waits. A maintainer approves it with the code in Studio under **Organization settings** > **Builders**. See [Connect a builder and approve it](/developers/builders/configure/connect-and-approve/). 2. In the project, start the loop with your agent or your generator: 
   ```bash
   cratis build run --agent claude
   cratis build run --exec "<your command>" --verify "<your test command>"
   ```
 After `cratis build init --agent <agent>`, a plain `cratis build run` uses the kit and the agent you installed. Add `--once` to stop after one slice, or `--no-push` to keep the commits local. 3. In Studio, choose the builder for the application, its backend, or one slice. See [Choose who builds](/developers/builders/configure/choose-builders/). Mark a slice **Ready for implementation** and watch the terminal: the CLI says when it gets work, what the agent or generator does, what it committed and how Studio settled the slice. See [Follow a build and troubleshoot](/developers/builders/configure/follow-and-troubleshoot/). ## Keep it running [Section titled “Keep it running”](#keep-it-running) On macOS and Linux, install the loop as a user service, so the machine stays a builder without a terminal open: 
```bash
cratis build service install --agent claude
cratis build service status
```
 The service starts when you sign in and restarts when it stops. It takes the same options as `run`. Each project gets its own service, and `cratis build service uninstall` removes it. Windows has no supported service manager; run `cratis build run` there. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/): every field of the order and the outcome. * [Use the reference builder](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/): the `cratis build` commands, build kits and placeholders. * [Talk to Studio directly](/developers/builders/protocol/): the HTTPS conversation the CLI holds for you, for a builder that cannot run the CLI.

# Sample: a generator in C#

> A generator for the Cratis CLI in one C# file run with dotnet run: it reads the build order, writes a sheet per slice and reports the outcome.

This sample is a generator in one C# file, run with `dotnet run` as a file-based app: no project file and no packages, only `System.Text.Json`. It is a *generator*: the Cratis CLI runs it for every build order, and does everything else a builder does, from connecting to Studio to committing and reporting. See [Build with a generator](/developers/builders/build/deterministic/) for how the two fit together. For every slice in the order, the generator writes a Markdown sheet with the slice’s acceptance criteria to `slices/<feature>/<slice>.md` in the project, and reports what it wrote. The comment in the code marks where real code generation goes. The [Bash](/developers/builders/build/shell/) and [TypeScript](/developers/builders/build/typescript/) samples do exactly the same thing, and write the same sheets. The language is your choice: anything the shell can start works. ## What you need [Section titled “What you need”](#what-you-need) * The [Cratis CLI](https://www.cratis.io/cli/getting-started/), with `cratis build`. * The .NET 10 SDK or later, which runs a single `.cs` file with `dotnet run`. * A clean clone of the application’s repository. ## The code [Section titled “The code”](#the-code) Save the file as `~/slice-sheets/generate.cs`, outside the application’s repository. The first run compiles it, which takes a few seconds; later runs reuse the build. generate.cs 
```csharp
// Slice sheets: a generator for `cratis build`. For every slice in the build order it writes
// a Markdown sheet with the slice's acceptance criteria into the project.
using System.Text;
using System.Text.Json;
using System.Text.Json.Nodes;
using System.Text.RegularExpressions;


var outcomeFile = Environment.GetEnvironmentVariable("STUDIO_BUILD_OUTCOME")!;
var json = new JsonSerializerOptions { WriteIndented = true };
void WriteOutcome(JsonObject outcome) => File.WriteAllText(outcomeFile, outcome.ToJsonString(json));


try
{
    var order = JsonNode.Parse(File.ReadAllText(Environment.GetEnvironmentVariable("STUDIO_BUILD_ORDER")!))!;
    var feature = Text(order["scope"]!["feature"]!["name"]);
    if (!Safe(feature)) throw new InvalidOperationException($"The feature name '{feature}' cannot be used as a folder name.");
    var specifications = order["specifications"]!.AsArray();


    var files = new JsonArray();
    foreach (var slice in order["scope"]!["slices"]!.AsArray())
    {
        var name = Text(slice!["name"]);
        if (!Safe(name)) throw new InvalidOperationException($"The slice name '{name}' cannot be used as a file name.");
        var file = $"slices/{feature}/{name}.md";


        // This is where real generation goes: run your code generator or templates for the
        // slice here and write the files they produce. This sample writes one sheet per slice.
        var sheet = new StringBuilder()
            .Append($"# {name}\n\nA {Text(slice["type"])} slice in {feature}.\n\n## Acceptance criteria\n");
        foreach (var specification in specifications.Where(s => Text(s!["sliceId"]) == Text(slice["id"])))
        {
            var given = specification!["given"]!.AsArray().Select(Text).ToList();
            sheet.Append($"\n### {Text(specification["name"])}\n\n")
                .Append($"- Given {(given.Count > 0 ? string.Join(", ", given) : "nothing")}\n")
                .Append($"- When {Text(specification["when"])}\n")
                .Append($"- Then {string.Join(", ", specification["then"]!.AsArray().Select(Text))}\n");
        }


        if (File.Exists(file) && File.ReadAllText(file) == sheet.ToString()) continue;
        Directory.CreateDirectory(Path.GetDirectoryName(file)!);
        File.WriteAllText(file, sheet.ToString());
        files.Add((JsonNode)file);
    }


    if (files.Count == 0)
    {
        WriteOutcome(new JsonObject { ["kind"] = "nothingToDo" });
    }
    else
    {
        WriteOutcome(new JsonObject
        {
            ["kind"] = "built",
            ["files"] = files,
            ["specifications"] = new JsonArray([.. specifications.Select(s => (JsonNode)new JsonObject { ["id"] = Text(s!["id"]), ["result"] = "notRun" })]),
            ["assumptions"] = new JsonArray("Wrote a slice sheet for each slice; no code was generated.")
        });
    }
}
catch (Exception error)
{
    WriteOutcome(new JsonObject { ["kind"] = "failed", ["error"] = error.Message, ["retryable"] = false });
}


static string Text(JsonNode? node) => node?.GetValue<string>() ?? string.Empty;
static bool Safe(string name) => Regex.IsMatch(name, "^[A-Za-z0-9_ -][A-Za-z0-9._ -]*$");
```
 ## Run it once, offline [Section titled “Run it once, offline”](#run-it-once-offline) `cratis build once` runs the generator on an order from a file, without connecting to Studio, and commits what it built just as a real build does. 1. Save the [example backend order](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-order) as `~/order.json`. 2. In the clone of the repository, run the generator on it: 
   ```bash
   cratis build once --order ~/order.json --exec "dotnet run $HOME/slice-sheets/generate.cs" --verify "test -d slices" --outcome ~/outcome.json
   ```
 `--verify` is where your build and tests go, for example `dotnet test` or `npm test`. Every `--verify` command runs after the generator; one that fails turns the outcome into `failed`. 3. Check the result. `~/outcome.json` says `built` and lists `slices/Invoicing/PayInvoice.md`, and `git log` shows the commit. Run the same command again: the sheet is unchanged, so the outcome is `nothingToDo` and nothing is committed. ## Run it for real [Section titled “Run it for real”](#run-it-for-real) 1. Register the machine with your organization, once, and have a maintainer approve it with the code it shows. See [Connect a builder and approve it](/developers/builders/configure/connect-and-approve/). 
   ```bash
   cratis build connect --studio --organization <your organization>
   ```
 2. In the clone of the repository, start the build loop with the generator: 
   ```bash
   cratis build run --exec "dotnet run $HOME/slice-sheets/generate.cs" --verify "test -d slices"
   ```
 3. In Studio, [choose the builder](/developers/builders/configure/choose-builders/) for the application, and mark a slice **Ready for implementation**. The terminal shows the generator running, the commit, and how Studio settled the slice. The CLI pushes each commit with your own Git credentials. Add `--no-push` to keep the commits local, or `--once` to stop after one slice. ## Keep it running [Section titled “Keep it running”](#keep-it-running) On macOS and Linux, install the loop as a user service, from the clone of the repository: 
```bash
cratis build service install --exec "dotnet run $HOME/slice-sheets/generate.cs" --verify "test -d slices"
```
 It starts when you sign in and restarts when it stops. `cratis build service status` says where its output goes, and `cratis build service uninstall` removes it. ## How it works [Section titled “How it works”](#how-it-works) ### Reading the order [Section titled “Reading the order”](#reading-the-order) `JsonNode.Parse` reads the file `STUDIO_BUILD_ORDER` names, and `Text` reads one string from it. [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/) lists every member, with a JSON Schema you can generate classes from. The program runs in the project folder, so the paths it writes are relative to the project. ### Writing the sheet [Section titled “Writing the sheet”](#writing-the-sheet) The loop builds the sheet for one slice: its name and type, then each specification whose `sliceId` matches, as Given, When and Then. This is where your own generation goes: call a code generator, fill templates, or write the command and event files for the slice. ### Nothing to do [Section titled “Nothing to do”](#nothing-to-do) A sheet that is already in the project with the same content is skipped. When every sheet is unchanged, the program reports `nothingToDo`, so the CLI makes no empty commit. ### The outcome [Section titled “The outcome”](#the-outcome) `WriteOutcome` writes `built` with the files the program wrote, each specification as `notRun` (the sample runs no tests), and one assumption. The CLI adds the order’s id, the contract version and the commit. A file-based app is set up for native AOT, which turns off reflection-based JSON serialization, so the program builds the outcome from `JsonNode` values instead of serializing a class. ### Failing [Section titled “Failing”](#failing) Any exception, such as a feature or slice name that cannot be a file name, is caught and written as a `failed` outcome with `retryable` set to `false`, and the program exits with 0 because it wrote an outcome. Set `retryable` to `true` for an error another attempt could get past. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Build with a generator](/developers/builders/build/deterministic/): the generator contract, step by step. * [Deterministic with AI on top](/developers/builders/build/deterministic-and-ai/): a generator that writes the structure, and a coding agent that completes it. * [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/): everything in the order the generator reads. * [Use the reference builder](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/): every `cratis build` command, and build kits.

# Build with a generator

> Build slices deterministically with a generator of your own in any language. The CLI runs it per order; it reads one JSON file and writes another.

A generator is any program that turns a build order into code the same way every time. The Cratis CLI runs it for every order with `--exec`, and does everything else a builder does, from talking to Studio to verifying and committing. The same order always gives the same code, so builds are fast, cheap and easy to review. Choose this way when your conventions are fixed and you can describe them as code. For how this compares with building with AI, see [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/#three-ways-to-build). ## The generator contract [Section titled “The generator contract”](#the-generator-contract) `cratis build run --exec "<command>"` runs your command through the shell (`/bin/sh`, or `cmd.exe` on Windows) in the project folder, once for every build order. Three environment variables name the files it works with: | Variable | What it names | | ---------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `STUDIO_BUILD_ORDER` | The build order, `order.json`, in a folder outside the project. The order’s Screenplay documents are written beside it, in a `model` folder | | `STUDIO_BUILD_OUTCOME` | The file your command writes its outcome to, `outcome.json` | | `STUDIO_BUILD_PROMPT` | The order written out as a prompt, `prompt.md`, for a generator that wants to hand it to a language model | The order is Studio’s [build order](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-order). The outcome is a JSON object with a `kind`, and only the fields for that kind: | `kind` | Fields | Meaning | | ------------- | ---------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `built` | `files`, `specifications`, `assumptions` | You wrote code. `files` lists the paths you wrote, relative to the project; a folder stands for everything under it. `specifications` lists `{ "id", "result", "message" }` with `result` `passed`, `failed` or `notRun`. `assumptions` lists what you decided where the model was silent | | `nothingToDo` | none | The code already matches the order | | `blocked` | `reason`, `questions` | You cannot build the slices until a person answers | | `failed` | `error`, `retryable` | Something went wrong. `retryable` is `true` unless you say otherwise; Studio offers the slice again while attempts are left | 
```json
{
    "kind": "built",
    "files": ["slices/Invoicing/PayInvoice.md"],
    "specifications": [{ "id": "8e1f2a3b-4c5d-4e6f-8a9b-0c1d2e3f4a5b", "result": "notRun" }],
    "assumptions": ["Amounts are rounded to two decimals"]
}
```
 Write only `kind` and its fields. The CLI adds `contractVersion` and `orderId`, and the `commit` and `branch` once it has committed, then sends the outcome to Studio. The rest of the contract: * **Exit with 0** when you have written an outcome, whatever its kind. A non-zero exit becomes a `failed` outcome that Studio retries. A command that exits with 0 without writing an outcome is run again, up to twice, before the build fails the same way. * **List the files you wrote.** After `built`, the CLI commits the files you listed that changed (every changed file, when you list none). Report `nothingToDo` rather than `built` when nothing changed: an empty commit fails. * **Verification decides.** Every `--verify` command runs after `built` or `nothingToDo`; one that fails turns the outcome into `failed`. See [What every build has in common](/developers/builders/build/#what-every-build-has-in-common). ## Try it offline [Section titled “Try it offline”](#try-it-offline) 1. Save the [example backend order](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-order) as `order.json`, outside the project. 2. In a clean clone of the repository, run the generator once: 
   ```bash
   cratis build once --order ../order.json --exec "<your command>" --verify "<your test command>" --outcome ../outcome.json
   ```
 3. Read `outcome.json`, and check the commit with `git log`. Run the same order again: a generator that finds nothing to change reports `nothingToDo`. Then run it as a builder with `cratis build run --exec "<your command>" --verify "<your test command>"`, as [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/#run-it-as-a-builder) describes. ## Start from a sample [Section titled “Start from a sample”](#start-from-a-sample) Each sample is a complete generator that writes a Markdown sheet with the acceptance criteria of every slice in the order, reports `built`, `nothingToDo` or `failed`, and shows where real code generation goes. They are equivalent: pick the language you like, or port one to any other. [Generator in Bash ](/developers/builders/build/shell/)One script with jq. Runs anywhere a shell does. [Generator in TypeScript ](/developers/builders/build/typescript/)One file, run by Node.js with no dependencies. [Generator in C# ](/developers/builders/build/csharp/)One file, run by dotnet run with no packages. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Deterministic with AI on top](/developers/builders/build/deterministic-and-ai/): let a generator write the structure and a coding agent fill in the rest. * [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/): every field of the order your generator reads. * [Use the reference builder](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/): the `cratis build` commands and build kits.

# Deterministic with AI on top

> Let a generator write the structure of every slice from the build order, and a coding agent fill in only the parts that need judgment. Checks decide.

A generator is good at what follows a rule: file names, type names, function signatures, wiring, one test per specification. A coding agent is good at what needs judgment: the fields of an event, the business rule in a function, the data a test needs. **Deterministic with AI on top** splits a build between them. The generator writes the predictable part from the order and marks every place that needs judgment. The agent fills in only those places. Your checks decide whether the result is committed. The result is code with your structure every time, and the agent’s effort spent where it counts. For how this compares with the other ways to build, see [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/#three-ways-to-build). ## How it fits together [Section titled “How it fits together”](#how-it-fits-together) It is one agent build, run by `cratis build` with a [build kit](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/#build-kits). The kit carries the generator, and: * **permits the generator as a command**, so the agent may run it; * **tells the agent, in its prompt, to run the generator first** and then fill in only the marked places. The prompt passes the generator the path of the order with the `{order}` placeholder; * **verifies with the generator itself**, in a check mode that fails while a file, a test or a marked place is missing, followed by the type check and the tests. The generator never overwrites a file, so running it again on a later order adds what is new and leaves the agent’s work alone. ## Sample: a generator and an agent for TypeScript [Section titled “Sample: a generator and an agent for TypeScript”](#sample-a-generator-and-an-agent-for-typescript) This sample builds state change slices in plain TypeScript on Node.js, tested with Vitest, like the kit in [Build with AI](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/#sample-a-kit-of-your-own). Here the generator writes each slice’s file and test file, decides every name from the order, and leaves `TODO(agent)` where the agent works. Make a folder `ts-vitest-generated` with these files: 
```text
ts-vitest-generated/
├── build-kit.yaml           # the manifest
├── AGENTS.md                # the stack instructions
├── build-slice.md           # the prompt for one build order
├── generator/
│   └── generate.mts         # the slice generator
└── scaffold/                # the files of a new project, as in Build with AI
    ├── .gitignore
    ├── package.json
    └── tsconfig.json
```
 The `scaffold` folder holds the same three files as in [the Build with AI sample](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/#the-scaffold). ### The generator [Section titled “The generator”](#the-generator) The generator reads the order and, for every state change slice, writes `src/<feature>/<slice>.ts` and `src/<feature>/<slice>.test.ts` when they are not there yet. It takes the names from the specifications: `event Invoice Issued` becomes the type `InvoiceIssued`, and `command Pay Invoice` becomes the type `PayInvoice` and the function `payInvoice`. With `--check`, it changes nothing and fails while any file, test or `TODO(agent)` is missing. Node.js 22.18 or later runs it as it is. ts-vitest-generated/generator/generate.mts 
```ts
// The slice generator: writes the predictable part of every state change slice in a build order,
// with TODO(agent) where judgment is needed. With --check, it fails while any of that is missing.
//   node generate.mts <order.json>          write what is missing, never overwrite
//   node generate.mts --check <order.json>  exit 1 while a file, a test or a filled-in place is missing
import { existsSync, mkdirSync, readFileSync, writeFileSync } from 'node:fs';


type Slice = { id: string; name: string; type: string };
type Specification = { sliceId: string; name: string; given: string[]; when: string; then: string[] };
type Order = { scope: { feature: { name: string }; slices: Slice[] }; specifications: Specification[] };


const check = process.argv.includes('--check');
const orderFile = process.argv.slice(2).find(argument => argument !== '--check') ?? process.env.STUDIO_BUILD_ORDER;
if (!orderFile) throw new Error('Name the build order: node generate.mts <order.json>');
const order: Order = JSON.parse(readFileSync(orderFile, 'utf8'));


const header = 'Generated from the build order by the slice generator.';
const todo = 'TODO(agent)';
const safe = (name: string) => /^[A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9]*$/.test(name);
// "event Invoice Issued" -> "InvoiceIssued"; "command Pay Invoice" -> "PayInvoice".
const typeName = (element: string) => element.replace(/^(event|command)\s+/, '').split(/\s+/).map(word => word[0].toUpperCase() + word.slice(1)).join('');
const camel = (name: string) => name[0].toLowerCase() + name.slice(1);
const isEvent = (element: string) => element.startsWith('event ');


function source(slice: Slice, command: string, events: string[]): string {
    const lines = [`// ${header}`, `// ${slice.name}: a state change in ${order.scope.feature.name}. Keep the names; fill in only the marked places.`, ''];
    for (const event of events) {
        lines.push(`export type ${event} = {`, `    type: '${event}';`, `    // ${todo}: the fields of ${event}.`, '};', '');
    }
    lines.push(
        `export type Event = ${events.length > 0 ? events.join(' | ') : 'never'};`, '',
        `export type ${command} = {`, `    // ${todo}: the fields of ${command}.`, '};', '',
        `export function ${camel(command)}(history: Event[], command: ${command}): Event[] {`,
        `    // ${todo}: decide from the history which events ${command} appends, or throw an Error to reject it.`,
        `    throw new Error('${command} is not built yet.');`,
        '}');
    return lines.join('\n') + '\n';
}


function test(slice: Slice, command: string, specifications: Specification[]): string {
    const lines = [
        `// ${header}`,
        `// One test per specification of ${slice.name}, named like it. Fill in only the marked places.`,
        `import { describe, expect, it } from 'vitest';`,
        'import {', `    ${camel(command)}, type Event, type ${command}`, `} from './${slice.name}';`, '',
        `describe('${slice.name}', () => {`];
    for (const [index, specification] of specifications.entries()) {
        if (index > 0) lines.push('');
        lines.push(
            `    // Given ${specification.given.join(', ') || 'nothing'}; when ${specification.when}; then ${specification.then.join(', ')}.`,
            `    it('${specification.name.replace(/'/g, "\\'")}', () => {`,
            `        const history: Event[] = [${specification.given.length > 0 ? `/* ${todo}: ${specification.given.map(typeName).join(', ')} */` : ''}];`,
            `        const command: ${command} = { /* ${todo} */ };`,
            specification.then.every(isEvent)
                ? `        expect(${camel(command)}(history, command)).toEqual([/* ${todo}: ${specification.then.map(typeName).join(', ')} */]);`
                : `        expect(() => ${camel(command)}(history, command)).toThrow(); // ${todo}: ${specification.then.join(', ')}`,
            '    });');
    }
    lines.push('});');
    return lines.join('\n') + '\n';
}


const feature = order.scope.feature.name;
if (!safe(feature)) throw new Error(`The feature name '${feature}' cannot be used as a folder name.`);
const problems: string[] = [];


for (const slice of order.scope.slices) {
    if (slice.type !== 'stateChange') throw new Error(`The generator only writes state change slices, not ${slice.name}.`);
    if (!safe(slice.name)) throw new Error(`The slice name '${slice.name}' cannot be used as a file name.`);
    const specifications = order.specifications.filter(specification => specification.sliceId === slice.id);
    const elements = specifications.flatMap(specification => [...specification.given, ...specification.then]);
    const events = [...new Set(elements.filter(isEvent).map(typeName))];
    const command = specifications[0] ? typeName(specifications[0].when) : slice.name;
    const files = {
        [`src/${feature}/${slice.name}.ts`]: source(slice, command, events),
        [`src/${feature}/${slice.name}.test.ts`]: test(slice, command, specifications)
    };


    for (const [file, content] of Object.entries(files)) {
        const existing = existsSync(file) ? readFileSync(file, 'utf8') : undefined;
        if (check) {
            if (existing === undefined) problems.push(`${file} is missing.`);
            else if (!existing.includes(header)) problems.push(`${file} lost its generated header.`);
            else if (existing.includes(todo)) problems.push(`${file} still has places marked ${todo}.`);
        } else if (existing === undefined) {
            mkdirSync(`src/${feature}`, { recursive: true });
            writeFileSync(file, content);
            console.log(`Wrote ${file}`);
        } else {
            console.log(`Kept ${file}, which is already there.`);
        }
    }


    const tests = existsSync(`src/${feature}/${slice.name}.test.ts`) ? readFileSync(`src/${feature}/${slice.name}.test.ts`, 'utf8') : '';
    for (const specification of specifications.filter(specification => !tests.includes(`it('${specification.name.replace(/'/g, "\\'")}'`))) {
        const message = `src/${feature}/${slice.name}.test.ts has no test named '${specification.name}'.`;
        if (check) problems.push(message); else console.log(`Add a test: ${message}`);
    }
}


if (problems.length > 0) {
    console.error(problems.join('\n'));
    process.exit(1);
}
```
 For the [example backend order](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-order), it writes this source file. It already compiles; only the marked places are left: src/Invoicing/PayInvoice.ts

```ts
}
```
 and this test file, with one test per specification, named like it: src/Invoicing/PayInvoice.test.ts 
```ts
// Generated from the build order by the slice generator.

import { describe, expect, it } from 'vitest';
import {

} from './PayInvoice';


describe('PayInvoice', () => {
```

 ### The manifest [Section titled “The manifest”](#the-manifest) ts-vitest-generated/build-kit.yaml 
```yaml
kitVersion: "1"
name: ts-vitest-generated
version: 1.0.0
description: TypeScript slices on Node.js whose structure a generator writes and a coding agent completes.
sliceTypes: [stateChange]
scaffold:
  path: ./scaffold
  commands:
    - npm install
instructions: ./AGENTS.md
prompt: ./build-slice.md
verify:
  - node .cratis/build/kit/generator/generate.mts --check {order}
  - npx tsc --noEmit
  - npx vitest run src/{feature}
git:
  commit: "Build {slices}"
permissions:
  paths: ["src/**"]
  commands:
    - node .cratis/build/kit/generator/generate.mts
    - npx tsc --noEmit
    - npx vitest run
requires:
  - command: node
    version: ">=22.18"
  - command: npm
```
 What differs from the [Build with AI](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/#the-manifest) kit: | Lines | What they do | | ---------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `sliceTypes` | Only state changes: the generator writes nothing else. The CLI refuses other orders before the agent starts | | `verify` | First the generator’s check, with the order’s path filled in for `{order}`: every file, every test and no `TODO(agent)` left. Then the type check and the tests | | `permissions.commands` | The generator, so the agent can run it, with or without `--check`. A permitted command is a prefix, so the arguments after it are allowed | | `requires` | Node.js 22.18 or later, which runs the generator’s TypeScript directly | The generator lives in the kit, so `cratis build init` installs it into the project at `.cratis/build/kit/generator/generate.mts`, and the commands name it there. Install the kit with `init` rather than passing `--kit <folder>` to a build, or the path does not exist. ### The instructions [Section titled “The instructions”](#the-instructions) ts-vitest-generated/AGENTS.md 
```md
# TypeScript slices, generated and completed


This project is a set of event-sourced slices in plain TypeScript on Node.js. A generator writes the structure of every
slice; you complete it.


- The slice generator, `node .cratis/build/kit/generator/generate.mts <order>`, writes `src/<feature>/<slice>.ts` and
  `src/<feature>/<slice>.test.ts` for every slice in a build order. It decides the file names, the type and function
  names, and one test per specification. It never overwrites a file.
- Every place that needs judgment is marked `TODO(agent)`: the fields of each event and command, the body of the
  function that decides which events a command appends, and the data and expectations of each test.
- Fill in those places and remove the marker. Change nothing else: keep the generated header comments, the names, the
  test names, and the files where they are. Do not add files.
- An event has a `type` field with its name. The decide function returns the events the command appends, or throws an
  `Error` when the history rejects it. It does no I/O.
- Strict TypeScript, no `any`, no new packages.
```
 ### The prompt [Section titled “The prompt”](#the-prompt) ts-vitest-generated/build-slice.md 
```md
# Build {slices}


Build {slices} in the feature `{feature}` of `{application}`. The build order is at `{order}`; this is attempt {attempt}.


1. Run the slice generator first: `node .cratis/build/kit/generator/generate.mts {order}`. It prints the files it wrote or kept, and any test that is missing.
2. Fill in every place marked `TODO(agent)` in those files, as the instructions describe. Add a test the generator says is missing, named exactly like its specification.
3. The order's `specifications` are the acceptance criteria. `context.feedback` and `context.hints` come from people on the board, and `context.previousFailure` says why the last attempt failed. Where the order does not say something, such as a field, decide it sensibly and list it as an assumption.
4. Run `npx tsc --noEmit`, then `npx vitest run src/{feature}`, then `node .cratis/build/kit/generator/generate.mts --check {order}`, until all three pass. Run one command at a time: chained or piped commands are refused.
5. Report the result of each specification as `passed` or `failed`, from its test. Write the outcome with your file editing tool.
```
 ## Validate and try the kit [Section titled “Validate and try the kit”](#validate-and-try-the-kit) 1. Check the kit: 
   ```bash
   cratis build kit validate ./ts-vitest-generated
   ```
 2. Run the generator by hand on the [example backend order](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-order), saved as `order.json`, in an empty folder with the scaffold. It writes the two files above, and its check fails until the marked places are filled in: 
   ```bash
   node ../ts-vitest-generated/generator/generate.mts ../order.json
   node ../ts-vitest-generated/generator/generate.mts --check ../order.json
   ```
 3. Create a project and install the kit in it: 
   ```bash
   mkdir shop && cd shop && git init
   git commit --allow-empty -m "Start the shop"
   cratis build init --kit ../ts-vitest-generated --agent claude
   git add . && git commit -m "Install the ts-vitest-generated kit"
   ```
 4. Build the order: 
   ```bash
   cratis build once --order ../order.json --agent claude --outcome ../outcome.json
   ```
 5. Look at the commit with `git show`. The generated header comments, names and test names are as the generator wrote them; the fields, the rule and the test data are the agent’s. ## Why the split works [Section titled “Why the split works”](#why-the-split-works) * **The structure never drifts.** Every slice has the same files, names and test layout, however the agent phrases its code, because the agent does not decide them. Reviews get easier: a reviewer reads the marked places, not the boilerplate. * **The agent works on less.** It reads two short files with clear gaps instead of designing a slice, so it finishes sooner, uses fewer tokens and goes wrong less often. * **The checks know the structure.** The generator’s own check knows exactly what must exist, so it catches a missing test or a gap the agent skipped, which a type check and a test run cannot see: an empty test passes. * **It grows with you.** Every time you notice the agent writing the same thing on every slice, move it into the generator and mark less. ## Tighten it [Section titled “Tighten it”](#tighten-it) * **Permit fewer paths.** `permissions.paths` can name the slice files’ folder only. The CLI commits only files inside it, whatever the agent changed. * **Check more in `verify`.** Add a linter or an architecture check that the agent cannot argue with. Make every command one the agent may run too, so it can see a failure before the CLI does. * **Generate more.** Wiring, registration and shared types are deterministic: generate them, and leave the agent the rules and the test data. * **Write conventions down.** Put naming and style rules in the instructions, and the ones a program can check in `verify`. ## The inverse: AI writes, deterministic checks decide [Section titled “The inverse: AI writes, deterministic checks decide”](#the-inverse-ai-writes-deterministic-checks-decide) You can split the work the other way round: the agent writes all the code, as in [Build with AI](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/), and deterministic tools hold it to your conventions. Put them in the kit’s `verify`, after the type check and the tests: 
```yaml
verify:
  - npx tsc --noEmit
  - npx vitest run src/{feature}
  - npx prettier --check src
  - npx eslint src
permissions:
  commands:
    - npx tsc --noEmit
    - npx vitest run
    - npx prettier
    - npx eslint
```
 Permit the same tools, so the agent runs them and fixes what they find before it reports. A command in `verify` that rewrites files, such as `npx prettier --write src`, runs before the commit, so its changes to the files the agent reported are committed with them. Add the tools to the scaffold’s `package.json`, with their configuration, so they are installed in every new project. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Build with AI](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/): building with an agent alone, and how to choose the agent. * [Build with a generator](/developers/builders/build/deterministic/): the generator contract, for a generator that builds on its own. * [Use the reference builder](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/#build-kits): every member of the kit manifest, and the placeholders.

# Build orders and outcomes

> The versioned JSON contract Studio hands a builder to say what to build, and the outcome a builder answers with, with examples of every document.

A *builder* is anything that turns slices of an event model into code: Studio’s own generation, a coding agent, or a team’s own tool on any stack. Studio and a builder speak through two JSON documents, both described by JSON Schema and both versioned together: * a **build order**, from Studio to the builder: what to build, described in Screenplay, with the context the builder needs; * a **build outcome**, from the builder back to Studio: what came of it. One contract serves every builder, Studio’s own generation included, so the built-in path and a third-party builder cannot drift apart. Both documents are validated against their schema before Studio reads anything from them. **Writing a generator for the Cratis CLI?** Your generator reads the order from the file named by `STUDIO_BUILD_ORDER`, and writes an outcome with only `kind` and the fields of that kind: the CLI adds `contractVersion` and `orderId`, and the `commit` and `branch` once it has committed. The CLI reads version 1 of this contract. See [Build with a generator](/developers/builders/build/deterministic/#the-generator-contract). A coding agent run with `--agent` is told where the order is and how to write its outcome; see [Build with AI](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/). The rest of this page is the full contract, for reference. ## The schemas [Section titled “The schemas”](#the-schemas) Studio serves every published schema at a stable address on its own host, and never changes one once it is released. No sign-in is needed, because the schemas hold no organization data: | Document | Address | | ------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- | | Build order, version 1 | `/api/builders/contract/1/build-order.schema.json` | | Build outcome, version 1 | `/api/builders/contract/1/build-outcome.schema.json` | | Build order, version 2 | `/api/builders/contract/2/build-order.schema.json` | | Build outcome, version 2 | `/api/builders/contract/2/build-outcome.schema.json` | | Build progress, version 2 | `/api/builders/contract/2/build-progress.schema.json` | | Build order, version 3 | `/api/builders/contract/3/build-order.schema.json` | | Build outcome, version 3 | `/api/builders/contract/3/build-outcome.schema.json` | | Build progress, version 3 | `/api/builders/contract/3/build-progress.schema.json` | Generate types from them in your builder’s language, or validate the documents you receive and send. A new version is published beside the old one. ## Versions [Section titled “Versions”](#versions) A builder says which contract versions it reads. Studio offers it an order only in one of them, the newest both speak, and offers nothing when they share none. Every document names its version in `contractVersion`. Version 2 keeps everything in version 1 and adds: * the **build progress** document, which a builder sends while it builds (see [Reporting progress](#reporting-progress)); * `usage` on every outcome: the `inputTokens` and `outputTokens` an agent builder used for the attempt; * `api` on a `built` outcome: the commands and queries a backend build exposes, each with its `kind` (`command` or `query`), `name`, and optionally the HTTP `method` and `route`; * `backend` on a `ui` order: for each target slice, what the backend builder reported (`commit`, `branch`, `pullRequest`, `files`) and the `api` it exposes. Where the backend builder reported no API, Studio describes it from the slice’s command and queries, by name. A builder that reads only version 1 keeps working unchanged. Version 3 keeps everything in version 2 and adds the **removal order** (see [Removing a deleted slice](#removing-a-deleted-slice)): an order with `part` set to `removal`, no slices to build, and the slices deleted from the feature listed as `removed` changes. Outcomes and progress reports are the same as in version 2. A builder that reads only versions 1 and 2 is never handed a removal order; the removal is listed in the next build of the feature instead. ## A build order [Section titled “A build order”](#a-build-order) An order targets one or more slices of one feature. It carries: * **`scope`**: the application, the feature with the module and parent features it sits in, and the target slices with their type and status. Ids are stable across renames. * **`model`**: the Screenplay `.play` documents and, when available, the canonical compiled model with its `schemaVersion` and `revision`. * **`changes`**: per target slice, whether it was `added`, `changed`, `renamed`, `moved` or `removed` since the builder’s last accepted revision, and which of its declarations changed. A builder that has never had an outcome accepted gets every slice as `added`. * **`specifications`**: each slice’s Given/When/Then as acceptance criteria, by specification id. The full specification, with its values, is in the Screenplay documents. * **`context`**: conventions and hints, people’s comments on the slices, the attempt number and why the previous attempt failed. * **`part`**: `backend`, or `ui` once the backend is built and the slice is waiting for a builder that builds user interfaces, or, from version 3, `removal` (see below). * **`deadline`**: when Studio stops waiting. An order never carries a credential. Repository access is handed to the builder separately, scoped and short-lived, and the schema refuses any member it does not declare. 
```json
{
    "contractVersion": "1",
    "orderId": "6f0c3d5e-2b1a-4f7e-9a51-0d2c8e4b7a10",
    "issued": "2026-11-06T09:00:00+00:00",
    "deadline": "2026-11-06T10:00:00+00:00",
    "part": "backend",
    "scope": {
        "application": { "id": "0b9d1f0e-6a4c-4c1e-8f5a-3e2d1c0b9a87", "name": "Shop" },
        "feature": { "id": "4a7e2c91-5d3b-4e8f-a1c2-9b8d7e6f5a40", "name": "Invoicing", "path": ["Billing"] },
        "slices": [
            { "id": "c1d2e3f4-a5b6-4c7d-8e9f-0a1b2c3d4e5f", "name": "PayInvoice", "type": "stateChange", "status": "readyForImplementation" }
        ]
    },
    "model": {
        "language": "screenplay",
        "schemaVersion": "1",
        "revision": "rev-17",
        "documents": [
            { "path": "Billing.play", "content": "module Billing\n  feature Invoicing\n    ..." }
        ],
        "canonical": null
    },
    "changes": [
        { "sliceId": "c1d2e3f4-a5b6-4c7d-8e9f-0a1b2c3d4e5f", "kind": "changed", "declarations": ["command Pay Invoice", "specification Paying an open invoice"] }
    ],
    "specifications": [
        {
            "sliceId": "c1d2e3f4-a5b6-4c7d-8e9f-0a1b2c3d4e5f",
            "id": "8e1f2a3b-4c5d-4e6f-8a9b-0c1d2e3f4a5b",
            "name": "Paying an open invoice",
            "given": ["event Invoice Issued"],
            "when": "command Pay Invoice",
            "then": ["event Invoice Paid"]
        }
    ],
    "context": {
        "conventions": ["Amounts are decimals in the invoice currency"],
        "hints": [],
        "feedback": [
            { "sliceId": "c1d2e3f4-a5b6-4c7d-8e9f-0a1b2c3d4e5f", "text": "Partial payments come later", "occurred": "2026-11-05T14:12:00+00:00" }
        ],
        "attempt": 2,
        "previousFailure": "The build timed out"
    }
}
```
 ## Removing a deleted slice [Section titled “Removing a deleted slice”](#removing-a-deleted-slice) When a slice that was built is deleted from the board, Studio does not wait for the next build of its feature. It offers a removal-only build of its own straight away, to the builder that builds the slice’s backend. Like any build it is claimed under a lease, retried when it fails and blocked when its attempts run out. A removal order has `part` set to `removal`, an empty `scope.slices`, no `specifications`, and lists in `changes` every slice deleted from the feature whose code is still there, each as `removed` with the name it had (`previousName`) and the `declarations` that were built for it. The model is the feature as it is now, without them. Remove what you built for those slices, and nothing else. See [the example](#example-a-removal-order). Answer it like any order: | `kind` | What happens to the removal | | ------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `built` | Carried out, with the commit, branch or pull request that removed the code | | `nothingToDo` | Carried out: the code was already gone | | `blocked` | Blocked with your reason and questions; it is not offered again, and the next build of the feature still lists it | | `failed` | Offered again with the next attempt number, or blocked once attempts run out | A slice that is restored before a builder claims its removal withdraws it, so nothing is removed. A removal a builder already holds finishes first. Builds of other slices in the feature keep listing the removals still waiting, so a removal is carried out by whichever build gets to it first. Studio’s own generation removes the files of deleted slices the next time it generates their feature. ## A build outcome [Section titled “A build outcome”](#a-build-outcome) An outcome answers one order, by its `orderId`, and is exactly one of four kinds: | `kind` | Means | Carries | | ------------- | ------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `built` | The slices are built | at least one of `commit`, `branch`, `pullRequest`; the `files` the builder owns; a `result` per specification (`passed`, `failed`, `notRun`); the `assumptions` it made | | `blocked` | The slices cannot be built until someone answers | the `reason` and the `questions` that need an answer | | `failed` | The attempt went wrong | the `error`, and whether a retry makes sense (`retryable`) | | `nothingToDo` | Nothing needed building | nothing more | 
```json
{
    "contractVersion": "1",
    "orderId": "6f0c3d5e-2b1a-4f7e-9a51-0d2c8e4b7a10",
    "kind": "built",
    "pullRequest": "https://github.com/acme/shop/pull/42",
    "branch": "build/pay-invoice",
    "files": ["Source/Billing/Invoicing/PayInvoice/PayInvoice.cs"],
    "specifications": [
        { "id": "8e1f2a3b-4c5d-4e6f-8a9b-0c1d2e3f4a5b", "result": "passed" }
    ],
    "assumptions": ["An invoice that is already paid is refused"]
}
```
 
```json
{
    "contractVersion": "1",
    "orderId": "6f0c3d5e-2b1a-4f7e-9a51-0d2c8e4b7a10",
    "kind": "blocked",
    "reason": "The model does not say which payment provider settles the invoice",
    "questions": ["Which payment provider?", "Are partial payments allowed?"]
}
```
 In version 2, any outcome may also carry `usage`, and a `built` outcome may carry `api`, as in [the example with usage and API](#example-a-built-outcome-with-usage-and-api). Studio validates every outcome against the schema of its version before it reads anything from it. A malformed outcome is refused with the reasons, each naming where in the document it went wrong, and changes nothing. More examples follow at the end of this page. ## Blocked and waiting slices [Section titled “Blocked and waiting slices”](#blocked-and-waiting-slices) A slice can be **Blocked**, with a reason and optionally questions, and **Waiting** for a builder that builds user interfaces. People set both from the slice’s status picker, and builders through Studio. Both show on the board and in the progress board. Unblocking a slice, or changing it, moves it back to Ready for implementation. Studio’s own generation does not take a blocked or waiting slice. ## Reporting progress [Section titled “Reporting progress”](#reporting-progress) While it holds an order, a builder speaking version 2 or later can report what it is doing. Each report names the order and carries at least one of: * `step`: what it is doing now, in a few words; * `log`: the lines written since its previous report, oldest first, at most 100, each with when it was written (`occurred`), its `level` (`information`, `warning` or `error`) and its `text`; * `usage`: the tokens used for the attempt so far, in total. 
```json
{
    "contractVersion": "2",
    "orderId": "6f0c3d5e-2b1a-4f7e-9a51-0d2c8e4b7a10",
    "step": "Running the specifications",
    "log": [
        { "occurred": "2026-11-06T09:12:03+00:00", "level": "information", "text": "dotnet test Billing.Specs" }
    ],
    "usage": { "inputTokens": 31877, "outputTokens": 4410 }
}
```
 A report does not renew the lease; keep sending heartbeats. Like a heartbeat, a report for an order whose lease was lost is refused, which tells the builder to stop. **Never send a credential.** Studio keeps the step, the log, errors, block reasons and assumptions for good and shows them on the board, so before it keeps any of them it removes what looks like a credential (tokens, keys, passwords, private keys, credentials in URLs) and cuts each text to 2000 characters. That is a safety net, not permission: strip secrets from tool output before you report it. ## What the board shows [Section titled “What the board shows”](#what-the-board-shows) Once a builder has claimed a slice, the slice header shows what it is doing, or how its last build went, and how many assumptions are still to review. Opening it shows the step and log of the build under way, every attempt with its part, builder, outcome, reason, time and tokens, each specification’s result beside the board’s own run, and the builder’s assumptions. Each specification on the board also shows the builder’s result beside its own run, and marks it when the two disagree: the builder says it passes and the board’s run fails, or the other way round. Each assumption can be **accepted**, when the builder filled the gap the way it should be filled, or **dismissed**, for example once the model has been changed to say it. ## Separate builders for the backend and the user interface [Section titled “Separate builders for the backend and the user interface”](#separate-builders-for-the-backend-and-the-user-interface) A slice with screens can be built in two steps: one builder builds its backend, another its user interface. The backend build then ends at **Waiting** instead of Ready for review, and the slice is offered to builders of user interfaces with a `ui` order that says what the backend built and the API it exposes. That happens when: * the builder that claimed the backend does not build user interfaces, or * the application names a backend builder and a user interface builder. In the application’s building settings, turn on **Build the user interface with a separate builder** and choose the two builders. A named application’s slices are then claimed only by the named builder for each part, and any other builder’s claim is refused. Turn the setting off to lift that. A slice without screens is done when its backend is built. A builder that builds both parts, with no other builder named, builds the whole slice in one order as before. ## Examples [Section titled “Examples”](#examples) Every example is a complete document that validates against the schema of its version. ### Example: a backend order [Section titled “Example: a backend order”](#example-a-backend-order) The order shown [above](#a-build-order) is a version 1 order for the backend part of one slice. ### Example: a user interface order [Section titled “Example: a user interface order”](#example-a-user-interface-order) A `ui` order, in version 2, adds `backend`: what the backend builder reported for each slice, with the API it exposes. 
```json
{
    "contractVersion": "2",
    "orderId": "6f0c3d5e-2b1a-4f7e-9a51-0d2c8e4b7a10",
    "issued": "2026-11-06T09:00:00+00:00",
    "deadline": "2026-11-06T10:00:00+00:00",
    "part": "ui",
    "scope": {
        "application": { "id": "0b9d1f0e-6a4c-4c1e-8f5a-3e2d1c0b9a87", "name": "Shop" },
        "feature": { "id": "4a7e2c91-5d3b-4e8f-a1c2-9b8d7e6f5a40", "name": "Invoicing", "path": ["Billing"] },
        "slices": [
            { "id": "c1d2e3f4-a5b6-4c7d-8e9f-0a1b2c3d4e5f", "name": "PayInvoice", "type": "stateChange", "status": "waiting" }
        ]
    },
    "model": {
        "language": "screenplay",
        "schemaVersion": "1",
        "revision": "rev-17",
        "documents": [
            { "path": "Billing.play", "content": "module Billing\n  feature Invoicing\n    ..." }
        ],
        "canonical": null
    },
    "changes": [
        { "sliceId": "c1d2e3f4-a5b6-4c7d-8e9f-0a1b2c3d4e5f", "kind": "changed", "declarations": ["command Pay Invoice", "specification Paying an open invoice"] }
    ],
    "specifications": [
        {
            "sliceId": "c1d2e3f4-a5b6-4c7d-8e9f-0a1b2c3d4e5f",
            "id": "8e1f2a3b-4c5d-4e6f-8a9b-0c1d2e3f4a5b",
            "name": "Paying an open invoice",
            "given": ["event Invoice Issued"],
            "when": "command Pay Invoice",
            "then": ["event Invoice Paid"]
        }
    ],
    "context": {
        "conventions": ["Amounts are decimals in the invoice currency"],
        "hints": [],
        "feedback": [
            { "sliceId": "c1d2e3f4-a5b6-4c7d-8e9f-0a1b2c3d4e5f", "text": "Partial payments come later", "occurred": "2026-11-05T14:12:00+00:00" }
        ],
        "attempt": 2,
        "previousFailure": "The build timed out"
    },
    "backend": [
        {
            "sliceId": "c1d2e3f4-a5b6-4c7d-8e9f-0a1b2c3d4e5f",
            "branch": "build/pay-invoice",
            "pullRequest": "https://github.com/acme/shop/pull/42",
            "files": ["Source/Billing/Invoicing/PayInvoice/PayInvoice.cs"],
            "api": [
                { "kind": "command", "name": "Pay Invoice", "method": "POST", "route": "/api/billing/invoicing/pay-invoice" },
                { "kind": "query", "name": "Open Invoices" }
            ]
        }
    ]
}
```
 ### Example: a removal order [Section titled “Example: a removal order”](#example-a-removal-order) A version 3 order with `part` set to `removal`. It has no slices to build, and lists the deleted slice under `changes`.

# Sample: a generator in Bash

> A generator for the Cratis CLI in one Bash script with jq: it reads the build order, writes a sheet per slice and reports the outcome.

This sample is a generator in one Bash script, with nothing but `jq`. It is a *generator*: the Cratis CLI runs it for every build order, and does everything else a builder does, from connecting to Studio to committing and reporting. See [Build with a generator](/developers/builders/build/deterministic/) for how the two fit together. For every slice in the order, the generator writes a Markdown sheet with the slice’s acceptance criteria to `slices/<feature>/<slice>.md` in the project, and reports what it wrote. The comment in the code marks where real code generation goes. The [TypeScript](/developers/builders/build/typescript/) and [C#](/developers/builders/build/csharp/) samples do exactly the same thing, and write the same sheets. The language is your choice: anything the shell can start works. ## What you need [Section titled “What you need”](#what-you-need) * The [Cratis CLI](https://www.cratis.io/cli/getting-started/), with `cratis build`. * Bash and [jq](https://jqlang.org/) 1.6 or later. On macOS, `brew install jq`; on Debian or Ubuntu, `apt install jq`. * A clean clone of the application’s repository. ## The code [Section titled “The code”](#the-code) Save the script as `~/slice-sheets/generate.sh`, outside the application’s repository, and make it executable with `chmod +x ~/slice-sheets/generate.sh`. generate.sh 
```bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Slice sheets: a generator for `cratis build`. For every slice in the build order it writes
# a Markdown sheet with the slice's acceptance criteria into the project.
set -euo pipefail


order="$STUDIO_BUILD_ORDER"
outcome="$STUDIO_BUILD_OUTCOME"


fail() {
  jq -n --arg error "$1" '{ kind: "failed", error: $error, retryable: false }' > "$outcome"
  exit 0
}


safe() {
  case "$1" in
    "" | *[!A-Za-z0-9._\ -]* | .*) return 1 ;;
  esac
}


feature=$(jq -r '.scope.feature.name' "$order")
safe "$feature" || fail "The feature name '$feature' cannot be used as a folder name."


files=()
while IFS=$'\t' read -r id name; do
  safe "$name" || fail "The slice name '$name' cannot be used as a file name."
  file="slices/$feature/$name.md"


  # This is where real generation goes: run your code generator or templates for the
  # slice here and write the files they produce. This sample writes one sheet per slice.
  sheet=$(jq -r --arg id "$id" '
    (.scope.slices[] | select(.id == $id)) as $slice
    | "# \($slice.name)",
      "",
      "A \($slice.type) slice in \(.scope.feature.name).",
      "",
      "## Acceptance criteria",
      (.specifications[] | select(.sliceId == $id)
        | "", "### \(.name)", "",
          "- Given \(if (.given | length) > 0 then .given | join(", ") else "nothing" end)",
          "- When \(.when)",
          "- Then \(.then | join(", "))")
  ' "$order")


  if [ -f "$file" ] && [ "$(cat "$file")" = "$sheet" ]; then
    continue
  fi
  mkdir -p "$(dirname "$file")"
  printf '%s\n' "$sheet" > "$file"
  files+=("$file")
done < <(jq -r '.scope.slices[] | [.id, .name] | @tsv' "$order")


if [ "${#files[@]}" -eq 0 ]; then
  jq -n '{ kind: "nothingToDo" }' > "$outcome"
  exit 0
fi


jq '{
  kind: "built",
  files: $ARGS.positional,
  specifications: [.specifications[] | { id, result: "notRun" }],
  assumptions: ["Wrote a slice sheet for each slice; no code was generated."]
}' "$order" --args "${files[@]}" > "$outcome"
```
 ## Run it once, offline [Section titled “Run it once, offline”](#run-it-once-offline) `cratis build once` runs the generator on an order from a file, without connecting to Studio, and commits what it built just as a real build does. 1. Save the [example backend order](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-order) as `~/order.json`. 2. In the clone of the repository, run the generator on it: 
   ```bash
   cratis build once --order ~/order.json --exec "$HOME/slice-sheets/generate.sh" --verify "test -d slices" --outcome ~/outcome.json
   ```
 `--verify` is where your build and tests go, for example `dotnet test` or `npm test`. Every `--verify` command runs after the generator; one that fails turns the outcome into `failed`. 3. Check the result. `~/outcome.json` says `built` and lists `slices/Invoicing/PayInvoice.md`, and `git log` shows the commit. Run the same command again: the sheet is unchanged, so the outcome is `nothingToDo` and nothing is committed. ## Run it for real [Section titled “Run it for real”](#run-it-for-real) 1. Register the machine with your organization, once, and have a maintainer approve it with the code it shows. See [Connect a builder and approve it](/developers/builders/configure/connect-and-approve/). 
   ```bash
   cratis build connect --studio --organization <your organization>
   ```
 2. In the clone of the repository, start the build loop with the generator: 
   ```bash
   cratis build run --exec "$HOME/slice-sheets/generate.sh" --verify "test -d slices"
   ```
 3. In Studio, [choose the builder](/developers/builders/configure/choose-builders/) for the application, and mark a slice **Ready for implementation**. The terminal shows the generator running, the commit, and how Studio settled the slice. The CLI pushes each commit with your own Git credentials. Add `--no-push` to keep the commits local, or `--once` to stop after one slice. ## Keep it running [Section titled “Keep it running”](#keep-it-running) On macOS and Linux, install the loop as a user service, from the clone of the repository: 
```bash
cratis build service install --exec "$HOME/slice-sheets/generate.sh" --verify "test -d slices"
```
 It starts when you sign in and restarts when it stops. `cratis build service status` says where its output goes, and `cratis build service uninstall` removes it. ## How it works [Section titled “How it works”](#how-it-works) ### Reading the order [Section titled “Reading the order”](#reading-the-order) `jq` reads the feature name and, one line per slice, the slice’s `id` and `name` from the file `STUDIO_BUILD_ORDER` names. The script runs in the project folder, so the paths it writes are relative to the project. ### Writing the sheet [Section titled “Writing the sheet”](#writing-the-sheet) A second `jq` program builds the sheet for one slice: its name and type, then each specification whose `sliceId` matches, as Given, When and Then. This is where your own generation goes: call a code generator, fill templates, or write the command and event files for the slice. ### Nothing to do [Section titled “Nothing to do”](#nothing-to-do) A sheet that is already in the project with the same content is skipped. When every sheet is unchanged, the script reports `nothingToDo`, so the CLI makes no empty commit. ### The outcome [Section titled “The outcome”](#the-outcome) The last `jq` writes `built` with the files the script wrote, each specification as `notRun` (the sample runs no tests), and one assumption. The CLI adds the order’s id, the contract version and the commit. ### Failing [Section titled “Failing”](#failing) A feature or slice name that cannot be a file name makes `fail` write a `failed` outcome with `retryable` set to `false`: another attempt would fail the same way. The script still exits with 0, because it wrote an outcome. If the script itself crashes, `set -e` makes it exit with an error, and the CLI reports a failure that Studio retries. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Build with a generator](/developers/builders/build/deterministic/): the generator contract, step by step. * [Deterministic with AI on top](/developers/builders/build/deterministic-and-ai/): a generator that writes the structure, and a coding agent that completes it. * [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/): everything in the order the generator reads. * [Use the reference builder](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/): every `cratis build` command, and build kits.

# Sample: a generator in TypeScript

> A generator for the Cratis CLI in one TypeScript file run by Node.js: it reads the build order, writes a sheet per slice and reports the outcome.

This sample is a generator in one TypeScript file. Node.js runs it as it is, with no build step and no packages. It is a *generator*: the Cratis CLI runs it for every build order, and does everything else a builder does, from connecting to Studio to committing and reporting. See [Build with a generator](/developers/builders/build/deterministic/) for how the two fit together. For every slice in the order, the generator writes a Markdown sheet with the slice’s acceptance criteria to `slices/<feature>/<slice>.md` in the project, and reports what it wrote. The comment in the code marks where real code generation goes. The [Bash](/developers/builders/build/shell/) and [C#](/developers/builders/build/csharp/) samples do exactly the same thing, and write the same sheets. The language is your choice: anything the shell can start works. ## What you need [Section titled “What you need”](#what-you-need) * The [Cratis CLI](https://www.cratis.io/cli/getting-started/), with `cratis build`. * Node.js 22.18 or later, which runs TypeScript files directly. * A clean clone of the application’s repository. ## The code [Section titled “The code”](#the-code) Save the file as `~/slice-sheets/generate.mts`, outside the application’s repository. The `.mts` extension tells Node.js it is a module. generate.mts 
```ts
// Slice sheets: a generator for `cratis build`. For every slice in the build order it writes
// a Markdown sheet with the slice's acceptance criteria into the project.
import { existsSync, mkdirSync, readFileSync, writeFileSync } from 'node:fs';
import { dirname } from 'node:path';


type Slice = { id: string; name: string; type: string };
type Specification = { sliceId: string; id: string; name: string; given: string[]; when: string; then: string[] };
type Order = { scope: { feature: { name: string }; slices: Slice[] }; specifications: Specification[] };


const order: Order = JSON.parse(readFileSync(process.env.STUDIO_BUILD_ORDER!, 'utf8'));
const outcomeFile = process.env.STUDIO_BUILD_OUTCOME!;
const writeOutcome = (outcome: object) => writeFileSync(outcomeFile, JSON.stringify(outcome, null, 2));
const safe = (name: string) => /^[A-Za-z0-9_ -][A-Za-z0-9._ -]*$/.test(name);


function sheetFor(slice: Slice): string {
    const lines = [`# ${slice.name}`, '', `A ${slice.type} slice in ${order.scope.feature.name}.`, '', '## Acceptance criteria'];
    for (const specification of order.specifications.filter(s => s.sliceId === slice.id)) {
        lines.push(
            '',
            `### ${specification.name}`,
            '',
            `- Given ${specification.given.length > 0 ? specification.given.join(', ') : 'nothing'}`,
            `- When ${specification.when}`,
            `- Then ${specification.then.join(', ')}`);
    }
    return lines.join('\n') + '\n';
}


try {
    const feature = order.scope.feature.name;
    if (!safe(feature)) throw new Error(`The feature name '${feature}' cannot be used as a folder name.`);


    const files: string[] = [];
    for (const slice of order.scope.slices) {
        if (!safe(slice.name)) throw new Error(`The slice name '${slice.name}' cannot be used as a file name.`);
        const file = `slices/${feature}/${slice.name}.md`;


        // This is where real generation goes: run your code generator or templates for the
        // slice here and write the files they produce. This sample writes one sheet per slice.
        const sheet = sheetFor(slice);


        if (existsSync(file) && readFileSync(file, 'utf8') === sheet) continue;
        mkdirSync(dirname(file), { recursive: true });
        writeFileSync(file, sheet);
        files.push(file);
    }


    if (files.length === 0) {
        writeOutcome({ kind: 'nothingToDo' });
    } else {
        writeOutcome({
            kind: 'built',
            files,
            specifications: order.specifications.map(s => ({ id: s.id, result: 'notRun' })),
            assumptions: ['Wrote a slice sheet for each slice; no code was generated.']
        });
    }
} catch (error) {
    writeOutcome({ kind: 'failed', error: error instanceof Error ? error.message : String(error), retryable: false });
}
```
 ## Run it once, offline [Section titled “Run it once, offline”](#run-it-once-offline) `cratis build once` runs the generator on an order from a file, without connecting to Studio, and commits what it built just as a real build does. 1. Save the [example backend order](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-order) as `~/order.json`. 2. In the clone of the repository, run the generator on it: 
   ```bash
   cratis build once --order ~/order.json --exec "node $HOME/slice-sheets/generate.mts" --verify "test -d slices" --outcome ~/outcome.json
   ```
 `--verify` is where your build and tests go, for example `dotnet test` or `npm test`. Every `--verify` command runs after the generator; one that fails turns the outcome into `failed`. 3. Check the result. `~/outcome.json` says `built` and lists `slices/Invoicing/PayInvoice.md`, and `git log` shows the commit. Run the same command again: the sheet is unchanged, so the outcome is `nothingToDo` and nothing is committed. ## Run it for real [Section titled “Run it for real”](#run-it-for-real) 1. Register the machine with your organization, once, and have a maintainer approve it with the code it shows. See [Connect a builder and approve it](/developers/builders/configure/connect-and-approve/). 
   ```bash
   cratis build connect --studio --organization <your organization>
   ```
 2. In the clone of the repository, start the build loop with the generator: 
   ```bash
   cratis build run --exec "node $HOME/slice-sheets/generate.mts" --verify "test -d slices"
   ```
 3. In Studio, [choose the builder](/developers/builders/configure/choose-builders/) for the application, and mark a slice **Ready for implementation**. The terminal shows the generator running, the commit, and how Studio settled the slice. The CLI pushes each commit with your own Git credentials. Add `--no-push` to keep the commits local, or `--once` to stop after one slice. ## Keep it running [Section titled “Keep it running”](#keep-it-running) On macOS and Linux, install the loop as a user service, from the clone of the repository: 
```bash
cratis build service install --exec "node $HOME/slice-sheets/generate.mts" --verify "test -d slices"
```
 It starts when you sign in and restarts when it stops. `cratis build service status` says where its output goes, and `cratis build service uninstall` removes it. ## How it works [Section titled “How it works”](#how-it-works) ### Reading the order [Section titled “Reading the order”](#reading-the-order) `JSON.parse` reads the file `STUDIO_BUILD_ORDER` names. The `Order` type declares only the members the sample uses; [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/) lists them all, with a JSON Schema you can generate types from. The script runs in the project folder, so the paths it writes are relative to the project. ### Writing the sheet [Section titled “Writing the sheet”](#writing-the-sheet) `sheetFor` builds the sheet for one slice: its name and type, then each specification whose `sliceId` matches, as Given, When and Then. This is where your own generation goes: call a code generator, fill templates, or write the command and event files for the slice. ### Nothing to do [Section titled “Nothing to do”](#nothing-to-do) A sheet that is already in the project with the same content is skipped. When every sheet is unchanged, the script reports `nothingToDo`, so the CLI makes no empty commit. ### The outcome [Section titled “The outcome”](#the-outcome) `writeOutcome` writes `built` with the files the script wrote, each specification as `notRun` (the sample runs no tests), and one assumption. The CLI adds the order’s id, the contract version and the commit. ### Failing [Section titled “Failing”](#failing) Any error, such as a feature or slice name that cannot be a file name, is caught and written as a `failed` outcome with `retryable` set to `false`, and the script exits with 0 because it wrote an outcome. Set `retryable` to `true` for an error another attempt could get past. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Build with a generator](/developers/builders/build/deterministic/): the generator contract, step by step. * [Deterministic with AI on top](/developers/builders/build/deterministic-and-ai/): a generator that writes the structure, and a coding agent that completes it. * [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/): everything in the order the generator reads. * [Use the reference builder](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/): every `cratis build` command, and build kits.

# Build with AI

> Let a coding agent build your slices with the Cratis CLI, inside the files, commands and checks of a build kit. Start from a registered kit or write your own.

A coding agent can build a slice from its build order: read the specifications, write the code and the tests, and run them until they pass. The Cratis CLI starts the agent, keeps it to the files and commands you permit, and runs your checks itself before anything is committed. You describe your stack once, in a **build kit**, and the agent follows it on every slice. This is the way to choose when you want the most code for the least effort, on any stack. Its quality depends on two things you control: the instructions the agent gets, and the checks that catch what it gets wrong. For how it compares with the other ways to build, see [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/#three-ways-to-build). ## Quick start with a registered kit [Section titled “Quick start with a registered kit”](#quick-start-with-a-registered-kit) Studio registers reference kits by name, such as `node` for TypeScript on the Cratis Chronicle client and `cratis-csharp` for .NET on Cratis Arc and Chronicle. See [Registered kits](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/#registered-kits). 1. In an empty Git repository for the application, install the kit for the agent you use: 
   ```bash
   cratis build init --kit node --agent claude
   ```
 The CLI checks that the kit’s tools are installed, scaffolds the project, copies the kit to `.cratis/build/kit`, and writes the kit’s instructions where the agent reads them: `CLAUDE.md` for Claude Code, `GEMINI.md` for Gemini CLI, `AGENTS.md` for the others. 2. Commit what `init` wrote. A build starts from a clean project. 3. Connect the machine once, and start building: 
   ```bash
   cratis build connect --studio --organization <your organization>
   cratis build run
   ```
 `run` uses the kit and the agent that `init` recorded. See [Run it as a builder](/developers/builders/build/#run-it-as-a-builder). To try it without Studio, save the [example backend order](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-order) as `order.json` beside the project and run `cratis build once --order ../order.json --outcome ../outcome.json` in the project. ## Choose the agent [Section titled “Choose the agent”](#choose-the-agent) Name the agent with `--agent`, on `init` (it is recorded for later builds), `run`, `once` or `service install`: | `--agent` | Agent | How the CLI talks to it | | --------------- | -------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `claude` | Claude Code | Runs `claude` directly, with the kit’s permissions as its allowed tools | | `codex` | Codex | Over the Agent Client Protocol, through `npx -y @zed-industries/codex-acp` | | `gemini` | Gemini CLI | Over the Agent Client Protocol, through `gemini --experimental-acp` | | `copilot` | Copilot CLI | Over the Agent Client Protocol, through `copilot --acp` | | `opencode` | OpenCode | Over the Agent Client Protocol, through `opencode acp` | | `cursor`, `acp` | Cursor, or any other agent | Over the Agent Client Protocol, through the command you give with `--agent-command` | `--agent-command` also replaces the command for a known agent, for example to run a particular installation. The agent must be installed and signed in on the machine: it builds with your own account and plan. ## What the agent gets, and what it may do [Section titled “What the agent gets, and what it may do”](#what-the-agent-gets-and-what-it-may-do) For every order, the CLI writes the order and its Screenplay documents to a folder outside the project, and starts the agent in the project with: * **the kit’s instructions and skills**, as the agent’s own instructions, * **the Screenplay guidance** and a Screenplay tool server for the order’s model, so the agent can read the model and run its specifications, * **the kit’s prompt**, with its [placeholders](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/#placeholders) filled from the order, followed by the rules for the outcome document: where to write it and what it may hold. The agent works inside the kit’s permissions, and the CLI answers every request it makes: * It may **read** the project and the order’s folder. * It may **write** only the files that match `permissions.paths`, and its outcome file. It never writes inside `.git`. * It may **run** only the commands that start with an entry of `permissions.commands` or a `verify` command, followed by a space or nothing. A command that chains, pipes, redirects or substitutes another (`&&`, `;`, `|`, `>`, `` ` ``, `$(`) is refused, whatever it starts with. * It never commits, pushes or switches branches. The CLI does that after verification. An agent is never run with its permission checks switched off, and Claude Code runs without your own user settings, hooks and plugins, so a build behaves the same on every machine. An agent that stops without writing an outcome, as Copilot CLI does when a request is refused, is asked twice to go on. Permissions are not a sandbox A permitted command runs with your user’s rights, and a program it starts is not checked again. Permit only commands you would run yourself, such as your compiler and your test runner. The `verify` commands run after the agent, whatever it wrote, so a slice is never committed unless they pass. ## Sample: a kit of your own [Section titled “Sample: a kit of your own”](#sample-a-kit-of-your-own) This sample is a small kit for slices in plain TypeScript on Node.js, tested with Vitest, with no framework. It shows every part a kit needs. Make a folder `ts-vitest` beside your projects, with these files: 
```text
ts-vitest/
├── build-kit.yaml         # the manifest
├── AGENTS.md              # the stack instructions
├── build-slice.md         # the prompt for one build order
└── scaffold/              # the files of a new project
    ├── .gitignore
    ├── package.json
    └── tsconfig.json
```
 ### The manifest [Section titled “The manifest”](#the-manifest) ts-vitest/build-kit.yaml 
```yaml
kitVersion: "1"
name: ts-vitest
version: 1.0.0
description: Plain TypeScript slices on Node.js, specified with Vitest.
sliceTypes: [stateChange, stateView]
scaffold:
  path: ./scaffold
  commands:
    - npm install
instructions: ./AGENTS.md
```
