```yaml
prompt: ./build-slice.md
verify:
  - npx tsc --noEmit
  - npx vitest run src/{feature}
git:
  commit: "Build {slices}"
permissions:
  paths: ["src/**"]
  commands:
    - npx tsc --noEmit
    - npx vitest run
requires:
  - command: node
    version: ">=22"
  - command: npm
```
 | Lines | What they do | | ---------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `kitVersion`, `name`, `version`, `description` | Identify the kit. `name` is lowercase words joined by hyphens | | `sliceTypes` | The kit builds state changes and state views. The CLI refuses an order for an automation or a translation with a `failed` outcome, before the agent starts | | `scaffold` | Copies the `scaffold` folder into a new, empty project and runs `npm install` there | | `instructions`, `prompt` | The stack instructions the agent always reads, and the prompt for one order | | `verify` | Run by the CLI after the agent, in the project: the type check, then the tests of the feature. `{feature}` is filled from the order. Vitest fails when it finds no tests, so a slice without tests is never committed | | `git.commit` | The commit message, here `Build PayInvoice` | | `permissions.paths` | The agent writes only under `src`. It cannot change the package, the scaffold or the kit | | `permissions.commands` | The agent may run the type check and the tests, and nothing else. It may not install a package | | `requires` | `init` refuses to install the kit unless Node.js 22 or later and npm are on the machine | ### The instructions [Section titled “The instructions”](#the-instructions) The instructions say how code is written in this stack: where a slice goes, what it is named, and how a specification becomes a test. Keep them about the stack; what to build comes from the order. ts-vitest/AGENTS.md 
```md
# TypeScript slices


This project is a set of event-sourced slices in plain TypeScript on Node.js, with no framework.


- Each feature is a folder under `src/`, named exactly like the feature: `src/Invoicing/`.
- Each slice is one file named after the slice, `src/Invoicing/PayInvoice.ts`, with its tests beside it in
  `src/Invoicing/PayInvoice.test.ts`.
- Events and commands are TypeScript types. An event has a `type` field with its name, such as
  `{ type: 'InvoicePaid', invoiceId: string, amount: number }`.
- A state change slice exports one function named after its command in camel case:
  `payInvoice(history: Event[], command: PayInvoice): Event[]`. It returns the events the command appends, or throws an
  `Error` when the history rejects it. It does no I/O.
- A state view slice exports one function named after its read model in camel case, which folds the events into the
  read model: `openInvoices(events: Event[]): OpenInvoices`.
- Each specification in the build order becomes one Vitest test, named exactly like the specification: its `given`
  events are the history, its `when` is the command, and its `then` is what the test expects. Add a test for every
  other rule you write, such as a rejection.
- Strict TypeScript, no `any`, no new packages.
```
 ### The prompt [Section titled “The prompt”](#the-prompt) The prompt is the task for one order. The CLI fills `{slices}`, `{part}`, `{feature}`, `{application}`, `{order}` and `{attempt}`, and adds the rules for the outcome document after it. ts-vitest/build-slice.md 
```md
# Build {slices}


Build the {part} of {slices} in the feature `{feature}` of `{application}`. The build order is at `{order}`; this is attempt {attempt}.


1. Read the build order. Its `specifications` for these slices are the acceptance criteria: build what they describe, nothing more. `context.feedback` and `context.hints` come from people on the board, and `context.previousFailure` says why the last attempt failed.
2. Work in the folder `src/{feature}/`. When a slice's file is already there, change it in place.
3. Write each slice to `src/{feature}/<slice name>.ts` and its tests to `src/{feature}/<slice name>.test.ts`, as the instructions describe. Where the order does not say something, such as a field, decide it sensibly and list it as an assumption.
4. Run `npx tsc --noEmit`, then `npx vitest run src/{feature}`, until both pass. Run one command at a time: chained or piped commands are refused.
5. Report the result of each specification as `passed` or `failed`, from its test. Write the outcome with your file editing tool.
```
 ### The scaffold [Section titled “The scaffold”](#the-scaffold) The files of a new project: the packages, the TypeScript settings and what Git ignores. ts-vitest/scaffold/package.json 
```json
{
  "name": "slices",
  "private": true,
  "type": "module",
  "scripts": {
    "typecheck": "tsc --noEmit",
    "test": "vitest run"
  },
  "devDependencies": {
    "typescript": "^7.0.0",
    "vitest": "^5.1.0"
  }
}
```
 ts-vitest/scaffold/tsconfig.json 
```json
{
  "compilerOptions": {
    "target": "ES2022",
    "module": "ESNext",
    "moduleResolution": "Bundler",
    "strict": true,
    "noEmit": true,
    "skipLibCheck": true
  },
  "include": ["src"]
}
```
 ts-vitest/scaffold/.gitignore 
```text
node_modules/
```
 ## Validate and try the kit [Section titled “Validate and try the kit”](#validate-and-try-the-kit) 1. Check the kit. The CLI validates the manifest, checks that every file it names is there and not empty, and that every placeholder is one it fills: 
   ```bash
   cratis build kit validate ./ts-vitest
   ```
 2. Create a project and install the kit in it: 
   ```bash
   mkdir shop && cd shop && git init
   git commit --allow-empty -m "Start the shop"
   cratis build init --kit ../ts-vitest --agent claude
   git add . && git commit -m "Install the ts-vitest kit"
   ```
 3. Save the [example backend order](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-order) as `../order.json`, and build it: 
   ```bash
   cratis build once --order ../order.json --agent claude --outcome ../outcome.json
   ```
 4. Read `../outcome.json`, and look at the commit with `git show`. The agent wrote `src/Invoicing/PayInvoice.ts` and `src/Invoicing/PayInvoice.test.ts`, the CLI ran the type check and the tests, and committed both files as `Build PayInvoice`. The outcome lists the files, the result of each specification, and the decisions the agent made where the order was silent. Studio shows those assumptions on the slice, so a person can confirm them or add what was missing to the model: 
```json
{
  "contractVersion": "1",
  "orderId": "6f0c3d5e-2b1a-4f7e-9a51-0d2c8e4b7a10",
  "kind": "built",
  "files": ["src/Invoicing/PayInvoice.ts", "src/Invoicing/PayInvoice.test.ts"],
  "specifications": [{ "id": "8e1f2a3b-4c5d-4e6f-8a9b-0c1d2e3f4a5b", "result": "passed" }],
  "assumptions": [
    "PayInvoice identifies an invoice by invoiceId.",
    "PayInvoice pays the full outstanding amount; partial payments are not supported."
  ],
  "commit": "3c9c782223c92da32371b49d5985dc7f6e93080b",
  "branch": "main"
}
```
 The code differs from run to run: an agent is not deterministic. What does not change is what the CLI checks. When you want parts of the code to come out the same every time, generate them: see [Deterministic with AI on top](/developers/builders/build/deterministic-and-ai/). ## Write good instructions [Section titled “Write good instructions”](#write-good-instructions) * **Point at the specifications.** Every specification in the order is an acceptance criterion. Ask for one test per specification, named like it, so the outcome’s results mean something and a reviewer can find them. * **Be concrete about the layout.** Name the folder, the file and the function, with an example. An agent asked for “a slice in the feature folder” picks a folder of its own; one shown `src/{feature}/<slice name>.ts` writes it there. * **Let the checks decide.** Put the type check and the tests in `verify`, and tell the agent to run the same commands until they pass. The CLI runs them again afterwards, and only their result counts. * **Permit little.** Permit the paths the slices live in and the commands the agent needs to check its work. Leave out package installs and anything that reaches the network. * **Ask for one command at a time.** Chained and piped commands are refused, and an agent that keeps asking for them wastes its turns. Ask it to write the outcome with its file editing tool rather than a shell command. * **Ask for assumptions.** Where the order is silent, a listed assumption is better than a silent decision: it comes back to the slice in Studio. * **Use `{conventions}` for existing code.** A kit that builds into a project it did not scaffold learns the project’s conventions once and passes them to every build. See [Existing codebases](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/#existing-codebases). ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Deterministic with AI on top](/developers/builders/build/deterministic-and-ai/): a generator writes the structure and the agent completes it. * [Use the reference builder](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/#build-kits): every member of the kit manifest, and the placeholders. * [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/): what the agent reads, and what it answers with.

# Choose who builds

> Choose the builder for an application, its backend or screens, or one slice, and decide who makes the branch, the commits and the pull request.

Approving a builder does not change who builds. Choose it for an application, for the backend or the screens of an application, or for a single slice. You also decide who handles the repository work and what happens when a built slice changes. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * The builder is approved for the organization. See [Connect a builder and approve it](/developers/builders/configure/connect-and-approve/). * You can change the application. Anyone who can change it can choose among the approved builders. Everything below is under **Settings** > **Building** inside the application, except the choice for one slice, which is on the slice, and the per-builder override, which a maintainer sets under **Organization settings** > **Builders**. ![The Building tab of an application's settings, with the Builder set to Studio: static code, Studio-managed repository handling, the branch name, commit message and pull request settings, and the switch to build the user interface with a separate builder](/_astro/application-settings-building.C1n_Ora2_QtVz4.webp) ## Choose the builder for an application [Section titled “Choose the builder for an application”](#choose-the-builder-for-an-application) 1. Open the application, then **Settings** > **Building**. 2. Set **Builder**. The list holds Studio’s own generation, as **Studio: static code**, **Studio: static code refined by AI** or **Studio: AI only** (the two that involve AI need an AI provider configured), and every approved builder of the organization. A builder that is offline can still be chosen, and its slices wait for it. 3. Select **Save**. From then on, a slice that becomes **Ready for implementation** is offered to that builder. ## Build the backend and the screens with different builders [Section titled “Build the backend and the screens with different builders”](#build-the-backend-and-the-screens-with-different-builders) To build the backend (commands, events and read models) with one builder and the screens with another: 1. Under **Separate backend and user interface builders**, turn on **Build the user interface with a separate builder**. 2. Choose the **Backend builder** and the **User interface builder**. They must be two different builders, so the organization needs at least two approved builders. A slice with screens then waits after its backend is built until the user interface builder has built the screens. ## Choose the builder for one slice [Section titled “Choose the builder for one slice”](#choose-the-builder-for-one-slice) 1. Open the slice’s menu and select **Builder ›**. 2. Choose another builder. The menu says why a builder cannot build the slice, and which builder the slice follows when it uses its application’s builder. The slice’s choice wins over the application’s. Choose **Application’s builder** to return the slice to its application’s builder. A slice whose builder is offline says it waits for that builder, and no other builder takes it. ## Decide who handles the repository [Section titled “Decide who handles the repository”](#decide-who-handles-the-repository) Someone has to make the branch, the commits and the pull request. Set **Repository**: | Choice | What happens | | ------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Studio-managed** | Studio names the branch and the commit message from the templates you set, and opens the pull request, one per slice or one per feature. The builder hands over its commit | | **Builder-managed** | The builder works in its own checkout and reports the commit, branch or pull request it produced. The slice links to it | With Studio-managed you also set: * **Branch name** and **Commit message**, as templates with the placeholders `{application}`, `{module}`, `{feature}`, `{slice}`, `{part}` and `{attempt}`. The defaults are `studio/{feature}/{slice}` and `Build {slice} ({part})`. * **Pull requests**: **One per slice**, or **One per feature**. With one per feature, the branch name is the same for every slice of the feature, so it cannot use `{slice}`, `{part}` or `{attempt}`. A single builder can override the application’s choice for everything it builds. A maintainer sets that under **Organization settings** > **Builders**, in the builder’s **Repository**: **As each application says**, **Always Studio-managed** or **Always builder-managed**. Studio never puts a repository credential in a build order, a prompt or a log. A builder that pushes uses credentials of its own. ## Decide what happens when a built slice changes [Section titled “Decide what happens when a built slice changes”](#decide-what-happens-when-a-built-slice-changes) Under **When a built slice changes**, choose: * **Mark it out of date**: the slice is marked out of date with what changed, and is rebuilt when someone makes it ready for implementation again. * **Rebuild it automatically**: the slice is made ready for implementation again, so its builder updates what it built. Deleting a built slice offers the removal of what was built for it to its builder. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Follow a build and troubleshoot](/developers/builders/configure/follow-and-troubleshoot/) * [Project and application settings](/organization/project-and-application-settings/#choose-the-builder) * [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/)

# Connect a builder and approve it

> Run the connect command on the machine that should build, approve the builder in Studio with the code it shows, and see it come online.

A builder connects from the machine that should build, and builds nothing until a maintainer of the organization approves it with a short code. Connect one when you want slices built on your own machine or build server, on another stack, or with your own coding agent. Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram From the connect command to a builder you can choose. Only the approval needs a maintainer. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * Approving a builder needs a maintainer of the organization. Anyone who can change the application can choose among the builders that are approved. A member who is not a maintainer sees *Only organization maintainers can see and approve builders. Connect yours, then ask a maintainer to approve it.* * The machine must reach Studio over HTTPS. Builders only dial out, so it needs no inbound port. * Until you choose another builder, an application builds with Studio’s own generation. Connecting a builder changes nothing by itself. ## Connect the builder [Section titled “Connect the builder”](#connect-the-builder) 1. Open the application, then **Settings** > **Building**. Under **Connected builders**, the **Connect a builder** panel shows the command for your organization. A maintainer finds the same panel in **Organization settings** > **Builders**. It looks like this: 
   ```shell
   cratis build connect --studio --organization <organization>
   ```
 `--studio` alone means the hosted Studio. On another Studio the command names its address: `--studio https://<studio host>`. 2. Select **Copy**, and run the command on the machine that should build. The [Cratis CLI](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/) is the builder: it runs your coding agent or [your own generator](/developers/builders/build/). A builder you wrote against [the builder protocol](/developers/builders/protocol/protocol/) registers at the same address, which the panel also shows. 3. The builder creates its own credential, registers, and shows a short code such as `K7QF-2M9D` on its own machine. In Studio it appears in the list as **Waiting for approval**, with the address and machine it asked from. ## Approve it [Section titled “Approve it”](#approve-it) 1. Open **Organization settings** > **Builders** and find the builder marked **Waiting for approval**. 2. Select **Approve**. In **Approve this builder?**, enter the code the builder showed in **Code**, and approve it. The code is good for 15 minutes. After that the builder registers again for a new one. app.cratis.studio ![The Builders page in the organization settings, with the Connect a builder instructions and the address builders register at](/_astro/settings-builders.FgHcNT-n_Z2tesGB.webp) Organization settings, Builders. Approve a builder with the code it shows on its own machine. Caution Approve only a builder you started yourself. The code and the address it asked from are how you tell it is the one you started. Select **Decline** for one you do not recognize. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The builder is now listed as **Online** while it is running, and **Offline** when it is not. Beside it you see what it **Builds** (its stacks, slice types and kit), the protocol version it speaks, when it was last seen, and what it is doing: **Idle**, building a slice, or removing what it built for one. | Status | What it means | | ------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Waiting for approval** | Registered, with a code, and not approved yet | | **Online** | Approved and running. It takes work as soon as it asks for it | | **Offline** | Approved, but not running right now. It can still be chosen, and its slices wait for it | | **Registration expired** | The code was not approved within 15 minutes. Run the command again for a new one | | **Declined** | A maintainer declined it. Register again to ask once more | | **Not connected yet** | The builder has not connected to Studio yet | A builder that speaks an older protocol is marked *This builder speaks an older protocol. Upgrade it.* It needs upgrading before it can take work. Approving does not change who builds. Next, [choose who builds](/developers/builders/configure/choose-builders/) for the application or for a slice. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Choose who builds](/developers/builders/configure/choose-builders/) * [Follow a build and troubleshoot](/developers/builders/configure/follow-and-troubleshoot/) * [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/): the Cratis CLI with a generator of your own, in any language.

# Use the reference builder

> Run the reference builder host from the Cratis CLI, and describe a stack once as a build kit so a builder can build slices with it without writing a loop.

`cratis build`, in the [Cratis CLI](https://www.cratis.io/cli/), is the reference builder. It holds the whole conversation with Studio: connecting, waiting for approval, asking for work, keeping the claim alive, running verification, committing, pushing and reporting the outcome. What turns a slice into code is yours to choose, in [three ways](/developers/builders/build/#three-ways-to-build): a coding agent with `--agent` ([Build with AI](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/)), any program in any language with `--exec` ([Build with a generator](/developers/builders/build/deterministic/)), or a generator that a coding agent runs and completes ([Deterministic with AI on top](/developers/builders/build/deterministic-and-ai/)). A stack author describes a stack once as a [build kit](#build-kits). 
```text
cratis build connect --studio --organization <name>
cratis build init --kit <name|git url|folder> [--agent <agent>]
cratis build run --agent claude|codex|gemini|copilot|opencode|cursor|acp
cratis build run --exec "<command>"
cratis build run --modeling --board <board> --agent <agent> [--unattended]
cratis build once --order order.json --exec "<command>" | --agent <agent>
cratis build status
cratis build service install|status|uninstall
```
 This page describes how the CLI works with Studio. The CLI’s own [build reference](https://www.cratis.io/cli/reference/build/) lists every option, and [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/) shows how to build with an agent, a generator, or both. ## What Studio exposes [Section titled “What Studio exposes”](#what-studio-exposes) Everything below is served by Studio Core. The contract documents, kits and session guidance need no sign-in; they hold no organization data. Work is only handed to a registered builder, through the builder registration and connection endpoints (see [The builder protocol](/developers/builders/protocol/protocol/)). | Purpose | Address | Page | | --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Build order schema | `GET /api/builders/contract/{version}/build-order.schema.json` | [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/) | | Build outcome schema | `GET /api/builders/contract/{version}/build-outcome.schema.json` | [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/) | | Kit manifest schema | `GET /api/builders/kits/schema/{version}/build-kit.schema.json` | [Build kits](#build-kits) | | Registered kits | `GET /api/builders/kits` | [Build kits](#build-kits) | | One kit’s manifest | `GET /api/builders/kits/{name}` | [Build kits](#build-kits) | | One kit as an archive | `GET /api/builders/kits/{name}/archive` | [Build kits](#build-kits) | | Validate a kit | `POST /api/builders/kits/validate` | [Build kits](#build-kits) | | Agent session descriptor | `GET /api/builders/session` | [Agent sessions](/developers/live-modeling/#agent-sessions) | | Screenplay guidance archive | `GET /api/builders/session/screenplay-session.zip` | [Agent sessions](/developers/live-modeling/#agent-sessions) | | Live modeling on a board | MCP tools `studio_connect_local_agent`, `studio_poll_local_agent`, `studio_take_local_agent_prompt`, `studio_answer_local_agent_prompt`, `studio_disconnect_local_agent` | [Live modeling with your own agent](/developers/live-modeling/) | Studio decides what gets built and settles every slice’s status; see [the build loop](/developers/builders/protocol/build-loop/). A builder never sets a status itself. ## The commands [Section titled “The commands”](#the-commands) ### `connect` [Section titled “connect”](#connect) 
```text
cratis build connect --studio [<url>] --organization <name> [--name <name>] [--mcp-client-id <id>] [--force] [--no-wait]
```
 Registers the machine as a builder of an organization with a short code a maintainer approves in Studio, and waits until they do. `--studio` alone means the hosted Studio, `https://app.cratis.studio`. The builder’s credential is kept in the operating system’s credential store, never in the project. `--mcp-client-id` also stores the organization’s MCP credential, so an agent gets Studio’s tools. See [Connect a builder and approve it](/developers/builders/configure/connect-and-approve/). ### `init` [Section titled “init”](#init) Installs a kit into a project: 
```text
cratis build init --kit <name|git url> [--agent <agent>] [--kit-folder <path>]
```
 A value matching the kit name rule (lowercase words joined by hyphens) is a registered kit; an existing folder is a kit on disk; anything else is a Git URL (`https://…`, `ssh://…` or `git@host:owner/repo.git`), optionally followed by `#<branch, tag or commit>`. `--kit-folder` names the folder inside the repository that holds `build-kit.yaml`; without it the manifest is at the repository’s root. 1. A registered kit: `GET /api/builders/kits/{name}` for the manifest, then `GET /api/builders/kits/{name}/archive`, refused unless the SHA-256 of the body equals `X-Checksum-Sha256`. A kit from Git: a shallow clone of the ref into a temporary folder outside the project, then the kit is validated; a kit with problems stops `init` with every problem shown and nothing copied. The CLI validates with the manifest schema and the checks listed under [Validating a kit](#validating-a-kit), the same checks Studio runs. 2. Every command in `requires` is checked for and its version compared; a missing or too old command stops `init`. 3. In an empty project with `scaffold`, the scaffold is copied and its `commands` run in the project, in order; any non-zero exit stops `init`. In a project that is not empty, the scaffold is not copied: the kit needs `existingCodebase`, otherwise `init` stops. With `existingCodebase`, the `learn` prompt runs once (on `init` or the first build) and the conventions are kept at the kit’s `conventions` path. 4. The `instructions` and `skills` are installed where the chosen agent reads them: | `--agent` | Instructions | Skills | | ------------------------------------------------------ | ------------ | ------------------------- | | `claude` | `CLAUDE.md` | `.claude/skills/<skill>/` | | `gemini` | `GEMINI.md` | `.agents/skills/<skill>/` | | `codex`, `cursor`, `copilot`, `opencode` (the default) | `AGENTS.md` | `.agents/skills/<skill>/` | The kit’s instructions are written between the lines `<!-- cratis-build-kit:<name> -->` and `<!-- /cratis-build-kit:<name> -->`, followed by one line per skill naming its `SKILL.md` and its `description`. A file that already exists keeps everything outside that block; running `init` again replaces only the block. This is the only guidance a kit leaves in the project, and only because the kit asks for it; the Screenplay guidance never is (see [Agent sessions](/developers/live-modeling/#agent-sessions)). 5. The kit is recorded in `.cratis/build/kit.json`: `name`, `version`, `sha256` of the archive (or `source` with the Git URL and the resolved commit), `agent`, and `files`, every project path `init` wrote. `run`, `once` and `service install` read the kit and the agent from this file. A kit is optional: without one, name the agent with `--agent` or the generator with `--exec`. ### `run` [Section titled “run”](#run) 
```text
cratis build run [--agent <agent> | --exec <command>] [--agent-command <command>] [--kit <path>] [--project <path>] [--verify <command>]... [--once] [--no-push] [--no-guidance]
```
 Connects, tells Studio what it can build, asks for work, and for every slice it is given: 1. switches to the branch Studio names for a Studio-managed build, or to the kit’s `git.branch` otherwise, and writes the order to a folder outside the project, with the order’s Screenplay documents in a `model` folder beside it; 2. hands the order to the agent, as described in [Agent sessions](/developers/live-modeling/#agent-sessions), with the kit’s filled prompt, or runs the `--exec` command (see [The generator contract](/developers/builders/build/deterministic/#the-generator-contract)); it renews the lease while the work goes on, and stops without reporting anything when Studio says the lease is lost; 3. answers the agent’s permission requests from the kit’s `permissions`, refusing everything else, and never starts an agent with its permission checks switched off; 4. on `built` or `nothingToDo`, runs every `verify` command of the kit and each `--verify`, in order, in the project; any non-zero exit makes the outcome `failed` and retryable, never `built`; 5. on `built`, commits the files the outcome lists (or every changed file when it lists none) that the kit permits, with Studio’s commit message for a Studio-managed build or the kit’s `git.commit` otherwise, pushes the branch with your own Git credentials unless `--no-push` is given, and adds the commit and branch to the outcome; 6. reports the outcome, keeping only what the build contract declares for its kind; 7. puts anything left uncommitted away in a Git stash named after the order, never discarding it, and switches back to the branch it started on. The project must be a Git repository without uncommitted changes. `--once` stops after one slice, and `Ctrl+C` stops the loop. What the builder tells Studio it can build comes from the kit: its name, its slice types (all of them without a kit), whether it builds user interfaces (`ui`), and the contract versions it shares with the CLI. Without a kit, the builder builds backends of every slice type. The CLI reads version 1 of [build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/), so it does not send progress reports, and Studio lists a deleted slice in the next build of its feature instead of sending it a removal order. With `--modeling`, the agent models on a board instead of building slices; see [Live modeling with your own agent](/developers/live-modeling/). An agent that stops without writing its outcome, as some do when one of their actions is refused, is asked to go on up to twice before the build fails. Local tools such as Screenplay’s are handed to an agent that only connects to MCP servers over HTTP, such as Copilot CLI, on a loopback address with a token of the session’s own. Credentials go to the agent session and the Git process, never into a prompt, an order, an outcome or a log. ### `once` [Section titled “once”](#once) 
```text
cratis build once --order <file> [--agent <agent> | --exec <command>] [--outcome <file>] [--verify <command>]... [--push]
```
 Builds one order from a file without connecting to Studio: steps 1 to 5 and 7 of `run`, without pushing unless `--push` is given. The outcome is written to the standard output, or to `--outcome`. It exits with `5` when the outcome is `failed`, so it fits in a CI job. Use it to try a generator, an agent or a kit. ### `status` [Section titled “status”](#status) Shows the Studio and the organization the machine is registered with, whether the builder credential and Studio’s MCP credential are stored, and the kit and agent of the current folder. It changes nothing. ### `service` [Section titled “service”](#service) Runs the build loop of a project as a user service that starts at sign-in and restarts when it stops, so a machine stays a builder without a terminal open: a launchd agent on macOS and a systemd user unit on Linux. Windows has no supported service manager; run `cratis build run` there. 
```bash
cratis build service install --agent claude
cratis build service install --exec "$HOME/slice-sheets/generate.sh"
cratis build service status
cratis build service uninstall
```
 `install` takes the options of `run` and needs a machine registered with `connect`, and a kit, `--agent` or `--exec`. The service gets the `PATH` of the shell that installed it and no credentials; the builder token stays in the credential store. Each project gets its own service. ## Build kits [Section titled “Build kits”](#build-kits) A *build kit* describes a stack to a builder host such as `cratis build`: how a new project is created, what the coding agent is told, how one slice is built and verified, and how the work lands in Git. A kit holds no loop, connection or retry code. The host does all of that the same way for every kit, and the [build loop](/developers/builders/protocol/build-loop/) in Studio decides what gets built. 
```text
my-kit/
├── build-kit.yaml        # the manifest
├── AGENTS.md             # stack instructions
├── build-slice.md        # the prompt for one build order
├── learn-conventions.md  # optional: learning an existing codebase
├── skills/               # optional: one folder per skill, each with a SKILL.md
└── scaffold/             # optional: the files of a new project
```
 ### The manifest [Section titled “The manifest”](#the-manifest) The manifest is `build-kit.yaml` (or the same document as `build-kit.json`) at the root of the kit. It is described by a JSON Schema that Studio publishes at a stable address and never changes once released: | Document | Address | | ----------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- | | Build kit manifest, version 1 | `/api/builders/kits/schema/1/build-kit.schema.json` | 
```yaml
kitVersion: "1"
name: cratis-csharp
version: 1.0.0
description: Event-sourced .NET applications on Cratis Arc and Chronicle.
contract:
  versions: ["1"]
screenplay:
  schemaVersions: ">=38"
sliceTypes: [stateChange, stateView, automation, translation]
ui: true
scaffold:
  path: ./scaffold
  commands:
    - dotnet restore CratisApp.slnx
instructions: ./AGENTS.md
skills: ./skills
prompt: ./build-slice.md
verify:
  - dotnet build $(ls *.slnx *.sln 2>/dev/null | head -n 1) -warnaserror
  - dotnet test $(ls *.slnx *.sln 2>/dev/null | head -n 1) --no-build --filter "FullyQualifiedName~{slice}" -- RunConfiguration.TreatNoTestsAsError=true
git:
  branch: "studio/{feature}"
  commit: "Build {slice}"
permissions:
  paths: ["**"]
  commands: [dotnet build, dotnet test, git status]
existingCodebase:
  learn: ./learn-conventions.md
  conventions: .cratis/build/conventions.md
requires:
  - command: dotnet
    version: ">=11"
```
 | Member | Required | Meaning | | --------------------------- | -------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `kitVersion` | yes | The manifest format version, the string `"1"`. | | `name` | yes | Lowercase words joined by hyphens, at most 64 characters. Registered kits are installed by it. | | `version` | yes | The kit’s own semantic version. | | `description` | yes | One sentence on the stack. | | `contract.versions` | no | The [build order and outcome](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/) versions the prompt is written for. Defaults to `["1"]`. | | `screenplay.schemaVersions` | no | The compiled Screenplay schema versions the kit understands: `">=38"`, `"=40"` or `">=38 <50"`. | | `sliceTypes` | yes | One or more of `stateChange`, `stateView`, `automation`, `translation`. A host takes no order for another type. | | `ui` | no | Whether the kit also builds the `ui` part of an order. Defaults to `false`. | | `scaffold` | no | A kit folder copied into a new project, as `./scaffold` or `{ path, commands }`. `commands` run in the new project afterwards. | | `instructions` | yes | The stack instructions, a Markdown file, installed in the form the chosen agent reads. | | `skills` | no | A folder holding one folder per skill, each with a `SKILL.md`. | | `prompt` | yes | The prompt template for one build order. | | `verify` | yes | Commands the host runs, in order, after the agent is done. A slice is reported built only when every one exits with 0. | | `git.branch`, `git.commit` | no | Templates for the branch to build on and the commit message. | | `permissions.paths` | no | Globs, relative to the project, the agent may write. Defaults to everything in the project. | | `permissions.commands` | no | Command prefixes the agent may run. The `verify` commands are always allowed. Everything else is refused. | | `existingCodebase` | no | How the kit builds into a project it did not scaffold; see below. | | `requires` | no | Commands the host checks for, each with an optional minimum version such as `">=10"`. | Every path inside the kit starts with `./` and never leaves the kit; every path inside the project is relative and never leaves it. A manifest member the format does not declare is refused, so a manifest never carries a token or a secret. ### Placeholders [Section titled “Placeholders”](#placeholders) The prompt, the learn prompt, the `verify` commands and the Git templates may use these placeholders. The host fills them from the build order. In a `verify` command, a value with spaces or shell characters is quoted. `permissions.commands` are not filled: a permitted command is a prefix, so permit `node tools/generate.mts` and have the prompt run it with `{order}` after it, as [Deterministic with AI on top](/developers/builders/build/deterministic-and-ai/) does. Any other name in single braces refuses the kit; double braces and braces with spaces, as in JSON or code, are left alone. | Placeholder | Filled with | | --------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `{order}` | The path of the build order document the host wrote for this build | | `{outcome}` | The path the agent writes its outcome document to | | `{slice}` | The name of the first target slice, as written in the model | | `{slices}` | The names of every target slice, separated by commas | | `{sliceId}` | The identifier of the first target slice | | `{feature}` | The name of the feature the slices belong to | | `{featurePath}` | The modules and parent features down to the feature, joined with `/` | | `{application}` | The name of the application | | `{part}` | `backend` or `ui` | | `{attempt}` | The attempt number, starting at 1 | | `{conventions}` | The project path of the recorded conventions, or empty when the kit records none | ### What the agent does, and what the host does [Section titled “What the agent does, and what the host does”](#what-the-agent-does-and-what-the-host-does) The agent only writes code and an outcome. The host does everything else, so a kit cannot get it wrong: 1. The host writes the build order to `{order}` and starts the agent with the instructions, the skills, the Screenplay guidance and the tools for the session (see [Agent sessions](/developers/live-modeling/#agent-sessions)), and the filled prompt. 2. The agent writes code and then an outcome document to `{outcome}`: `built` with its files, specification results and assumptions; `blocked` with a reason and questions; or `nothingToDo`. It leaves `commit`, `branch` and `pullRequest` out. 3. The host runs every `verify` command. If one fails, the outcome is `failed` with the command and its exit code, whatever the agent wrote. 4. When verification passes and the outcome is `built`, the host commits the agent’s files on the `git.branch`, adds the commit to the outcome and reports it. Permission requests from the agent are answered from `permissions`; the host never runs an agent with its permission checks switched off. ### Existing codebases [Section titled “Existing codebases”](#existing-codebases) A kit with `existingCodebase` can build into a project it did not scaffold. On the first build in such a project the host runs the `learn` prompt once: the agent reads the project and records its conventions at the `conventions` path, changing nothing else. Every later build passes that path as `{conventions}`, and the agent follows what was recorded. Delete the file to have the conventions learned again. ### Registered kits [Section titled “Registered kits”](#registered-kits) Studio registers the reference kits by name. Each is one archive a host unpacks as is. | Kit | Stack | Slice types | User interfaces | | --------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------ | --------------- | | `cratis-csharp` | .NET on Cratis Arc and Chronicle, React on Cratis Components | all four | yes | | `node` | Node.js in TypeScript on the Cratis Chronicle client, Vitest | state change, state view | no | These addresses need no sign-in; kits hold no organization data and no credentials. #### List the kits [Section titled “List the kits”](#list-the-kits) 
```text
GET /api/builders/kits
```
 `200 OK`, `Cache-Control: no-store`: 
```json
[
  {
    "name": "cratis-csharp",
    "version": "1.0.0",
    "description": "Event-sourced .NET applications on Cratis Arc and Chronicle, with a React frontend on Cratis Components.",
    "sliceTypes": ["stateChange", "stateView", "automation", "translation"],
    "ui": true,
    "contractVersions": ["1"],
    "sha256": "9f2c…"
  }
]
```
 #### Read a kit’s manifest [Section titled “Read a kit’s manifest”](#read-a-kits-manifest) 
```text
GET /api/builders/kits/{name}
```
 `200 OK` with the manifest as JSON, exactly as written in the kit, or `404 Not Found` for a name Studio does not register. #### Download a kit [Section titled “Download a kit”](#download-a-kit) 
```text
GET /api/builders/kits/{name}/archive
```
 `200 OK` with `Content-Type: application/zip` and the header `X-Checksum-Sha256` holding the lowercase hex SHA-256 of the body, which also matches `sha256` in the listing. Entries are paths relative to the kit’s root, such as `build-kit.yaml` and `scaffold/Program.cs`. The same kit always gives the same bytes. Verify the checksum before unpacking. `404 Not Found` for an unknown name. ### Installing from a Git URL [Section titled “Installing from a Git URL”](#installing-from-a-git-url) A host installs a kit that is not registered from a Git URL: it clones the repository (or the folder the URL names), finds `build-kit.yaml` or `build-kit.json` at its root, and validates the kit before anything is copied into the project. ### Validating a kit [Section titled “Validating a kit”](#validating-a-kit) A kit author, or a host installing a kit from Git, sends the kit as a zip: 
```text
POST /api/builders/kits/validate
Content-Type: application/zip
```
 The manifest may be at the root of the archive or inside one top folder, the way a Git host lays out an archive. The archive may be at most 5 MB, hold at most 2000 files and unpack to at most 20 MB, and no entry may leave the kit. The answer is always `200 OK`, `Cache-Control: no-store`: 
```json
{
  "valid": false,
  "problems": [
    "/sliceTypes/1: Value should match one of the values specified by the enum",
    "/prompt: {apiToken} is not a placeholder a host fills; use one of {order}, {outcome}, …",
    "/skills: the skill other has no SKILL.md."
  ],
  "kit": null
}
```
 Each problem starts with where it is: a JSON pointer into the manifest, or `(document)`. A valid kit answers `"valid": true`, no problems and `kit` in the same shape as a listing entry. Validation checks the manifest against the schema, that every file and folder it names is in the kit and not empty, that every skill folder has a `SKILL.md`, and that every placeholder is one from the table above. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Connect a builder and approve it](/developers/builders/configure/connect-and-approve/) * [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/): three ways to build, with a coding agent, a generator of your own, or both * [Build with AI](/developers/builders/build/with-ai/): write a build kit of your own, line by line * [The `cratis build` reference](https://www.cratis.io/cli/reference/build/) in the CLI documentation * [Talk to Studio directly](/developers/builders/protocol/), when you cannot run the CLI * [The build loop](/developers/builders/protocol/build-loop/) * [The builder protocol](/developers/builders/protocol/protocol/) * [Live modeling with your own agent](/developers/live-modeling/)

# Follow a build and troubleshoot

> See what a builder is doing on a slice, review its attempts, specifications and assumptions, cancel or rebuild, and fix the common problems.

A slice that has been built carries its build: what the builder is doing now, every attempt it made, what it says about the specifications, and the gaps it filled. Use it to review what was built, and to find out why a slice is not moving. ## Follow a build [Section titled “Follow a build”](#follow-a-build) Open the slice’s build with **Open the build of this slice**. You see: * **Progress**: the step the builder is on now and its **Log**. Anything that looks like a credential is removed before a log line is kept. * **Attempts**: for each attempt, the **Part** (**Backend**, **User interface** or **Removal**), the **Builder**, the **Outcome**, the **Time** and the **Tokens** used. * **Specifications**: what the builder says about each specification (**Builder says**) beside the board’s own run (**Board’s run**), and where they disagree. * **Assumptions**: gaps the builder filled. **Accept** one that is right, or **Dismiss** it once the model says it. An attempt ends as **Built**, **Nothing to build**, **Blocked**, **Failed**, **Builder stopped answering**, **Ran out of time** or **Cancelled**. You can cancel a build that is running. The builder loses its lease, and the slice goes back to the status it had. ## Rebuild a slice [Section titled “Rebuild a slice”](#rebuild-a-slice) A slice is built again when it becomes **Ready for implementation** again. What happens when a built slice changes depends on the application’s **When a built slice changes** setting: it is either marked out of date until someone makes it ready for implementation, or rebuilt automatically. See [Choose who builds](/developers/builders/configure/choose-builders/#decide-what-happens-when-a-built-slice-changes). ## Stop using a builder [Section titled “Stop using a builder”](#stop-using-a-builder) A maintainer removes a builder under **Organization settings** > **Builders** with **Remove**. Its next request is refused. A slice it holds is offered again once its lease runs out. Choose another builder for the applications that used it. ## Troubleshooting [Section titled “Troubleshooting”](#troubleshooting) | You see | What it means | | -------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | No code is shown after running the command | The builder could not reach the address. Builders only dial out, so check that the machine can reach Studio over HTTPS | | **Registration expired** | The code was not approved within 15 minutes. Run the command again for a new one | | **Declined** | A maintainer declined it. Register again to ask once more | | The list says only maintainers can see builders | You are not a maintainer. Connect the builder, then ask one to approve it | | A slice stays **Ready for implementation** | Its builder is offline, or busy with other work, or the slice waits for slices it builds on. It is taken as soon as the builder asks for work | | A slice is **Blocked** | The builder said it could not continue, or failed on its last attempt. The reason and questions are on the slice. After three failed attempts, the loop stops retrying | | **Builder stopped answering** | The builder did not renew its lease, so the attempt counted as failed | | *This builder speaks an older protocol. Upgrade it.* | The builder needs upgrading before it can take work | | `cratis build run` stops because the project has uncommitted changes | The CLI commits only a build’s own work. Commit or stash your changes, then start it again | | An attempt failed with *The generator … exited with* a number | The `--exec` command failed. Run it on the same order with `cratis build once` to see why | | An attempt failed with *Verification failed* | A `--verify` command, or one of the kit’s, exited with an error after the build. The end of its output is in the error | | A builder run by the Cratis CLI shows no **Progress** | The CLI does not send progress reports. Follow the build in its terminal or service log | | A builder is never offered the user interface of a slice | It does not build user interfaces. A Cratis CLI builder builds them only with a [build kit](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/#build-kits) that has `ui` | ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [The build loop](/developers/builders/protocol/build-loop/) explains offering, leases, retries and how a slice is settled. * [Build and deliver](/deliver/) shows the build from the board’s side. * [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/), when no existing builder fits your stack: the Cratis CLI with a generator of your own.

# Talk to Studio directly

> For a builder that cannot run the Cratis CLI or needs its own host - the HTTPS conversation with Studio, one request at a time.

The simple way to build slices is the Cratis CLI with a generator or a coding agent of your own: see [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/). The CLI holds the whole conversation with Studio for you. This section is for the cases where that does not fit: a machine that cannot run the CLI, or a team that wants its builder inside a host of its own. A builder that talks to Studio directly is any program that can make HTTPS requests and read and write JSON. It needs no SDK, no Cratis package and no inbound port, and it can be written in any language. The Cratis CLI is open source, so its [source on GitHub](https://github.com/Cratis/cli) is a complete, working implementation of everything on this page. Read it beside this walkthrough when you write your own. What you'll learn * the conversation a builder has with Studio, one request at a time * what a builder does between asking for work and reporting the outcome * where a generator or a coding agent plugs in ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * **The address and your organization.** Every request goes to `https://<studio address>/builders/connect/<organization>`. The hosted Studio is `https://app.cratis.studio`. The **Connect a builder** panel under **Settings** > **Building** shows the full address for your organization. * **A maintainer to approve the builder.** A new builder builds nothing until a maintainer of the organization approves it. See [Connect a builder and approve it](/developers/builders/configure/connect-and-approve/). * **A clone of the application’s repository** that the builder may commit to, with Git credentials of its own if it pushes. Studio never hands a builder a repository credential. * **An application that uses the builder.** Once the builder is approved, choose it for an application or a slice. See [Choose who builds](/developers/builders/configure/choose-builders/). ## The conversation [Section titled “The conversation”](#the-conversation) Every request goes from the builder to Studio. Studio never calls the builder. Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram The whole conversation. Between asking for work and reporting the outcome, the builder builds. The steps below show each exchange with a trimmed body. [The builder protocol](/developers/builders/protocol/protocol/) is the full reference, with every status code. Every request and answer is JSON, with camelCase members and enum values. ## 1. Register once [Section titled “1. Register once”](#1-register-once) The builder creates its own secret token first: 32 random bytes, written in URL-safe base64, is enough. It then registers with its name, the machine it runs on, and the token. 
```http
POST /builders/connect/acme/registrations
Content-Type: application/json


{ "name": "Build server", "machine": "ci-01", "token": "q4W1v9n2Jx0a8yT6pL3sZ7bK5eR1uH9cM2dF4gN8wQ0" }
```
 
```http
HTTP/1.1 201 Created


{ "builderId": "8f0c6a8e-3f0b-4c39-9a3e-2b6f8e1d7c44", "code": "K7QF-2M9D", "expires": "2026-11-09T12:15:00+00:00" }
```
 Keep the `builderId` and the token somewhere only the builder can read, so a restart reuses them. Studio keeps only a hash of the token and cannot give it back, so a builder that loses its token registers again. Show the `code` on the builder’s machine. A maintainer types it in Studio to approve the builder, within 15 minutes. The code proves that the builder in Studio’s list is the one you started. From now on, every request carries the builder’s credential, `<builderId>.<token>`, twice: 
```http
Authorization: Bearer 8f0c6a8e-3f0b-4c39-9a3e-2b6f8e1d7c44.q4W1v9n2Jx0a8yT6pL3sZ7bK5eR1uH9cM2dF4gN8wQ0
X-Studio-Builder-Credential: 8f0c6a8e-3f0b-4c39-9a3e-2b6f8e1d7c44.q4W1v9n2Jx0a8yT6pL3sZ7bK5eR1uH9cM2dF4gN8wQ0
```
 Send both headers The hosted Studio sits behind a proxy that removes `Authorization` from requests to the builder address. Studio reads `X-Studio-Builder-Credential` first. Against the hosted Studio, a builder that sends only `Authorization` is answered as unknown. ## 2. Connect, and wait for approval [Section titled “2. Connect, and wait for approval”](#2-connect-and-wait-for-approval) Each time the builder starts, it connects. It names the protocol versions it speaks and a manifest of what it can build. 
```http
POST /builders/connect/acme/connect
Content-Type: application/json


{
  "protocolVersions": [1],
  "manifest": {
    "stacks": ["dotnet"],
    "parts": ["backend", "ui"],
    "sliceTypes": [],
    "screenplayVersions": ["1"],
    "contractVersions": ["1", "2", "3"],
    "kit": "my-kit",
    "gitHandling": ["studioManaged", "builderManaged"]
  }
}
```
 Studio offers a builder only the slices its manifest covers. An empty `sliceTypes` means every slice type. `contractVersions` says which versions of [build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/) the builder reads; only a builder that reads version 3 gets removal orders. | Answer | What the builder does | | -------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `200` with `protocolVersion`, `waitSeconds` and `leaseSeconds` | It is connected. Keep the two numbers for the next steps | | `202` with `code` | A maintainer has not approved it yet. Keep showing the code and connect again in a few seconds | | `403` | The registration was declined or expired. Forget the credential and register again | | `401` | Studio does not know the builder, or a maintainer removed it. Stop, and tell the owner | | `409` | There is no protocol version both sides speak. The `reason` says which side to upgrade | | `422` | The manifest cannot be read. `problems` says why | ## 3. Ask for work [Section titled “3. Ask for work”](#3-ask-for-work) 
```http
POST /builders/connect/acme/work
```
 Studio holds the request open for up to `waitSeconds`, 20 by default, while it looks for a slice that is this builder’s to build. So give the request a timeout longer than that. * `204 No Content` means nothing yet. Ask again straight away. * `409` means the builder has not connected, or has said goodbye since. Go back to step 2. * `202`, `401` and `403` mean the same as when connecting: go back to step 2, which handles them. * `200 OK` means the builder now holds a slice: 
```json
{
  "sliceId": "0b9c7f4e-2a51-4f6d-8e3a-6c1d5b7a9e20",
  "leaseExpires": "2026-11-09T12:02:00+00:00",
  "git": {
    "gitHandling": "studioManaged",
    "branch": "studio/invoicing/pay-invoice",
    "commitMessage": "Build PayInvoice (backend)",
    "pullRequests": "perSlice"
  },
  "order": { "contractVersion": "3", "orderId": "6f0c3d5e-2b1a-4f7e-9a51-0d2c8e4b7a10", "part": "backend", "...": "..." }
}
```
 `order` is the [build order](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-order): the slices to build, the Screenplay model they live in, their specifications as acceptance criteria, and hints and feedback from people. `order.part` says whether to build the `backend`, the `ui`, or, for a `removal` order, to remove what was built for slices that were deleted. `git` says who does the repository work. ## 4. Renew the lease while you build [Section titled “4. Renew the lease while you build”](#4-renew-the-lease-while-you-build) The slice is held for the builder for `leaseSeconds`, 120 by default. Renew the lease on a timer, about every third of that, until you report the outcome: 
```http
POST /builders/connect/acme/work/0b9c7f4e-2a51-4f6d-8e3a-6c1d5b7a9e20/lease
Content-Type: application/json


{ "orderId": "6f0c3d5e-2b1a-4f7e-9a51-0d2c8e4b7a10" }
```
 `200` returns the new `leaseExpires`. `410 Gone` means the lease is lost: it ran out, the build passed its deadline, someone cancelled it, or another builder holds the slice. Stop, throw the work away, and do not report an outcome. ## 5. Report progress [Section titled “5. Report progress”](#5-report-progress) With contract version 2 or later, the builder can tell people what it is doing. Studio shows the step and the log on the slice while it builds. 
```http
POST /builders/connect/acme/work/0b9c7f4e-2a51-4f6d-8e3a-6c1d5b7a9e20/progress
Content-Type: application/json


{
  "contractVersion": "3",
  "orderId": "6f0c3d5e-2b1a-4f7e-9a51-0d2c8e4b7a10",
  "step": "Running the specifications",
  "log": [{ "occurred": "2026-11-09T12:01:10Z", "level": "information", "text": "dotnet test" }]
}
```
 A progress report does not renew the lease, so keep the timer running. Never put a credential in a step or a log line. See [Reporting progress](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#reporting-progress). ## 6. Build [Section titled “6. Build”](#6-build) This is the part that is yours. Studio does not care how the builder turns the order into code: * **Call a generator.** Read the Screenplay documents in `order.model` and render code from them with templates or your own generator. * **Hand the order to a coding agent.** Give it the model, the specifications as acceptance criteria, and the hints and feedback in `order.context`, and let it write the code. * **Run your checks.** Build the code and run its tests. Report each specification as `passed`, `failed` or `notRun`, and list the assumptions you made where the model was silent. People review them on the slice. Then do the repository work that `git` asks for: | `git.gitHandling` | The builder | | ----------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `studioManaged` | Commits on `git.branch` with `git.commitMessage` and pushes the branch. Studio opens the pull request | | `builderManaged` | Chooses the branch and the commit message itself, and may open a pull request of its own | See [Git handling](/developers/builders/protocol/protocol/#git-handling) for what each choice requires of the outcome. ## 7. Report the outcome [Section titled “7. Report the outcome”](#7-report-the-outcome) Report exactly one outcome for the order. It repeats the order’s `contractVersion` and `orderId`. 
```http
POST /builders/connect/acme/work/0b9c7f4e-2a51-4f6d-8e3a-6c1d5b7a9e20/outcome
Content-Type: application/json


{
  "contractVersion": "3",
  "orderId": "6f0c3d5e-2b1a-4f7e-9a51-0d2c8e4b7a10",
  "kind": "built",
  "commit": "4f2c9e1",
  "branch": "studio/invoicing/pay-invoice",
  "files": ["Source/Billing/Invoicing/PayInvoice/PayInvoice.cs"],
  "specifications": [{ "id": "8e1f2a3b-4c5d-4e6f-8a9b-0c1d2e3f4a5b", "result": "passed" }],
  "assumptions": ["An invoice that is already paid is refused"]
}
```
 | `kind` | When | Also carries | | ------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `built` | The code is built and committed | at least one of `commit`, `branch` and `pullRequest`, plus `files`, `specifications` and `assumptions` | | `nothingToDo` | Nothing needed to change, or a removal found the code already gone | nothing more | | `failed` | The attempt went wrong | `error`, and `retryable`: whether trying again can help | | `blocked` | The model leaves a question only a person can answer | `reason` and `questions` | A removal order is answered the same way: `built` with the commit that removed the code, or `nothingToDo` when there was nothing left to remove. Studio answers `200` with the status it settled the build on. `400` with `problems` means the outcome does not follow the schema; that is a bug in the builder, so report a `failed` outcome instead of pretending it worked. `409` means the lease is lost, or the outcome does not fit the repository handling; `reason` says which. Then go back to step 3 and ask for the next slice. ## 8. Disconnect [Section titled “8. Disconnect”](#8-disconnect) When the builder stops, it says goodbye, and Studio shows it as offline: 
```http
POST /builders/connect/acme/disconnect
```
 Studio answers `204`. A builder that stops without saying goodbye is shown as offline once Studio has not heard from it for three minutes. A slice it holds is offered again when its lease runs out. ## Start from the reference implementation [Section titled “Start from the reference implementation”](#start-from-the-reference-implementation) The `cratis build` command of the Cratis CLI is a complete builder with this whole conversation: it registers and keeps its credential in the operating system’s credential store, waits for approval, connects, asks for work, renews its lease on a timer, reports the outcome and disconnects. Its source is at [github.com/Cratis/cli](https://github.com/Cratis/cli), and [the CLI’s build reference](https://www.cratis.io/cli/reference/build/) describes what it does. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [The builder protocol](/developers/builders/protocol/protocol/): every request, answer and status code. * [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/): the documents a builder reads and writes, with their JSON schemas. * [The build loop](/developers/builders/protocol/build-loop/): how Studio offers slices, holds leases, retries and settles them. * [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/): the simple path, where the Cratis CLI holds this conversation for you. * [Use the reference builder](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/): the Cratis CLI’s builder, with its kits and options.

# The build loop

> How Studio offers slices to builders, holds each claim under a lease, retries failed attempts, and settles a slice's status from the outcome.

Studio runs one loop for every builder, so no builder has to get claiming, retries or status reporting right on its own. A builder only builds. It claims work, keeps its claim alive while it builds, and reports one [outcome](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/). Studio decides everything else. ## Who owns what [Section titled “Who owns what”](#who-owns-what) Studio owns the loop. A builder only builds. That split is deliberate. If every builder ran its own loop, each would get claiming, retries and status wrong in its own way, with slices retried forever, two builders working on one slice, or a slice left in progress after a crash. * **Studio decides** which slice is offered and when, who may claim it, how long a claim holds, how many attempts a slice gets, and what status the slice has afterward. Nobody can change a slice’s status by hand while a builder holds it. * **The builder decides** how to build: which agent, which tools, which files. It reports honestly, including what it assumed and which specifications it could not run. * **Studio’s own generation uses the same contract.** It is the first builder and runs through the same loop and statuses, so the built-in path and a third-party builder cannot drift apart. ## From ready to settled [Section titled “From ready to settled”](#from-ready-to-settled) Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram Ready for implementation, offered, claimed under a lease, reported, settled. A failed attempt with attempts left goes round again; after three, the slice is Blocked. 1. **Offered.** When a slice becomes *Ready for implementation*, Studio offers its backend. When a slice is *Waiting* for its user interface, Studio offers its user interface. When a slice that was built is deleted, Studio offers the removal of what was built for it (see [Removing a deleted slice](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#removing-a-deleted-slice)). Offering an offered slice again changes nothing, so a slice is never offered twice for the same attempt. 2. **Waiting on dependencies.** A slice that builds on slices that are not *Done* yet, such as a view over events another slice in the same module records, is held back until they are. It is offered as soon as they are done. 3. **Claimed.** A builder claims an offered slice for a part it builds, if the slice is its to build: assigned to it, or to its application’s builder (see [Choosing a builder](/developers/builders/protocol/protocol/#choosing-a-builder)). Exactly one builder wins when several claim at once, and the others are refused. The claim starts the slice, which shows as *In progress*, and carries the attempt number. 4. **Leased.** A claim holds the slice for a short lease, two minutes by default. The builder renews it with a heartbeat while it works, and may report its step, log and tokens used (contract version 2 and later). Once the lease has run out, or the attempt has passed its deadline (an hour by default), heartbeats and outcomes are refused, which tells the builder to stop. 5. **Settled.** Studio sets the slice’s status from the outcome. A builder never sets it itself, and while a builder holds a slice nobody can change its status by hand. | Outcome | What happens to the slice | | --------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `built` | Ready for review. Waiting when the backend of a slice with screens was built and another builder builds its user interface (see [Separate builders](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#separate-builders-for-the-backend-and-the-user-interface)) | | `nothingToDo` | Ready for review | | `blocked` | Blocked, with the builder’s reason and questions | | `failed`, retryable, attempts left | Back to the status it had, offered again with the next attempt number | | `failed`, last attempt or not retryable | Blocked, with the last failure as the reason | A lease that runs out, or an attempt that passes its deadline, counts as a failed attempt. After three failed attempts, the slice is Blocked. A removal-only build of a deleted slice settles the same way, except that there is no slice on the board to set a status on. `built` and `nothingToDo` carry the removal out, and where a build would block the slice, the removal is blocked instead. A blocked removal is not offered again, and the next build of the feature still lists it. ## Nothing is lost [Section titled “Nothing is lost”](#nothing-is-lost) Every claim, renewal, failure and offer is recorded on the slice as it happens, so attempts survive restarts. Every thirty seconds, a reconcile pass reclaims leases that ran out and makes any offer that was missed. An offer is missed, for example, when the board had not caught up yet at the moment the slice became ready, or when a slice’s dependencies have been built since. ## Cancelling a build [Section titled “Cancelling a build”](#cancelling-a-build) A person can cancel the build running for a slice. The builder loses its lease, so its next heartbeat or outcome is refused, and the slice goes back to the status it had before the build. It is not offered again until its status is set again. ## The numbers [Section titled “The numbers”](#the-numbers) These are Studio’s defaults. They are not settings of your organization. | What | Default | | ----------------------------------------------------- | ---------- | | Failed attempts before the slice is Blocked | 3 | | How long a claim holds without a heartbeat | 2 minutes | | How long one attempt may run in total | 1 hour | | How often leases are reclaimed and missed offers made | 30 seconds | | How long a registration waits for approval | 15 minutes | | How long a request for work is held open | 20 seconds | The agreed values for a connected builder are in the answer it gets when it [connects](/developers/builders/protocol/protocol/#connect), so a builder should read them instead of hard-coding these. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Builders](/developers/builders/) * [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/), where the Cratis CLI takes part in this loop for you * [The builder protocol](/developers/builders/protocol/protocol/) * [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/)

# The builder protocol

> The HTTP contract a builder speaks to register with an organization, agree a protocol version, take work, renew its lease and report its outcome.

A builder connects to Studio from wherever it runs: a laptop, a build server, a container in another cloud. It needs no inbound port and no SDK. It speaks plain HTTPS and JSON to one address per organization, and every request goes from the builder to Studio. This page is the contract. The `cratis build` command of the Cratis CLI implements it, and any other builder can implement it from this page. The CLI is open source, so its [source](https://github.com/Cratis/cli) is a working reference implementation to read beside this page. If you only want to build slices, you do not need any of this: [Build a builder](/developers/builders/build/) shows how the CLI holds the conversation while your generator writes the code. ## The conversation [Section titled “The conversation”](#the-conversation) 
```text
register ─► (a maintainer approves the code) ─► connect ─► work ─► lease … ─► outcome ─► work …
                                                              └── 204: nothing yet, ask again
```
 1. **Register** once. The builder creates its own token, sends it, and gets back its id and a short code. 2. A **maintainer approves** the builder in Studio, under **Settings** > **Builders**, by typing the code the builder shows. Until then the builder builds nothing. 3. **Connect** each time the builder starts. It names the protocol versions it speaks and what it can build. Studio agrees a version or refuses with the reason. 4. **Ask for work.** Studio holds the request open until a slice is the builder’s to build, or answers that there is none yet. 5. While it builds, the builder **renews its lease** with a heartbeat. When it is done, it **reports one outcome**. ## The address [Section titled “The address”](#the-address) 
```text
https://<studio address>/builders/connect/<organization>
```
 `<organization>` is the organization’s name in Studio. The **Connect a builder** panel in Studio shows the full address. Every path below is relative to it. Every request and answer is JSON with camelCase members and camelCase enum names, the same shape as [build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/). Every answer carries `Cache-Control: no-store`. ## Credentials [Section titled “Credentials”](#credentials) A builder creates its own token before it registers. The token has at least 256 random bits, written as 43 to 512 characters of letters, digits, `-`, `_`, `.` and `~`. URL-safe base64 of 32 random bytes is enough. The builder keeps the token itself, for example in the operating system’s credential store. Studio never stores the token. It keeps only a one-way hash of it, so nothing Studio stores can be presented back to it. A lost token cannot be recovered, so register again. Every request after registering carries the builder’s id and token: 
```text
Authorization: Bearer <builderId>.<token>
```
 Studio’s hosted address sits behind a proxy that removes `Authorization` from requests to this path. Send the same value in `X-Studio-Builder-Credential: <builderId>.<token>` as well; Studio reads it first when it is present, and the Cratis CLI sends both. The credential belongs to one organization. A builder of one organization is unknown to every other. ## Register [Section titled “Register”](#register) 
```text
POST /registrations
```
 
```json
{
  "name": "Build server",
  "machine": "ci-01",
  "token": "q4W1v9n2Jx0a8yT6pL3sZ7bK5eR1uH9cM2dF4gN8wQ0"
}
```
 | Member | Required | Meaning | | --------- | -------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `name` | yes | What maintainers see, 1 to 100 characters | | `machine` | no | The machine it runs on, up to 200 characters, shown to the maintainer who approves it | | `token` | yes | The token the builder created | `201 Created`: 
```json
{
  "builderId": "8f0c6a8e-3f0b-4c39-9a3e-2b6f8e1d7c44",
  "code": "K7QF-2M9D",
  "expires": "2026-11-09T12:15:00+00:00"
}
```
 Show the code on the builder’s own machine and ask a maintainer to approve it in Studio before `expires`, which is 15 minutes. The code is not a secret on its own. Together with the address the request came from, which Studio shows beside it, it tells the maintainer that this builder is the one they started. | Status | When | | ------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `400` | The body cannot be read. `problems` names each problem | | `404` | There is no such organization | | `429` | More than 10 registrations a minute from one address for one organization | ## Connect [Section titled “Connect”](#connect) 
```text
POST /connect
Authorization: Bearer <builderId>.<token>
```
 
```json
{
  "protocolVersions": [1],
  "manifest": {
    "stacks": ["dotnet"],
    "parts": ["backend", "ui"],
    "sliceTypes": ["stateChange", "stateView", "automation", "translation"],
    "screenplayVersions": ["1"],
    "contractVersions": ["1"],
    "kit": "cratis-arc",
    "gitHandling": ["studioManaged", "builderManaged"]
  }
}
```
 The manifest says what the builder can build: | Member | Meaning | | -------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `stacks` | The stacks it writes, for people choosing a builder. Studio does not interpret them | | `parts` | `backend`, `ui` or both. At least one | | `sliceTypes` | The slice types it builds, as the build order names them. None means every type | | `screenplayVersions` | The Screenplay schema versions it reads. Studio writes version `1` | | `contractVersions` | The [build contract](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/) versions it reads. At least one. Only a builder that reads version 3 is handed [removal orders](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#removing-a-deleted-slice) | | `kit` | The kit or template it builds with, shown to people | | `gitHandling` | `studioManaged`, `builderManaged` or both. At least one. See [Git handling](#git-handling) | ### The protocol handshake [Section titled “The protocol handshake”](#the-protocol-handshake) The protocol version says what the conversation on this page means. It is agreed once, when the builder connects: 1. `protocolVersions` names every version the builder speaks. 2. Studio picks the **highest version both speak**. 3. A builder that names no versions is older than the agreement and speaks version 1. It is accepted while Studio still speaks version 1. 4. When no version is shared, Studio refuses with `409` and says which side must be updated. Retrying sooner cannot change the answer. Wait a minute, or until the builder or Studio is upgraded. Studio speaks protocol version **1** today. `200 OK` means the builder is welcomed: 
```json
{
  "builderId": "8f0c6a8e-3f0b-4c39-9a3e-2b6f8e1d7c44",
  "name": "Build server",
  "protocolVersion": 1,
  "studioProtocolVersions": [1],
  "needsUpgrade": false,
  "waitSeconds": 20,
  "leaseSeconds": 120
}
```
 `needsUpgrade` is `true` when the agreed version is older than Studio’s newest. The builder still works and should tell its owner to upgrade. `leaseSeconds` is how long a claim holds without a heartbeat, so renew well inside it. The refusals: | Status | Body | When | | ------ | ----------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `202` | `{ "reason": "…", "code": "K7QF-2M9D" }` | The registration waits for approval. Keep showing the code and connect again in a few seconds | | `401` | `{ "reason": "…" }` | Unknown builder, wrong token, or removed. Stop, and register again only if the owner asks | | `403` | `{ "reason": "…" }` | The registration was declined or expired. Register again | | `409` | `{ "reason": "…", "minimum": 1, "current": 1 }` | No protocol version in common | | `422` | `{ "reason": "…", "problems": ["…"] }` | The manifest cannot be read | An unknown builder and a wrong token answer the same `401`, so a caller learns nothing without the whole credential. ## Ask for work [Section titled “Ask for work”](#ask-for-work) 
```text
POST /work
Authorization: Bearer <builderId>.<token>
```
 Studio holds the request open for up to `waitSeconds` while it looks for a slice that is this builder’s to build. Ask again as soon as it answers. `204 No Content` means nothing yet. `200 OK` means a slice is claimed: 
```json
{
  "sliceId": "0b9c7f4e-2a51-4f6d-8e3a-6c1d5b7a9e20",
  "leaseExpires": "2026-11-09T12:02:00+00:00",
  "git": {
    "gitHandling": "studioManaged",
    "branch": "studio/ordering/place-order",
    "commitMessage": "Build Place order (backend)",
    "pullRequests": "perSlice"
  },
  "order": { "contractVersion": "1", "orderId": "…", "…": "the build order" }
}
```
 `order` is a [build order](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-order) in the highest contract version both sides speak. The claim is already made. The slice is *In progress* and held by this builder. A slice is offered to a builder only when all of these hold: * it is offered by the [build loop](/developers/builders/protocol/build-loop/) and nobody holds it, * it is this builder’s: the slice is assigned to it, or it is not assigned and this builder is its application’s builder (see [Choosing a builder](#choosing-a-builder)), * the builder’s manifest covers the slice’s type, the part offered, Screenplay version `1`, a shared contract version and the application’s Git handling. A slice that no connected builder may take is not given to another one. It waits. `409` with a `reason` means the builder has not connected yet, or said goodbye on `/disconnect` since. Connect again. A registration still waiting for approval answers `202`, a declined or expired one `403`, and an unknown or removed builder `401`, as on `/connect`. The same holds for every call below. ## Renew the lease [Section titled “Renew the lease”](#renew-the-lease) 
```text
POST /work/<sliceId>/lease
Authorization: Bearer <builderId>.<token>


{ "orderId": "…" }
```
 `200 OK` with `{ "leaseExpires": "…" }`. `410 Gone` with a `reason` means the lease is lost: it ran out, the attempt passed its deadline, a person cancelled the build, or the slice is held by another builder.
