Stop building that slice and discard the work. ## Report progress [Section titled “Report progress”](#report-progress) 
```text
POST /work/<sliceId>/progress
Authorization: Bearer <builderId>.<token>
Content-Type: application/json
```
 The body is a [progress report](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#reporting-progress) in contract version 2 or later: the order, the step the builder is on, new log lines and the tokens used so far. People see it on the slice while the build runs. A report does not renew the lease, so keep sending heartbeats. `200 OK` with `{ "recorded": true }`. `400` with `problems` when the report does not follow the progress schema. `410 Gone` with a `reason` when the builder no longer holds the build or its lease is lost, the same as for a heartbeat. ## Report the outcome [Section titled “Report the outcome”](#report-the-outcome) 
```text
POST /work/<sliceId>/outcome
Authorization: Bearer <builderId>.<token>
Content-Type: application/json


{ "kind": "built", "contractVersion": "1", "orderId": "…", "commit": "4f2c9e1", "branch": "studio/ordering/place-order", "…": "…" }
```
 The body is a [build outcome](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/#a-build-outcome). `200 OK` with `{ "settled": "built" }`, or `blocked`, `failed` or `nothingToDo`. The slice’s status is set as [the build loop](/developers/builders/protocol/build-loop/) describes. | Status | When | | ------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `400` | The outcome does not follow the schema. `problems` names each problem | | `409` | The lease is lost, or the outcome does not fit the build’s Git handling. `reason` says which. While the lease holds, a corrected outcome may be sent | ## Disconnect [Section titled “Disconnect”](#disconnect) 
```text
POST /disconnect
Authorization: Bearer <builderId>.<token>
```
 `204`. The builder shows as offline until it connects again. A slice it holds keeps its lease until the lease runs out. ## Git handling [Section titled “Git handling”](#git-handling) Each application chooses who does the repository work, under **Settings** > **Building**. A builder’s own setting, under **Settings** > **Builders**, overrides it for every build that builder does. The choice is fixed when a slice is claimed, and is handed to the builder in `git`. | | Studio-managed (`studioManaged`) | Builder-managed (`builderManaged`) | | ----------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- | | Branch | Studio names it in `git.branch` | The builder chooses | | Commit message | Studio writes it in `git.commitMessage` | The builder chooses | | Pull request | Studio opens it from the branch, one per slice or per feature (`git.pullRequests`) | The builder opens it, or does not | | A `built` outcome must report | `commit`, and `branch` if given must be `git.branch`. Never `pullRequest` | At least one of `commit`, `branch`, `pullRequest` | | Shown on the slice | The branch and commit | The reported commit, branch or pull request, linked | With one pull request per feature, Studio reuses the open pull request of the build’s branch. The branch name must then be the same for every slice of the feature, so it may not use `{slice}`, `{part}` or `{attempt}`. Branch name and commit message are templates with the placeholders `{application}`, `{module}`, `{feature}`, `{slice}`, `{part}` and `{attempt}`. In a branch name, every value is written in lowercase with `-` between words. The defaults are `studio/{feature}/{slice}` and `Build {slice} ({part})`. Studio never puts a repository credential in a build order, a prompt or a log. A builder that pushes uses credentials of its own. ## Choosing a builder [Section titled “Choosing a builder”](#choosing-a-builder) Every application has a builder. It is Studio’s own until someone chooses another under **Settings** > **Building**. A person can assign a single slice to another builder from the slice’s menu. The slice’s assignment wins over the application’s builder, and clearing it returns the slice to its application’s builder. Only approved builders of the organization can be chosen. The menu shows why a builder cannot build the slice, and a slice whose builder is offline says it waits for that builder. ## Revoking a builder [Section titled “Revoking a builder”](#revoking-a-builder) A maintainer removes a builder under **Settings** > **Builders**. Its next request with the same token answers `401`. A slice it holds keeps its lease until the lease runs out, and is then offered again. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Builders](/developers/builders/) * [Talk to Studio directly](/developers/builders/protocol/) * [The build loop](/developers/builders/protocol/build-loop/) * [Build orders and outcomes](/developers/builders/build/orders-and-outcomes/)

# Live modeling with your own coding agent

> Connect the coding agent you run on your own machine to an event model board, hand it work from the board chat, and see what it changes, and how agent sessions work.

People who already run a coding agent (Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, Cursor, GitHub Copilot CLI or OpenCode) can connect it to an event model board and model with it there, on their own subscription. The Cratis CLI does the connecting with `cratis build run --modeling`. Studio queues the work, shows the agent on the board and attributes what it changes. ## How it looks on the board [Section titled “How it looks on the board”](#how-it-looks-on-the-board) * The board chat header lists the agents connected to the board, and what each says it is doing. * Anyone on the board hands an agent work by mentioning it in a chat topic, for example `@Claude Code add a Pay invoice slice to Billing`. * The agent answers in the same topic, under its own name. * By default the agent proposes a change and waits. Its reply shows **Go ahead** and **Don’t**, and the agent hears the answer and acts on it. In unattended mode it changes the board and then says what it did. * Everything the agent changes is recorded as the agent’s, on behalf of the organization credential that connected it. ## Connect an agent [Section titled “Connect an agent”](#connect-an-agent) You need the organization’s MCP credential (see [Connect an AI agent over MCP](/developers/mcp/)) and the [Cratis CLI](https://www.cratis.io/cli/). 
```text
cratis build run --modeling --board <eventModelId|board address> --agent claude|codex|gemini|cursor|copilot|opencode [--name "<name>"] [--unattended]
```
 | Option | Meaning | | -------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `--board` | The board’s identifier, or its address in Studio. The identifier is the part after `event-model/` | | `--agent` | The agent to start, as for `cratis build run` | | `--name` | What the agent is called on the board. It defaults to the agent’s product name, such as `Claude Code`, `Codex` or `Gemini CLI` | | `--unattended` | Connects in unattended mode. Without it, the agent proposes and waits | The command asks for the credential once and keeps it in your operating system’s credential store, never in the project, a prompt or a log. ## What the host does [Section titled “What the host does”](#what-the-host-does) This is the host’s side of the session, for anyone writing their own. It is only calls to Studio’s MCP server. 
```text
studio_connect_local_agent  -> sessionId
loop:
  studio_poll_local_agent   (at least every 30 seconds)
  for each prompt:
    studio_take_local_agent_prompt
    work through the board tools, every call carrying x-cratis-studio-local-agent: <sessionId>
    studio_answer_local_agent_prompt
studio_disconnect_local_agent
```
 1. **Token.** Exchange the MCP credential for a bearer token, as described in [Connect an AI agent over MCP](/developers/mcp/). Exchange it again when a call answers `401`. 2. **Connect.** Call `studio_connect_local_agent`. If it answers `connected: false`, stop and show the `outcome` text. 3. **Start the agent** with Studio’s MCP server configured on its session, with two headers: `Authorization: Bearer <token>` and `x-cratis-studio-local-agent: <sessionId>`. The agent works on the board only through that server, so everything it changes is attributed to it. 4. **Poll** at least every 30 seconds, with `activity` set to a short line on what the agent is doing. Two minutes without a poll shows the agent as gone. 5. **Handle each prompt**, oldest first. Take it, work on it, answer it. See the table below. 6. **Leave.** Call `studio_disconnect_local_agent` on exit. Prompts that were queued and not taken stay queued and are not given to another session. | Prompt state | Mode | What the agent does | Answer with | | ------------ | ------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `Queued` | `ProposeAndConfirm` | Reads the board and describes the change it would make, without changing anything | `isProposal: true` and the description | | `Queued` | `Unattended` | Makes the change | `isProposal: false` and what it changed | | `Confirmed` | either | Makes the change it proposed | `isProposal: false` and what it changed | | `Declined` | either | Nothing on the board | `isProposal: false` and a short acknowledgement, or `isProposal: true` with a different proposal | Every prompt that was taken is answered, also when the agent fails. The reply then says that it failed and why, without credentials or local paths. ### `studio_connect_local_agent` [Section titled “studio_connect_local_agent”](#studio_connect_local_agent) | Argument | Required | Meaning | | -------------- | -------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `eventModelId` | yes | The board, as reported by `studio_get_structure` | | `name` | yes | What the agent is called and mentioned by: letters, digits, spaces, `.`, `-` and `_`, starting with a letter or digit, at most 60 characters | | `mode` | no | `ProposeAndConfirm` (the default) or `Unattended` | It returns `{ "connected": true, "outcome": "…", "sessionId": "<guid>" }`, or `connected: false` with the reason and no session. ### `studio_poll_local_agent` [Section titled “studio_poll_local_agent”](#studio_poll_local_agent) | Argument | Required | Meaning | | -------------- | -------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `sessionId` | yes | From `studio_connect_local_agent` | | `eventModelId` | yes | The board the session is connected to | | `activity` | no | What the agent is doing, at most 200 characters, shown beside it on the board. Empty while it waits | It renews the session and returns what waits for the agent, oldest first: 
```json
{
  "connected": true,
  "prompts": [
    {
      "id": "<promptId>",
      "eventModelId": "<guid>",
      "sessionId": "<guid>",
      "topicId": "<guid>",
      "commentId": "<guid>",
      "requestedBy": "<userId>",
      "prompt": "@Claude Code add a Pay invoice slice to Billing",
      "state": "Queued",
      "replyId": null,
      "queued": "2026-11-09T12:00:00+00:00"
    }
  ]
}
```
 `state` is `Queued` for a new message, `Confirmed` when a person said go ahead to the proposal named by `replyId`, and `Declined` when a person said don’t. `connected: false` means the session was disconnected or is not on that board. Connect again with a new session. ### `studio_take_local_agent_prompt` [Section titled “studio_take_local_agent_prompt”](#studio_take_local_agent_prompt) Arguments: `sessionId`, `eventModelId`, `promptId`. It takes the prompt so the next poll does not return it. A prompt can be taken once per state: once when it is queued, and once more after a person answers a proposal. A prompt of another session or board is refused as if it did not exist. ### `studio_answer_local_agent_prompt` [Section titled “studio_answer_local_agent_prompt”](#studio_answer_local_agent_prompt) | Argument | Required | Meaning | | --------------------------------------- | -------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `sessionId`, `eventModelId`, `promptId` | yes | As above. The prompt must be taken | | `reply` | yes | What to say, in Markdown. Not empty | | `isProposal` | no | `true` to describe a change and wait for **Go ahead** or **Don’t**. It defaults to `false`, which closes the prompt | It posts the reply in the topic the message came from, authored by the session, and returns `{ "answered": true, "replyId": "<guid>" }`. ### `studio_disconnect_local_agent` [Section titled “studio_disconnect_local_agent”](#studio_disconnect_local_agent) Arguments: `sessionId`, `eventModelId`. It ends the session and the agent leaves the board. Its earlier replies keep its name. ## Attribution [Section titled “Attribution”](#attribution) The agent changes the board with the ordinary board tools. The host adds the header `x-cratis-studio-local-agent: <sessionId>` to every MCP request of the session. While the session is connected, what a call changes is recorded as the agent’s, with its session and name, on behalf of the credential. A header naming a session that never connected, has disconnected or belongs to another organization is ignored, and the call is attributed to the credential as usual. The header never widens what the call may do. ## What a mention does [Section titled “What a mention does”](#what-a-mention-does) When a chat message on an event model board mentions a connected agent by name, written as `@` followed by the name in any case and not followed by another letter or digit, Studio queues a prompt for that agent’s session. A message is handed to an agent once however often it is delivered, and a message the agent itself wrote is never handed on. People’s answers to a proposal go to the same session. ## Agent sessions [Section titled “Agent sessions”](#agent-sessions) A coding agent that builds a slice has to read the slice’s Screenplay model, check it and run its specifications. A builder host such as `cratis build` gives every agent session it starts what it needs for that, so nothing has to be installed in the repository and nothing is left behind. Studio publishes the two parts: the Screenplay guidance, and the tools to configure. Neither needs a sign-in, and neither holds organization data. ### The session descriptor [Section titled “The session descriptor”](#the-session-descriptor) 
```text
GET /api/builders/session
```
 The answer is JSON and is not cached: 
```json
{
  "guidance": {
    "name": "screenplay-session",
    "version": "1.0.0",
    "screenplayVerifiedWith": "6.4.0",
    "archive": "/api/builders/session/screenplay-session.zip",
    "sha256": "…",
    "forms": {
      "claude-code": { "plugin": ".", "systemPrompt": "system-prompt.md" },
      "agents-md": { "instructions": "AGENTS.md" },
      "context": { "document": "context.md" }
    },
    "skills": [
      { "name": "screenplay-language", "description": "…", "path": "skills/screenplay-language/SKILL.md" }
    ]
  },
  "tools": [
    {
      "name": "screenplay",
      "transport": "stdio",
      "purpose": "Reads and checks the Screenplay model of the order and runs its specifications. …",
      "command": "cratis",
      "arguments": ["screenplay", "mcp", "{workspace}"],
      "tools": ["open-workspace", "read-workspace", "run-specifications"]
    },
    {
      "name": "studio",
      "transport": "http",
      "purpose": "Studio's board tools for the organization. …",
      "url": "https://mcp.cratis.studio/mcp",
      "tokenUrl": "https://mcp.cratis.studio/.cratis/token",
      "authentication": "clientCredentials"
    }
  ],
  "acp": [
    { "name": "screenplay", "command": "cratis", "args": ["screenplay", "mcp", "{workspace}"], "env": [] },
    { "type": "http", "name": "studio", "url": "https://mcp.cratis.studio/mcp", "headers": [] }
  ]
}
```
 | Member | Meaning | | ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `guidance` | The Screenplay session bundle Studio is pinned to: its version, the Screenplay version it was verified with, where to download it and its SHA-256 | | `guidance.forms` | The bundle in the form each kind of agent reads. Paths are inside the archive | | `guidance.skills` | The skills in the bundle, with the path of each `SKILL.md` inside the archive | | `tools` | The MCP servers to configure on the session, described once. Members that do not apply to a transport are left out | | `acp` | The same servers as an [Agent Client Protocol](https://agentclientprotocol.com) `session/new` request takes them in `mcpServers` | The `studio` server is listed only when Studio’s MCP service is available for the environment. ### The guidance archive [Section titled “The guidance archive”](#the-guidance-archive) 
```text
GET /api/builders/session/screenplay-session.zip
```
 The answer is a ZIP with the header `X-Checksum-Sha256`, the lowercase hex SHA-256 of the body, which is the same as `guidance.sha256`. Verify the checksum before you unpack anything. The archive holds a `bundle.json` that lists every file with its own checksum. Refuse a bundle whose `bundle.json` schema version you do not know. ### Starting a session [Section titled “Starting a session”](#starting-a-session) For every build, a host: 1. Unpacks the archive, cached by checksum, into a folder **outside** the repository. 2. Writes the order’s Screenplay documents to a workspace folder, also outside the repository, and puts that folder in place of `{workspace}` in the `screenplay` server’s arguments. 3. Gets a bearer token for the `studio` server from `tokenUrl` with the organization’s MCP credential, and adds it as an `Authorization: Bearer …` header on the session only. The token never goes into a prompt, a file in the repository or a log. 4. Starts the agent with the guidance in the form it reads: * **Claude Code**: `--plugin-dir <bundle>` and `--append-system-prompt-file <bundle>/system-prompt.md`, with the MCP servers passed through `--mcp-config`. * **An ACP agent** (Codex, Gemini CLI, Cursor, GitHub Copilot CLI, OpenCode): `session/new` with `cwd` set to the repository and `mcpServers` set to `acp`. The first prompt starts with `context.md` from the bundle. * **Any other agent**: the content of `context.md` at the start of its prompt. 5. Deletes the unpacked bundle and the workspace when the session ends, so the repository holds only what the agent wrote. It checks that the repository has no `.claude`, `.cratis/ai` or `AGENTS.md` change it did not have before, and reports a session that left one behind as failed. With the `screenplay` server, the agent opens the workspace (`open-workspace`), reads its diagnostics to check the model (`read-workspace`) and runs the slice’s specifications against the model (`run-specifications`). ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [MCP tools and command catalog](/developers/mcp-tools/) * [Use the reference builder](/developers/builders/configure/cratis-build/) * [Agent context](/developers/agent-context/)

# Connect an AI agent over MCP

> Give an AI agent access to your organization's event models over the Model Context Protocol, with the credential from Settings and a client configuration you can copy.

Studio exposes your organization’s models to AI agents over the [Model Context Protocol](https://modelcontextprotocol.io) (MCP). An agent that is connected can read your projects, event models, brainstorming boards and concepts, and change them. Ask it to add a slice and it adds the slice, instead of describing how you would. MCP access is not read-only An agent that holds the organization’s credential can change the organization’s models, not only read them. Connect only agents you trust with that, and treat the credential like a password. [MCP security for maintainers](/developers/mcp-security/) explains what a connected agent can and cannot reach. Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram The credential decides the organization. An agent that can read the models can change them too. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * Issuing or rotating the credential needs a **maintainer** of the organization. Anyone who can open **Settings** > **MCP** can read it, so keep that page to people you would trust with the agent’s access. * Each organization has one credential: a client ID and a client secret. The agent works inside that organization and cannot see another one. * An MCP-capable client: Claude Code, an editor’s assistant, or any program that can talk to an MCP server over HTTP and send a bearer token. ## Find the endpoint and the credential [Section titled “Find the endpoint and the credential”](#find-the-endpoint-and-the-credential) 1. Open **Settings** > **MCP**. 2. Read the **Endpoint** and the **Client ID**. The client ID is your organization’s name, so it is not a secret. 3. Next to **Client secret**, select **Show** to reveal the secret. Select **Hide** when you are done. 4. If the page says the organization has no MCP credential yet, select **Issue credential**. If that fails, the organization’s encryption key may not be ready yet; try again in a moment. Select **How to connect** for a dialog that fills these snippets with your own values. ## Get a token [Section titled “Get a token”](#get-a-token) The client secret is exchanged for a short-lived bearer token. The token is valid for an hour. The answer also carries a refresh token, which you can exchange for a new token without sending the secret again. 
```bash
curl -X POST https://mcp.cratis.studio/.cratis/token \
  -d grant_type=client_credentials \
  -d client_id=<client-id> \
  -d client_secret=<client-secret>
```
 Use the endpoint address shown on the **MCP** settings page if it differs from the example. ## Point your agent at Studio [Section titled “Point your agent at Studio”](#point-your-agent-at-studio) Most MCP clients take a URL and a header. With Claude Code: 
```bash
claude mcp add --transport http cratis-studio https://mcp.cratis.studio/mcp \
  --header "Authorization: Bearer <access_token>"
```
 For a client that reads its servers from a configuration file, the equivalent is: 
```json
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "cratis-studio": {
      "type": "http",
      "url": "https://mcp.cratis.studio/mcp",
      "headers": { "Authorization": "Bearer <access_token>" }
    }
  }
}
```
 The header name and the bearer scheme are the same in every client. If your client does not support custom headers, put a small proxy in front that adds the header. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) Ask your agent to run `studio_whoami`. It should answer with your organization’s name. Then ask it to list your event models. If it can, the connection works. ## What the agent can do [Section titled “What the agent can do”](#what-the-agent-can-do) The agent gets Studio’s tools. The ones you will use most: * **Read the model.** List and read event models and brainstorming boards, find an item by name, and read the structure of a project. * **Change the model.** Create event models and brainstorming boards, add and move events, regions, sticky notes and chats, set a sticky note’s color, and add concepts, constraints, state machines and workflows. * **Edit an event model in Screenplay.** Open a session on an event model, read and propose typed edits in Screenplay, and apply the accepted result to the board. * **Read board findings.** Get what the board’s checks report: compiler errors and the gaps in the model. * **Export context.** Hand a slice, a feature, an event model or a domain to a coding agent as Markdown, JSON, YAML or TOON. See [Agent context](/developers/agent-context/). * **Reach everything else.** A catalog of every Studio command and query lets an agent do anything that has no named tool. [MCP tools and command catalog](/developers/mcp-tools/) lists every tool. ## What the agent cannot do [Section titled “What the agent cannot do”](#what-the-agent-cannot-do) * It cannot reach another organization. The organization is fixed by the credential, not by anything the agent asks for. * It cannot widen its own access by asking. * It cannot remove a module whose slices have started: Studio refuses the removal unless every slice in it is still **Not started**. Changes the agent makes show up on the board like anyone else’s, and in the board’s history. ## Rotate the secret [Section titled “Rotate the secret”](#rotate-the-secret) If the secret leaks, or you lose track of who has it, select **Rotate secret** under **Settings** > **MCP**. Studio generates a new secret immediately, and the old one stops working at once. Every agent that used the old one has to be reconfigured with a new token. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [MCP tools and command catalog](/developers/mcp-tools/) * [MCP security for maintainers](/developers/mcp-security/) * [Live modeling with your own agent](/developers/live-modeling/) * [Your organization](/organization/) for roles and settings

# MCP security for maintainers

> What the MCP credential is, who can use it, how to rotate it, and what it does and does not protect you from.

Studio’s MCP server is the one surface Studio deliberately exposes to software it does not control. An AI agent behind it is driven by a language model, and a model can be talked into asking for things nobody intended. The design assumes that. The promise is narrow and specific: **a call made with your organization’s credential can only reach your organization’s data.** Everything on this page is either how that is kept, or what you must do yourself because Studio cannot. ## What the credential is [Section titled “What the credential is”](#what-the-credential-is) Each organization has one MCP credential, made of two parts: | Part | What it is | Secret? | | ----------------- | ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------ | | **Client ID** | Your organization’s name | No. It names something your organization already knows | | **Client secret** | A generated secret | Yes. Whoever holds it acts as your organization | An agent exchanges the pair for a bearer token, and the token is what it sends with each call. The token names the organization it was issued for, and Studio signs and encrypts it, so an agent cannot edit it to name another one. Studio also discards any identity information a client sends along with a request, so a header an agent sets can never pass for one Studio produced. The organization is not a parameter of any tool. A model that is persuaded to ask for another organization’s model has nothing to ask with. ## How the secret is kept [Section titled “How the secret is kept”](#how-the-secret-is-kept) * Studio stores the secret **encrypted with your organization’s own key**, so that members can read it back under **Settings** > **MCP**. * Studio answers the same way for an unknown client ID, a wrong secret and a revoked credential. Nobody can use the token endpoint to find out which organizations exist or whether one has a credential. ## Studio’s own agents use the same door [Section titled “Studio’s own agents use the same door”](#studios-own-agents-use-the-same-door) When you ask one of Studio’s agents to do something on a board, it does not reach into Studio. It exchanges your organization’s credential for a token and connects to the public MCP address, exactly as an outside agent does. That is deliberate. Studio’s agents are confined by the same rules as yours, with no second, more privileged path for a model to be talked onto. Studio ships no AI model of its own. Until an organization has configured a provider and an agent that can reach it, the AI features are off and say why. A reply is recorded as having acted only when a tool was actually called, not when the model says it did something. ## Issue, show, rotate [Section titled “Issue, show, rotate”](#issue-show-rotate) | Task | Where | Who | | ----------------------------- | --------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------- | | Issue a credential | **Settings** > **MCP** > **Issue credential** | A maintainer | | Read the client ID and secret | **Settings** > **MCP**, then **Show** | Anyone who can open the page | | Rotate the secret | **Settings** > **MCP** > **Rotate secret** | A maintainer | Every organization is given a credential automatically when its encryption key is created. **Issue credential** is the way back when that did not happen. Rotation takes effect immediately. The old secret stops working the moment the new one exists, and every agent that used it has to be reconfigured. Rotate when: * the secret was pasted somewhere it should not be, such as a chat, a ticket or a repository, * someone who knew it has left the team, * you do not know who holds it any more. A token that was already issued can keep working until it expires, which is an hour after it was issued. ## What this does not protect against [Section titled “What this does not protect against”](#what-this-does-not-protect-against) The guarantee is narrower than “safe”. Know the limits: * **An agent acting inside your organization is fully authorized there.** A model talked into removing a module your organization owns is making a legitimate call. Two things stand in the way: the tool descriptions tell the agent to remove only when a person asked, and Studio refuses to remove anything unless every slice in it is still **Not started**. The tenant boundary does not help here. * **Anyone who holds the secret is your organization.** There is no second factor. Rotation is the remedy, and it is immediate. * **The secret is shown to people in your organization on purpose.** Anyone who can open **Settings** > **MCP** can read it. Encryption protects it at rest, not from your own members. * **MCP access is not read-only.** There is no read-only credential today. Connect an agent only when you are comfortable with it changing your models. ## Good practice [Section titled “Good practice”](#good-practice) * Keep the secret in a secrets manager or your operating system’s credential store, never in a repository or a prompt. * Give each agent its own short-lived token, and let the refresh token renew it, instead of sharing one long-lived token. * Rotate on a schedule that matches how often people come and go. * Review what agents changed in the board history. Their changes are recorded like anyone else’s. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Connect an AI agent over MCP](/developers/mcp/) * [MCP tools and command catalog](/developers/mcp-tools/) * [Your organization](/organization/) for who is a maintainer

# MCP tools and command catalog

> Every tool the Studio MCP server offers an agent, how the command catalog reaches everything else, and how generated concept values are defined.

An agent connected to Studio over [MCP](/developers/mcp/) sees a list of tools. Tool names start with `studio_`. This page lists them, then explains the command catalog, which reaches every Studio operation that has no named tool. Studio has far too many commands for one tool each: that would fill an agent’s context before it read a single description, so the common operations have named tools and everything else goes through the catalog. ## Read access and write access [Section titled “Read access and write access”](#read-access-and-write-access) Every tool is either a read tool or a write tool. An organization’s MCP credential allows both. A tool that changes the model says so in its description, and the descriptions of destructive tools tell the agent to use them only when a person asked. Every tool that takes an identifier checks that it is a real identifier, and when it is not, tells the agent to read the structure or the model first instead of inventing one. When a tool creates something, it makes the new identifier itself and reports it back. ## Start here: read and navigate [Section titled “Start here: read and navigate”](#start-here-read-and-navigate) | Tool | What it does | | ---------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `studio_whoami` | Says which organization the agent is connected to | | `studio_get_structure` | Reads the projects, applications, domains and boards the agent can reach | | `studio_locate` | Turns names into the identifiers other tools need | | `studio_list_event_models` | Lists the event models | | `studio_get_event_model` | Reads one event model | | `studio_list_brainstorming_boards` | Lists the brainstorming boards | | `studio_get_brainstorming_board` | Reads what is on a brainstorming board | | `studio_get_board_findings` | Returns what the event model board shows as findings: compiler errors and the gaps in the model, such as read model fields no event fills, command properties no event captures, events nothing consumes and slices without specifications | ## Event models in Screenplay [Section titled “Event models in Screenplay”](#event-models-in-screenplay) Event models are described in Screenplay. Studio forwards edits to the Screenplay tools instead of carrying its own copy of every edit, so an outside agent gets the same read, propose, validate and apply steps that Studio’s own agents get. | Tool | What it does | | --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `studio_create_event_model` | Creates a new event model | | `studio_screenplay_open` | Exports an event model into a scratch workspace and returns a session and the Screenplay tools | | `studio_screenplay_call` | Runs one Screenplay tool in the session: read the syntax, look at the model, propose typed edits, apply an accepted proposal to the workspace | | `studio_screenplay_apply` | Turns what changed in the workspace into Studio’s own changes, so it shows on the board and in its history, and reports what was done and what could not be applied | | `studio_screenplay_close` | Abandons a session | A session is tied to the one event model it was opened on and to the organization that opened it. A session that is not used for thirty minutes is closed, and an organization can hold five open at a time. The workspace holds the board’s screens, with their navigation, toolbar slice buttons and template references, and the sample data chosen for the application. Applying changes saves changed screens and adds new sample data packs. Some changes cannot be applied: edits to an existing sample data pack, removal of a pack or its selection, and removal of a screen from a slice. Studio reports these as not translated instead of applying them. Concepts do not travel through this path yet. Use the concept tools below. ## Brainstorming boards [Section titled “Brainstorming boards”](#brainstorming-boards) | Tool | What it does | | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `studio_create_brainstorming_board` | Creates a board | | `studio_add_brainstorming_event`, `studio_rename_brainstorming_event`, `studio_move_brainstorming_event`, `studio_remove_brainstorming_event` | Work with events | | `studio_add_brainstorming_sticky_note`, `studio_update_brainstorming_sticky_note`, `studio_update_brainstorming_sticky_note_colour`, `studio_move_brainstorming_sticky_note`, `studio_remove_brainstorming_sticky_note` | Work with sticky notes | | `studio_add_brainstorming_region`, `studio_rename_brainstorming_region`, `studio_move_brainstorming_region`, `studio_remove_brainstorming_region` | Group the board in regions | | `studio_add_brainstorming_event_to_region`, `studio_add_brainstorming_sticky_note_to_region` | Place items in a region | | `studio_add_brainstorming_chat`, `studio_add_brainstorming_chat_to_region`, `studio_send_brainstorming_chat_message` | Add a chat, place it, and send a message, optionally as a persona of the domain | | `studio_arrange_brainstorming_board` | Lays the board out as a column per region with the items in a grid. Arranging twice gives the same board | A sticky note’s color carries its meaning: yellow for a question, pink for a risk, green for a decision and blue for an assumption. Orange and purple are free. ## Concepts, constraints, state machines and workflows [Section titled “Concepts, constraints, state machines and workflows”](#concepts-constraints-state-machines-and-workflows) | Tool | What it does | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------- | | `studio_list_concepts`, `studio_add_concept`, `studio_update_concept` | Work with the domain’s concepts | | `studio_list_constraints`, `studio_add_constraint`, `studio_add_event_to_constraint`, `studio_configure_constraint_event`, `studio_remove_event_from_constraint` | Work with constraints | | `studio_list_state_machines`, `studio_add_state_machine`, `studio_add_state_machine_state`, `studio_add_state_machine_transition` | Work with state machines | | `studio_list_workflows`, `studio_add_workflow`, `studio_add_workflow_node`, `studio_connect_workflow_nodes` | Work with workflows | ## Sticky notes on the other canvases [Section titled “Sticky notes on the other canvases”](#sticky-notes-on-the-other-canvases) The constraints canvas, the personas canvas, the event sources board, the given contexts board and the seed data board each have the same six tools for their sticky notes. Replace `<canvas>` with `constraint`, `persona`, `event_source`, `given_context` or `seed_data`: `studio_list_<canvas>_sticky_notes`, `studio_add_<canvas>_sticky_note`, `studio_update_<canvas>_sticky_note`, `studio_update_<canvas>_sticky_note_colour`, `studio_move_<canvas>_sticky_note` and `studio_remove_<canvas>_sticky_note`. Every one of these takes the identifiers of the board it works on. ## Context for a coding agent [Section titled “Context for a coding agent”](#context-for-a-coding-agent) | Tool | What it does | | ----------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `studio_export_context` | Exports a slice, a feature, an event model or a domain with its specifications, its Screenplay and the Screenplay guidance. See [Agent context](/developers/agent-context/) | ## Live modeling [Section titled “Live modeling”](#live-modeling) Five tools let a coding agent on your machine take part in a board’s chat. They are described in [Live modeling](/developers/live-modeling/): `studio_connect_local_agent`, `studio_poll_local_agent`, `studio_take_local_agent_prompt`, `studio_answer_local_agent_prompt` and `studio_disconnect_local_agent`. ## The command catalog [Section titled “The command catalog”](#the-command-catalog) Use the catalog when an operation has no named tool. It lets an agent discover Studio’s commands and queries, read their arguments and call them in your organization. | Tool | What it does | | -------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------- | | `studio_list_areas` | Lists the areas the catalog is grouped in | | `studio_find_commands`, `studio_find_queries` | Searches commands or queries by what they do | | `studio_describe_command`, `studio_describe_query` | Reads a command’s or query’s arguments | | `studio_execute_command` | Runs a command with a payload built from its description | | `studio_perform_query` | Runs a query | The workflow is always the same. Find the command, describe it, then execute it with the payload the description shows. The agent should not guess a payload from the command’s name. Use the queries afterward to read the resulting state. Take identifiers from the structure and model tools, never from memory. For example, to define how a concept’s values are generated, find `DefineConceptGeneration`, read its arguments, then execute it. ## Generated values [Section titled “Generated values”](#generated-values) A concept names a domain value, such as an order identifier. Give a concept a generation strategy when Studio should describe how to create the value, as with order references or invite codes. Do not use one for facts a person supplies, such as a name. A concept can keep one of these strategies: `uuid`, `uuidv7`, `ulid`, `typeid`, `nanoid` or `bytes`, together with its prefix, letter case, size, alphabet, byte count and encoding where they apply. Screenplay decides which options fit each strategy and primitive when you export. The concepts canvas has no generation controls yet, so use the command catalog: * `DefineConceptGeneration` takes the concept’s identifier and a strategy, with settings that fit the strategy. `bytes` needs both a byte count and an encoding (`base64url`, `hex` or `base32`). A size must be from 1 to 256 and a byte count from 1 to 512. Other settings you leave out use the strategy’s defaults. * `RemoveConceptGeneration` clears the strategy without deleting the concept. Both refuse a concept that does not exist. Studio also refuses to define generated values on a concept marked as personal data. ### How export treats generated values [Section titled “How export treats generated values”](#how-export-treats-generated-values) Export writes the strategy as a Screenplay `generate` clause, and import keeps the six strategies and their options. A plain `uuid` strategy on a `Uuid` concept needs no clause, because it is already the default. Import warns before it drops `pattern`, `random` or `rule` strategies, and warns when a concept already exists and is left unchanged. Rules on a generated concept are checked against the strategy when you export. Each rule comes back as one of: * **Proven.** Every value the strategy can make satisfies the rule. * **Refuted.** The strategy can make a value that breaks the rule. Export refuses that rule and names the strategy and a value that breaks it. The rest of the model still exports, and the concept is left out until you change the rule or the strategy. * **Unproven.** Screenplay could not decide, for example for an ambiguous pattern. Export succeeds and warns, naming the concept, the rule and the reason. Two queries give the same answers without exporting. `AllConceptGenerationStrategies` lists the strategies Screenplay publishes, with their primitives, options and ranges. `ConceptAssessmentsForDomain` returns, for each generated concept in a domain, its findings, the proof status of each rule and the entropy of the strategy. ### Personal data on events already released [Section titled “Personal data on events already released”](#personal-data-on-events-already-released) When a concept’s personal-data protection changes after an event revision was released, rendering and generation refuse the affected slices and ask for a new event generation. The refusal names the event, the concept and the release it was admitted in. Studio reads the release baseline from the connected repository’s default branch to know what was released; this is release evidence, not a report from a live Chronicle. If the repository or the baseline cannot be read, Studio keeps a warning instead of refusing. After you configure a strategy or change protection, [preview a slice](/deliver/) that uses the concept to see the planned output and any diagnostics before you generate.

# Screenplay files

> Treat the Screenplay .play model as source. Export a board, import it somewhere else, keep it in a Git repository and round-trip it between Studio and your editor.

An event model on a Studio board is a [Screenplay](https://www.cratis.io/screenplay/) model: modules, features, slices, commands, events, read models, screens and specifications, written in `.play` files. Studio is one place to edit that model. The `.play` files are another, and they can be the source of truth that lives next to your code. This page covers the developer’s side: getting the files out, getting them back in, and keeping them in Git. The language itself is described at [cratis.io](https://www.cratis.io/screenplay/). Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram Two ways to move a model: by hand, as an export and an import, or automatically, by keeping the board in sync with a repository. ## What an export holds [Section titled “What an export holds”](#what-an-export-holds) Open **Project options** > **Export** to download the open event model. You can choose: | Export | What you get | | ----------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Screenplay file** | One `.play` file holding the whole event model | | **Screenplay folder** | A ZIP of `.play` files, one per module, feature and slice, laid out as folders | | **Mermaid** | A Mermaid `eventmodeling` diagram. There is also **Export feature to Mermaid** for a single feature | | **Event Modeling JSON** | A document in the official Event Modeling Specification format | Use the Screenplay folder when you want the model in a repository, because small files give small diffs and sensible merges. Use the single file when you want to hand the model to a person or a tool in one piece. ### The folder layout [Section titled “The folder layout”](#the-folder-layout) The folder export mirrors the board. Module folders hold feature folders, and feature folders hold slice folders. The names below are only an illustration: 
```text
model/
  Catalog/                    module
    Products/                 feature
      AddProduct/             slice
        ...
  Sales/
    Orders/
      ...
```
 Beside the `.play` documents, the folder carries everything the model names that a `.play` file cannot hold: * the sketches, mockups and images of your screens, saved byte for byte, * `.screenplay/notes.json`, which holds the issue links of your slices. The export reports what the board holds that the files could not carry. Read those diagnostics before you decide the files are a complete copy. ## Import [Section titled “Import”](#import) **Project options** > **Import** reads a model into a board: | Import | Use it for | | ----------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | **Screenplay file** | A single `.play` file | | **Screenplay ZIP** | A ZIP of `.play` files imported as one application. The ZIP can be up to 8 MiB | | **Screenplay folder** | A folder of `.play` files you pick from disk | | **Event Modeling JSON** | An official Event Modeling Specification document | Studio compiles what you pick before it changes anything, and shows the result as **Preview import**. Compile errors are listed with their line and column. Anything the import cannot carry shows under **Some of the document will not be added**. Select **Accept & Import** to import, or **Cancel** to leave the board as it is. An import brings the screens’ sketches, mockups, images and issue links along with the model. ## Round-tripping [Section titled “Round-tripping”](#round-tripping) A model can go out and come back. These are the limits worth knowing: * Concepts that already exist on the board are left unchanged by an import, so their generation strategy and rules do not change. Studio warns when this happens. * Generation strategies that Screenplay cannot express (`pattern`, `random` and `rule`) are dropped with a warning on import. * A concept that is marked as personal data and also generated keeps its personal-data classification and loses generation, with a warning. * Rules on a generated concept are checked against the strategy on export. A rule that can never hold is refused on export and its concept is left out, while the rest of the model still exports. Read the import preview’s warnings every time. They name what an import will not carry. ## Keep the files in Git [Section titled “Keep the files in Git”](#keep-the-files-in-git) Instead of exporting by hand, connect a board to a repository. Studio then commits the model there as you work. You need a project with a repository connected. See [Build and deliver](/deliver/) for connecting one. 1. Open **Project options** > **Keep in sync with Git**. 2. Choose the **Branch** and the **Folder** inside the repository, for example `model`. The folder’s contents are replaced by the board’s files. 3. Optionally turn on **Offer changes pushed to these files for import into the board**. 4. Select **Keep in sync**. What Studio commits is exactly what the Screenplay folder export holds. Each commit message names the board and lists the files added, changed and removed. Studio waits until the board has been quiet for a while, so a modeling session becomes a few commits, not one per keystroke. A board that keeps changing is still committed after a longer delay. Studio reaches the repository through the application’s GitHub App installation. No Git credentials are stored for the sync. ### When the repository changes [Section titled “When the repository changes”](#when-the-repository-changes) Studio never overwrites a change it has not seen. When someone changes the board’s files in the repository, the status says **Someone changed the board’s files in the repository** and Studio stops committing. The dialog lists the files that differ, and you choose: * **Import the repository’s version**, which goes through the usual import preview and brings the sketches, mockups, images and issue links in the folder back with it. * **Commit Studio’s version on top**, which keeps the repository’s change in history and then replaces the folder’s files with the board’s. With **Offer changes pushed to these files for import into the board** on, a change in the repository with no change on the board is offered for import. When both sides changed, Studio reports the conflict and overwrites neither. A sync that fails shows why. Select **Stop syncing** to end it. Nothing already committed is removed. ### Working with the files in your editor [Section titled “Working with the files in your editor”](#working-with-the-files-in-your-editor) Because the files are plain text, an editor, a pull request review or a script can work on them. The safest flow: 1. Let Studio commit the board to the branch. 2. Change the `.play` files on that branch, or on a branch of your own. 3. Merge into the synced branch, and use **Import the repository’s version** in Studio to bring the change onto the board. Edit the model in one place at a time. If the board and the files both change between two syncs, you will be asked to choose. ## Read a model without signing in [Section titled “Read a model without signing in”](#read-a-model-without-signing-in) The viewer at `https://view.cratis.studio/?url=<URL-encoded address>` fetches a public `.play` document, compiles it, and draws it on the same board Studio uses, read-only, with pan and zoom. It needs no sign-in. It is a convenient way to put a model in a README or a blog post. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Agent context](/developers/agent-context/) hands part of a model to a coding agent. * [Connect an AI agent over MCP](/developers/mcp/) edits a model through Screenplay sessions. * [Event modeling](/event-modeling/) explains the model behind the files. * [The Screenplay language](https://www.cratis.io/screenplay/) at cratis.io.

# Webhooks

> Let other systems react to what happens on your boards. The delivery payload, the signature header, the retry schedule and how to add and rotate a webhook.

A webhook tells another system when something changes on a board. Each delivery is a signed JSON `POST` to an address you choose, and Studio retries it with a growing delay when it fails. Use one to start a pipeline when a slice becomes ready, to post to a chat, or to keep another tool in step with the board. ## Add a webhook [Section titled “Add a webhook”](#add-a-webhook) You need to be a maintainer of the organization. 1. Open **Settings** > **Webhooks** and select **Add webhook**. 2. Enter a **Name** for your own reference. 3. Enter the **Address** Studio should post to. It has to be an `https` address on a public host. Addresses inside a private network, loopback addresses and plain `http` are refused. 4. Narrow what the webhook listens to, or leave everything on **Any**: * **Board**: one board, or any board. * **Kinds of board**: event model, brainstorming, concepts or design. * **What changed**: the kinds of item, such as a slice or a comment. * **How it changed**: added, renamed, removed, moved, updated, status changed, commented or restored. 5. Select **Save**. 6. Copy the **Signing secret** now. It is shown only once. A webhook receives only changes that happen after you add it. Each webhook’s **Deliveries** list shows what was sent and how it went: **Delivered**, **Retrying** or **Given up**, with the attempt and, for a retry, the time of the next one. ## What a delivery looks like [Section titled “What a delivery looks like”](#what-a-delivery-looks-like) Studio sends a `POST` with a JSON body and these headers: | Header | Meaning | | --------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `Content-Type` | `application/json; charset=utf-8` | | `User-Agent` | `Cratis-Studio-Webhooks/1` | | `X-Studio-Signature` | The signature, described below | | `X-Studio-Delivery` | The delivery’s id. The same id is sent on every retry of the same delivery | | `X-Studio-Event` | The kind of change, such as `slice.statusChanged` | | `X-Studio-Delivery-Attempt` | `1` for the first try, `2` for the first retry, and so on | The body: 
```json
{
  "id": "5d3c0f64-8a7e-4b8e-9a1f-0c2e6d4b7a31",
  "kind": "slice.statusChanged",
  "organization": "Acme",
  "occurred": "2026-11-09T12:00:00.0000000+00:00",
  "sequenceNumber": 4182,
  "board": { "kind": "EventModel", "id": "9f1b2c3d-4e5f-4a6b-8c7d-0e1f2a3b4c5d" },
  "item": { "kind": "Slice", "id": "c1d2e3f4-a5b6-4c7d-8e9f-0a1b2c3d4e5f", "name": "Place order" },
  "action": "StatusChanged",
  "event": {
    "type": "…",
    "content": { }
  }
}
```
 | Member | Meaning | | ---------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `id` | The delivery’s id, the same as `X-Studio-Delivery` | | `kind` | The item, then what happened to it, in camel case: `slice.statusChanged`, `feature.added`, `comment.commented` | | `organization` | Your organization | | `occurred` | When the change happened | | `sequenceNumber` | The change’s position in your organization’s log of changes, which only grows | | `board` | The board’s `kind` (`EventModel`, `Brainstorming`, `Concepts` or `Design`) and `id` | | `item` | What changed: its `kind`, `id` and `name` | | `action` | What happened: `Added`, `Renamed`, `Removed`, `Moved`, `Updated`, `StatusChanged`, `Commented` or `Restored` | | `event` | The underlying change, with its `type` and `content` | The item kinds are `Board`, `ModuleCollection`, `Module`, `Feature`, `Slice`, `Command`, `Event`, `ReadModel`, `Actor`, `Specification`, `StickyNote`, `Comment`, `Chat` and `Region`. Treat `event.content` as informational and tolerate members you do not know. A retry sends exactly the same body as the first attempt. ## Verify the signature [Section titled “Verify the signature”](#verify-the-signature) Every delivery carries a signature in the form: 
```text
X-Studio-Signature: t=<unix seconds>,v1=<hex HMAC-SHA256>
```
 The signature is an HMAC-SHA256, in lowercase hex, of the string `<t>.<body>`, computed with the webhook’s signing secret. `<t>` is the number in the header and `<body>` is the raw request body, exactly as received. To verify a delivery: 1. Read the raw body before parsing it. Re-serializing the JSON changes the bytes and breaks the check. 2. Split the header on commas and read `t` and every `v1`. 3. Refuse the delivery when `t` is more than five minutes from the time on your clock. This stops someone replaying a captured delivery later. 4. Compute the HMAC of `<t>.<body>` with your secret and compare it with each `v1` in constant time. Accept the delivery if one matches. In Node.js: 
```javascript
import { createHmac, timingSafeEqual } from 'node:crypto';


export function isValid(secret, header, rawBody, toleranceSeconds = 300) {
  const parts = Object.fromEntries(header.split(',').map(p => p.trim().split('=')));
  const t = Number(parts.t);
  if (!Number.isFinite(t) || Math.abs(Date.now() / 1000 - t) > toleranceSeconds) return false;


  const expected = createHmac('sha256', secret).update(`${parts.t}.${rawBody}`).digest('hex');
  const given = Buffer.from(parts.v1 ?? '', 'utf8');
  const wanted = Buffer.from(expected, 'utf8');
  return given.length === wanted.length && timingSafeEqual(given, wanted);
}
```
 ## Respond, retries and duplicates [Section titled “Respond, retries and duplicates”](#respond-retries-and-duplicates) Answer with any `2xx` status within 10 seconds. Anything else counts as a failed attempt: a non-`2xx` status, no answer in time, or a connection that cannot be made. Studio keeps the start of your response, or the reason it got none, in the delivery list to help you debug. A failed delivery is tried again after 1 minute, then 5 minutes, 30 minutes, 2 hours and 6 hours. After the sixth attempt it is **Given up**, and Studio does not retry it again. Delivery is **at least once**. A delivery that was interrupted between sending and recording is sent again with the same `X-Studio-Delivery` id. Deduplicate on that id, and make your handler safe to run twice. Deliveries are not guaranteed to arrive in order, especially when one is retried. Use `sequenceNumber` and `occurred` when order matters. ## Rotate the secret [Section titled “Rotate the secret”](#rotate-the-secret) Select **Rotate secret** next to a webhook. Studio signs deliveries with a new secret from then on, and shows it once. The receiver must be given the new secret, or its checks fail. A delivery that fails the signature check is simply retried, so rotate and update the receiver together. Select **Remove** to delete a webhook. Nothing more is delivered to it. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Connect an AI agent over MCP](/developers/mcp/) reads and changes boards instead of reacting to them. * [Webhooks in your organization’s settings](/organization/webhooks/) for the same page seen from the maintainer’s side. * [Your organization](/organization/) for roles and the rest of the settings.

# Discover your domain

> Capture what your business does, name its words, and describe who does what, before and while you draw the event model.

Discover your domain ## Understand the business before you draw it A domain is one part of the business with its own words and its own rules. Studio gives each domain a set of canvases for getting that understanding out of people’s heads and into a shared, checkable form. The [event model board](/board/) is where behavior is drawn. The canvases in this section are where you do the thinking around it: the facts that happen, the words the business uses, the people involved and the rules that must never be broken. You can start with any of them, and you can come back to them whenever the model raises a question. app.cratis.studio ![The Acme big picture brainstorming board with a region full of orange event sticky notes and a yellow decision note](/_astro/brainstorming-big-picture.BDiD3SPV_Zt47Uf.webp) A brainstorming board for the Storefront domain: events written in the past tense, grouped in regions, with decisions and questions on sticky notes. Most canvases share the same habits. They are free-form, so you drag cards where they read best. They have a toolbar down the left edge, with **Add Sticky Note** for the thinking that is not settled yet and **Arrange** to tidy the cards. Changes are saved as you make them. Look for **Take the guided tour** on a canvas to have it explained step by step. ## What you can do here [Section titled “What you can do here”](#what-you-can-do-here) [Brainstorm events ](/discover/brainstorming/)Write down everything that happens, in any order, then turn it into slices. [Concepts ](/discover/concepts/)Give the business's words a precise type, with rules and privacy built in. [Constraints ](/discover/constraints/)Say what must never happen twice, such as two orders with one number. [Personas ](/discover/personas/)Describe the people your application is for and what they want. [Policies and roles ](/discover/policies-and-roles/)Decide who may do what in the whole application. [Event sources and streams ](/discover/event-sources/)Describe what the business keeps a history of, such as a basket or an order. [State machines and workflows ](/discover/state-machines-and-workflows/)Draw the stages something goes through and the steps that follow. [Given contexts and seed data ](/discover/given-contexts-and-seed-data/)Name the starting situations for your examples and for a new application. [Context map and diagrams ](/discover/context-map-and-diagrams/)See how the domains relate, and view the model as UML diagrams. [Start from an existing system ](/discover/prologue/)Let Prologue observe a running system and propose a first model (beta). [Import a model ](/discover/import-a-model/)Bring in a Screenplay file, Event Modeling JSON or a Miro board. ## Where to find them [Section titled “Where to find them”](#where-to-find-them) Open the project drawer and expand an application, then a domain. Under a domain you find **Event Models**, **Brainstormings**, **Personas**, **Concepts**, **Constraints**, **State machines**, **Workflows**, **Given contexts**, **Seed data** and **Event Sources**. Under an application you find **Prologues**, **Policies** and **Context map**, among others. Selecting one opens its canvas. The sample project **Acme Commerce** has examples of most of them, and every picture in this section comes from it. ## Where to go next [Section titled “Where to go next”](#where-to-go-next) * [Event modeling in Studio](/event-modeling/) explains the ideas behind the board that these canvases feed. * [The event model board](/board/) is where the facts you capture here become slices. * The [glossary](/reference/glossary/) explains every term. * [Talk to your board](/working-with-ai/talk-to-your-board/) lets you ask an agent about what you have captured.

# Brainstorm events

> Capture everything that happens in your domain on a loose board, group it in regions and hand it over to an event model.

A **brainstorming board** is a place to get what happens in your domain out of your head, before you decide where any of it belongs. There are no columns and no rules about where things go. When a cluster of events is ready, you hand it over to an [event model](/event-modeling/) and Studio turns it into slices. What you'll learn * start a brainstorming board and capture events and questions on it * group events in regions and line them up as a timeline * turn a group of events into slices on an event model ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) Your domain needs to exist. To hand events over, it also needs an event model to receive them. You do not need a model to start brainstorming. ## Start a brainstorming board [Section titled “Start a brainstorming board”](#start-a-brainstorming-board) 1. In the project drawer, expand your domain and select **Brainstormings**. 2. Select **Add Brainstorming**, enter a name such as `How we got to Sales`, and confirm. 3. The new board opens empty, with a toolbar on the left edge. The toolbar has four tools. From top to bottom: | Tool | What it adds | | --------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------- | | **Add Event** (the bolt) | An orange event card: one fact, named in the past tense. | | **Add Sticky Note** | A yellow note for a half-formed thought. | | **Add Region** (the grid) | A named frame to group cards. | | **Add Chat Bubble** (the speech bubble) | A conversation pinned to the board. | Once the board has something on it, two whole-board actions appear at the bottom of the toolbar: **Arrange the board: a column per region, the items in a grid**, and **Create slices from all events on the board**. app.cratis.studio ![The Acme big picture board: a region titled Chaos storm full of orange event cards beside a Merchandising region with a decision note](/_astro/brainstorming-big-picture.BDiD3SPV_Zt47Uf.webp) The Acme big picture board starts with a region called Chaos storm: everything the team could think of, in no particular order. ## Capture facts [Section titled “Capture facts”](#capture-facts) Write down the **facts** of the business. Each one has already happened somewhere, so name it in the past tense: *Product added*, *Customer ordered*, *Parcel lost*. 1. Select **Add Event**. A new card appears in the middle of the view, named **New Event**. 2. Double-click the card and type the name of the fact. 3. Keep going as they come to mind. The order does not matter yet, and capturing them is what counts. 4. Drag a card anywhere you like. The board is loose on purpose. Use **Add Sticky Note** for anything that is not a fact yet: *Do we charge before shipping?* Double-click a note to rewrite it. Select it to choose a color. The colors mean the same on every board: yellow is a question, pink a risk, green a decision, blue an assumption. Orange and purple are free for your team’s own use. Use **Add Chat Bubble** to pin a conversation to the board, so a question and its answers stay next to the cards they are about. A message can be written as one of the domain’s [personas](/discover/personas/). Tip If you reach for an empty field or a “maybe” on an event, you probably have two different facts. Write both. ## Group facts in regions [Section titled “Group facts in regions”](#group-facts-in-regions) A **region** is a frame with a name. Select **Add Region**, then drag it over the events that belong together, or drag the events into it. Everything inside a region moves with it when you drag its title bar, and you can resize it from its edges. Double-click the name to rename it. On the sample board for the Sales domain, each region is a step of the story: a storm of how an order lived in email, then a lane for the SalesRep, then one for the SalesManager. app.cratis.studio ![The How we got to Sales board with a storm region of orange events, a SalesRep lane and a SalesManager lane, with decision and risk notes under them](/_astro/brainstorming-how-we-got-to-sales.BuaKiD5E_JTHn4.webp) Regions turn a pile of facts into a story. The notes under each region record the decisions and risks that came out of it. ## Line events up as a timeline [Section titled “Line events up as a timeline”](#line-events-up-as-a-timeline) An event model reads left to right as time passes, so put the events in order too. Drag the events roughly into the order things happen, then select **Arrange events as a timeline** in the region’s title bar. Studio spaces the events evenly on one line and keeps their left-to-right order. ## Turn events into slices [Section titled “Turn events into slices”](#turn-events-into-slices) 1. Select the events that are ready. Click a card, hold Shift to add more, or drag a rubber band around them. A selection toolbar appears above them. 2. Select **Transfer selected events to an event model**. If the domain has more than one event model, choose which one. 3. Studio creates a module named after the region (or the board) the events came from. It puts a feature inside it and adds one [state change slice](/event-modeling/slices/) for each event: the event itself, with its properties if you defined any, and a command named after it, so *Product added* gets a command called *Add product*. 4. Select **Open** in the confirmation to go to the event model. To hand over everything a region frames without selecting it, use **Create slices from the events in this region** in its title bar. To hand over the whole board, use **Create slices from all events on the board**. That button only appears when the domain has an event model to receive the events. The selection toolbar also has **Distribute and align selected events**, **Duplicate selection** and **Delete selection**. ## What happens next [Section titled “What happens next”](#what-happens-next) You continue on the event model board with the same facts, now as slices ready for their [commands, read models and rules](/event-modeling/commands-events-and-read-models/). The brainstorming board stays where it is, so you can keep it as a record of how you got there. Recap You captured events in the past tense, grouped them in regions, lined them up as a timeline and handed them over as slices. Next, give the business’s words a precise type with [concepts](/discover/concepts/), or describe who is involved with [personas](/discover/personas/).

# Concepts

> Name the values your domain speaks in, give each a precise type, mark personal data once and attach the rules it must obey.

A **concept** is a value the business names, such as an order number, a quantity or an email address, wrapped in a type of its own so that it can never be confused with any other text or number. You define it once on the **Concepts** canvas, and every command, event and read model in the domain can use it. Naming and typing these values precisely is some of the most valuable modeling you do. Everything on the domain’s boards speaks this vocabulary. What you'll learn * add a concept and choose the type it wraps * mark a concept as personal data or a secret * add rules that the value must satisfy everywhere it appears * use a concept as the type of a property on the board app.cratis.studio ![The Concepts canvas of the Catalog domain with cards such as BasketId, CustomerId and OrderId, each a Unique identifier](/_astro/concepts-catalog.BraNnhIy_Z2d1hiK.webp) The Concepts canvas of the Catalog domain. Each card is one value the business talks about. ## Add a concept [Section titled “Add a concept”](#add-a-concept) 1. In the project drawer, expand your domain and select **Concepts**. 2. Select **Add Concept** in the toolbar. A card named **NewConcept** appears in the middle of the view, typed as **Text**. 3. Double-click the name and type what the domain calls the value, for example `OrderNumber`. Press Enter. 4. Under **Description**, write a sentence about what it means. 5. Set **Type** to what the value is underneath: **Text**, **Number**, **Whole number**, **Boolean**, **Date**, **Timestamp**, **Unique identifier** or **Enumeration**. An enumeration lets you list its members. The type says what the value *is*; the concept says what it *means*. A `Quantity` is a number: how many of a product a basket line holds. A `BasketId` is a unique identifier, and the concept is what keeps it from being mistaken for any other identifier in the system. The **Generated value** row says whether the code should create values of this concept itself, for example as a UUID. **Not generated** is where every concept starts. A concept that holds personal data cannot have generated values. ## Mark personal data [Section titled “Mark personal data”](#mark-personal-data) Some values belong to a person. Tick the **PII** box on the card to mark the concept as personally identifiable information. Wherever the value appears from then on, it is treated as protected data, so you decide this once on the concept instead of on every property that carries the value. An email address is personal whether it sits on a command, an event or a read model. **Secret** is for a different kind of value: not about a person, but never to be shown, such as a password, a token or a card number. Tick the **Secret** box and the concept is marked as a secret. The box is not offered for an enumeration. ## Add rules [Section titled “Add rules”](#add-rules) At the foot of every card is **Rules**: what the value must satisfy wherever it turns up. 1. Select the **+** (**Add rule**) under **Rules**. A rule row appears. 2. Choose the operator, such as **required**, **equal**, **contains**, **starts with**, **min length**, **max length** or **matches pattern**, and give it a value if it needs one. The operators on offer follow the concept’s type: a number offers comparisons such as **greater than** or **at most** instead. 3. Optionally select the warning sign beside the rule and write the message people see when they break it, for example *Order numbers look like ORD-123456*. There is no save button: a card saves as you change it. ## Use a concept on the board [Section titled “Use a concept on the board”](#use-a-concept-on-the-board) Open the properties of a command, an event or a read model on an event model and select **+ Add property**. When you choose the property’s type, your concepts are listed under **Concepts**. Use the same `OrderNumber` on the command, on the event and in the read model, and it carries the same rules and the same protection everywhere. ## Arrange and discuss [Section titled “Arrange and discuss”](#arrange-and-discuss) * Drag a card by its header to place it. The canvas remembers where it is. * Use **Arrange** at the foot of the toolbar to lay the cards out clear of one another. * Use **Add Sticky Note** for thoughts that surround the concepts without being one: *Is ProductCode ours, or does it belong to Catalog?* * Select the speech bubble in a card’s header to discuss a concept. Mention a teammate or an agent to bring them in. * To remove a concept, use the trash can in its header. You are asked to confirm, because the model may be using it. Tip Add a concept as soon as the same kind of value appears on two cards. It is much easier to agree on one `OrderNumber` early than to reconcile three spellings later. Recap You defined a concept, typed it, marked personal data and added rules. Next, say what must never happen twice with [constraints](/discover/constraints/), or see how a concept is used on a [command](/board/commands/) and an [event](/board/events/). The [glossary](/reference/glossary/) explains the other terms.

# Constraints

> Say what the domain must never let happen twice, and choose the events and properties that make up the rule.

A **constraint** is a rule that the event store holds at the moment an event is recorded: an order number that can only be taken once, an order that can only be confirmed once. Because the rule is held there, rather than checked beforehand, two people racing for the same order number cannot both win. The second one is refused. Constraints belong to the domain. You define each one once on the **Constraints** canvas, and events from any of the domain’s event models can take part in it. What you'll learn * add a constraint and write the message people see when they hit it * choose between a unique-by-properties and a unique-event-type constraint * decide when a claim is given back and where the rule applies * put events into a constraint, from the canvas or from the event model board app.cratis.studio ![The Constraints canvas of the Sales domain with the cards OneConfirmationPerOrder and OneOpenOrderPerBasket and two yellow decision notes explaining why](/_astro/constraints-sales.Br1rLFwA_Z1mGRVM.webp) Two constraints of the Sales domain. The sticky notes on the right record why each rule exists. ## Add a constraint [Section titled “Add a constraint”](#add-a-constraint) 1. In the project drawer, expand your domain and select **Constraints**. 2. Select **Add Constraint** (the padlock at the top of the toolbar). A card named **NewConstraint** appears, set to **Unique by properties**. 3. Double-click the name and state the rule as a promise, for example `OrderNumberIsUnique`. A constraint’s name is unique within its domain, however it is capitalized. 4. Under **Message**, write what the person who runs into the rule should read: *An order with this number already exists.* ## Choose the kind [Section titled “Choose the kind”](#choose-the-kind) The **Kind** row says what is unique. * **Unique by properties**: no two events may carry the same values for the properties you chose. It can span several events. A `CustomerEmailIsUnique` constraint can take the email of both a registration event and an email-changed event, so an address claimed through either cannot be claimed again. Tick **Ignore casing** to treat *<Ada@Example.com>* and *<ada@example.com>* as the same address. * **Unique event type**: an event of that type may happen only once for an event source. A basket can be checked out only once, so a one-checkout constraint takes the checkout event. This kind takes exactly one event and has no properties to choose. Property checkboxes only appear for **Unique by properties**, so check the constraint’s events if you change its kind. ## Add events to the constraint [Section titled “Add events to the constraint”](#add-events-to-the-constraint) A constraint holds nothing until events take part in it. 1. In the **Events** section of the card, open the search box and choose from the events of all the domain’s event models. Each option shows the event with its model and slice. 2. For **Unique by properties**, tick the properties that make up the key. Tick several and it is the combination of their values that must be unique. 3. To take an event out, select the cross beside its name. The event stays on its event model. You can also start from the event. On an event model, open an event and select **Open Constraint Editor**. The **Constraints** dialog lists the domain’s constraints. Choose the ones the event takes part in and, for each, the properties it contributes. The dialog does not define constraints; its **Open the constraint board** button takes you back to this canvas. ## Release and scope [Section titled “Release and scope”](#release-and-scope) Two more rows shape how long a claim is held and where the rule applies. * **Released by** names an event that gives back what the constraint holds. When a customer closes their account, their email address may be used again, so a `CustomerAccountClosed` event releases it. The list has the events of the domain’s event models, plus **Never released**, which holds a claim for good. * **Scope** says what the rule is evaluated per. Tick any of **Per event source type**, **Per event stream type** and **Per event stream id** to hold the rule per that part instead of everywhere at once. Leave all three clear for a constraint with no scope. [Event sources and streams](/discover/event-sources/) explains those words. Every change on the card is saved as you make it.
