Question *Should a cancelled order give its number back?* Put a question like that on a sticky note next to the constraint. Once it is answered, it may become a release. ## Remove a constraint [Section titled “Remove a constraint”](#remove-a-constraint) Use the trash can in the card’s header. You are asked to confirm. The events that took part stay on their event models. Recap You defined a constraint, chose its kind, added events and set when it is released. Next, describe the people behind the model with [personas](/discover/personas/), or see the [events on the board](/board/events/) that take part in constraints. A [specification](/check/specifications/) can expect a modeled constraint to refuse an event.

# Context map and diagrams

> See how an application's domains relate through the events they publish and consume, and view an event model as UML diagrams.

Once a model has grown past one board, it helps to see it from further away. Studio draws two kinds of read-only picture from your model: the **context map** of an application’s domains, and **diagrams** of an event model. You never edit them. They are always drawn from the model and follow it as it changes. What you'll learn * open the context map and read its notation * open a UML or entity diagram of an event model * narrow a diagram to one module or feature ## The context map [Section titled “The context map”](#the-context-map) A [domain](/reference/glossary/#domain) is a part of the business with its own language. The **context map** shows how the domains of one application relate: which domain publishes events and which takes them. In Acme Commerce, for example, the Catalog publishes price changes and Sales takes them in. ### Open it [Section titled “Open it”](#open-it) In the project drawer, expand an application and select **Context map**. If the application has no domains yet, the map says so. If an event model could not be read, the map says that too, rather than quietly leaving out what it should say, and offers **Try again**. ### Read it [Section titled “Read it”](#read-it) The map uses the notation of context mapping. * Every domain is a box marked **«bounded context»**, listing the **Published language** it offers: the public events other domains can take. * Systems outside the application are drawn with a dashed outline. Other applications that a contract is published to are shown under **Other applications**. * Every relationship is a line between two domains. Its upstream end is marked **U** and its downstream end **D**, with the patterns each side takes: open host service (OHS) and published language (PL) upstream, anticorruption layer (ACL) and conformist (CF) downstream. The line is labeled with the events that cross it. Its tooltip reads in full, for example *Sales takes these events from Catalog*. Select a domain to open its board. Select a relationship to open the slice that takes its events. ## Diagrams of an event model [Section titled “Diagrams of an event model”](#diagrams-of-an-event-model) Studio can draw an event model in four more ways. Open **Project options**, choose **Diagrams** and pick one: app.cratis.studio ![The Project options menu open on an event model, with Diagrams expanded to UML state diagram, UML activity diagram, UML sequence diagram and Entity data model](/_astro/diagrams-menu.DlZN5KPy_Km3pc.webp) Project options > Diagrams lists the four diagrams you can draw from an event model. | Diagram | What it shows | | ------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **UML state diagram** | The states that the event model’s events lead to. | | **UML activity diagram** | The flow of the slices: decisions, forks and merges, where a command is rejected, and what the system does. | | **UML sequence diagram** | One feature as messages between the user, screens, the command API, the event store, read models and external systems. | | **Entity data model** | The read models as entities and how they relate. | 1. Select **Project options** at the top left and choose **Diagrams**. 2. Pick the diagram you want. It opens full screen. 3. At the top of the diagram, choose which event model to draw if the project has more than one. In the picture below it reads **Catalog / Catalog**. 4. For the state, activity and entity diagrams, narrow it with **All modules**, the second selector. For the sequence diagram, choose a **Feature** instead. app.cratis.studio ![The UML State Diagram full screen for the Catalog event model with All modules chosen: the states ProductDrafted, ProductPriceSet and ProductDescribed joined by labeled transitions, zoomed to 88 percent](/_astro/uml-state-diagram.DTSa_gdn_Z38HQ3.webp) Each box is a state, such as ProductDrafted, and each arrow is a transition labeled with the command that causes it, such as SetProductPrice. Use Zoom Out, Reset Zoom and Zoom In at the lower right to move around. A diagram can say it has nothing to draw: for example, a feature in which nothing sends a message produces no sequence diagram. That is the model telling you it has not been drawn that far yet. The diagrams are read-only, so to change what they show, change the model on the [event model board](/board/). Recap You opened the context map and read how domains publish and take events, and you viewed an event model as UML diagrams. Next, [brainstorm events](/discover/brainstorming/) for another domain, or read how [translations carry events between applications](/event-modeling/automations-and-translations/). To take the model out as a file, see [export](/share/export/).

# Event sources and streams

> Describe each kind of thing your domain keeps a history of, divide that history into streams and decide when two changes must not both win.

An **event source** is one *kind of thing* your domain keeps a history of, such as a basket or an order. Every single basket has its own sequence of events. The event source is the description of what a basket is: what identifies it, and the **streams** its history is divided into. It is also where **concurrency** is decided, so that two commands racing to change the same history cannot both win. What you'll learn * add an event source and say what identifies it * divide its history into streams * see which commands append to which stream * choose a concurrency scope on a command app.cratis.studio ![The Event Sources canvas with cards Basket, Customer and Location, each with an identifier concept and a stream](/_astro/event-sources-sales.Bn5df7c6_ZHrkky.webp) Three event sources: Basket, Customer and Location. Each has an identifier and at least one stream. ## Add an event source [Section titled “Add an event source”](#add-an-event-source) 1. In the project drawer, expand your domain and select **Event Sources**. 2. Select **Add Event Source**. A card named **New Event Source** appears with a first stream already on it. 3. Double-click the name and type what the domain calls it, for example `Basket`. 4. Under **Description**, write what it covers: *A shopper’s basket before checkout.* Fields on a card are saved when you leave them. There is no save button. ## Say what identifies it [Section titled “Say what identifies it”](#say-what-identifies-it) Three fields on the card say what a basket *is*. * **Identifier concept** names what tells one basket from another. Type the name of the [concept](/discover/concepts/), here `BasketId`. The field is free text and does not check the name against your concepts, so spell it the way the concept is spelled. * **Event source type** is the name of this kind of thing as the concurrency rule sees it, for example `Basket`. * **Scope to the event source id** decides whether each individual basket gets a scope of its own. Ticked, two shoppers never get in each other’s way: only two things happening to the *same* basket can collide. Unticked, every basket shares one scope, so only one change at a time can win across all baskets. Leave it ticked unless the rule you want really is “one at a time, for the whole kind of thing”. ## Divide the history into streams [Section titled “Divide the history into streams”](#divide-the-history-into-streams) A **stream** is a named part of the history that commands append to. A new event source opens with one. 1. Select **Add stream** at the foot of the card. Another stream opens with four fields. 2. Fill in **Name** (what the domain calls it, such as `Checkout`), **Event stream type**, **Event stream id** and **Description**. The event stream type and id make a stream a place of its own. You can leave both empty when the event source’s own settings are all the scope you need. To remove a stream, select the cross in its header. There is no confirmation, and commands that already used it keep what they had. ## See which commands use a stream [Section titled “See which commands use a stream”](#see-which-commands-use-a-stream) Once the model uses a stream, the commands that append to it are listed under it, each with the events its slice records and the event model it belongs to. These lines are links the model makes. A command shows up under a stream when its concurrency rule names that stream. A stream with no commands listed is one the model does not use yet. ## Choose a concurrency scope on a command [Section titled “Choose a concurrency scope on a command”](#choose-a-concurrency-scope-on-a-command) A command can carry a concurrency rule: a scope within which only one change wins at a time. The scope is built from up to four parts. | Part | Where it comes from | | ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Event source id | The individual basket or order, when **Scope to the event source id** is ticked | | Event source type | The card’s **Event source type** | | Event stream type | The stream’s **Event stream type** | | Event stream id | The stream’s **Event stream id** | On a command on the event model board, select **Define concurrency rule** to open the **Concurrency** dialog. The command editor’s **Concurrency** step is described in [commands](/board/commands/). 1. Pick the **Event source** and the **Stream**. Every part is then taken from them. With no stream picked, the event source’s defaults apply. 2. For any part, you can instead choose a **Fixed value**, or **From command property** to scope the rule to a value the command carries, such as the order number in its own request. 3. Select **Save**. The rule stores the values it worked out. Editing a card later does not change what a command that was already modeled does. For the same reason, removing a stream or an event source leaves the commands that used it alone. ## Arrange and remove [Section titled “Arrange and remove”](#arrange-and-remove) Drag a card by its header to place it. **Arrange** tidies every card and note into a grid. **Add Sticky Note** keeps unsettled questions beside the cards, such as *Should a returned order get a stream of its own?* To remove an event source and all its streams, use the bin in its header and confirm. Recap You described an event source, divided it into streams and saw how a command’s concurrency scope is built from them. Next, put the starting situations of your examples on [given contexts](/discover/given-contexts-and-seed-data/), or read about [commands on the board](/board/commands/). [Commands, events and read models](/event-modeling/commands-events-and-read-models/) explains the ideas.

# Given contexts and seed data

> Name the starting situations your specifications begin from, and describe what a brand-new application starts with.

Two canvases hold the *starting points* of your model. **Given contexts** name the situations that [specifications](/check/specifications/) begin from. **Seed data** says what a brand-new application starts with. Both are written once per domain and reused. What you'll learn * create a given context from events that already happened, or from the state of a read model * use a given context in a specification * add a seed data collection and put the starting events in it ## Given contexts [Section titled “Given contexts”](#given-contexts) A specification reads *given* this has happened, *when* this is done, *then* this follows. A **given context** is one of those *givens* with a name: either the **events that have already happened**, or the **state a read model is in**. Every specification that starts from the same place uses the context by name, instead of spelling the same history out again. app.cratis.studio ![The Given contexts canvas of the Sales domain with one purple card named A price in the price list holding a PriceListUpdated event, and a decision note](/_astro/given-contexts-sales.DyBrR3gv_Z1Uxjvx.webp) A given context named A price in the price list. It holds one event, and the note beside it explains why it exists. ### Add a context of events [Section titled “Add a context of events”](#add-a-context-of-events) 1. In the project drawer, expand your domain and select **Given contexts**. 2. Select **Add Given Context** in the toolbar. A card named **New given context** appears. Its **Kind** says **Events**. 3. Double-click the name and say what the situation is, for example `Basket with two items`. A name is unique within the domain, ignoring case. 4. Under **Events**, open the **Add event** list. It shows every event of every event model in the domain, each with its model and slice. Choose one and it joins the context. 5. Under each event, select **Edit values**, fill in what the event carried, and select **Save values**. Select **Cancel** to leave the values as they were. The same event can be in a context more than once, so a basket with two items has the item-added event twice. The list is the order the events happened in, and new events are added at the end. To take an event out, select **Remove event**. Every change is saved as you make it. ### Add a context of a read model state [Section titled “Add a context of a read model state”](#add-a-context-of-a-read-model-state) Some specifications begin with a state rather than a history. They describe a view, and a view is specified by the read model it starts from. 1. Select **Add Read Model Context** in the toolbar. Its **Kind** says **Read model**. 2. Use **Select read model** to choose one of the domain’s read models. A context holds exactly one, and choosing another replaces the first and clears its values. 3. Select **Edit values** and set the state the read model is in when the specification starts, then **Save values**. ### Use a context in a specification [Section titled “Use a context in a specification”](#use-a-context-in-a-specification) On the event model board, open a specification’s **Given** section. * On a **state change** slice, select **Use given context** beside the given events. It offers the events contexts of the domain. A specification can use several. * On a **state view** slice, the button offers the read model contexts. A view uses one. Once it uses a context, or has a read model of its own, the button is disabled. * An **automation** slice offers no contexts. The context’s name on the specification opens it on this canvas. The cross beside the name, **Remove context**, stops the specification using it. The context stays on the canvas. When a specification runs, and when the model is exported as Screenplay, the contexts it uses are spelled out into its given. An events context contributes its events first, in the order the context lists them. If a context cannot be spelled out, the run stops with a message that names the specification and the problem, rather than quietly ignoring it. A specification that used a deleted context shows **Deleted context** until you take it off. Because a context is written once and spelled out each time, editing it changes every specification that uses it. That is the point, and the reason to keep each context to one situation. To remove a context, use **Delete given context** in its header and confirm, because specifications may be using it. ## Seed data [Section titled “Seed data”](#seed-data) **Seed data** answers one question: *what does this application start with?* It is organized in **collections**. A collection is a named set of events that belong together, such as the products the catalog starts with, and each is a card of its own. app.cratis.studio ![The Seed data canvas of the Catalog domain with collections named Catalog and Catalog 2, each listing ProductDrafted, ProductDescribed, ProductPriceSet and ProductApprovedForSale events](/_astro/seed-data-catalog.DFiInEJW_Z2g9bLS.webp) Seed data in the Catalog domain: collections of the events a new application begins with, grouped by the event source each one happens to. ### Add a collection [Section titled “Add a collection”](#add-a-collection) 1. In the project drawer, expand your domain and select **Seed data**. 2. Select **Add Seed Data Collection** in the toolbar. A card named **New seed data** appears. 3. Double-click the name and say what it holds, such as `Starter cart`. Names are unique in a domain, ignoring case. 4. In the card, select **Add seed event for a new event source id** and choose from every event of every event model in the domain. Type in the search field to narrow a long list. 5. Each event lands as a row with its name, the **Event source id** it happens to (a new row gets a fresh one) and its values, which start as **No values set**. Select **Edit values**, fill them in and select **Save values**. Events stay in the order you added them, which is the order the application starts with them. To put an event into the same basket or product as another, select **Add seed event to this event source id** in that group instead. The bin on a row removes just that event. The bin in the card’s header removes the whole collection after a confirmation. If an event is later removed from its event model, its row says **Missing catalog event** and keeps the name it was recorded with. ## Arrange both canvases [Section titled “Arrange both canvases”](#arrange-both-canvases) Drag a card by its header to place it. **Arrange**, the last button on the toolbar, lays out every card and note. **Add Sticky Note** keeps unsettled thinking beside the cards: *Do we seed approved products only, or also a draft and a withdrawn one?* Recap You named a starting situation for specifications and a starting set of events for the application. Next, [write specifications](/check/specifications/) that use your givens, or [import a model](/discover/import-a-model/) that already has them. The [event sources](/discover/event-sources/) page explains what an event source id is.

# Import a model

> Bring an event model into a board from a Screenplay file, an Event Modeling JSON document or a Miro board.

You may already have a model somewhere else: a Screenplay file in a repository, an export from another event modeling tool, or a board on Miro. Import reads it into a board in Studio so that you carry on from there instead of redrawing it. What you'll learn * find the **Import** menu and what it offers * import a Screenplay file, folder or ZIP after previewing it * import an Event Modeling JSON document * import a Miro board, and read what was skipped ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * Open an event model on the board. The **Import** menu only appears when an event model is open, and what you import is added to that event model. * Importing a Miro board by its link needs your organization to be [connected to Miro](/organization/miro/). Importing from a saved file does not. ## Open the Import menu [Section titled “Open the Import menu”](#open-the-import-menu) Select **Project options** at the top left of the board, then **Import**. app.cratis.studio ![The Project options menu with the Import submenu open, listing Screenplay file, Screenplay folder, Screenplay ZIP, Event Modeling JSON and Import from Miro](/_astro/import-menu.Dg3k5jAd_ZUlG9b.webp) The Import submenu lists every format Studio can read. | Choice | Use it for | | ----------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------- | | **Screenplay file** | A single Cratis Screenplay (`.play`) file. | | **Screenplay folder** | A folder of Screenplay files, merged and read together. | | **Screenplay ZIP** | A ZIP of Screenplay files as one application. Up to 8 MiB. | | **Event Modeling JSON** | An official Event Modeling Specification JSON document. | | **Import from Miro** | A Miro board laid out as an event model. | ## Import a Screenplay file, folder or ZIP [Section titled “Import a Screenplay file, folder or ZIP”](#import-a-screenplay-file-folder-or-zip) [Screenplay](/developers/screenplay-files/) is the text format Studio exports a model to. Studio shows you what it found before anything changes. 1. Choose **Screenplay file**, **Screenplay folder** or **Screenplay ZIP**. 2. Pick the file, folder or ZIP on your computer. A folder is read as one Screenplay application. 3. The **Preview import** dialog compiles the document and shows what would be added. If the compiler found problems, **What the compiler reported** lists them with their line and column. 4. If **Some of the document will not be added** appears, read it. Those parts are left out. 5. Select **Accept & Import**, or **Cancel** to change nothing. When it is done, the dialog says **Import complete**. Sketches, mockups, images and issue links that came with the model are kept too. If they could not be, the dialog says so. A folder with no `.play` files, a folder that cannot be read as one application and a ZIP over the size limit are each refused with a message. ## Import Event Modeling JSON [Section titled “Import Event Modeling JSON”](#import-event-modeling-json) Event Modeling JSON is the official Event Modeling Specification format. To learn the method behind it, see [event modeling](https://www.cratis.io/event-modeling/). 1. Choose **Event Modeling JSON** and pick the `.json` file. 2. Studio starts the import and shows **Import started**. The slices appear on the board as they are added. 3. If the document is not valid, Studio shows **Import failed** with the reasons. Unlike a Screenplay import, JSON has no preview step. Studio checks the document and imports it straight away. ## Import from Miro [Section titled “Import from Miro”](#import-from-miro) **Import from Miro** reads a Miro board laid out as an event model into the board you have open. Studio reads the sticky notes by color: | Miro sticky note | Becomes | | ---------------- | ------------- | | Orange | An event | | Blue | A command | | Green | A read model | | Purple | An automation | | Grey | A screen | You can also say it outright. Text that starts with `Event:`, `Command:`, `Read model:`, `Automation:` or `Screen:` is read as that kind, whatever its color. Each Miro frame becomes a slice, ordered left to right as the timeline. Notes outside any frame are grouped by the column they stand in. 1. Choose **Import from Miro**. 2. Paste the **Miro board link** and select **Read the board**. If your organization has no Miro connection, choose **Read a saved file…** and pick a file of the board’s items saved from Miro’s REST API instead. 3. Studio reports what it found, such as how many slices and elements, and lists everything **Not imported** with a reason. When everything was read, it says so. 4. Select **Import into this board**. The slices appear on the board as they are added. Nothing is imported until you select **Import into this board**. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) Look at the board. Each imported slice is there with its commands, events and read models, or the preview told you why something was left out. Open a few slices and check that the names and fields are the ones you expect. Recap You imported a model from a file, from Event Modeling JSON or from Miro. Next, [open the board](/board/) and tidy it up, [check the model](/board/check-the-model/), or look at how to [keep a board in sync with Git](/deliver/git-sync/) if the source of truth is a repository. To go the other way, see [export](/share/export/).

# Personas

> Describe the people your application is for, with their goals, roles and policies, so every board can speak about them by name.

A **persona** is a kind of person who uses the system: a shopper who browses and orders, a warehouse clerk who ships. It is not an account and not one individual. It is the part someone plays, described once for the whole domain. What you'll learn * add a persona with a description, goals and an image * say which roles and policies the persona holds * use a persona on the event model board, in specifications and in chat app.cratis.studio ![The Personas canvas of the Storefront domain with the Shopper card, showing description, goals, roles and policies](/_astro/personas-storefront.Cs5vQUCc_9blzc.webp) The Shopper persona in the Storefront domain, with the roles it holds and the policies it states. ## Add a persona [Section titled “Add a persona”](#add-a-persona) 1. In the project drawer, expand your domain and select **Personas**. 2. Select **Add persona** in the toolbar. A card named **New persona** appears. 3. Double-click the name and type what the domain calls them, for example `Shopper`. Press Enter. 4. Under **Description**, write who they are in a sentence: *A shopper who browses the catalog and places orders.* Changes on a card are saved when you leave the field. There is no save button. ## Add goals [Section titled “Add goals”](#add-goals) Under the description is **Goals**: what the persona is trying to achieve. They are why the persona exists, and the commands and read models on the domain’s boards are there to serve them. Type a goal into the empty **Add a goal…** field and click away. A fresh empty field appears for the next one. To change a goal, edit its wording. To remove one, empty its field. Goals keep the order you stated them in. ## Add an image [Section titled “Add an image”](#add-an-image) Until you upload one, a persona has a colored circle with its initials. Select **Upload image**, pick a picture and drag the crop over the part you want. The image is saved as a square and appears wherever the persona does, on its card and on the event model board. ## Say what they may do [Section titled “Say what they may do”](#say-what-they-may-do) A persona states what its people are allowed to do with two groups on its card. Neither is created here; both belong to the application, so the card only chooses among them. * **Roles** lists the application’s [roles](/discover/policies-and-roles/) that the persona holds, as chips. Select **Add role** and choose one. Take it away with the cross on its chip. * **Policies** works the same way, with **Add policy**. A policy is a named rule of the application, such as `IsShopper`. Granting or withdrawing a role or a policy here never changes the role or policy itself. Together they answer: *what may this person do?* app.cratis.studio ![The Personas canvas of the Warehouse domain with the WarehouseClerk card and its role and policy chips](/_astro/personas-warehouse.BTswrUok_tJIuX.webp) The WarehouseClerk persona holds the WarehouseClerk role and states three policies. ## Use a persona elsewhere [Section titled “Use a persona elsewhere”](#use-a-persona-elsewhere) * **On the board.** An actor row shows who each slice is for. Open the actor’s badge and choose **Choose persona**. The actor takes the persona’s name and avatar. Choosing **Custom…** goes back to describing the actor by hand. * **In specifications.** A specification can name a persona as its caller instead of listing roles by hand. A specification can also expect a command to be denied, which is how the model says that a warehouse clerk may not place an order. * **In previews and chat.** A screen preview can be shown **As persona**, and a chat bubble on a brainstorming board can speak as one. * **In an exported model.** The domain’s personas travel with the exported Screenplay file, and importing a model brings its personas onto this canvas. ## Arrange and remove [Section titled “Arrange and remove”](#arrange-and-remove) Drag a card by its header to move it, or drag its lower right corner to resize it. **Arrange**, the last tool on the toolbar, lays out every card and note in tidy rows. Sticky notes are for what is not settled: *Does a guest checkout count as a customer?* To remove a persona, use the trash can in its header. You are asked to confirm. The actors that pointed at it stay on the event model but show no persona, and the application’s roles and policies are not changed. Recap You described a persona, gave it goals and linked it to roles and policies. Next, learn how roles and policies are defined in [policies and roles](/discover/policies-and-roles/), or see how [actors and personas](/board/actors-and-personas/) work on the board. [Screens and personas](/event-modeling/screens-and-personas/) explains the idea.

# Policies and roles

> Define the roles of your application and the named rules that say who may do what, once, for the whole application.

Two ideas answer the question *who may do what?* A **role** is something a person holds, such as `Shopper` or `SalesManager`. A **policy** is a named rule that admits some callers and turns the rest away, such as `CanWorkOrders`, which admits a `SalesRep` or a `SalesManager`. Both belong to the whole application, not to one domain. Commands and queries ask for a policy by name, so a rule is written once and every command that needs it points at it. What you'll learn * add the roles of an application * add a policy and say what it requires * decide who may do what with a custom rule when conditions are not enough ## Add roles [Section titled “Add roles”](#add-roles) Roles are managed in the application’s settings. 1. In the project drawer, select the gear on the application row to open **Application Settings**. 2. Open the **Roles** tab. 3. Select **Add Role**, enter a **Name** and a **Description**, and select **Add**. app.cratis.studio ![The Roles tab of Application Settings for Acme Sales with its list of roles and an Add Role button](/_astro/application-settings-roles.DewDQiPt_2rdPH7.webp) The Roles tab of Application Settings. Personas, policies and specifications all choose from this list. To remove a role, select **Remove** beside it and confirm. Roles are used by [personas](/discover/personas/), by policies and by specifications that name a caller’s roles. ## Add a policy [Section titled “Add a policy”](#add-a-policy) Policies live on their own canvas under the application. 1. In the project drawer, select **Policies** under your application. 2. Select **Add Policy**. A card named **NewPolicy** appears. If that name is taken, the new one is numbered, such as **NewPolicy2**. 3. Double-click the name and type what the application calls it, for example `IsSalesManager`. Press Enter. 4. Select **Edit policy**. In the **Requirement** pane, select **Add condition** and choose its type: **Authenticated**, **Role** or **Claim**. 5. Select **Save requirement**. The card shows the requirement the way Screenplay writes it, such as `role "SalesManager"`, so what you read on the canvas is what the model says. A policy with no conditions yet says so, instead of showing an empty box. app.cratis.studio ![The Policies canvas of Acme Sales with purple cards IsAuthenticated, IsShopper, IsMerchandiser, IsSalesRep, IsSalesManager and CanWorkOrders, each showing its requirement](/_astro/policies-sales.CVjx7cQg_Z1oFED.webp) The Policies canvas of Acme Sales. CanWorkOrders admits a SalesRep or a SalesManager. ### Combine conditions [Section titled “Combine conditions”](#combine-conditions) Select **Add group** to combine conditions. Groups are joined by *and* or *or*. A group with more than one condition is shown in parentheses. For example, a policy can admit an accountant, *or* anyone whose `department` claim matches the department of the invoice. ### Decide with custom logic [Section titled “Decide with custom logic”](#decide-with-custom-logic) When a rule is more than conditions can say, a policy can be decided by custom logic instead. The card then shows **C# decides**, and **Edit description and logic** opens an editor with the description beside the code. This is a developer’s task, and most policies need only conditions. ## Use a policy [Section titled “Use a policy”](#use-a-policy) You do not apply a policy on this canvas. Commands and queries (or their feature or module) ask for a policy by name where you model them on the [event model board](/board/commands/). A persona can also state the policies that describe it. ## Arrange and remove [Section titled “Arrange and remove”](#arrange-and-remove) * **Add Sticky Note** puts a note anywhere on the canvas, in any of six colors. * **Arrange**, the last tool in the toolbar, tidies the cards into columns. * To remove a policy, select the cross in its header. You are asked first, because commands and queries may be asking for it by name. Recap You defined roles in the application’s settings and policies on the Policies canvas. Next, see how [personas](/discover/personas/) hold them, or write a [specification](/check/specifications/) that checks a command is denied to the wrong caller. [Roles and permissions](/organization/roles-and-permissions/) is about who may do what in your organization, which is a different thing.

# Start from an existing system

> Use Prologue to observe a running system and propose a first event model from what it actually does.

Most systems are not new. When one already exists, **Prologue** reverse-engineers an event model from what the system does, instead of code archaeology and interviews. You get a starting point that you then open and refine on the event model board. Beta, and a separate tool Prologue is a beta feature. It needs the Studio Prologue tool, which you install and run in your own environment, next to the system you want to understand. Studio does not run it for you. What you'll learn * understand what Prologue observes, and what it never records * start a Prologue and choose what to capture * answer the interpreter’s questions and import the result ## What Prologue observes [Section titled “What Prologue observes”](#what-prologue-observes) As the system runs, Prologue observes its technical shape: the HTTP endpoints that are called, the database tables and columns that are touched, and the telemetry the system emits. It captures **metadata only**: table, column and endpoint names. It never records row values, field contents or any business data. The one setting you control is the OpenTelemetry attribute keys: Prologue records values only for the keys you name. From that evidence, Prologue’s interpreter reconstructs a structured event model: which commands there must be, which facts they produce and how they flow. Where the evidence is ambiguous, the interpreter asks you a question, and your answers refine the model. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * A system you can observe while people use it. The more activity, or the longer Prologue runs, the better the result. * A place to install and run the Studio Prologue tool as a continuous service. * For a database source, the database firewall must allow the address that Prologue connects from. The wizard shows the address. ## Start a Prologue [Section titled “Start a Prologue”](#start-a-prologue) 1. In the project drawer, select **Prologues** under your application. Or choose **Start Prologue** in **Project options**. 2. In the list, select **Start Prologue**. Read the short note on what Prologue captures, then give the Prologue a name. 3. Choose what to capture from. You can pick more than one source: * **Database**: choose **SQL Server** or **PostgreSQL**, enter the connection string and select **Connect**, then choose the tables to include. If Prologue cannot connect, all tables are captured. * **API**: give the **Base path** and optionally the **HTTP Methods** and the paths to exclude. * **OpenTelemetry**: point your OTLP exporter at the Prologue engine. You can limit it to some service names, leave the list empty to capture them all, and say which attribute keys to capture values for. 4. Choose where the captured data is written: **Rolling JSON files** or **API**. 5. On the **Setup** step, download the Studio Prologue tool and its configuration from the links shown, install the tool in your environment and run it as a continuous service. app.cratis.studio ![The Prologues list for Acme Catalog with a Start Prologue button and an empty table with columns Name, Sources, Output and Status](/_astro/prologues-catalog.CVFnJ-9E_cpSP5.webp) The Prologues list. Each Prologue you start appears here, with its sources, its output and its status. ## Follow it through its stages [Section titled “Follow it through its stages”](#follow-it-through-its-stages) The list shows each Prologue with its **Status**: | Status | What it means | | ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------ | | **Recording** | The tool is observing the system. | | **Ready for extraction** | The capture can now be interpreted. | | **Extracting…** | The interpreter is reconstructing a model. | | **Waiting for answers** | The interpreter has questions for you. | | **Extracted** | The model is ready to import. | Select **Start extracting** when it is ready. When the interpreter has questions, the Prologue shows a count and an **Answer questions** button, and Studio tells you when new ones arrive. Each question offers answers to choose from, or **Other** to answer in your own words. **Later** leaves it for now. ## Bring the model in [Section titled “Bring the model in”](#bring-the-model-in) When the Prologue is extracted: * With **Rolling JSON files** output, select **Import results** and choose the capture files the tool produced to bring the results into Studio. The list shows import progress, and **Resume import** if one was interrupted. * Or select **Download Screenplay** to get the model as a [Screenplay file](/developers/screenplay-files/). Open the result on the event model board and refine it like any other model. Recap You started a Prologue, answered its questions and imported a first model from a running system. Next, refine it on the [event model board](/board/), or [import a model](/discover/import-a-model/) you already have as a file. [Talk to your board](/working-with-ai/talk-to-your-board/) is a quick way to ask what an imported model means.

# State machines and workflows

> Draw the stages something goes through in its life, and the steps that run once something starts them, then try both out before anything is built.

A **state machine** describes the stages something goes through in its life, such as an order that is placed, paid, packed, shipped and delivered, or cancelled along the way. A **workflow** describes what happens, step by step, once something starts a run: an order that is paid has to be reserved, picked, shipped and followed up. Where a state machine records *where* something is in its life, a workflow does the work along the way. Newer parts of Studio State machines and workflows are newer canvases than the rest of this section and are still taking shape, so expect their editors to change. The screenshots on this page show empty canvases, which is what you see before you add the first one. What you'll learn * add a state machine, draw its states and say which events move it * start a workflow from an event, a state, a schedule or by hand * try a machine or a workflow out in **Simulate** without touching anything real ## State machines [Section titled “State machines”](#state-machines) A state machine **follows a read model** and moves between states as the domain’s events arrive. It follows the facts and never decides them: an order is not *Shipped* because the machine says so, but because a shipment was dispatched. app.cratis.studio ![The State machines canvas of the Sales domain, empty, with a message that this domain has no state machines yet](/_astro/state-machines-sales.DRSVuTkI_Z2gskIg.webp) The State machines canvas of a domain that has none yet. The message tells you what to do: add one, choose the read model it follows, then draw its states. ### Add a state machine [Section titled “Add a state machine”](#add-a-state-machine) 1. In the project drawer, expand your domain and select **State machines**. 2. Select **Add State Machine** in the toolbar. A card named **NewStateMachine** appears. 3. Double-click the name and call it for what it tracks, such as `Order lifecycle`. 4. Select **Open the state machine editor** on the card and, under **Follows**, choose the read model it follows, such as `Order`. Do this before you draw anything that depends on it, because guards are written over that read model. Each card shows a live thumbnail of its diagram, the read model it follows, its states and the events it reacts to, the workflows it starts and how its specifications fare. ### Draw the states [Section titled “Draw the states”](#draw-the-states) The editor has three tabs besides the generated code: **Design**, **Simulate** and **Used in**. **Design** is where you draw the machine. * Select **+ Add state** for each state the thing can be in: *Placed*, *Paid*, *Packed*, *Shipped*, *Delivered* and *Cancelled*. Each has a name and a description of what being there means. * Exactly one state is the **starting state** (**Starts here**), where something new begins. * States that end the story, such as *Delivered* and *Cancelled*, are marked **Final** (**Mark final**). A final state allows no transitions out of it. ### Say which events move it [Section titled “Say which events move it”](#say-which-events-move-it) A **transition** says: on this event, move from one state to another. For example, a payment-captured event moves *Placed* to *Paid*. To add a transition, drag the dot on a state onto another state, then choose the event under **On event**. You choose events from the domain’s own events. An event that arrives when no transition leaves the current state is ignored, so the machine stays where it is rather than guessing. A transition can have a **guard**, a condition over the read model the machine follows. Select **+ Add a guard** and compare a property with equals, not equals, greater than, less than, is set or is not set. A return event might move *Shipped* to *Refunded* only when the order’s return reason is set. ### Do something on entering a state [Section titled “Do something on entering a state”](#do-something-on-entering-a-state) Select a state and **+ Add what happens** to have entering it send a command or start a workflow. The machine says that something should happen when the thing gets there, and the command or workflow does the work. The workflows a machine starts are listed on its card under **Starts**, and the editor’s **Used in** tab lists them with the state that starts each. ### Try it out [Section titled “Try it out”](#try-it-out) The **Simulate** tab runs the machine without touching anything real. Choose an event and see which state the machine moves to, or why it did not, and say whether a guard holds. Step by step you can walk an order from *Placed* to *Delivered*, or watch a bad sequence get ignored. Select **Save as specification** to keep a simulated run as a **specification**: the events that arrived, the guards that held and the state the machine should end in. Specifications run again every time the machine changes, and the card’s footer reports how they fare. The editor also checks the machine for problems, such as states that can never be reached, dead ends and specifications that no longer hold. The card shows how many problems it found. ## Workflows [Section titled “Workflows”](#workflows) A workflow is a graph of steps. Something starts a run, and each step does its work and leaves through a named **outlet** to the next one. app.cratis.studio ![The Workflows canvas of the Warehouse domain, empty, with the toolbar on the left](/_astro/workflows-warehouse.cCVla8Ha_Kw4r4.webp) The Workflows canvas of the Warehouse domain, which has no workflows yet. ### Add a workflow [Section titled “Add a workflow”](#add-a-workflow) 1. In the project drawer, expand your domain and select **Workflows**. 2. Select **Add Workflow** in the toolbar. A card named **NewWorkflow** appears. 3. Double-click the name and call it for what it does, such as `Fulfill order`. 4. Select **Open the workflow editor** on the card and, under **One run per**, choose the **subject**, a read model such as `Order`. A workflow runs once per subject, so there is one run for each order, and the card says so under **One run per**. ### Choose what starts it [Section titled “Choose what starts it”](#choose-what-starts-it) Every workflow starts with a **trigger**. In the editor, under **Starts**, there are four kinds: * **On an event**: a domain event arrives. * **On a state**: a state machine enters a state. * **On a schedule**: a time comes round. * **By hand**: a person starts a run. A workflow that follows a state machine, such as *Fulfill order* following *Order lifecycle*, starts when an order enters *Paid*. The card names the trigger under **Started by**. ### Add the steps [Section titled “Add the steps”](#add-the-steps) Add steps from the **Add a step** palette. The kinds are: * An **action** sends a command. It can try again when it fails, and it has two outlets: *next*, and *failed* for when the tries run out. * A **decision** chooses a path. You name its outlets yourself, such as *in stock* and *backorder*. * **For each** does the same work for every item in a list, then carries on once every item is done. * A **wait** pauses a run until an event arrives or a time has passed. If the time runs out, the run leaves through *expired* instead of *resumed*. * An **approval** asks a person, for example a warehouse lead, to accept a backorder within a day. Its outlets are *approved*, *rejected* and *expired*. * A **join** is for where branches meet. It waits for all of its branches, for the first one, or for at least a number of them. Give every wait and approval an expiry, so a run that never hears back still ends up somewhere. ### Connect the outlets [Section titled “Connect the outlets”](#connect-the-outlets) Drag from an outlet to the step it should lead to. Every outlet has a name, and a name is a promise about what happened: *next*, *failed*, *approved*, *expired*. A run that leaves through a bad outlet (*failed*, *rejected* or *expired*) with nothing connected to it **fails**. Send those outlets somewhere a person will see them, such as a step that opens a case for support. ### Try it out [Section titled “Try it out”](#try-it-out-1) The **Simulate** tab walks a run through the graph without sending anything. Select **Start a run**, then answer each step the way it could really go, so you can follow the happy path, the backorder or a failure. The editor also looks the workflow over while you draw, for example for steps that are never reached or waits that can last forever. The card reports the number of problems, or **No problems**. ## Arrange and remove [Section titled “Arrange and remove”](#arrange-and-remove) **Arrange**, the last tool on both canvases, lays the cards and notes out in tidy rows. **Add Sticky Note** keeps unsettled questions beside the cards. To remove a state machine or a workflow, use the trash can in its header and confirm. The workflows a removed machine started stay where they are, and runs that are under way are not affected when a workflow goes. Recap You drew a state machine, started a workflow from it and tried both out in **Simulate**. Next, describe the people and rules around them with [policies and roles](/discover/policies-and-roles/), or read about [automations](/board/automations/) on the board. A [specification](/check/specifications/) can check what your model does, and [Automations and translations](/event-modeling/automations-and-translations/) explains the idea.

# Event modeling in Studio

> Event modeling describes a system as a timeline of what people ask for, what happens and what they see afterwards. Here is how Studio's board puts that idea to work.

**Event modeling** is a way to describe how a system behaves by laying the whole story out on one timeline: what people do, what the system records, and what people see afterwards. Studio’s [event model board](/board/) is where a team draws that timeline together. You do not need to know anything about software design to read one. If you can follow a storyboard, you can follow an event model. ## Why teams model this way [Section titled “Why teams model this way”](#why-teams-model-this-way) Most misunderstandings in a project are not about code. They are about what the business actually does, and in which order. An event model gives product owners, domain experts, designers and developers one picture that all of them can check. * **Everyone speaks the same words.** Things on the board are named in the language of the business: *Add to basket*, *Order placed*. * **The whole behavior is visible.** Nothing hides in a hallway conversation or a developer’s head. If something happens in the system, it is on the board. * **Gaps show early.** A screen that shows data nothing ever records, or a step nobody triggers, stands out on the timeline long before anyone builds it. * **The model can be checked.** Each piece of behavior can carry examples, and Studio can [run the model](/event-modeling/specifications-and-play/) to see how it behaves. ## The shape of a board [Section titled “The shape of a board”](#the-shape-of-a-board) A board reads **left to right, as time passes**. A shopper opens a page, then puts something in a basket, then checks out. Each step lands to the right of the one before. Everything on the board is one of a few kinds of element, and each has its own color in Studio: | Element | Color | What it is | How it is named | | --------------- | ------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------- | | **Command** | Blue | Something a person or the system *asks for*. It can be refused. | An instruction: *AddToBasket* | | **Event** | Orange | Something that *happened*. A fact that is never taken back. | Past tense: *BasketLineAdded* | | **Read model** | Green | What someone *looks at* afterwards, built from events. | A noun: *BasketContents* | | **Sticky note** | Yellow | A question, a risk or a thought that has no home in the model yet. | Plain words | Screens, automations and translations also appear on the board, and later pages explain them. The first three are the heart of it: a command goes in, an event comes out, and a read model shows the result. Here is one step of the Acme Commerce shop, the sample project that this documentation uses throughout. A shopper adds a mug to their basket: The shopper presses a button on a screen, which asks for **AddToBasket**. The shop accepts the request and records the fact **BasketLineAdded**. The basket page then shows **BasketContents**, which is built from everything that has been added. Rows on the board belong to *who* does the work. A row for the Shopper holds what the shopper sees and asks for, and the system has a row of its own for the things it does without being asked. Events sit in a row of their own, so the facts of the system read as one running history. Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram A piece of the Storefront board. Each column is one slice; the rows say who acts, what is asked for or shown, and what happened. ## How Studio’s board maps to the method [Section titled “How Studio’s board maps to the method”](#how-studios-board-maps-to-the-method) Studio follows the event modeling method closely. It adds the things a team needs to keep a model alive: roles, checks and a way to carry on to working software. | In the method | In Studio | | -------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | The timeline | The board, read left to right | | A step in the story | A [slice](/event-modeling/slices/): one column with one behavior | | Swimlanes for people and systems | Rows that belong to [actors](/event-modeling/screens-and-personas/) | | Screens and wireframes | [Screens](/event-modeling/screens-and-personas/) attached to slices, and the design canvas | | Grouping a big model | [Modules and features](/event-modeling/modules-and-features/) | | Examples that prove a step works | [Specifications](/event-modeling/specifications-and-play/) written as Given, When, Then | | Checking that nothing is missing | **Findings** that point at gaps, such as a slice with no specification | | Systems reacting to each other | [Automations and translations](/event-modeling/automations-and-translations/) | ## One more example: from basket to order [Section titled “One more example: from basket to order”](#one-more-example-from-basket-to-order) A model is more than one step. This is the Storefront’s checkout, where the shopper confirms the order and the rest of the shop reacts: The shopper places the order and the shop records that checkout completed. The basket then empties by itself, and the shop announces to the rest of the company that an order was placed. Nobody pressed a button for those last two steps. ## Go deeper [Section titled “Go deeper”](#go-deeper) This section explains the ideas. The how-to pages show where to click. [Commands, events and read models ](/event-modeling/commands-events-and-read-models/)The three things everything on a board is built from. [Slices ](/event-modeling/slices/)The four kinds of behavior a board can hold. [Work on the board ](/board/)Add modules, slices and cards, step by step. [Discover your domain ](/discover/)Start from a brainstorm instead of a blank board. For the method itself, its history and the thinking behind it, read the [event modeling guide on cratis.io](https://www.cratis.io/event-modeling/). New terms are explained in the [glossary](/reference/glossary/).

# Automations and translations

> How a model describes work the system does on its own, and how separate areas of a business tell each other what happened without sharing data.

Not everything in a business starts with a person pressing a button. Orders are confirmed automatically, reminders go out on a schedule, and one department reacts to what another did. An event model has two ways to describe this: **automations**, where the system reacts, and **translations**, where facts cross the border between two areas. ## Automations: the system reacts [Section titled “Automations: the system reacts”](#automations-the-system-reacts) An [automation](/reference/glossary/#automation) watches for a fact and acts on it. In Acme Commerce, when checkout completes, the shop empties the basket: No shopper asked for that, so the automation has no screen and no person in front of it. Its row on the board belongs to the module’s **System Actor**, the cogwheel, which stands for the system acting on its own. Because the automation leaves a fact of its own, even unprompted work shows up in the history like everything else. ### What starts an automation [Section titled “What starts an automation”](#what-starts-an-automation) Studio lets you choose the trigger: | Trigger | Means | | ---------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Event** | Run when a specific fact happens. | | **Timer** | Run on a fixed schedule. | | **Custom** | Describe in words when it should run, and let generation use the description. | ### A to-do list for the system [Section titled “A to-do list for the system”](#a-to-do-list-for-the-system) Event modelers often explain an automation as a **to-do list**. A read model lists what is waiting, such as orders that need a confirmation email, and the automation works through that list one item at a time. The idea helps when you are unsure whether the system has really handled something: if the item is still on the list, it has not. Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram Acme Purchasing's replenishment. ReplenishmentQueue is the to-do list: a request that still says requested has not been handled yet. ### When the command is refused [Section titled “When the command is refused”](#when-the-command-is-refused) An automation usually carries out a command, and a command can be refused. The model can say what then happens, separately for each kind of refusal: any refusal, a failed validation, a broken constraint or a missing permission. For each, the automation can acknowledge the refusal and move on, or record an event of its own. ## Translations: facts cross a border [Section titled “Translations: facts cross a border”](#translations-facts-cross-a-border) Two areas of the business should not reach into each other’s data. The Storefront must not look in the Catalog’s tables for product information, and Sales must not look in the Storefront’s basket. They tell each other what happened instead, and each area turns what it hears into its own words. That is a [translation](/reference/glossary/#translation). Studio’s slice type for this is called **Translator**. ### Public events: what an area promises the world [Section titled “Public events: what an area promises the world”](#public-events-what-an-area-promises-the-world) A private event is nobody’s business outside its area. An event can be made **public**, which makes it a contract that the application publishes. The Acme Commerce shop’s five parts talk to each other only through such public events. ### Coming in [Section titled “Coming in”](#coming-in) When the Catalog publishes a product, the Storefront hears it and records **ProductListed** in its own words, with its own fields and its own meaning. The slice **ListProduct** does this: On the board, you reference the outside fact, and then either add a command that carries it out or capture the outside data as facts of your own. ### Going out [Section titled “Going out”](#going-out) The reverse happens when the shop has something to say. After checkout, the shop’s private **CheckoutCompleted** is turned into the public **OrderPlaced**, which Sales picks up: A projection maps the private fact into the public shape, so the shop decides exactly what it reveals. ### Why bother? [Section titled “Why bother?”](#why-bother) * **Each area can change independently.** As long as the public event keeps its shape, the Storefront can rework its basket without Sales noticing. * **Each area keeps its own language.** Sales may call it an *order* and the warehouse a *shipment*. Translations are where those words are reconciled. * **The flow stays visible.** The board shows which area depends on which fact, and the application’s context map draws the same picture for whole domains. ## Between modules and between applications [Section titled “Between modules and between applications”](#between-modules-and-between-applications) The same idea works at two sizes. Two **modules** on one board can be connected with a line, and a slice in one can reference an event of the other. Two **applications**, such as Acme Catalog and Acme Shop, talk through public events and are drawn together on the context map. Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram Acme Sales tells Acme Warehouse that an order is confirmed through a public event. Each side translates it into its own words. ## Put it to work [Section titled “Put it to work”](#put-it-to-work) * [Add an automation](/board/automations/) or [a translation](/board/translations/) on the board * [Check them with specifications](/check/specifications/) * [Slices](/event-modeling/slices/)

# Commands, events and read models

> The three things every event model is built from - what is asked for, what happened, and what people look at afterwards.

Everything on an [event model board](/board/) is a **command**, an **event** or a **read model**. A command asks for something, an event records that it happened, and a read model shows the result. This page uses the Acme Commerce shop’s basket to show how the three fit together. ## Commands: what someone asks for [Section titled “Commands: what someone asks for”](#commands-what-someone-asks-for) A [command](/reference/glossary/#command) is an **intent**. A shopper wants to put a mug in their basket, so the shop receives the command **AddToBasket**, with the product and the quantity the shopper chose. Commands are blue on the board. Because a command is only a request, it can be **refused**. Before anything is recorded, the shop checks: * **Rules on the values.** A quantity must be a number. An email address must look like one. * **Rules about the situation.** **AddToBasket** is refused with the message *That product is out of stock* when the product cannot be bought. **CheckOut** is refused when the basket is empty. * **Constraints.** Some things must never happen twice, such as two customer accounts with the same email address. A [constraint](/reference/glossary/#constraint) is how the model says so. * **Permission.** A [policy](/reference/glossary/#policy) says who may ask. See [Screens and personas](/event-modeling/screens-and-personas/). A refused command leaves no trace in the history. Nothing happened, so there is nothing to record. Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram A command is a request. Accepted, it leaves a fact behind; refused, it leaves nothing. ## Events: what happened [Section titled “Events: what happened”](#events-what-happened) An [event](/reference/glossary/#event) is a **fact**. Once the shop accepts **AddToBasket**, it records **BasketLineAdded**: this product, this quantity, this price, in this basket. Events are orange on the board. Events follow three habits: * **They are in the past tense.** *Basket line added*, not *Add basket line*. The name says what was true, not what someone wanted. * **They are never changed or taken back.** If the shopper changes their mind, the model records a new fact, such as **BasketLineRemoved**, and leaves the old one where it is. * **They carry what is needed and nothing else.** Each event says one thing. When two different things can happen, they are two events. Taken together, the events of a system are its memory. Everything the system knows is something that happened, in order. That is why you can ask later *how did we get here?* and get a real answer. ### Private and public events [Section titled “Private and public events”](#private-and-public-events) Most events are private: they are the business of one area of the shop. An event can be made **public**, which makes it a promise to the rest of the company. When checkout completes, the Storefront publishes **OrderPlaced** so that Sales can pick it up. See [Automations and translations](/event-modeling/automations-and-translations/). ## Read models: what people look at [Section titled “Read models: what people look at”](#read-models-what-people-look-at) A [read model](/reference/glossary/#read-model) is what someone sees afterwards: the basket page, an order list, a product catalog. Read models are green on the board. A read model is not a store of its own. It is **derived** from events. A [projection](/reference/glossary/#projection) says how: when **BasketLineAdded** happens, add a line to **BasketContents**. When **BasketLineRemoved** happens, take it away. This has two consequences worth knowing: * **A read model is never the source of truth.** The events are. If a read model looks wrong, the fix is to change how it is built, and Studio can rebuild it from the events. * **You can have as many read models as you need.** The basket page, a count in the top bar and a checkout summary can all be built from the same events, each shaped for what it is shown on. A [query](/reference/glossary/#query) is how a screen asks a read model a question, such as *the basket with this id* or *products in this category*. ## Putting the three together [Section titled “Putting the three together”](#putting-the-three-together) Read a slice from top to bottom and you see the whole loop: someone asks, something happens, someone sees the result. The shopper enters an address, the shop records that the address was entered, and the checkout page shows the progress so far. A [slice](/event-modeling/slices/) is exactly one such loop. ## The words a model speaks in [Section titled “The words a model speaks in”](#the-words-a-model-speaks-in) Two more ideas keep the loop honest, and both belong to a whole [domain](/reference/glossary/#domain) rather than to a single slice: * **Concepts** are the typed values the business talks about: a quantity, an order number, an email address. A concept can carry its own rules, so the rule is written once and holds everywhere the value appears. A concept can also be marked as personal information. * **Constraints** are the things that must never happen twice. Both have their own canvases in a domain. See [Discover your domain](/discover/). ## Put it to work [Section titled “Put it to work”](#put-it-to-work) * [Add commands on the board](/board/commands/), [events](/board/events/) and [read models](/board/read-models/) * [Discover your domain](/discover/) * [Slices](/event-modeling/slices/)

# Modules and features

> How Studio organizes a model, from an organization's projects down to the slices on a board.

A real system has too many behaviors to draw on one flat timeline. Studio organizes them in layers, so that a team can find its way and so that each part of the business keeps its own space. This page explains the layers from the outside in. ## The layers [Section titled “The layers”](#the-layers) | Layer | What it is | Acme Commerce example | | ------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------- | | [Organization](/reference/glossary/#organization) | Your company or team in Studio. It holds the people, the AI setup and the billing. | Cratis | | [Project](/reference/glossary/#project) | The top level of the work, typically one per team. Everything the team builds lives inside it. | Acme Commerce | | [Application](/reference/glossary/#application) | One system the team builds. | Acme Shop | | [Domain](/reference/glossary/#domain) | One area of the business inside an application. It owns the boards and canvases that describe that area. | Storefront | | Event model | The [board](/board/) itself: the timeline of that domain. | Storefront | | [Module](/reference/glossary/#module) | A whole area of the business inside a board. | Shop | | [Feature](/reference/glossary/#feature) | A group of related behavior inside a module. Features can nest. | Basket, Checkout | | [Slice](/event-modeling/slices/) | One behavior. | AddToBasket | Acme Commerce has five applications, one for each part of the company: **Acme Catalog**, **Acme Shop**, **Acme Sales**, **Acme Purchasing** and **Acme Warehouse**. They never share a database. They only tell each other what happened, through [public events](/event-modeling/automations-and-translations/). Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram From the organization down to one slice. Each layer holds the one below it. ## What a domain owns [Section titled “What a domain owns”](#what-a-domain-owns) A domain is more than a board. It is the home of everything that describes one area of the business: * **Event models**: the timelines. * **Brainstorming boards**: free-form boards for the early, messy thinking. * **Personas**: the people who use the area. See [Screens and personas](/event-modeling/screens-and-personas/). * **Concepts** and **constraints**: the typed values and the things that must never happen twice. * **State machines** and **workflows**: the stages something goes through, and what happens step by step once something starts. * **Event sources**: the kinds of thing the domain keeps a history of, such as a basket or an order. * **Given contexts** and **seed data**: named starting situations for [specifications](/event-modeling/specifications-and-play/) and the sample data for a running model. Some things belong to the whole application rather than to one domain: the **policies** that say who may do what, the **UI profile** that decides how the application looks, the **design canvas** with all its screens, and the application-wide list of events. The **context map** shows how the domains of an application relate through the events they publish and consume. ## Modules: areas of the business on a board [Section titled “Modules: areas of the business on a board”](#modules-areas-of-the-business-on-a-board) Inside a board, a module is one area of the business. The Storefront’s board has a module called **Shop**. If the board also described, say, payments, payments would get a module of its own beside it. The rule of thumb is short: **different area, different module.** Two modules share nothing except events. A module can announce a fact, and another can reference it, so you can see a flow cross from one area to the next without one reaching into the other. ## Features: related behavior [Section titled “Features: related behavior”](#features-related-behavior) A feature groups the slices that belong together. The Shop module holds the features **Browse**, **Basket**, **Checkout** and **My orders**. A feature can contain sub-features when it grows too big to read. Features are also where a team tracks progress and focus: * A feature can be raised to *Draft*, *Analysis* or *Ready for implementation*. Working statuses such as *In progress* are set on each slice. * You can **focus on a feature**, so that only it is drawn and the rest of the board stays out of the way. * A feature can be **assigned**, and its content can be generated or suggested for in one go. ## Reading a big board [Section titled “Reading a big board”](#reading-a-big-board) A large model is read in layers too. Start with the modules to see the areas. Open a feature to see its behaviors. Read one slice to see exactly what happens. The **Overview** detail level and the focus view exist so that you never have to see everything at once. ## Put it to work [Section titled “Put it to work”](#put-it-to-work) * [Find your way around projects, applications and domains](/get-started/find-your-way-around/) * [Add modules and features on the board](/board/modules-and-features/) * [Model your first behavior](/get-started/first-event-model/)

# Screens and personas

> Who uses the system, what they see and who is allowed to do what - the people side of an event model.

An event model is not only about data. It also says **who** does each thing and **what they see** while they do it. This page explains the three ideas that carry that: actors, screens and policies. ## Actors: who does it [Section titled “Actors: who does it”](#actors-who-does-it) Every row on a board belongs to an [actor](/reference/glossary/#actor), so that the board always shows *who* does what. Studio has three kinds: | Kind | What it represents | | ---------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **UI Actor** | A person using screens. | | **System Actor** | The system acting on its own. Each module has one, and it owns the module’s [automations](/event-modeling/automations-and-translations/). | | **Chat Actor** | A conversation with an AI agent, given a system prompt. | In the Storefront, the UI actor is the **Shopper**. The shopper’s row holds the screens they see and the commands they ask for. The system’s row holds what the shop does without being asked, such as emptying the basket after checkout. ## Personas: a person, not a job title [Section titled “Personas: a person, not a job title”](#personas-a-person-not-a-job-title) A [persona](/reference/glossary/#persona) describes one kind of person in the domain: who they are, what they want to get done, and which [roles](/reference/glossary/#role) they hold. A UI actor on the board can be given a persona, which ties the row to a real person’s goals. Acme Commerce has six personas: | Persona | Part of the business | What they do | | ------------------ | -------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Shopper** | Storefront | Browses, fills a basket, checks out and follows orders. | | **Merchandiser** | Catalog | Curates the catalog: decides what is sold, at what price and when a product is withdrawn. | | **SalesRep** | Sales | Works incoming orders and may discount up to 15%. | | **SalesManager** | Sales | Approves what a rep may not, and sees the pipeline. | | **Buyer** | Purchasing | Chooses suppliers, submits purchase orders and receives the goods. | | **WarehouseClerk** | Warehouse | Reserves, picks, packs, dispatches and counts stock. | Personas are useful far beyond the board. [Specifications](/event-modeling/specifications-and-play/) can be written as a persona, a [screen preview](/screens/) can show a screen as that persona sees it, and a brainstorming conversation can speak as a persona. ## Screens: what they see [Section titled “Screens: what they see”](#screens-what-they-see) A [screen](/reference/glossary/#screen) is what a person looks at and clicks. In an event model, a screen is tied to a slice in one of two ways: * On a **state change**, the screen is where the command is asked for. A button on the screen issues it. * On a **state view**, the screen shows what the read model holds. The **Checkout** screen shows the draft built so far, and its buttons ask for the next steps. The board notices when a screen and a model do not fit: a command that no screen issues, a screen with nothing behind it, or a field that the read model does not hold. Screens can be drawn in several ways, from a rough sketch to a working prototype: * **Sketches, mockups and images** capture an idea quickly. * A **prototype** is a real screen built from components, filled with data from the model. * A **template** gives a whole module or feature a common frame, so screens look like they belong to the same application. * The application’s **UI profile** decides how all of this looks: the component libraries, the styling and the ready-made blueprints. Studio can **preview** a screen with sample data, so that a team can see the result before anything is built. Read more in [Design screens](/screens/). ## Policies: who may do what [Section titled “Policies: who may do what”](#policies-who-may-do-what) A [policy](/reference/glossary/#policy) is a named rule that admits some callers and turns the rest away. Policies belong to the whole application. A command or a query, or a whole feature or module, asks for a policy by name. A policy states a requirement, such as *callers must hold the SalesManager role*, and can add custom logic when a requirement needs more than roles or claims. The application’s [roles](/reference/glossary/#role) and identity details are defined in the application’s settings, and personas hold the roles. Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram In Acme Sales, RejectOrder asks for the policy IsSalesManager. The same request has a different outcome for each persona. Studio uses these rules everywhere a model is examined: * A specification can name who is calling, and expect a command to be **denied**. * **See as a caller** shows which actions and data each screen offers a given caller. * A preview **as a persona** hides or disables what the persona’s policies do not allow, and says why. * A finding points at a command that reads recorded state but that nothing authorizes. The model describes, the server decides What a screen offers a caller is a preview of the rules. When the application runs, the server always makes the final decision. ## Put it to work [Section titled “Put it to work”](#put-it-to-work) * [Describe personas](/discover/personas/) and [policies](/discover/policies-and-roles/) * [Design screens](/screens/) * [Put an actor on the board](/board/actors-and-personas/)

# Slices

> A slice is one behavior of the system, drawn as one column. There are four kinds - state change, state view, automation and translation.

A [slice](/reference/glossary/#slice) is **exactly one behavior** of the system, drawn as one column on the board. Everything needed for that behavior sits in the column: the screen, the command, the event, the read model and the examples that prove it works. Slicing the model this way has a practical payoff. A slice is small enough for one person to understand, to discuss with the business, to give to a builder or an AI agent, and to check on its own. You can move a slice, [copy it](/board/copy-and-paste/), mark it done or block it without touching the rest. Studio offers four kinds of slice. When you add a slice, Studio asks you to choose a vertical slice type. | Kind in Studio | What it does | Acme Commerce example | | ---------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------- | | **State Change** | A person asks for something, and facts are recorded. | **AddToBasket** | | **State View** | A person looks at something built from earlier facts. | **BasketView** | | **Automation** | The system reacts to a fact and acts on it. | **EmptyBasketAfterCheckout** | | **Translator** | Facts cross the border between two areas. | **ListProduct**, **PublishOrder** | Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram The four kinds of slice. Read each one from top to bottom. ## State change: someone asks, something happens [Section titled “State change: someone asks, something happens”](#state-change-someone-asks-something-happens) Studio describes a state change as *UI drives a command that produces events*. This is the most familiar slice: the shopper presses **Add to basket**, the shop checks the request and records what happened. The command is where the rules live. **AddToBasket** reads what the shop knows about the product and refuses it when it is out of stock. A state change slice is also where you ask what a command must know, what it may refuse and which facts it leaves behind. Read more in [Commands, events and read models](/event-modeling/commands-events-and-read-models/). ## State view: someone looks at something [Section titled “State view: someone looks at something”](#state-view-someone-looks-at-something) A state view is described as *UI reads a projection built from events*. It changes nothing. It turns the facts that already happened into something a person can read. **BasketView** builds the basket from every line that was added, changed or removed, and the **Basket** screen shows it. A view can only show what earlier slices record. Studio helps you keep that honest: if you drag a view to the left of the events it depends on, the board refuses the drop and says that those events are not produced before this position. ## Automation: the system reacts [Section titled “Automation: the system reacts”](#automation-the-system-reacts) An automation is *the system reacts to events and issues commands*. No person is involved, so there is no screen: it watches for a fact and acts. When checkout completes, **EmptyBasketAfterCheckout** empties the basket. An automation can start from an event, from a timer, or from a custom description of when it should run.
