Read more in [Automations and translations](/event-modeling/automations-and-translations/). ## Translation: facts cross a border [Section titled “Translation: facts cross a border”](#translation-facts-cross-a-border) Studio calls this slice type **Translator**: it *translates external events into internal commands*, and the same idea works the other way round. It is how one area of the business tells another what happened, without either reaching into the other. **ListProduct** hears that the Catalog published a product and records it in the shop’s own words. **PublishOrder** does the reverse: it turns the shop’s private fact that checkout completed into a public **OrderPlaced** for the rest of the company. ## A slice grows through its life [Section titled “A slice grows through its life”](#a-slice-grows-through-its-life) A slice has a **status**, from *Not started* through *Draft*, *Analysis* and *Ready for implementation* to *In progress*, *Ready for review* and *Done*, with *Blocked* and *Waiting for user interface* for the times it cannot move. The status is how a team sees progress across a whole model. The full list and what each means is in [Statuses and colors](/reference/statuses-and-colors/). ## Put it to work [Section titled “Put it to work”](#put-it-to-work) * [Add and manage slices on the board](/board/slices/) * [Check a slice with specifications](/check/specifications/) * [Track how far each slice has come](/deliver/track-progress/)

# Specifications and Play

> How a team checks an event model before anything is built - with examples written as Given, When, Then, and by running the model as a working application.

A model that nobody can check is only a drawing. Studio gives you two ways to check one: [specifications](/reference/glossary/#specification), which are examples of a single behavior, and [Play](/reference/glossary/#play), which runs the whole model as an application. ## Specifications: examples that prove a slice [Section titled “Specifications: examples that prove a slice”](#specifications-examples-that-prove-a-slice) A specification is a concrete example, written in three parts that read like a sentence: [Given, When, Then](/reference/glossary/#given-when-then). | Part | Question it answers | Acme Commerce example | | --------- | -------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | **Given** | What was already true? | The Acme mug is listed in the shop and 9 are available. The caller is a signed-in shopper. | | **When** | What happens? | The shopper asks to add 2 mugs to their basket. | | **Then** | What should be the result? | The basket gets a line of 2 mugs at 49, and the basket page shows it. | Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram A specification reads top to bottom, like a sentence: given, when, then. Because the example uses real values, nobody has to guess what a rule means. Two specifications on the same slice often tell the whole story. A second one for **AddToBasket** starts from a product with none available, asks for the same mug and expects the refusal *That product is out of stock*. ### What the three parts can hold [Section titled “What the three parts can hold”](#what-the-three-parts-can-hold) * **Given** can be facts that already happened, the state a read model is in, or a named starting situation called a [given context](/reference/glossary/#given-context) that several specifications share. Given can also say who is calling. * **When** is the thing that triggers the slice: a command for a state change, an event for an automation or a translation, or a query for a view. * **Then** is what must come out: events, the state of read models, the result of a query, or exactly one error. A specification can also say that **nothing** should happen. A specification states either success or a refusal. Mixing the two in one example is not allowed, because it would not be clear what you are checking. ### Who is calling [Section titled “Who is calling”](#who-is-calling) A specification can name the caller: nobody in particular, anonymous, authenticated, a particular [persona](/reference/glossary/#persona) or a set of roles. It can also expect the command, or the query, to be **denied**. That is how a model proves that a rule about permission holds. ### Where they come from [Section titled “Where they come from”](#where-they-come-from) You can write specifications by hand, or ask Studio to generate them with AI from the slice. Either way, a person reviews them: a generated example is only a proposal until the team agrees that it describes the business correctly. Studio runs a slice’s specifications in a sandbox, so a run changes nothing real. Each specification shows its own result: | Result | Means | | ----------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Not run** | It has not been run yet. | | **Running…** | The run is in progress. | | **Last run succeeded** | The model does what the example says. | | **Last run failed** | The model does something else. The result shows the difference. | | **Last run was inconclusive** | The run could not decide. | | **Could not run** | The run did not start, for example because the model has a problem. | Studio also looks for gaps. A **finding** flags a slice that has no specifications at all. To write and run specifications, read [Check a slice with specifications](/check/specifications/). ## Play: run the model [Section titled “Play: run the model”](#play-run-the-model) Specifications check one behavior at a time. **Play** runs the whole event model as if it were the finished application, so that people can use it before anything is built. Play starts a sandbox from the model and gives you four ways to look at it: * **Frontend**: the screens, so you can click through the model as a user. * **Backend API**: the interface that the screens talk to. * **Chronicle Workbench**: the events of your session, as they are recorded. * **Read models**: what each read model holds at the moment. Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram Play runs the model. Everything in the sandbox is gone when the session ends. Click through a checkout and you can watch the events appear and the read models fill in. The sandbox also shows when something is refused and why, for example because the caller is **Denied (403)** or the **Validation refused (400)** the request. Play is a rehearsal, not a deployment Play runs in a sandbox. Its data lives only for the session and is gone afterwards. Nothing you do in Play reaches a real system. ### Play and Preview [Section titled “Play and Preview”](#play-and-preview) They are different tools for different questions. **Preview** draws a single screen with sample data, so a designer can judge how it looks and how it behaves for a persona. **Play** runs the whole model, so a team can judge whether the behavior is right. To run a model, read [Play an event model](/check/play/). ## Why both [Section titled “Why both”](#why-both) Specifications are precise and cheap, and they keep a model honest as it changes. Play is broad and convincing: it lets a product owner see the thing working. Together, they let a team find a misunderstanding on the board, where fixing it costs a conversation instead of a rewrite. The specifications also stay with the slice when it is built: a build reports where its own results and the board’s run of the specifications disagree. See [Build and deliver](/deliver/). ## Put it to work [Section titled “Put it to work”](#put-it-to-work) * [Write and run specifications](/check/specifications/) * [Generate specifications with AI](/check/generate-specifications/) * [Play an event model](/check/play/)

# What is Cratis Studio

> Cratis Studio is where a team draws how a system behaves, checks it with examples, tries it out and carries it on to working software. Start here to see what it does and where to go first.

**Cratis Studio** is a workspace where a team describes how a system should behave, together, before anyone builds it. You lay the behavior out on one big board, check it with examples, try it out as a running application, and then carry it on to working software. app.cratis.studio ![The Storefront event model of the Acme Commerce sample: columns of slices with screens, blue commands, orange events and green read models](/_astro/storefront-board.Cocj6HLG_AQgEy.webp) An event model on Studio's board. It reads left to right, like time. Each column is one behavior of the system. You do not need to be a developer to read this board, or to work on it. If you can follow a storyboard, you can follow an event model. ## Who it is for [Section titled “Who it is for”](#who-it-is-for) Studio is made for the whole team: product owners, domain experts, business analysts, designers and developers. They all work on the same picture, at the same time, and everyone sees what the others change. * **Product owners and domain experts** put the business into words: what happens, in what order, and what the rules are. * **Analysts and designers** add the screens people will see and the examples that prove each step works. * **Developers** check that the model is precise enough to build from, then build it by hand, with a builder, or with an AI agent. ## What a team does in Studio [Section titled “What a team does in Studio”](#what-a-team-does-in-studio) Most teams move through the same few stages. You can start at any of them, and go back whenever you learn something. Swipe sideways to see the whole diagram The stages a team moves through. The cards below say where each one happens. 1\. Think it through Capture everything you can think of on a [brainstorming board](/discover/brainstorming/), then sort it into regions and turn it into the start of a model. 2\. Draw how it behaves Put the behavior on the [event model board](/board/): what people ask for, what the system records and what people see afterwards. 3\. Design the screens Give each step a [screen](/screens/), so everyone sees what the person at the keyboard sees. 4\. Check and try it Write [examples](/check/) that say what should happen, and run the whole model as a small working application. 5\. Build and deliver Hand the model on, track how far along each piece is, and generate or build the software. See [Build and deliver](/deliver/). 6\. Share it Export the model, share a link to a snapshot of it, or keep it in step with a Git repository. See [Share and export](/share/). ## The main ideas, in plain words [Section titled “The main ideas, in plain words”](#the-main-ideas-in-plain-words) A few ideas carry the whole product. They are explained properly in [Event modeling](/event-modeling/); here is the short version. **A system is a story told in time.** You describe what happens in the order it happens. A shopper opens the basket, adds a mug, and later checks out. Each step lands to the right of the one before. **Every step is one of three things.** Something a person or the system *asks for* is a **command**, and it can be refused. Something that *happened* is an **event**: a fact that is never taken back. Something you *look at afterwards* is a **read model**, built from the facts. Here is one step of the Acme Commerce shop: **One step is one slice.** A column on the board, with one behavior, is called a [slice](/event-modeling/slices/). Related slices are grouped into [features](/event-modeling/modules-and-features/), and features into modules: one module for each area of your business. **Your work is organized in a project.** A project holds one or more **applications**, the systems your team builds. Each application holds **domains**, the areas of the business, and each domain holds its boards. You will meet this structure in [Find your way around](/get-started/find-your-way-around/). **Examples make the model trustworthy.** Each slice can carry a few examples in the form *given these facts, when this happens, then this should follow*. Studio runs them for you, so a passing example means the model does what you said. **You can try it before it exists.** Studio can run the model as an application you can click through, so you see how it behaves while it is still cheap to change. app.cratis.studio ![The brainstorming board of Acme Commerce zoomed out: a large cloud of orange events and yellow sticky notes, sorted into columns by area](/_astro/big-picture-brainstorming.nVkk0Y53_FftNf.webp) Before the model comes the storm. A brainstorming board collects everything the team can think of, then sorts it. app.cratis.studio ![The Products screen of Acme Shop with the shop logo and product cards with prices](/_astro/screen-design.NN5kwhZd_25FxVf.webp) Screens belong to the model too, so the team can see what a person sees at each step. ## Where AI fits [Section titled “Where AI fits”](#where-ai-fits) Studio can use AI to help you model, write examples and build. It does not come with a language model of its own: your organization connects its own AI provider and gives its agents a model, and until then the AI features stay switched off. Everything in [Model your first behavior](/get-started/first-event-model/) works without AI. ## Where to go next [Section titled “Where to go next”](#where-to-go-next) [Sign up and start your trial ](/get-started/sign-up-and-trial/)Create your organization, or join one you were invited to. [Find your way around ](/get-started/find-your-way-around/)The top bar, the project drawer and the places you will spend your time. [Explore the Acme Commerce sample ](/get-started/explore-the-sample/)Read a finished model, left to right, to see what good looks like. [Model your first behavior ](/get-started/first-event-model/)A fifteen-minute tutorial: from an empty project to a model you have run. [Take the guided tours ](/get-started/guided-tours/)Let Studio walk you through a board, narrated or at your own pace. [Event modeling in Studio ](/event-modeling/)The ideas behind the board, with no steps to follow.

# Explore the Acme Commerce sample

> Read a finished event model, step by step, to see what a good model looks like before you build your own.

The quickest way to learn what an event model is, is to read a good one. **Acme Commerce** is a model of a mid-size online shop, and every example in this documentation comes from it. This page walks you through it in the order a newcomer would read it, and tells you what to notice. ## What the sample contains [Section titled “What the sample contains”](#what-the-sample-contains) Acme Commerce is one project with five applications, one for each area of the shop. They never share a database. They talk to each other only through a few public facts, such as a product being published. That is how real systems with several teams stay manageable. | Application | Domain | What it covers | | --------------- | ---------- | ----------------------------------------------------- | | Acme Catalog | Catalog | Products, the price list and the product lifecycle | | Acme Shop | Storefront | Browsing, the basket, checkout and my orders | | Acme Sales | Sales | Order intake, approval, confirmation and order status | | Acme Purchasing | Purchasing | Suppliers, offers, replenishment and purchase orders | | Acme Warehouse | Warehouse | Reservation, receiving, picking and dispatch | Six personas, the people the system is for, appear on its boards: Shopper, Merchandiser, SalesRep, SalesManager, Buyer and WarehouseClerk. app.cratis.studio ![The project drawer with Acme Commerce expanded to show its five applications, Acme Shop opened to show its rows](/_astro/drawer-acme-commerce.B3pLGL21_Z4YRzq.webp) Acme Commerce in the drawer: one project, five applications. ## Read the Storefront board [Section titled “Read the Storefront board”](#read-the-storefront-board) Start where a customer starts. In the drawer, open **Acme Shop** > **Domains** > **Storefront** > **Event Models** > **Storefront**. 1. **Stand back.** The board opens zoomed out so you can see its shape. Each column is a [slice](/event-modeling/slices/): one behavior. Blue cards are commands, orange are events, green are read models. app.cratis.studio ![The Storefront board zoomed out: the Basket feature with several slices, each a column with a screen, a blue command, an orange event, and examples underneath](/_astro/storefront-board.Cocj6HLG_AQgEy.webp) The Storefront board zoomed out. The Basket feature is on the left; its slices run left to right. 2. **Zoom in on one slice.** Zoom in on the first slice of the **Basket** feature, **AddToBasket**. Read it from the top down: first the screen the Shopper sees, then the command **AddToBasket**, then the event **BasketLineAdded**. A person asks, a fact is recorded. app.cratis.studio ![The first three slices of the Basket feature at full detail: AddToBasket, ChangeBasketQuantity and RemoveFromBasket, each with a Shopper screen, a blue command and an orange event](/_astro/detail-full.UfKDw9mn_1DnTHC.webp) AddToBasket, ChangeBasketQuantity and RemoveFromBasket. Notice the row labels on the left: who does it, and what kind of thing it is. 3. **Read across.** Move right. **ChangeBasketQuantity** records **BasketLineQuantityChanged**, and **RemoveFromBasket** records **BasketLineRemoved**. Every event is in the past tense, because it is a fact. 4. **Find the view.** Keep going to the **BasketView** slice. It has no command. It shows the read models **BasketSize** and **BasketContents**, built from the events to its left. Select the **BasketContents** card to open its editor. app.cratis.studio ![The editor of the BasketContents read model, with the events BasketLineAdded, BasketLineRemoved, BasketCleared and BasketLineQuantityChanged on the left, joined by lines to the properties of the read model on the right](/_astro/read-model-editor.BZ0jnvlv_Zn3bJ9.webp) BasketContents is built from four events. The lines show which event property fills which read model property. 5. **Read the examples.** Underneath each slice are its examples, each in the form *given, when, then*. Below **AddToBasket** you will find one where a listed product is added and **BasketLineAdded** follows, and one where the product is out of stock and the answer is *That product is out of stock*. These examples are how the model proves it does what it says. Notice three things as you read. The names are the business’s own words, not technical ones. The events tell the whole story, so nothing is hidden. And a column never reaches into another’s data: a view only shows what the events to its left said. ## See how the areas talk to each other [Section titled “See how the areas talk to each other”](#see-how-the-areas-talk-to-each-other) The Storefront does not look inside Catalog’s data. When a product is published for sale, Catalog says so by publishing a **public event**. The Storefront receives that fact and translates it into its own words, so a change in Catalog’s vocabulary never leaks into the shop. Look for a slice of the **Translator** type in the Storefront’s browsing feature to see where this happens. To see the whole picture at once, open **Acme Shop** > **Context map**. It draws how the application’s domains relate through the events they publish and consume. Compare it with the same map for **Acme Sales**. ## Read an automation and its examples [Section titled “Read an automation and its examples”](#read-an-automation-and-its-examples) Now open **Acme Sales** > **Domains** > **Sales** > **Event Models** > **Sales**. This board has people in more than one row: a **SalesManager**, a **SalesRep** and the **System**. app.cratis.studio ![The Sales board zoomed out, with many slices in a row and their examples underneath](/_astro/sales-board-overview.COo2UAlt_6G1qO.webp) The Sales board at a glance. Find the slice **AutoApproveSmallOrders**. It has a cogwheel where the others have screens. That is an **automation**: no person presses a button. The system reacts when an order is received, and approves it on its own. app.cratis.studio ![The AutoApproveSmallOrders slice on the Sales board, with a cogwheel in the System row, the blue command AutoApproveSmallOrders and the slice RejectOrder beside it with a screen](/_astro/automation-slice-sales.CMvvOfMh_ZLf7qx.webp) An automation. The cogwheel stands for the system acting on its own. Its first example reads *A Small Order Approves Itself*: given nothing, when **OrderReceived**, then **OrderApproved**, **OrderConfirmationIssued**, **OrderConfirmed** and **OrderStatusChanged** follow. Next to it, an example for **RejectOrder** says who is asking (*Authenticated*, as a **SalesRep**) and expects the command to be denied. Examples can say who may do what, too. app.cratis.studio ![Two examples on the Sales board: A Small Order Approves Itself, with the event OrderReceived and the resulting events, and ARepCannotReject, expecting the RejectOrder command to be denied for a SalesRep](/_astro/specifications-sales.Bj7mAU7S_1E0glY.webp) Examples are written in the same language as the model: given, when, then. ## Look at where it began [Section titled “Look at where it began”](#look-at-where-it-began) Models do not start as neat columns. Open **Acme Shop** > **Domains** > **Storefront** > **Brainstormings** > **Acme big picture**. app.cratis.studio ![The Acme big picture brainstorming board: a cloud of orange event cards and sticky notes, with the events sorted into columns for Merchandising, Browsing and Ordering](/_astro/big-picture-brainstorming.nVkk0Y53_FftNf.webp) The first column is the chaos storm: everything the team could think of. The columns beside it are what they sorted it into. The first region is the **chaos storm**: every event the team could think of, in no order. The regions beside it are what they sorted it into: **Merchandising**, **Browsing**, **Ordering**. Sticky notes record the questions, risks, decisions and assumptions that came up along the way. In this sample, each note’s text begins with its kind, such as *\[DECISION]* or *\[RISK]*. A board like this is where an event model starts. ## See the same ideas in your own project [Section titled “See the same ideas in your own project”](#see-the-same-ideas-in-your-own-project) You cannot open Acme Commerce from another organization, but you can see everything above for yourself. * **Build the same shape.** [Model your first behavior](/get-started/first-event-model/) builds a small basket the way the Storefront does it: a state change slice with a screen, a command and an event, a view that counts what happened, and an example that checks it. * **Read the sample’s source.** The whole of Acme Commerce is written as Screenplay files in the public [Cratis Screenplay repository](https://github.com/Cratis/Screenplay/tree/main/Samples/AcmeCommerce), with a [walkthrough](https://github.com/Cratis/Screenplay/blob/main/Documentation/screenplay/acme-commerce-walkthrough.md) that follows a product from the catalog to dispatch. You do not need a Studio account to read them. To see a Screenplay file drawn as a board, see [Screenplay files](/developers/screenplay-files/). * **Start from your own brainstorm.** [Brainstorm](/discover/brainstorming/) the way the *Acme big picture* board did, then turn the regions into slices. ## What happens next [Section titled “What happens next”](#what-happens-next) * [Model your first behavior](/get-started/first-event-model/): your turn. * [Event modeling in Studio](/event-modeling/): why the board is shaped this way. * [Take the guided tours](/get-started/guided-tours/): Studio can walk you through boards like these.

# Find your way around

> A tour of Cratis Studio's top bar, the project drawer, the board's toolbars, the jobs panel and the help menu, so you always know where to look.

Studio has few places to be. A top bar that stays with you everywhere, a drawer that lists your work, and the boards where you do it. This page shows each one, using the Acme Commerce sample. ## How your work is organized [Section titled “How your work is organized”](#how-your-work-is-organized) Everything you build lives in a **project**, usually one for each team. A project holds one or more **applications**, the systems you are building. Each application holds **domains**, the areas of the business it covers. And each domain holds the boards where you do the work, the **event model** first among them. Project, application, domain, board. In Acme Commerce, each of the five areas of the shop is an application of its own. You rarely need to think about this structure once you are in a board, but it explains where everything is kept, and the drawer shows it as a tree. ## The top bar [Section titled “The top bar”](#the-top-bar) The top bar sits across the top of every page. It has two groups of buttons. On the left: | Button | What it does | | ------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Home** | Goes back to the home page. | | **Projects** | Opens and closes the project drawer. | | The project name | Shows which project you are in. | | **Project options** (the three dots) | Opens a menu with **Project Settings**, **Progress board**, **Diagrams**, **Export** and **Import**. | On the right: | Button | What it does | | --------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | | **See what Studio is working on** | Opens the jobs panel. See [Jobs](#see-what-studio-is-working-on). | | **Report Issue** | Opens a menu to report a problem or follow your reports. | | **Questions? Ask on Discord** | Opens the Cratis community on Discord. | | **About** | Shows the Studio version and its release notes. | | **Account** (your avatar) | Opens your account menu. | On a board, a few more buttons join the right-hand group: the play button that runs your model, who else is on the board, the board’s history, its topics, and a **View** menu. They are described [below](#the-board). app.cratis.studio ![The overview page of the Acme Commerce project, listing its five applications and Add Application, under the top bar with Home, Projects and the project name](/_astro/project-overview.xgfwi9Ax_Z2jSozF.webp) The overview of a project lists its applications. The top bar is the same on every page. ## The project drawer [Section titled “The project drawer”](#the-project-drawer) Select **Projects** to slide the drawer in from the left. It lists every project you can open as a tree, and it is how you move around. app.cratis.studio ![The project drawer open on Acme Commerce, with its five applications; Acme Shop is expanded to show Files, Prologues, Design, UI Profile, Policies, Context map and Domains](/_astro/drawer-acme-commerce.B3pLGL21_Z4YRzq.webp) The drawer. Select a row to open it, and the arrow beside it to show what is inside. The lock beside Acme Commerce means the project is protected. At the top of the drawer are three buttons: **Take the guided tour**, **Generate Project with AI** and **Add Project**. Hover over any row and a **+** appears for adding what belongs under it, for example an application under a project. Rows that have settings show a gear. An application holds these rows: | Row | What it is for | | --------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Domains** | The areas of the business, each with its own boards. | | **Context map** | How the application’s domains relate through the events they publish and consume. | | **Design** | All the application’s screens on one canvas. | | **UI Profile** | How the application looks and what its screens are built from. | | **Policies** | The rules about who may do what. | | **Prologues** | Capturing an existing system to understand it. | | **Files** | The application’s source files and how they changed. | A domain holds the boards and canvases of one area of the business: | Row | What it is for | | ------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Event Models** | The [event model boards](/board/), where behavior is drawn. | | **Brainstormings** | [Brainstorming boards](/discover/brainstorming/), where ideas are collected before they become a model. | | **Personas** | The people the domain is for. | | **Concepts** | The domain’s vocabulary: named values with their own rules. | | **Constraints** | Rules such as “this value must be unique”. | | **State machines** and **Workflows** | How something moves through states, and how a longer process runs. | | **Given contexts** and **Seed data** | Reusable starting situations for examples, and starting events for a running model. | | **Event Sources** | What the model’s events are about and how they are grouped. | app.cratis.studio ![The project drawer expanded to the Storefront domain of Acme Shop, listing Event Models, Brainstormings, Personas, Concepts, Constraints, State machines, Workflows, Given contexts, Seed data and Event Sources](/_astro/drawer-storefront-domain.Dioq7cMQ_OcDsn.webp) Everything a domain holds, as the drawer shows it. Studio remembers which parts of the tree you had open. Opening a board from the drawer takes you straight to it. ## The board [Section titled “The board”](#the-board) The event model board is where you will spend most of your time. Here it is with its toolbars. app.cratis.studio ![The Storefront board with the toolbar down the left edge, the top bar controls, and the minimap and zoom controls in the bottom right corner](/_astro/board-minimap-and-zoom.BccHb5mN_1oUDv6.webp) The toolbar on the left, the top bar above, and the zoom controls at the lower right. **The toolbar on the left edge** adds and works on things. From top to bottom: **Add module**, **Focus on a feature**, **Add Sticky Note**, the clipboard tools (**Copy**, **Paste**, **Paste as linked copy**, **Duplicate**), the board’s findings, **Storylines**, **See as a caller**, **Trace a value**, and **Take the guided tour** at the bottom. **The controls at the lower right** move you around the canvas: **Toggle minimap**, **Zoom Out**, **Reset Zoom** and **Zoom In**. **The top bar** gains five controls on the right, from left to right: * **Run Solution**, the play button, runs the model as an application you can try. The arrow beside it opens **Run options**. * **Active collaborators** shows who else is on the board right now. It says *Only you* when you are alone. * **See what has happened on this board** opens the board’s history. * **Topics on this event model** opens the conversations about the board. * **View** changes how the board is drawn. app.cratis.studio ![The View menu of the board with the detail level Full and Overview, Properties, Arrows and Lines, the text sizes Small, Medium and Large, and where the chat sits: Inline or Sidebar](/_astro/view-menu.BA3yIHzR_1czVX2.webp) The View menu. Choose Overview to see the whole board at a glance, or Full to see every property. When AI is set up for your organization, a chat bar along the bottom of the board lets you talk to your agents about the board. See [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/). Each part of the board is covered in [The event model board](/board/), starting with [Board basics](/board/board-basics/). ## See what Studio is working on [Section titled “See what Studio is working on”](#see-what-studio-is-working-on) Some work takes a while: importing a model, generating from a description, building. Studio does it in the background and keeps a list. Select **See what Studio is working on** in the top bar to open the **Jobs** panel. **Recent** shows what ran lately, and **All jobs** shows everything. A job shows its steps and its state, such as *Running*, *Done* or *Failed*. When nothing has run, the panel says *Nothing has run recently.* app.cratis.studio ![The Jobs panel opened over the right side of the Storefront board, with the tabs Recent and All jobs and the text Nothing has run recently](/_astro/jobs-panel.C9_nF0zd_ZwAp6I.webp) The Jobs panel, with nothing running. ## Get help [Section titled “Get help”](#get-help) The **Report Issue** button opens a menu with two items: **Report an issue** and **My issues**. Report a problem in a few sentences and Studio attaches where you were, so the team can find it. **My issues** follows your reports from *Received* to *Done*. app.cratis.studio ![The right side of the top bar with the Report Issue menu open, showing Report an issue and My issues](/_astro/report-issue-menu.BAOnMIEe_Zb46FY.webp) The Report Issue menu. **About** shows the version of Studio you are using and links to its release notes. For questions and conversation, use **Questions? Ask on Discord**. All the ways to get help are on [Get help](/reference/get-help/). ## Your account and organization [Section titled “Your account and organization”](#your-account-and-organization) Select your avatar to open the account menu. app.cratis.studio ![The account menu with Account profile, Organization settings and Log out](/_astro/account-menu.Dvl5cP_v_Z8z7ci.webp) The account menu. * **Account profile** is where you change your details, the sign-in providers you use, how Studio looks and how it talks. See [Profile and appearance](/reference/profile-and-appearance/). * **Organization settings** is where your organization’s people, AI, integrations and billing are managed. Everyone can look; changing things is for maintainers. See [Your organization](/organization/). * **Switch organization** appears when you belong to more than one. * **Log out** signs you out. ## What happens next [Section titled “What happens next”](#what-happens-next) * [Explore the Acme Commerce sample](/get-started/explore-the-sample/): walk through a finished model. * [Take the guided tours](/get-started/guided-tours/): let Studio show you around its own boards. * [Model your first behavior](/get-started/first-event-model/): start your own.

# Model your first behavior

> A fifteen-minute tutorial. Start with an empty project and model one behavior, a shopper adding a mug to a basket, with a screen, an example and a run.

What you'll learn * create a project, an application and a domain * open an event model board and add a module and a feature * add a state change slice with a command, an event and a screen * add a state view that shows what happened * write an example that checks the behavior, and run the model In this tutorial you model one small behavior from scratch: a shopper adds an item to a basket, and the basket keeps count. It is the same shape as the **Basket** feature in the Acme Commerce sample, built from an empty project. It takes about fifteen minutes, and every step ends with something you can see. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * You need to be signed in with a seat in your organization. If you are not, start with [Sign up and start your trial](/get-started/sign-up-and-trial/). * You do not need AI. Everything here is done by hand. * The screenshots in this tutorial come from the Storefront board of the Acme Commerce sample, which has the same controls as the board you will build. Your own board will look emptier, because it starts empty. * Names must be unique in your organization, for a project and for an application. If Studio refuses a name, add your initials to it. ## Part 1: Set up your project (3 minutes) [Section titled “Part 1: Set up your project (3 minutes)”](#part-1-set-up-your-project-3-minutes) 1. Select **Projects** in the top bar to open the project drawer, and then select **Add Project** (the **+** at the top of the drawer). 2. Enter `Corner Shop` as the **Name**. You can leave **Domain Description** empty. It is only used by AI assistance. Select **Add Project**. Studio opens the project. Its overview says *No applications yet* and *Add the first application to start modeling this project.* 3. Select **Add Application**. Enter `Corner Shop Online` as the **Name**, and describe it in a few words if you like. Select **Add Application**. The application appears under your project in the drawer. 4. In the drawer, expand your application, hover over **Domains** and select the **+** that appears. Its tooltip reads **Add Domain**. Enter `Storefront` as the **Name** and select **Add**. 5. Under your new domain, hover over **Event Models** and select the **+**, whose tooltip reads **Add Event Model**. Enter `Storefront` as the **Name** and select **Add**. Studio opens the new, empty board. app.cratis.studio ![The overview of the Acme Commerce project, listing its applications and Add Application](/_astro/project-overview.xgfwi9Ax_Z2jSozF.webp) A project's overview lists its applications. Yours lists none until you add one. Here is the sample's, with five. You now have the whole structure: a project, an application, a domain and an event model. The board is empty and hints that you add a module with the sitemap icon. If you want to know why the work is organized this way, see [Find your way around](/get-started/find-your-way-around/#how-your-work-is-organized). ## Part 2: Add a module and a feature (2 minutes) [Section titled “Part 2: Add a module and a feature (2 minutes)”](#part-2-add-a-module-and-a-feature-2-minutes) A [module](/reference/glossary/#module) is one area of the business. A [feature](/reference/glossary/#feature) groups related behavior inside it. 1. In the toolbar on the left edge of the board, select **Add module**. It is the first button, the one with the sitemap icon. 2. Studio puts a module near the middle of your view. Double-click its name and rename it to `Shop`. 3. In the header of the module, open the **Add** menu and choose **Add Feature**. Double-click the feature’s name and rename it to `Basket`. You should see a module called **Shop** with a feature called **Basket** inside it. The feature is empty, and says so. More about both in [Add modules and features](/board/modules-and-features/). ## Part 3: Add a behavior that changes something (4 minutes) [Section titled “Part 3: Add a behavior that changes something (4 minutes)”](#part-3-add-a-behavior-that-changes-something-4-minutes) Now the heart of it. A **state change** slice is one behavior where a person asks for something and the system records it. [Slices](/board/slices/) come in four types; this is the one you will use most. 1. In the header of the **Basket** feature, select **Add** (the **+** button), choose **Add Slice**, and then choose **State Change**. Studio describes it as *UI drives a command that produces events*. 2. A new column appears. Double-click its name and rename it to `AddToBasket`. The column holds a command called **Command** and an event called **Event**, and a row that says who does it. Studio adds a row named **UI** for the person using the screen. 3. Double-click the blue command card and rename it to `AddToBasket`. Commands are named like instructions. 4. Double-click the orange event card and rename it to `BasketLineAdded`. Events are named in the past tense, because they are facts that already happened. app.cratis.studio ![The Add Slice chooser open in a feature, listing State Change, State View, Automation and Translator with a description of each](/_astro/add-slice-chooser.CD0lTpGG_sLQlO.webp) The chooser asks which kind of behavior the slice is. You pick State Change. You should see a column with a blue **AddToBasket** card above an orange **BasketLineAdded** card, the same shape as the first columns of the sample’s Basket feature: app.cratis.studio ![The first three slices of the Basket feature: AddToBasket, ChangeBasketQuantity and RemoveFromBasket, each with a screen on top, a blue command and an orange event](/_astro/detail-full.UfKDw9mn_1DnTHC.webp) In the sample's Basket feature, each column is one change: the screen, the command, the event. ### Say what the command carries [Section titled “Say what the command carries”](#say-what-the-command-carries) Select the **AddToBasket** command card to open the command editor. 1. Select **+ Add property**, double-click the new property’s name, and rename it to `basketId`. In the panel on the right, tick **Identifier - names what changes**. This says which basket the command is about. 2. Add a second property, `sku`, with the type **Text**. 3. Add a third property, `quantity`, and change its type to **Number**. Use the type menu in the panel on the right, or the type field on the property. 4. Make sure all three are **Required - the sender must give it**. 5. Close the command editor. app.cratis.studio ![The command editor open on AddToBasket, with the properties basketId, sku and quantity, each with a type and a Required box, and the panel for the selected property on the right](/_astro/command-editor.CDuIhQWV_2jyS4M.webp) The command editor of the sample's AddToBasket, with the same kind of properties. Each property has a type, and can be required or the identifier. ### Say what the event records [Section titled “Say what the event records”](#say-what-the-event-records) Select the **BasketLineAdded** event card to open the event editor. An event records what happened, so it carries what changed: not which basket (that is the identifier, and Studio keeps it with the event), but what was added. 1. Add the property `sku` with the type **Text**, and the property `quantity` with the type **Number**. Use the same names as on the command: the event takes its values from the command property with the same name. 2. Close the event editor. Open the command editor once more and look at the bar at the bottom. It lists any problems with the model, and says how many warnings there are. It should say that everything is fine. If a warning names a property that no command property supplies, check that the names on the event match the names on the command exactly. ## Part 4: Give it a screen (2 minutes) [Section titled “Part 4: Give it a screen (2 minutes)”](#part-4-give-it-a-screen-2-minutes) A screen shows what the person sees while the slice happens. 1. In the **UI** row of the slice, select **Add UI prototype** (the small **+** in the corner), and choose **From scratch**. 2. The screen editor opens over the board. Select **Data**, and then **Generate the form**. Studio makes a field for each property of the command. The drawer says *Every property is on the form*. 3. Close the editor. It saves your screen. You should see a small thumbnail of the screen on the slice, in the **UI** row, above the command. app.cratis.studio ![The screen editor open on a screen of the Storefront board, with the parts of the screen on the left, the screen in the middle and the Inspector on the right](/_astro/prototype-editor.C4oAmbiF_1ODRgN.webp) The screen editor, here on the sample's ProductBrowse screen. Yours holds the form you generated. To go further with screens, see [Add a screen to a slice](/screens/screens-on-slices/) and [The screen editor](/screens/screen-editor/). ## Part 5: Show what happened (2 minutes) [Section titled “Part 5: Show what happened (2 minutes)”](#part-5-show-what-happened-2-minutes) Facts are hard to read on their own. A **state view** shows them as something a person can look at: here, how many lines the basket has. 1. In the **Basket** feature, select **Add** again, choose **Add Slice**, and then choose **State View**. Rename the new column to `BasketView`. Studio places it to the right of the slice you already have, which matters: a view can only show events produced to its left. 2. In the events row of the new slice, select **Add existing event**, search for `BasketLineAdded`, and pick it. The event card appears in the view. It points back at the slice where the event is produced. 3. Double-click the green read model card (it is called **Read Model**) and rename it to `BasketSize`. 4. Select the **BasketSize** card to open its editor. The read model starts with an `id` property, which is how one basket is found. In the card on the right, add a property, name it `lineCount` and give it the type **Number**. 5. Drag the **BasketLineAdded** card, on the left, onto **lineCount**. A small menu asks what the event does: *Each time BasketLineAdded happens, lineCount:*. Choose **Adds one**. A line joins the two, and Studio now counts the events. 6. Close the editor. The changes are saved when it closes. app.cratis.studio ![The editor of the sample's BasketContents read model: events on the left, the read model's properties on the right, and lines between them](/_astro/read-model-editor.BZ0jnvlv_Zn3bJ9.webp) The read model editor, here on the sample's BasketContents. Events on the left, the read model on the right, and a line for each thing an event fills in. Yours has one line, from the event to the count. You should see the **BasketLineAdded** card in the view, joined to the green **BasketSize** card. The read model is derived state: it can always be thrown away and rebuilt from the events, so it is never a second source of truth. For more on mapping events, see [Read models, projections and queries](/board/read-models/). ## Part 6: Write an example (2 minutes) [Section titled “Part 6: Write an example (2 minutes)”](#part-6-write-an-example-2-minutes) An example says what should happen, in the form *given these facts, when this happens, then this follows*. It is how you prove the model does what you meant. 1. Go back to the **AddToBasket** slice. Under it, select **Add Specification**. Double-click the name it gets, and rename it to `Adding an item puts a line in the basket`. 2. In the **When** row, select the **+** and choose the command **AddToBasket**. Open the card and enter values: `basket-1` for `basketId`, `MUG-1` for `sku`, and `1` for `quantity`. 3. In the **Then** row, select the **+** and choose the event **BasketLineAdded**. Open the card and enter `MUG-1` for `sku` and `1` for `quantity`. 4. Leave **Given** empty: nothing needs to have happened before you add the first item. 5. Select **Run specification**. Studio runs the example against your model and checks the result. When it passes, the specification shows a green mark, and its status reads *Last run succeeded*. If it shows a notice about missing required values, open the step it points to and fill in the property it names. app.cratis.studio ![Two examples under a slice of the Sales board, each with the rows Caller, Given, When and Then, a green check mark and a run button in the header](/_astro/specifications-sales.Bj7mAU7S_1E0glY.webp) Examples on the sample's Sales board. Each one has its own rows, a result mark, and a button to run it. ## Part 7: Run it (1 minute) [Section titled “Part 7: Run it (1 minute)”](#part-7-run-it-1-minute) Examples check a step. **Play** runs the whole model as a small working application, so you can try it. 1. In the top bar of the board, select **Run Solution**, the play button. Studio starts your model in a safe session of its own. 2. When the application appears, run the **AddToBasket** command with `basket-1`, `MUG-1` and `1`. 3. Select **Read models** in the Play toolbar and open **BasketSize**. You should see one entry for `basket-1`, with a `lineCount` of 1. Run the command again and the count goes up. 4. Select **Stop Solution** to return to the board. If Studio refuses to start the model, it tells you why. The usual reason is a warning on the command or event, such as a required value that nothing fills in. Fix what it names and try again. Play is a place to try things: its data lives only for the session, and it is not a deployment. See [Check behavior](/check/) for more. Recap You created a project, an application, a domain and an event model. You added a module and a feature, then a state change slice with a command, an event and a screen, and a state view that counts what happened. You wrote an example that checks the behavior, and ran the whole model in Play. That is the loop every model follows: a command asks, an event records, a read model shows. Everything else on the board is a variation of it. Next, [take the guided tour of the event model board](/get-started/guided-tours/), or move on to [the event model board](/board/), where each step you took here has a guide of its own.

# Take the guided tours

> Let Cratis Studio walk you through its boards, with a voice-over and subtitles or at your own pace, and find the tour for each board.

Studio can show you around itself. A **guided tour** is a short, narrated walk through one part of Studio: the screen you are looking at moves with the story, a pointer shows where to look, and subtitles carry the words. You can follow a tour in a few minutes, pause it whenever you like, or read it instead, at your own pace. app.cratis.studio ![The Getting around tour playing over the project drawer: a bubble beside Acme Commerce says One project, typically one per team; a list of chapters on the right and the player controls along the bottom](/_astro/guided-tour-start.BI_-pTp4_1eTBxY.webp) A tour playing. The chapters are on the right, the controls are along the bottom, and the subtitles sit just above them. ## Start a tour [Section titled “Start a tour”](#start-a-tour) 1. Open the part of Studio you want to learn about. For the project drawer, open the drawer. For a board, open the board. 2. Select **Take the guided tour**. In the project drawer it is the graduation cap at the top of the drawer. On a board it is the last button of the toolbar on the left edge. 3. The tour starts playing. Beside the button you may see the nudge *Start here to learn more*. app.cratis.studio ![The project drawer with its header buttons: the graduation cap for the guided tour, the spark for Generate Project with AI and the plus for Add Project](/_astro/drawer-acme-commerce.B3pLGL21_Z4YRzq.webp) The graduation cap in the drawer header starts the tour of the drawer. ## Use the player [Section titled “Use the player”](#use-the-player) The player works the same way in every tour. | Control | What it does | | -------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- | | **Play** and **Pause** | Start the narration or stop it where you are. | | **Previous chapter** and **Next chapter** | Jump between chapters. | | The progress bar | Shows where you are in the tour. | | **Turn the voice-over off** / **Turn the voice-over on** | Mutes or restores the narrator’s voice. | | **Hide subtitles** / **Show subtitles** | Hides or shows the words at the bottom of the screen. | | **Read the tour at your own pace** | Switches to the written version. See below. | | **Close the tour** | Ends the tour and takes you back to your work. | The **Chapters** list on the right shows every chapter with the time it starts. Select a chapter to jump to it. Chapters are grouped under headings, so a long tour reads like a small course. ## Read it at your own pace [Section titled “Read it at your own pace”](#read-it-at-your-own-pace) Prefer reading to listening? Select **Read the tour at your own pace**. The narration is replaced by a written walkthrough of the same chapters. It says what the idea is, and how to do it on a real board, with pictures. Select a chapter in the list, or use the previous and next buttons, and the board follows along, showing what the chapter talks about. Select **Return to the narrated tour** to go back to the narration. ## Your own work stays untouched [Section titled “Your own work stays untouched”](#your-own-work-stays-untouched) A tour never changes your project. The tour of the project drawer points at your real drawer. The tour of the event model board draws its own small example, a shop’s ordering system, in front of you. Nothing in it is saved, so you can watch it being built without any risk to your model. ## Which tour for which board [Section titled “Which tour for which board”](#which-tour-for-which-board) Each place in Studio has its own tour. Start the one that matches the board you are on. | Where | Tour | What its chapters cover | | ------------------- | -------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Project drawer | Getting around | Projects, Applications, Domains and Prologue. | | Event model board | Event modeling | What we are building, Modules and features, Commands and events, Read models, Automations, What makes it run, Across modules, Connecting modules and The whole picture. Then the Properties on the cards, Constraints, Rules and Projections. | | Brainstorming board | Brainstorming | A place to think, The tools, Organizing and Into an event model. | | Concepts | Concepts | The domain’s vocabulary, The primitive underneath, Personal data, Rules and Into the model. | | Constraints | Constraints | What a constraint is, The events that take part, Two kinds of uniqueness and Release and scope. | | Personas | Personas | Who it is for, What they want, Roles and policies, A face for the persona and The whole cast. | | Event sources | Event sources | What an event source is, Identity, Streams, Concurrency dimensions and Used by the model. | | Given contexts | Given contexts | What a given context is, Events that have happened, Read model state and Using them in specifications. | | Seed data | Seed data | Collections of starting events, Adding events, Event source and values and What seed data does. | | State machines | State machines | What a state machine is, States, Events and guards, Entering a state and Simulate and specify. | | Workflows | Workflows | What a workflow is, What starts a run, Actions, decisions and loops, Waiting and asking, Outlets and failure and Simulate and check. | | Policies | Policies | What a policy is, The requirement and Groups and custom logic. | ## What happens next [Section titled “What happens next”](#what-happens-next) * [Find your way around](/get-started/find-your-way-around/): the written version of the first tour. * [Model your first behavior](/get-started/first-event-model/): put what you learned to work. * [Event modeling in Studio](/event-modeling/): the ideas behind the event modeling tour.

# Sign up and start your trial

> Create your Cratis Studio organization and start the 14-day free trial, or join an organization you were invited to.

There are two ways in. Either you start a new organization for your team and get a 14-day free trial, or someone who already has an organization invites you to it. Find the one that matches you below. ## Start a new organization [Section titled “Start a new organization”](#start-a-new-organization) You need: * An account with one of the sign-in providers Studio supports, such as GitHub, Microsoft, Google or Apple. The sign-in page shows the ones that are available. You do not create a separate Studio password. * A name for your organization. Organization names are unique across Studio, so a very common name may already be taken. 1. Open the [sign-up page](https://lobby.cratis.studio/register). It is headed *Start your 14-day free trial*. 2. Sign in with the account you want to use. 3. Read and accept the terms and conditions and the privacy policy. 4. Enter a name for your organization. 5. Wait while Studio sets the organization up. The page says *Setting up your organization…*, and then *Signing you in…*. If the sign-up page does not let you register, new organizations may not be accepted at the moment. Try again later, or ask someone who already uses Studio to invite you. ### Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) You land in Studio, on a home page that asks *What are you building today?*. The first time, a short welcome says that Studio is still in beta. Select **Got it, let’s go** to carry on. app.cratis.studio ![The Studio home page with the prompt What are you building today? and a project picker](/_astro/home.DTL1luYl_Z2aP3yP.webp) The home page. A new organization starts with no projects. You are your organization’s **maintainer**, and one of the trial seats is assigned to you, so you can start working straight away. ### What the trial includes [Section titled “What the trial includes”](#what-the-trial-includes) * **14 days.** The trial starts when your organization is created. * **No credit card.** The sign-up page says so: you create your organization and use Studio free for 14 days. * **The full product for a small team.** The sign-up page promises the full product for you and two colleagues. You invite them from **Organization settings** > **Users**; see [Your organization](/organization/). * **One free trial for each sign-in.** If you register more organizations with the same sign-in, they are created without a trial. ## Join with an invitation [Section titled “Join with an invitation”](#join-with-an-invitation) A maintainer of an organization can invite you by email. The email holds a link that is meant for you, so do not forward it. 1. Open the link in the invitation email. 2. Sign in with an account Studio supports. 3. Read and accept the terms and conditions and the privacy policy. 4. Studio adds you to the organization. You join with the role the maintainer chose when they invited you: **Maintainer** or **Member**. You land in Studio and can open the organization’s projects. ### If you cannot use Studio yet [Section titled “If you cannot use Studio yet”](#if-you-cannot-use-studio-yet) * **There is no seat for you.** Everyone who works in Studio needs a seat. If none was free when you were invited, you can sign in but cannot use Studio until a maintainer assigns you one or adds more seats. Ask a maintainer to open **Organization settings** > **Users**. * **The link has stopped working.** Ask a maintainer to select **Refresh invitation** next to your name in **Users**. Studio sends you a new link. * **You belong to more than one organization.** Open the account menu and choose **Switch organization**. Each organization is separate: its projects, AI settings and billing never mix. app.cratis.studio ![The account menu opened from the avatar in the top right corner, with Account profile, Organization settings and Log out](/_astro/account-menu.Dvl5cP_v_Z175ryr.webp) The account menu is where you reach your profile and your organization's settings. Switch organization appears here when you belong to more than one. ## The trial banner, and when the trial ends [Section titled “The trial banner, and when the trial ends”](#the-trial-banner-and-when-the-trial-ends) While the trial runs, a banner counts it down: *N days left of your free trial (ends date).* On the last day it says *Today is the last day of your free trial.* Maintainers see a **Choose a plan** button. Members see *Ask your organization’s maintainer to choose a plan.* When the trial ends, Studio says *Your free trial has ended*. Nothing is deleted: *Your organization and its work are kept.* A maintainer chooses a plan to continue. Studio charges per seat; see the pricing on [cratis.studio](https://cratis.studio), and [Your organization](/organization/) for how seats and plans work. ## What happens next [Section titled “What happens next”](#what-happens-next) * [Find your way around](/get-started/find-your-way-around/): a tour of the places you will spend your time. * [Explore the Acme Commerce sample](/get-started/explore-the-sample/): see what a finished model looks like. * [Model your first behavior](/get-started/first-event-model/): build one yourself.

# Organization settings at a glance

> Run your team in Cratis Studio - invite people, manage seats and billing, set up AI, connect your tools and decide who may do what.

Everything your team shares in Cratis Studio is set up once, for the whole **organization**: who belongs to it, which AI services its agents use, which tools it connects. This section is for the people who look after that: team admins and maintainers. app.cratis.studio ![Organization settings open on the Users page, with the list of members and agents and the seat counter](/_astro/settings-users.CUABBLwi_ZnzPYB.webp) Organization settings. The list on the left is the map of this section. ## Open the settings [Section titled “Open the settings”](#open-the-settings) Open the **Account** menu and select **Organization settings**. A full-screen **Settings** panel opens. integrations](/organization/integrations/) | | **Transcribing** | Tidying dictated text | [Conversation and dictation settings](/organization/conversation-settings/) | | **Conversation** | Whether settings, but only a maintainer can change them. Pages say so when you are not one. ## What would you like to do? [Approve builders and issue an MCP credential ](/organization/builders/)Let machines and agents work with your organization. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Sign up and start your trial](/get-started/sign-up-and-trial/) * [Roles and permissions](/organization/roles-and-permissions/) * [Glossary](/reference/glossary/)

# Configure agents and skills

> Choose the provider and capability tier each Studio agent runs on, give it an avatar and a voice, and teach it your way of working with skills.

Studio gives every organization the same team of AI agents. Each has a fixed name and specialization: **Atlas** models, **Merlin** writes code, **Quill** writes documentation, **Sherlock** reviews, **Echo** carries the conversation, **Hunch** suggests what to model next (it is the predictive modeling agent) and **Iris** designs screens. You cannot add, remove or rename an agent. What you decide is how each one works for you. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * You are a maintainer. Other members see the page read-only: *You are not a maintainer of this organization, so the agents are read-only here.* * At least one [AI provider](/organization/ai-providers/) is set up. Without one, the page says *Configure an AI provider under the AI tab first - an agent runs on one.* app.cratis.studio ![The Agents page listing Atlas, Merlin, Quill, Sherlock, Echo and the other agents with their specialization, model and status](/_astro/settings-agents.DZTuzOVl_Z1wE2n5.webp) The roster. An agent without a provider shows Needs a provider. ## Give an agent a provider [Section titled “Give an agent a provider”](#give-an-agent-a-provider) 1. Open **Organization settings** and select **Agents**. 2. On the agent’s row, select **Edit**. The **Edit agent** dialog opens. 3. Under **Runs on**, choose a configured provider or a [provider pool](/organization/ai-providers/#spread-work-across-several-providers-with-a-pool). A pool is marked *(pool)*. 4. Choose the **Capability tier** that says how much model this agent needs: **Fast**, **Balanced**, **Powerful** or **Premier**. The model comes from the tier you set on the provider. When the provider lists no tiers, enter **Model to use** yourself. 5. Select **Save**. The **Status** column changes from **Needs a provider** to **Ready**. ## Choose an avatar and a voice [Section titled “Choose an avatar and a voice”](#choose-an-avatar-and-a-voice) In the same dialog, pick one of the **Ready-made avatars** or select **Upload image** to use your own. Under **Voice**, choose the voice the agent speaks with and select **Hear this voice** to try it. If the speech service is not configured, the dialog says *Text-to-speech is not configured, so agents do not speak yet.* ## Add skills [Section titled “Add skills”](#add-skills) A **skill** is a named piece of guidance, such as how your team names events or what a good slice description looks like. Skills belong to your organization; another organization’s agents never see them. 1. Save the agent first, then open it with **Edit** again. 2. Under **Skills**, select **Add skill**. 3. Enter a **Name** that says what the agent is good at, such as *Event modeling*, and the **Content** in markdown: what the agent knows and how it should apply it. 4. Select **Add**. Open a skill to change it, or select **Remove skill** to delete it. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Set up AI](/organization/ai-providers/) * [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/) * [Conversation and dictation settings](/organization/conversation-settings/), for the agents’ voices in conversation

# Set up AI - providers, pools and the decision engine

> Bring your own AI provider, choose the model for each capability tier, group providers into pools and pick the decision engine.

Studio does not come with a language model. To use its AI agents you connect an AI provider that your organization pays for, with your own API key, and then point the agents at it. Until at least one agent has both a provider and a model, every AI feature stays switched off. You need to be a maintainer. The steps in order are: add a provider, choose its models, then [give an agent that provider](/organization/agents-and-skills/). ## Add a provider [Section titled “Add a provider”](#add-a-provider) 1. Open **Organization settings** and select **AI**. 2. Select **Add provider**. The **Add AI provider** dialog opens. 3. Choose the **Type**: **OpenAI**, **Azure OpenAI**, **Anthropic**, **OpenAI compatible**, **Z.ai**, **OpenAI Codex** or **GitHub Copilot**. 4. Enter a **Name** you will recognize, such as *Fast drafting*. 5. Enter the **API Key**. Azure OpenAI and OpenAI compatible services also ask for an **Endpoint**. A link under the key, **How do I get an API key?**, explains where to find one. 6. Select **Add**. app.cratis.studio ![The AI page of Organization settings listing a provider with its type, endpoint and health](/_astro/settings-ai-providers.CUzHMFPL_nQwq0.webp) Each provider has a type, a health and actions to test, rename, reconfigure or remove it. What Studio sends to your provider Once you configure a provider, Studio sends it what the AI features work on: your boards, chat messages, dictated text when cleanup is on, and, when generating and reviewing code, the generated changes with the Screenplay models and neighboring files from your repository. The provider handles that data under its own terms. Configuring a provider is your organization’s consent to this. Removing every provider stops further sharing but does not recall what was already sent. Keys are encrypted at rest and never shown again. When you **Reconfigure** a provider, leave the **API Key** blank to keep the current one. ## Test it and choose its models [Section titled “Test it and choose its models”](#test-it-and-choose-its-models) Select **Test provider** to check that Studio can reach it; the **Health** column shows the result. If the test fails, check the key and try again. Then select **Reconfigure** on the provider and set **Capability tiers**: choose a model for each of **Fast**, **Balanced**, **Powerful** and **Premier**, or type the model’s identifier if the list cannot be loaded. **Refresh available models** asks the provider again. An agent asks for a tier rather than a specific model, so you can change the model behind a tier without touching the agents. Anthropic and OpenAI providers can also keep a **Usage reporting key**: a separate admin-scoped key those vendors require for usage and cost reports. The key used for completions is refused there. It is optional. ## Spread work across several providers with a pool [Section titled “Spread work across several providers with a pool”](#spread-work-across-several-providers-with-a-pool) A **provider pool** is a named group of providers that work is spread across, so a burst of work does not all land on one account. An agent can run on a pool instead of a single provider. 1. Select **AI Provider Pools**, then **Create pool**. 2. Enter a **Name**, such as *Reasoning pool*, and select **Create**. 3. Select **Add provider** on the pool and choose a **Provider**. Repeat for each provider. app.cratis.studio ![The AI Provider Pools page](/_astro/settings-ai-provider-pools.uat56viR_Z1QY600.webp) A pool lists the providers that share its work. **Remove from pool** takes one provider out. Removing a pool stops every agent that draws from it until you point it somewhere else. ## Choose the decision engine [Section titled “Choose the decision engine”](#choose-the-decision-engine) Studio also asks a **decision engine** bounded questions with a fixed set of answers, such as which of a few options fits best. It never writes text, and when it cannot answer, Studio falls back to its normal behavior. It is separate from your AI providers. 1. Select **Decision engine**. 2. Choose the **Engine**: **Built-in Decision Engine** needs no setup and is the default; **Jev (TypeSafe AI)** needs a **TypeSafe API key**. 3. Select **Save**. app.cratis.studio ![The Decision engine page with the engine chooser and the Save button](/_astro/settings-decision-engine.L_TFlmbf_lBQtz.webp) The page shows which engine is in force and whether it can be reached. For Jev, leave **Model** and **Endpoint** blank unless TypeSafe told you otherwise. Leave the key field blank to keep the key already recorded. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) On **Settings** > **Agents**, an agent shows the status **Ready** once it has a usable provider and model. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Agents and skills](/organization/agents-and-skills/) * [Conversation and dictation settings](/organization/conversation-settings/) * [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/)

# Billing and your subscription

> Buy seats, add or reduce them later, keep your company details on invoices, and cancel the subscription.

Seats are paid for under **Settings** > **Billing**. Only maintainers can buy or change them. Plans and prices are on the [pricing page](https://cratis.studio/pricing/); this page does not repeat them, so they cannot go out of date. ## What the page shows [Section titled “What the page shows”](#what-the-page-shows) * **Your seats**: **Plan**, **Seats**, **Cycle**, **Paid**, **Purchased** and **Renews** for each purchase. * **Subscription**: **Plan**, **Seats**, **Amount**, **Renews** and a **Status**: **Awaiting payment**, **Active**, **Past due** or **Cancelled**. * **Billing history**: every order, with its plan, seats, cycle, total and date. * **Company details**: **Company name** and **VAT number** for your invoices. app.cratis.studio ![The Billing page of Organization settings: the Subscription card with its Add seats, Decrease seats and Cancel subscription buttons, and the Your seats table below it](/_astro/settings-billing.CuB3JRx4_1xQILO.webp) The Subscription card is on top, with its three actions. Each row of Your seats has its own Downgrade button. Plans appear by their plan id here, and Seats can show a dash. ## Buy seats [Section titled “Buy seats”](#buy-seats) 1. Open **Organization settings** and select **Billing**. 2. Select **Add seat(s)**. The **Add seats** dialog opens on the plans. 3. Choose a plan card. Some cards say **Contact sales** or **Unlimited seats**. 4. Choose **Monthly** or **Yearly (save more)**, and set the **Quantity**. 5. Select **Continue** and read your order under **Review your order**. 6. Tick *I have read and accept the Terms and Conditions and the Privacy Policy.* You must accept them to continue. 7. Select **Complete purchase**. Studio sends you to its payment provider to pay securely. When you come back, the subscription shows **Active** and the seats are in your pool. [Assign them to people](/organization/seats/) next. ## Add seats to a running subscription [Section titled “Add seats to a running subscription”](#add-seats-to-a-running-subscription) Under **Subscription**, select **Add seats**, or select **Add seat(s)** at the top of the page. The **Add seats** dialog asks for a **Quantity**. Studio tells you what you are charged now for the rest of the current period and what the new recurring amount is. Select **Complete purchase** to buy them, or **Cancel** to leave things as they are. app.cratis.studio ![The Add seats dialog with Quantity set to 1 and a note saying what is charged now and the new recurring amount](/_astro/add-seats-dialog.B3hYQ2o-_2aUK4B.webp) Read the note under Quantity before you select Complete purchase: it shows the charge for the rest of this period and the new recurring amount. ## Reduce the number of seats [Section titled “Reduce the number of seats”](#reduce-the-number-of-seats) Select **Decrease seats** under **Subscription**, or **Downgrade** under **Your seats**, and enter the **New number of seats**. Select **Schedule downgrade**. The change takes effect at your next renewal, and the page shows *Decreases to N seats on* that date until then. ## Keep your company details [Section titled “Keep your company details”](#keep-your-company-details) Under **Company details**, select **Add company details** (or **Edit**) and enter your **Company name** and **VAT number**, then **Save**, so invoices carry your legal entity. ## Cancel the subscription [Section titled “Cancel the subscription”](#cancel-the-subscription) 1. Under **Subscription**, select **Cancel subscription**. 2. Under **Why are you cancelling?**, choose a reason: *It is too expensive*, *It is missing features I need*, *We do not use it enough*, *We switched to another tool*, *It is too difficult to use*, *It was temporary, or the project ended* or *Other*. 3. Add a comment if you like. It is optional. 4. Select **Cancel subscription**. Select **Keep subscription** instead to change your mind. The subscription stops charging and does not renew. You keep full access to your seats until the end of the period you have already paid for, and the page tells you the date. If a payment fails The status turns **Past due** and seats beyond your granted capacity are suspended until the payment is recovered. Your organization and its work are kept. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Seats](/organization/seats/) * [See what your organization uses](/organization/usage/) * [Pricing](https://cratis.studio/pricing/)

# Approve builders

> Approve a builder that wants to turn your modeled slices into code, choose it for an application, and remove it again.

A **builder** turns modeled slices into code, on any machine that can reach Studio. A builder registers itself, shows a code on its own machine, and builds nothing until a maintainer approves it in Studio with that code. This page is for the maintainer who approves it. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * You are a maintainer. Other members see *Only organization maintainers can see and approve builders.* * You, or a developer on your team, started the builder. How to run one is in [Connect a builder and approve it](/developers/builders/configure/connect-and-approve/). ## Approve a builder [Section titled “Approve a builder”](#approve-a-builder) 1. Open **Organization settings** and select **Builders**. 2. Select **Connect a builder** for the command to run on the machine that should build. **Copy** copies it. Run it there. 3. The builder shows a code on its own machine. Back in Studio, find the builder in the list, marked **Waiting for approval**, with where it was asked from and on which machine. 4. Select **Approve** and type the code. You have 15 minutes. app.cratis.studio ![The Builders page with the Connect a builder instructions and the address builders register at](/_astro/settings-builders.FgHcNT-n_Z2tesGB.webp) The Builders page. Approve a builder with the code it shows on its own machine. Caution Only approve a builder you started yourself, and check the code and the machine named in the list match. Select **Decline** for one you do not recognize. ## Choose it [Section titled “Choose it”](#choose-it) Approving does not change who builds. Choose the builder for an application on **Application Settings** > **Building**, or for a single slice from its menu. Until then Studio keeps building as before. See [Project and application settings](/organization/project-and-application-settings/#choose-the-builder). ## Follow what builders are doing [Section titled “Follow what builders are doing”](#follow-what-builders-are-doing) Each builder shows whether it is **Online** or **Offline**, when it was **Last seen**, and what it is doing: **Idle**, **Building** a slice, or removing what it built. A builder that speaks an older protocol shows *This builder speaks an older protocol. Upgrade it.* Other states are **Registration expired**, **Declined** and **Not connected yet**. You can **Rename** a builder. Under **Repository** you decide whether it follows each application’s choice, or is always Studio-managed or builder-managed. ## Remove a builder [Section titled “Remove a builder”](#remove-a-builder) Select **Remove**. Its credential is refused from its next call. A slice it was building is offered again once its lease runs out. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Builders](/developers/builders/) * [Project and application settings](/organization/project-and-application-settings/)

# Conversation, dictation and code generation settings

> Allow or stop conversation mode for your organization, tidy dictated text with a language model, and set the copyright header on generated code.

Three small settings pages shape how people talk to Studio and what generated code looks like. Only a maintainer can change them. ## Allow conversation mode [Section titled “Allow conversation mode”](#allow-conversation-mode) **Conversation mode** lets members talk with the agents hands-free: the microphone stays open while a conversation lasts, and agents answer aloud. Turn it off and members can still dictate with the shortcut, and the conversation toggle explains why it is gone. 1. Open **Organization settings** and select **Conversation**. 2. Tick or clear **Allow conversation mode**. 3. Select **Save**. app.cratis.studio ![The Conversation page with the Allow conversation mode switch](/_astro/settings-conversation.lhE2FhYj_1Q0zDn.webp) One switch for the whole organization. Good to know: * Each member can hold one conversation at a time. A conversation in which nobody finishes a sentence for 30 minutes ends on its own. * The audio goes only to Studio’s own speech service, which turns it into text, and is never stored. What is heard is not sent to your AI provider to be tidied. * A spoken line that is meant for the agents is handled like a typed message. One that is not stays in the chat bar until dismissed and is neither stored nor sent on. How members use it is in [Conversation mode](/working-with-ai/conversation-mode/). ## Tidy dictated text [Section titled “Tidy dictated text”](#tidy-dictated-text) Dictation shows words the moment they are recognized. With cleanup on, each finished phrase is then tidied (punctuation, filler words, false starts) without holding up what you see. If the model is unavailable or slow, the original text is kept. 1. Select **Transcribing**. 2. Tick **Tidy up finished phrases** under **Clean up with a language model**. 3. Choose an **AI provider** from your configured providers, then a **Model**. 4. Select **Save**. app.cratis.studio ![The Transcribing page with the cleanup switch, provider and model](/_astro/settings-transcribing.BHYLY2Ul_1OMn4G.webp) Cleanup uses one of your own AI providers. Cleanup sends the dictated text to the provider you choose.
