The **Account Profile** window has four pages in a list on the left. | Page | What it is for | | -------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- | | **Account Profile** | Your name, email address, bio and photo. | | **Credentials** | The ways you can sign in. | | **Appearance** | The background behind your boards. | | **Voice and conversation** | How agents speak to you and how conversations behave. | ## Account Profile [Section titled “Account Profile”](#account-profile) Under **Personal Information** you can set your **First Name**, **Middle Name**, **Last Name** and **Display Name**, and write a short **Bio**. Select **Save** to keep your changes. The display name is how you appear to other people. app.cratis.studio ![The Account Profile window with the pages on the left and the avatar, First Name, Middle Name, Last Name, Display Name and Bio fields](/_astro/account-profile.BjQuPwPy_Z16C36A.webp) Your name and photo. The photo is blurred here. To change your **Email**, select **Update Email**. Studio sends invitations and a few notices, such as changes to the terms, to this address. There are no notification settings to adjust. ### Your photo [Section titled “Your photo”](#your-photo) Under **Avatar**, select **Upload Photo** and choose a JPEG, PNG or GIF file. Drag the circle in the **Crop Photo** window to choose the area to use, then confirm. Select **Remove Photo** to go back to your initials. ## Credentials [Section titled “Credentials”](#credentials) You can sign in to your account with any of the identity providers listed here. The providers on offer include GitHub, Microsoft, Google and Apple. * Select **Add Credential**, then choose a provider, to add another way to sign in. * Select **Remove** next to a credential to take it away. You can no longer sign in with it. * You cannot remove your only credential. At least one has to remain. app.cratis.studio ![The Credentials page of the account profile with a Remove button and the Add Credential button](/_astro/account-profile-credentials.BpLbnAge_10ARHL.webp) Add another way to sign in, or remove one you no longer use. ## Appearance [Section titled “Appearance”](#appearance) Choose a **Background** for the canvas behind your boards: **Default**, **Aurora**, **Dusk**, **Ocean**, **Ember** or **Meadow**. A live preview shows a board and a dialog on the background you point at, so you can see how it looks before you keep it. **Reset to defaults** takes you back to where you started. New users start on **Ocean**. Your choice is remembered in the browser you are using, so on a second browser you choose again. app.cratis.studio ![The Appearance page of the account profile with the background choices Default, Aurora, Dusk, Ocean, Ember and Meadow](/_astro/account-profile-appearance.Cr-Ww_gd_18Vmd2.webp) Point at a background to preview it behind a board and a dialog. ## Voice and conversation [Section titled “Voice and conversation”](#voice-and-conversation) Studio’s agents can talk. These settings decide how, on every board, and they follow you whichever browser you use. Changes are saved as you make them. | Setting | What it does | | ---------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Speak replies** | Agents read their replies aloud. | | **Speak replies to typed lines** | Also reads replies aloud when you typed what you said. | | **Hands-free** | Keeps listening after an agent has spoken. When this is off, each turn needs the shortcut. | | **Turn eagerness** | How soon an agent answers once you stop talking: **Relaxed**, **Normal** or **Quick**. | | **End conversation after silence** | A conversation that stays silent this long ends by itself: 5 minutes, 10 minutes, 30 minutes or **Never**. | | **Speech rate** | How fast replies are spoken, for every voice: **Slower**, **Normal** or **Faster**. | app.cratis.studio ![The Voice and conversation page of the account profile with the Speak replies switch and two drop-down lists](/_astro/account-profile-voice-and-conversation.Cl9brvcu_QyRCA.webp) These settings follow you to every browser. Conversation mode itself is switched on for the organization by a maintainer. See [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/), and [Keyboard shortcuts](/reference/keyboard-shortcuts/) for how to start and end a conversation. ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Keyboard shortcuts](/reference/keyboard-shortcuts/) * [Conversation mode](/working-with-ai/conversation-mode/) * [Your organization](/organization/), for members, seats and billing * [Get help](/reference/get-help/)

# Release notes

> Where to see what changed in each release of Cratis Studio.

Studio ships improvements continually. The release notes tell you what is new, what changed and what was fixed. ## In Studio [Section titled “In Studio”](#in-studio) The notes live inside the product, so they always match the version you are running. * **After an update.** The first time you open Studio after a new release, a **What’s new** window shows the notes of every release since your last visit, starting with the newest. Use **Older release** and **Newer release**, or the release picker, to move between them. Select **Show all releases** to browse the whole history. Closing the window marks them as seen, so it does not reappear for the same releases. * **Any time.** Select the information button in the top bar to open **About**, then choose **Release notes**. **About** also shows the **Version** and **Build** you are on. If you are new to Studio, the window stays quiet on your first visit. There is nothing to catch up on. ![The About dialog showing the Studio version and build and a Release notes link](/_astro/about-dialog.6cHyRor__Z1N8gVC.webp) *The About dialog. Select **Release notes** to read what changed.* ## On the web [Section titled “On the web”](#on-the-web) Product news and the public website are at [cratis.studio](https://cratis.studio). ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Get help](/reference/get-help/) for announcements and how to reach the team. * [Get started](/get-started/), if Studio is new to you. * Issues you reported show the version they were done in. See [Statuses and colors](/reference/statuses-and-colors/#issue-statuses).

# Statuses and colors

> What slice statuses, sticky note colors, build statuses and issue statuses mean in Cratis Studio.

Studio uses statuses to show where work stands, and colors to say what kind of thing something is. This page lists them all. ## Slice statuses [Section titled “Slice statuses”](#slice-statuses) Every [slice](/event-modeling/slices/) has a status. Select **Change status** in the slice header, or open the slice’s menu and choose **Set status**, to change it. See [Track progress](/deliver/track-progress/). | Status | What it means | | ------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | | **Not started** | Nobody has worked on the slice yet. | | **Draft** | The slice is sketched on the board but not settled. | | **Analysis** | The team is working out the details: fields, rules and examples. | | **Ready for implementation** | The model is settled and the slice can be built. | | **In progress** | The slice is being built. | | **Ready for review** | The slice has been built and is waiting for someone to review it. | | **Done** | The slice is built and accepted. | | **Blocked** | The slice cannot be built yet. | | **Waiting for user interface** | The slice is waiting for the builder of its user interface. | A few things to know: * **Blocking a slice asks two questions.** *Why can it not be built?* and *Questions that need an answer, one per line*. A slice with a block reason always shows as **Blocked**. The **Progress board** lists these questions. * **Studio sometimes manages the status for you.** While a slice is assigned to the System member or to an AI agent, Studio moves it along and says so. While Studio generates a slice, its status cannot be changed until the generation has ended. When a generation moves a slice to **In progress** and then **Ready for review**, a failed run restores the previous status. * **Features have a simpler set.** A feature can be raised to **Draft**, **Analysis** or **Ready for implementation**. The working statuses are set on each slice. * **Linked issues can follow along.** When an application turns on **Issue status sync**, a slice that becomes done closes its linked issues, and the reverse. See [Build and deliver](/deliver/). The **Progress board** under **Project options** shows every slice by status, and you can filter it by event model, module or feature. See [Build and deliver](/deliver/). ## Sticky note colors [Section titled “Sticky note colors”](#sticky-note-colors) A sticky note is yellow until you change its color. The colors have a shared meaning, so a board can be scanned at a glance. They are the same on every board and canvas. | Color | Meaning | | ------ | ------------------------------------ | | Yellow | A **question**. This is the default. | | Pink | A **risk**. | | Green | A **decision**. | | Blue | An **assumption**. | | Orange | Free to use as your team likes. | | Purple | Free to use as your team likes. | ## Element colors on the board [Section titled “Element colors on the board”](#element-colors-on-the-board) The event model board uses color to tell its elements apart. | Color | Element | | ------ | ----------- | | Blue | Command | | Orange | Event | | Green | Read model | | Yellow | Sticky note | The sticky note colors above are a separate palette: a yellow sticky is yellow because it is a question, not because it is an element. ## Build statuses [Section titled “Build statuses”](#build-statuses) When a [builder](/reference/glossary/#builder) builds a slice, the slice shows the status of its latest build. Open the build of a slice to see its progress, attempts, specification results and assumptions. | Status | What it means | | ----------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------- | | **Building** | A builder is working on the slice. | | **Built** | The build finished. | | **Nothing to build** | The slice needed no work. | | **Blocked** | The build cannot go on. | | **Failed** | The build did not succeed. | | **Builder stopped answering** | The builder went quiet while it held the work. | | **Ran out of time** | The build did not finish before its deadline. | | **Cancelled** | The build was stopped. | A build can also report **assumptions to review**: gaps the builder filled in. Accept one if the builder filled the gap the way it should be filled, or dismiss it, for example once the model says it. When the builder’s own results and the board’s run of the specifications differ, the build says that they disagree on a number of specifications. ## Specification results [Section titled “Specification results”](#specification-results) | Status | What it means | | ----------------------------- | -------------------------------------- | | **Not run** | The specification has not been run. | | **Running…** | A run is in progress. | | **Last run succeeded** | The model behaves as the example says. | | **Last run failed** | The model behaves differently. | | **Last run was inconclusive** | The run could not reach a verdict. | | **Could not run** | The run did not start. | ## Issue statuses [Section titled “Issue statuses”](#issue-statuses) Your own reports are listed under **My issues**, each with a **State** and its **Last activity**. A report you have just sent shows as **Reported**. See [Get help](/reference/get-help/#follow-your-reports). Issues that are linked to a slice show their own state, **Open**, **Closed** or **Unknown**, taken from the tracker the issue lives in. See [Link slices to issues](/deliver/issue-links/). ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Track progress](/deliver/track-progress/) * [Glossary](/reference/glossary/) * [Check the model](/board/check-the-model/)

# Design screens

> Show what people will see at each step of your model. Sketch it, build a screen, give the whole application one look, and try it out before anything is built.

A model that nobody can picture is hard to agree on. In Studio you can show the screens right next to the behavior they belong to: first as a rough sketch, then as a real screen that you can click through. app.cratis.studio ![The Products screen of Acme Shop with the shop logo, a filter box and product cards with prices](/_astro/screen-design.NN5kwhZd_25FxVf.webp) The Products screen of Acme Shop, drawn the way every screen of the application will be drawn. You do not need to write any code to do this. Designers, product owners and domain experts can all work here, and an AI agent can help when you describe what you want. ## Two places to design [Section titled “Two places to design”](#two-places-to-design) Screens are designed in two places, and each answers a different question. * **On the event model board**, a screen belongs to a slice. It shows what one person sees while one thing happens, such as the Shopper browsing products. This is where you add a screen, sketch it and edit its content. * **On the design canvas**, you see the screens of the whole application side by side, and the **UI profile** decides how all of them look: the logo, the colors, the font and the menu. ## What you can do here [Section titled “What you can do here”](#what-you-can-do-here) [Screens on slices ](/screens/screens-on-slices/)Add a screen to a slice, start empty or from a template, and see how it is opened. [The screen editor ](/screens/screen-editor/)Place components, bind them to data and to commands, and check the screen on a phone. [Sketches, mockups and images ](/screens/sketches-mockups-and-images/)Show a screen early with a drawing, a page of HTML and CSS, or a picture. [The design canvas ](/screens/design-canvas/)Design all the screens of an application together, by hand or by asking agents. [UI profile, blueprints and themes ](/screens/ui-profile-and-themes/)Choose the shell, logo, colors, font and menu once for the whole application. [Screen templates ](/screens/screen-templates/)Give a module or a feature a ready-made layout that its screens share. [Preview screens ](/screens/preview-screens/)Click through screens with sample data, as the person who will use them. ## Where this fits [Section titled “Where this fits”](#where-this-fits) Screens are one of the building blocks of an event model. To read about the idea first, see [Screens and personas](/event-modeling/screens-and-personas/). To add the slices that screens attach to, see [The event model board](/board/). When the screens look right, you can [check behavior](/check/) and [build and deliver](/deliver/) the application.

# Design all screens on the design canvas

> See the screens of an application side by side, change them by hand or by asking agents, and keep every change on a history you can step back through.

The design canvas is a big board for the screens of one application. Where the [screen editor](/screens/screen-editor/) shows you a single screen of a slice, the canvas shows the layouts that many screens share, side by side, so you can judge them together. ## Open the canvas [Section titled “Open the canvas”](#open-the-canvas) In the project drawer, open the application and select **Design**. app.cratis.studio ![The design canvas of Acme Shop with three artboards named ProductGrid, SideSummary and StorefrontShell, a tool rail on the left and the chat bar at the bottom](/_astro/design-canvas-shop.9vhcdtSL_1VeUAw.webp) The design canvas of Acme Shop. Each artboard is one screen layout of the application. Each **artboard** is a screen layout of the application, drawn at a size: **1280** wide for a regular screen and **390** wide for a phone. Every template gets an artboard the first time you open the canvas. Removing an artboard (**Remove from the canvas**) removes only the drawing; the template stays. Everything is drawn from the application’s [UI profile](/screens/ui-profile-and-themes/): its blueprint templates, the components they accept, and the theme, accent, font and menu. The screens of single slices are still designed on the event model board with [the screen editor](/screens/screen-editor/). ## Move around the canvas [Section titled “Move around the canvas”](#move-around-the-canvas) Drag the empty canvas to pan. Pinch, or hold Cmd (Mac) or Ctrl and scroll, to zoom. The controls at the bottom right offer **Zoom out**, **Zoom in**, **Zoom to 100%** and **Fit everything**. Delete removes what is selected. The tool rail on the left has: | Tool | What it does | | ---------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | **Select (V)** | Select and drag artboards, notes and regions. | | **Add note (N)** | Click where a sticky note should go. | | **Add region (R)** | Drag to draw a region around artboards and notes that belong together. Double-click its name to rename it, or use the wrap button to wrap it around what it holds. | | **Add chat bubble (C)** | Click where a comment thread should go, on the canvas or on an element of a screen. | | **Components** | Open the components you can place. | | **New screen from a template** | Add a screen that starts from a blueprint template. | | **Phone artboard for the active screen** | Add the phone layout of the selected screen. | | **UI profile look** | Open the **Look** panel of the Inspector. | Notes take the same sticky note colors as on the board. See [Notes, comments and topics](/board/notes-comments-and-topics/). ## Add a screen [Section titled “Add a screen”](#add-a-screen) 1. Select **New screen from a template** on the rail. 2. Under **Name**, enter a name such as `Orders`. 3. Under **Start from**, choose a blueprint screen template and select **Create**. 4. The new screen appears as an artboard. Select it to change it in the Inspector. ## Change a screen [Section titled “Change a screen”](#change-a-screen) Select an artboard or something on it. The **Inspector** has four tabs: **Properties**, **Structure**, **Look** and **Tweaks**. * A component from the application’s shell is marked **Inherited**. A screen cannot change it; the UI profile changes it for every screen at once. * **Show alternatives from other templates** tries your screen in the layouts of other templates. * **Look** sets the name, logo initials, shell, menu, color scheme, theme, accent, font and corners of the application. It is the same Look as the [UI profile](/screens/ui-profile-and-themes/). ## Ask agents to design [Section titled “Ask agents to design”](#ask-agents-to-design) The chat bar at the bottom talks to the agents of your organization. Say what you want, such as “give the order summary a total at the bottom”, and the first line starts a **design topic**. Later lines continue it. See topics under **Topics**. * The agent answers with edits. A card under the answer says how many were **applied** and how many were **refused**, and why. A refusal changes nothing and offers a nearby alternative as a button when there is one. * When you ask for different layouts, the agent offers **Variations**, labeled **Option A**, **Option B** and so on. Select **Keep** on the one you like. It replaces the current layout. * Ask for sliders (“give me sliders for this screen”) or select **Suggest tweaks for this screen**. The **Tweaks** tab then shows controls tied to settings of the profile. Dragging applies the change live, and one drag is one undo step. If no agent is set up for screen design, a built-in helper answers from what the profile offers. The card says **built-in**. To set up agents, see [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/). The chat bubbles you pin to a screen hold comment threads. A thread can be handed to the agents. They answer in a design topic that starts with the comment and the screen element it is pinned to. ## Step back, and find problems [Section titled “Step back, and find problems”](#step-back-and-find-problems) * **History** lists every change as a named step, whether you made it, the Inspector made it or an agent did, for example “Add Dropdown”. Select **Undo** or **Redo**, or pick a step to go back to it. * **Problems** lists what is wrong, by screen: a blocker, a warning or just information. Typical problems are an empty slot, a component whose query or command is gone, or an accent color without enough contrast to read. Select a problem to go to it. ## Preview and export [Section titled “Preview and export”](#preview-and-export) * **Preview** shows an artboard full size, with the application’s menu working so you can click between screens. Leave with **Exit preview** or Esc. For previews with sample data and personas, see [Preview screens](/screens/preview-screens/). * **Export** shows the design as **Screenplay**, as the stored **UI profile**, or as the **Scene document**. Select **Copy** to take it with you. Share the Screenplay with a developer; see [Screenplay files](/developers/screenplay-files/). ## What happens next [Section titled “What happens next”](#what-happens-next) * Set the look of every screen in [UI profile, blueprints and themes](/screens/ui-profile-and-themes/). * Share layouts with [screen templates](/screens/screen-templates/). * Try the result with [Preview screens](/screens/preview-screens/).

# Preview screens with sample data and personas

> Click through a module, a feature or a slice the way the person using the application will, with sample data on the screens and as a persona.

Preview shows your screens working, before anything is built. You see the Basket with items in it, press its buttons, move between screens and find out what a Shopper can and cannot do. Nothing is saved and nothing is started: it is safe to try anything. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) You need screens on your slices. See [Add a screen to a slice](/screens/screens-on-slices/). The look comes from the application’s [UI profile](/screens/ui-profile-and-themes/), not from Studio’s own dark theme. ## Start a preview [Section titled “Start a preview”](#start-a-preview) Preview starts from the place you are working: * **On the board**, select the eye icon, **Preview**, in the header of a module, a feature or a slice. A module or a feature previews inside the application. A slice previews only itself: its dialog on its own, or its page. * **In the screen editor**, select **Preview this screen with the changes not yet saved**. The screens you have open and have not saved are used, so you see your edit straight away. * **On the design canvas**, select **Preview** in the top bar, or the preview button on an artboard. The board stays where it was under the preview. When you leave with **Exit preview · Esc**, you are back at the same selection, and the screen editor, if it was open, is still open. ## Use the preview strip [Section titled “Use the preview strip”](#use-the-preview-strip) A strip across the top says what you are previewing, for example *Previewing Storefront › Basket › Add to basket*. Use it to change what the preview shows. 1. Under **Sample data:**, choose the data the screens show. **Generated** makes up rows from each field’s type. **My data sets** are the ones you added, and **Blueprints** are packs that blueprints come with. 2. Under **As persona:**, choose who is looking. **Nobody in particular** shows everything. Choose a persona, such as the Shopper or the Merchandiser, to see the application as that person. What the persona’s policies do not allow is hidden or disabled, with the reason. 3. Turn on **Bindings lens** to see what each element is connected to: the slice it opens, the query it reads and the filter it feeds. Sample text that is bound to nothing is flagged, so you can make it a real field before building. 4. Click a menu entry or a button to move between screens. Your choice of sample data in the strip is for this preview only. The application’s own choice is made once, in [the UI profile](/screens/ui-profile-and-themes/), under **Sample data**. That is also where you add your own data sets: paste rows as JSON or CSV, or load a file, and map each set to the read model it stands in for. Production builds never include sample data. ## When it cannot be previewed [Section titled “When it cannot be previewed”](#when-it-cannot-be-previewed) If the model cannot be drawn, for example because a feature has no screen or a slice has no command, the strip says **This cannot be previewed** and why, with a link to the part of the model it is about. You are never left with a blank screen. ## Preview or Play? [Section titled “Preview or Play?”](#preview-or-play) Preview draws screens with sample data in place. It is quick, and you can start it from any slice. [Play](/check/play/) runs the whole modeled application, with its commands and events working for real. Use Preview while you design, and Play when you want to try the behavior. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The preview shows your screens with rows of sample data. Switching **As persona:** changes what is shown or disabled, and each disabled thing says why. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [The screen editor](/screens/screen-editor/) * [Screens and personas](/event-modeling/screens-and-personas/) * [Check behavior](/check/)

# Design a screen in the screen editor

> Place components on a screen, bind them to the data and commands of the slice, and check the result on a desktop and a phone.

The screen editor is where a screen of a slice takes shape. You drop components onto it, such as text, product cards, a filter or a button, and connect them to what the slice knows: the fields of its command and the rows of its read models. Nothing here needs code. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * A slice with a [screen](/screens/screens-on-slices/). The editor opens over the board and the board stays visible behind it. * A slice that is done or under review opens **Read only**: its screen can be looked at but not changed. ## Open the editor [Section titled “Open the editor”](#open-the-editor) On the slice, select the wireframe icon, **Open prototype editor**. The editor opens as a window titled with the screen, the slice type and the person it is for, for example *ProductBrowse · screen of State View slice · Shopper*. app.cratis.studio ![The screen editor on the ProductBrowse screen, with Design, Describe and Screenplay tabs, a Components and Data bar, the screen in the middle and the Inspector with Properties and Structure on the right](/_astro/prototype-editor.C4oAmbiF_1ODRgN.webp) The editor: the parts of the screen on the left, the screen in the middle and the Inspector on the right. Closing the window saves the screen and returns you to the board. While you work, the window says **Unsaved changes** or **All changes saved**. Use **Save**, **Undo** and **Redo** in the title bar as you need them. If you close with unsaved changes, the editor asks whether to **Keep editing** or to **Discard changes and close**. ## Find your way around the editor [Section titled “Find your way around the editor”](#find-your-way-around-the-editor) | Where | What it is for | | -------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | **Design** tab | The surface where you build the screen. | | **Describe** tab | A plain-language description of the screen: where it opens, which template it uses, what data it is bound to and which slots it has. | | **Screenplay** tab | The screen as it was last saved, written in Screenplay. Read it, or share it with a developer. | | Parts of the screen (left) | **Opens & fits**, **Data context**, **Form**, **Outlets**, **Slots** and **Issued from**. Select one to see its details. | | Inspector (right) | **Properties** for the selected component and **Structure** for the tree of the whole screen. | ## Add components [Section titled “Add components”](#add-components) 1. On the **Design** tab, select **Components**. The drawer lists the components your application offers. Type in **Filter components** to find one, or look under **Frequently used**. 2. Drag a component onto the screen. A container shows **Empty - drop a component here**; drop between two elements to put it between them. 3. Select the component. The **Properties** tab of the Inspector shows what you can set on it. Changes show on the screen straight away. 4. To change the order, drag the component, or open **Structure** and use **Move up**, **Move down**, **Move into previous sibling** and **Move out of parent**. 5. To remove a component, select it and use **Delete selected component**. You decide how components sit together with the layout containers in the **Layout** drawer: Stack, Row, Grid, Container, Wrap and Canvas. ## Bind the screen to data [Section titled “Bind the screen to data”](#bind-the-screen-to-data) Select **Data** to open the Data drawer. It lists what the slice can use. * On a **State Change** slice, it lists the properties of the slice’s command. Drag a property onto the screen, or select it, to make a field for it. **Generate the form** makes a field for every property at once, and the drawer tells you when **Every property is on the form**. * On any slice, it lists the queries of the feature. Drag a query onto the screen to show its rows as a table or its result as a summary. Selecting a column in the drawer shows or hides it. * Select a component and look at **Properties** to choose a **Query** or to clear a binding. The editor checks the screen as you go. At the bottom you see **CHECKED No problems found**, or what is wrong: a field that the command does not have, or a form that submits a command the application does not have. ## Let a button do something [Section titled “Let a button do something”](#let-a-button-do-something) A button can open a dialog or run the command of a slice. 1. Add a button from the **Components** drawer. 2. Select it. In **Properties**, the button offers **Open a dialog** and **Run a slice command**. Use **Choose what it opens or runs** to pick the target. When the button runs a command, the slice that owns the command shows that it is **issued from** this screen. See [Add a screen to a slice](/screens/screens-on-slices/). ## Choose where the screen fits [Section titled “Choose where the screen fits”](#choose-where-the-screen-fits) In **Opens & fits**, tick where the screen can appear: **dialog**, **drawer**, **pane**, **panel** or **page**. The order matters: when a button opens the screen, Studio picks the first place that the page has free. Each place has arrows to change the order, named for it, such as **Prefer dialog earlier** and **Prefer dialog later**. If the screen does not say where it fits, Studio assumes a dialog. ## Check it on a phone [Section titled “Check it on a phone”](#check-it-on-a-phone) The title bar has **Desktop preview** and **Mobile preview**. Mobile shows the screen at a phone width, where layouts can change. Two more buttons help you see the whole picture: * **Show application shell** draws the menu and header of the application around the screen. * **Show the template around this content** draws the template that frames the screen. To click through the screen as a real user would, select **Preview this screen with the changes not yet saved**. See [Preview screens](/screens/preview-screens/). ## Draft with AI [Section titled “Draft with AI”](#draft-with-ai) If your organization has an agent set up for screen design, **Draft with AI** proposes additions for you. You describe them in a sentence, select **Generate draft**, read the proposed additions, and then **Preview draft**, **Apply draft** or **Discard**. A draft only adds labels, ordinary inputs and Stack and Row containers. It does not bind data or run commands, so do that yourself after you apply it. Applying a draft is one step you can undo. To set up agents, see [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/). ## What happens next [Section titled “What happens next”](#what-happens-next) * Give every screen of the application one look with [the UI profile](/screens/ui-profile-and-themes/). * Share a layout between screens with [screen templates](/screens/screen-templates/). * Check the screen with [Preview](/screens/preview-screens/).

# Give a module or feature a screen template

> Choose a ready-made layout for a module or a feature, let its screens inherit it, and browse, copy and make your own templates.

A screen template is a layout that screens share: where the header goes, where the filter sits and where the list is drawn. Instead of laying out every screen of the Storefront by hand, you choose a template once on the module or the feature, and every screen below it is drawn with it. ## How templates are inherited [Section titled “How templates are inherited”](#how-templates-are-inherited) A template chosen on a level applies to everything below it, unless a lower level chooses its own: **application root template → module → feature → slice** A feature in the Storefront module that sets no template uses the module’s. A slice that sets no template uses its feature’s. The header of each level says where its template comes from, for example **Using ProductGrid, inherited from the module**. Screens that are pages are always framed by the application’s own root template or shell as well. That one is not chosen on the board but in the [UI profile](/screens/ui-profile-and-themes/). The header says so and offers **Open the UI profile**. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * A module or a feature on the board. See [Modules and features](/event-modeling/modules-and-features/). * To use a blueprint’s templates, the blueprint must be part of the application’s UI profile. See [UI profile, blueprints and themes](/screens/ui-profile-and-themes/). ## Choose a template for a module or a feature [Section titled “Choose a template for a module or a feature”](#choose-a-template-for-a-module-or-a-feature) 1. In the header of the module or the feature, select the **Screen Template** icon to open its screen template section. It is titled **Template of this module** or **Template of this feature**. If nothing is chosen, it reads **No template**. 2. Select **Choose a template** (or the arrow at the right of the **No template** bar). The picker opens with **Filter templates…** at the top. It lists **My templates**, your own, and **Blueprints**, the ready-made ones, that fit the level. When none fits, it says **No template applies here.** Below the list are **Browse all…** and **New template from current…**. 3. Select a template. The header shows a preview of it and says **Using** that template. 4. To go back to what the level above uses, select **Use the inherited template**. app.cratis.studio ![The template picker open on the Basket feature, below the No template bar: a Filter templates box, the line No template applies here, and the choices Browse all and New template from current](/_astro/template-picker.BHBHYyP4_Z1BkBM7.webp) The picker for the Basket feature. Here no template applies, so look to the two choices at the bottom: Browse all... opens the gallery, and New template from current... makes a template from what you have. If a template is later removed, or no longer fits the level, the header says so, and you can choose another or go back to the inherited one. ## Change what the screens contain [Section titled “Change what the screens contain”](#change-what-the-screens-contain) The header has two ways to design the template’s content, and they differ in who shares the change: * **Design the content** opens the template designer. Every level that chose this template shares your change. * **Customize the content** makes a template of its own for this level from the one in use, chooses it here, and opens the designer on it. Only this level changes. In the designer you drag components into the slots of the template, the same way as in [the screen editor](/screens/screen-editor/). ## Hook a state change into a template [Section titled “Hook a state change into a template”](#hook-a-state-change-into-a-template) A template can have **outlets**, places that the screens below it fill, such as a place for actions. A level can hook a state change into an outlet of its template: select **Hook a state change into** the outlet, then **Find a state change**. A level can only hook into the outlets that its template offers. If it offers none, the header says so and you can **Add an outlet to the template**. ## Browse all templates [Section titled “Browse all templates”](#browse-all-templates) Select **Browse the gallery** in the header, or **Browse all…** in the picker, to open **Templates**: every template you can build screens from, yours to manage and those of every blueprint to browse, preview and copy. * Narrow the list by **Category**, or under **Where templates come from** choose **All templates**, **My templates** or **Blueprints**. * Select a template to see its facts in the pane: the blueprint it belongs to, the category, the levels it fits, the slice kinds, the license and the kind of template: **Shell**, **Screen template** or **Dialog template**. * A preview shows it as **Populated**, **Empty**, **Loading** or **Error**, at **Desktop**, **Tablet** or **Phone** width. **Preview the whole blueprint** shows a blueprint’s whole application. * Badges tell you more: **Mine**, **Blueprint**, **In use**, **Blueprint not added** and **Does not fit here**. For a template that does not fit, the pane says why. What you can do with a template: | Action | When | | ----------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | **Use this template** | Choose it for the level you came from. | | **Copy to my templates** | Take a blueprint template as your own, so that you can change it. | | **Add blueprint to this application** | The template belongs to a blueprint that is not part of the application yet. You can look at it first. | | **New template**, **Edit**, **Rename**, **Duplicate** | Make and change templates of your own. | | **Delete** | Remove one of your templates. Anything still using it no longer finds it, and this cannot be undone. | | **Import** and **Export** | Bring a template in from a file, or take one out. | ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The header of the module or the feature says **Using** your template, and the screens below it are drawn with it. [Preview](/screens/preview-screens/) them to see. ## What happens next [Section titled “What happens next”](#what-happens-next) * [Preview screens](/screens/preview-screens/) with sample data. * Set the look of every template in the [UI profile](/screens/ui-profile-and-themes/). * Read about the idea in [Screens and personas](/event-modeling/screens-and-personas/).

# Add a screen to a slice

> Give a state change or state view slice a screen, start it empty or from a template, and see how that screen is reached.

A screen shows what one person sees while a slice happens. A Shopper browsing the catalog sees the product list; the same Shopper sees the basket when they add an item. Adding a screen to the slice puts that picture next to the commands and read models it uses. You can add a screen to a **State Change** slice, where the screen takes the fields of the command, and to a **State View** slice, where the screen shows what a read model holds. For the four kinds of slice, see [Slices](/event-modeling/slices/). ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * You need a board with a slice that has a command or a read model. See [The event model board](/board/). * A screen belongs to the person it is for. Put the screen in that person’s row, such as the Shopper row of the Storefront. * A slice that is marked done or is under review cannot be changed. Its screen can be looked at, and the editor says so with **Read only**. ## Add a screen [Section titled “Add a screen”](#add-a-screen) 1. On the slice, in the row of the person it is for, select **Add UI prototype**. 2. Choose how to start: * **From scratch** starts with an empty prototype. * **From template** starts from a copy of an existing screen template. Pick one from the list. Templates are described in [Screen templates](/screens/screen-templates/). 3. The screen appears on the slice as a small thumbnail. Select the wireframe icon, **Open prototype editor**, to work on it in [the screen editor](/screens/screen-editor/). app.cratis.studio ![The screen editor open on the ProductBrowse screen of the Storefront board, with the parts of the screen listed on the left and the Inspector on the right](/_astro/prototype-editor.C4oAmbiF_1ODRgN.webp) The ProductBrowse screen opened from its slice. The list on the left shows the parts of the screen. ## See how a screen is reached [Section titled “See how a screen is reached”](#see-how-a-screen-is-reached) A screen is only useful if someone can get to it. The slice shows where it comes from: | What the slice says | What it means | | ------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **own screen** | The slice has a screen of its own. When the screen opens in a dialog or a drawer, the slice says which. | | **no screen** | The slice has no screen. A command can still be run from a button on another screen. | | **issued from** | A button on another screen runs this command. It shows the screen, the place on it and the button, for example *Product browse ▸ actions ▸ Add to basket*. | | **issued nowhere** | No screen has a button that runs this command. The slice reads: “No screen and no hook. Add a button that issues it, or delete the slice.” | When a slice has a screen but no button anywhere opens it, the slice reads “Nothing opens this screen yet”. Select the hint to go to a screen where a button can be added. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The slice now shows a thumbnail of its screen. The editor lists the parts of the screen on the left: **Opens & fits**, **Data context**, **Form**, **Outlets**, **Slots** and **Issued from**. ## What happens next [Section titled “What happens next”](#what-happens-next) * Place components and bind them in [the screen editor](/screens/screen-editor/). * Try the screen the way the Shopper sees it with [Preview](/screens/preview-screens/). * Read about the idea in [Screens and personas](/event-modeling/screens-and-personas/).

# Show a screen with a sketch, a mockup or an image

> Put a drawing, a page of HTML and CSS, or a picture on a screen before it is designed, and turn it into a real screen when you are ready.

Early in modeling you often need to show a screen before you have designed it. A screen on a slice can carry three kinds of visual, beside its real screen or instead of one: | Kind | What it is | Good for | | ---------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------ | | **Sketch** | A wireframe of boxes, pen strokes and labels that you draw in Studio. | A quick idea in a workshop. | | **Mockup** | One or more pages of HTML and CSS that you write. | A closer look at layout and wording. | | **Image** | A screenshot or a design export: PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP, up to 5 MB. | A design made in another tool. | The visual sits in the screen’s cell on the slice, in the row of the person the screen is for. The newest one shows there, and selecting it opens it larger. The screen also shows how many of each it has: **Sketches**, **mockup pages** and **images**. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) You need a slice with a [screen cell](/screens/screens-on-slices/) in the row of the person it is for. ## Add a sketch [Section titled “Add a sketch”](#add-a-sketch) 1. On the screen, select **Add a sketch, mockup or image to the screen**. 2. Choose **Draw a sketch**. The description reads “Wireframe the screen with a pen, boxes and labels”. 3. Pick a **Drawing tool**: **Pen** to draw freely, **Box** to drag out a rectangle, or **Label** to write text. For a label, type the **Label text**, then click where it goes on the **Sketch canvas**. 4. Use **Undo** to take back the last stroke, or **Clear** to start again. Give the sketch a **Title**. 5. Select **Save**. ## Write a mockup page [Section titled “Write a mockup page”](#write-a-mockup-page) 1. Select **Add a sketch, mockup or image to the screen** and choose **Write a mockup page**. It writes the screen “as an HTML and CSS page”. 2. Type or paste your page into **HTML and CSS**. The **Mockup preview** shows how the page looks. 3. Give it a **Title** and select **Save**. A mockup is a picture, not a program The preview is shown in a sandbox. Scripts do not run, nothing is sent anywhere, and styles and images have to be written inline in the page. Markup that relies on script is shown as you wrote it, without its behavior. This keeps a mockup safe to open, whoever wrote it. ## Attach an image [Section titled “Attach an image”](#attach-an-image) 1. Select **Add a sketch, mockup or image to the screen** and choose **Attach an image**. The description reads “A screenshot or a picture of the screen”. 2. Select **Choose an image** and pick a PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP file of at most 5 MB. Other kinds of file are refused, and so is SVG. 3. Select **Save**. ## Turn a visual into a real screen [Section titled “Turn a visual into a real screen”](#turn-a-visual-into-a-real-screen) When the idea has settled, **Convert to prototype** writes a real screen from the visual. It is available in the sketch, the mockup page and the image. * The conversion is done by your organization’s screen design agent, so one has to be set up. See [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/). * The new screen is checked before it is saved. If it does not check out, Studio tells you why and changes nothing. * If the slice already has a screen, Studio asks **Replace the prototype?** first. The visual is kept either way. After the conversion, continue in [the screen editor](/screens/screen-editor/). ## Remove a visual [Section titled “Remove a visual”](#remove-a-visual) Open the visual and select **Remove**. Studio asks you to confirm with **Remove from the screen**. ## Share and export [Section titled “Share and export”](#share-and-export) Visuals travel with the model. When you export the board as Screenplay files, every visual is saved as a line on its screen and a file beside it. Importing those files brings the visuals back onto the same screens. If an imported model names a file that the archive does not carry, Studio reports it and finishes the rest of the import. Importing the same model again updates the visuals; it does not add copies. Keeping a board in sync with Git carries them too. For the files themselves, see [Screenplay files](/developers/screenplay-files/). ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The visual shows in the screen’s cell, and the count of sketches, mockup pages and images on the screen has gone up by one. ## Related pages [Section titled “Related pages”](#related-pages) * [Add a screen to a slice](/screens/screens-on-slices/) * [The screen editor](/screens/screen-editor/) * [Export and import a model](/share/export/)

# Set the look of an application with its UI profile

> Choose the name, logo, shell, colors, font and menu once, and every screen of the application is drawn the same way.

The UI profile says how an application looks and what its screens are built from. You set it once, and every screen, preview and run of the application follows it. That is why the Acme Shop screens all share one logo, one accent color and one menu. ## Open the UI profile [Section titled “Open the UI profile”](#open-the-ui-profile) In the project drawer, open the application and select **UI Profile**. The designer opens with a live preview of the application on the left and the settings on the right. app.cratis.studio ![The UI profile designer for Acme Shop: the Products screen drawn in a preview on the left, and the App settings with Name, Target platform, New screens start from and Logo on the right](/_astro/ui-profile-shop.BhpQ2FqQ_OUL4I.webp) The UI profile of Acme Shop. Change a setting on the right and the preview follows. The designer saves as you go. The top bar says **All changes saved**, or **Saving…** while it works. Use **Undo** and **Redo** there; they name the step, such as “Undo accent”. **Desktop** and **Mobile** switch the size of the preview. At the bottom, the status bar tells you about the size you are looking at and counts **problems**. You can also select something in the preview, such as the logo or the menu, to jump to its setting. The status bar reminds you: **Click the preview to edit it.** ## Pick a starting point [Section titled “Pick a starting point”](#pick-a-starting-point) A new application starts with the default look. If you have not chosen anything yet, the designer offers **Choose a starting point**: **Start from a blueprint**, **Use my own shell** or **Keep the defaults**. Everything stays editable and every change can be undone. A **blueprint** is a ready-made application to start from: a shell, screen templates and sample data. Adding one brings its templates into the template picker. See [Screen templates](/screens/screen-templates/). ## The sections of the UI profile [Section titled “The sections of the UI profile”](#the-sections-of-the-ui-profile) | Section | What you set there | | --------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **App** | The **Name** of the application, its **Logo**, which template **New screens start from** and the **Default size class**. | | **Shell** | The frame around every screen. **Change shell…** swaps it for another blueprint’s shell. **Create new shell…** starts your own. | | **Look** | The **Color scheme** (light or dark), the **Theme**, the **Accent** color, the **Font** and the **Corners**. | | **Menu** | The **Menu mode** and the **Menu theme**. | | **Packages** | Where components, styling and themes come from, in priority order. | | **Sample data** | What the preview and screens show while you design. | ### Name and logo [Section titled “Name and logo”](#name-and-logo) The name you give the application is used everywhere. Renaming it here renames it everywhere. For the logo, choose **Initials**, **Icon** or **Image**: * **Initials**: one or two characters on the accent color. Empty uses the first letter of the name. * **Icon**: pick one, such as Box, Layers, Home, Package or Palette. * **Image**: **Choose an image**, a PNG, JPG, SVG or WebP of up to 400 KB. Square images work best. **Replace image** and **Remove image** change it later. Switch **Show the name next to the logo** on or off. Every blueprint draws the logo and name in its own way: sidebar, top bar, slim rail or mobile header. ### Color scheme, theme, accent and font [Section titled “Color scheme, theme, accent and font”](#color-scheme-theme-accent-and-font) Under **Look**, choose **Light** or **Dark**, then a **Theme**. A theme that needs another package says **Needs** that package and adds it when you select the theme. Set the **Accent**, the color of buttons and links; leave it empty to keep the theme’s own. Designers should look at the contrast line, for example *Contrast against white: 4.6:1 (AA)*. Studio flags an accent that **fails** or is suitable for large text only. The **Font** applies to the whole application and shows a sample, and **Corners** are **Sharp**, **Theme default** or **Round**. ### Menu [Section titled “Menu”](#menu) **Menu mode** is how the shell shows navigation: **Static**, **Slim**, **Slim+**, **Overlay**, **Drawer**, **Reveal** or **Horizontal**. Overlay, Drawer and Reveal respond in the preview. **Menu theme** colors the menu only: **Light**, **Dark** or **Primary**, which uses the accent. On a touch screen the menu is a drawer. ### Packages [Section titled “Packages”](#packages) Packages hold the components, styling and icons that screens are built from. When two packages supply the same component, the one higher in the list wins. Drag a package to change the order, or use **Move … up** and **Move … down**. Use **Add a package** to bring in another, and **Which package supplies a component?** to look up where a component comes from. ### Sample data [Section titled “Sample data”](#sample-data) Pick the **Sample data pack** that previews, screens and runs show. Production builds never include sample data. See [Preview screens](/screens/preview-screens/). ## Fix problems [Section titled “Fix problems”](#fix-problems) The status bar counts **problems**. Select it to open the list: each is a **Blocker**, a **Warning** or **Info**, with **Go to** the section that needs attention and often a **Fix** button. ## What happens next [Section titled “What happens next”](#what-happens-next) * See the result on [the design canvas](/screens/design-canvas/). * Choose a layout for each module and feature with [screen templates](/screens/screen-templates/).

# Share and export

> Get a model out of Studio and in front of other people, as a link, a file, a Miro board or a read-only view that needs no account.

Share and export ## Put the model in front of the people who need it Not everyone who needs to see a model has an account, or wants one. Studio gives you a link to look at, files to keep, and a board to draw on Miro. Everything on this page starts in the same place: **Project options** at the top left of the board. Its **Export** submenu holds the ways to send a model out of Studio, and its **Import** submenu, covered in [Import a model](/discover/import-a-model/), brings one in. app.cratis.studio ![The Project options menu on an event model board, listing Export, Import, Progress board, Diagrams and Project Settings](/_astro/project-options-menu.DJhB1qFE_Zl6zzf.webp) Project options holds Export, Import and Diagrams for the open event model. ## What you can do here [Section titled “What you can do here”](#what-you-can-do-here) [Share a read-only snapshot link ](/share/snapshot-link/)Let anyone see the board as it is now, without signing in. [Export the model ](/share/export/)Download a Screenplay file or folder, a Mermaid diagram or Event Modeling JSON. [Draw a board on Miro ](/share/miro/)Put the model on a Miro board, a frame per slice. [Show a model anywhere with the Viewer ](/share/viewer/)Open any public Screenplay file as a read-only board. ## Which one should I use? [Section titled “Which one should I use?”](#which-one-should-i-use) | You want to… | Use | | ----------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------- | | Let someone look at the board right now, without an account | A [snapshot link](/share/snapshot-link/) | | Keep the model in a repository, or move it to another tool | An [export](/share/export/) | | Run a workshop where people already use Miro | [Draw on Miro](/share/miro/) | | Put a model in a README or a blog post | The [Viewer](/share/viewer/) | | Keep the model in step with a repository as you work | [Git sync](/deliver/git-sync/) | ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Import a model](/discover/import-a-model/), to bring a model in * [Connect Miro](/organization/miro/), the one-time setup for Miro * [Screenplay files](/developers/screenplay-files/)

# Export the model

> Download an event model as a Screenplay file or folder, a Mermaid diagram or Event Modeling JSON, and read what the export could not carry.

Export gives you the model as a file you can keep, put in a repository, hand to a colleague or open in another tool. The board is not changed by an export. What you'll learn * choose the right format for what you want to do * export the open event model, or only a feature * read the warnings that say what a file could not carry * look at the model as UML diagrams ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) Open the event model you want to export. Export works on the open event model. ## Export a model [Section titled “Export a model”](#export-a-model) 1. Select **Project options** at the top left, then **Export**. app.cratis.studio ![The Project options menu with the Export submenu open](/_astro/export-menu.Cr3REasF_Z1Hxy7E.webp) The Export submenu lists every format Studio can write. 2. Choose a format from the table below. 3. Your browser downloads the file. A notice says **Export ready**, or **Export ready - some of the model could not be carried** when the file leaves something out. | Choice | What you get | Use it for | | ----------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | **Screenplay file** | One Cratis Screenplay (`.play`) file for the whole event model. | Handing the model to a person or a tool in one piece. | | **Screenplay folder** | A ZIP of `.play` files, one for each module, feature and slice, laid out as folders. | Keeping the model in a repository: small files give small differences and sensible merges. | | **Mermaid** | A Mermaid event modeling diagram, as text. | Putting a picture of the model in a README or a wiki that renders Mermaid. | | **Event Modeling JSON** | A document in the official Event Modeling Specification format. | Moving the model to another event modeling tool. | Screenplay is the text format that describes a model. The [Screenplay files](/developers/screenplay-files/) page shows what is inside and how it round-trips with a repository. To read a file back into a board, see [Import a model](/discover/import-a-model/). ### Export only a feature or a module [Section titled “Export only a feature or a module”](#export-only-a-feature-or-a-module) For Mermaid you can narrow down. Open the options of a module or a feature on the board and choose **Export module to Mermaid** or **Export feature to Mermaid**. ## Read what the export could not carry [Section titled “Read what the export could not carry”](#read-what-the-export-could-not-carry) A model on the board can hold more than a file format can express. Studio tells you when that happens, in the **Export ready - some of the model could not be carried** notice and in the file’s diagnostics. Read them before you treat a file as a complete copy. Typical causes you may meet: * two specifications in one slice whose names are the same once generated: rename one, then export again (see [names](/check/specifications/#names)); * a redelivery in a specification that cannot be pinned to one given event; * a rule on a concept that can never hold: the concept is left out and the rest still exports. ## Diagrams are not exports [Section titled “Diagrams are not exports”](#diagrams-are-not-exports) **Project options** also has **Diagrams**, which draws the model as a **UML state diagram**, **UML activity diagram**, **UML sequence diagram** or **Entity data model** on screen. app.cratis.studio ![The Project options menu with the Diagrams submenu open, listing four UML and entity diagrams](/_astro/diagrams-menu.DlZN5KPy_Km3pc.webp) Diagrams are drawn inside Studio from the model. They are for looking at, and they follow the model as it changes. See [Context map and diagrams](/discover/context-map-and-diagrams/). ## More ways to hand a model over [Section titled “More ways to hand a model over”](#more-ways-to-hand-a-model-over) The Export submenu has more than files: * **Share snapshot link** gives someone a read-only view with no sign-in. See [Share a read-only snapshot link](/share/snapshot-link/). * **Draw on Miro** draws the board on a Miro board. See [Draw a board on Miro](/share/miro/). * **Context for an agent** hands part of the model to an AI coding agent. See [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/). ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) Open the downloaded file. A Screenplay file is plain text, and a folder export unzips into folders that mirror the board’s modules, features and slices. If you want to be sure it is whole, import it into an empty project and compare. Recap You exported a model as a Screenplay file or folder, a Mermaid diagram or Event Modeling JSON, and learned where to look when something could not be carried. Next, [share a read-only link](/share/snapshot-link/) or [show the model with the Viewer](/share/viewer/).

# Draw a board on Miro

> Draw your event model on a Miro board, a frame per slice with every element as a sticky note in its event modeling color.

Many teams run workshops on Miro. **Draw on Miro** puts your event model there, so people who live in Miro can see it, move things around and comment, without needing Studio. What you'll learn * draw an event model on a Miro board * know how the drawing is laid out * know what Studio does and does not keep in step ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * Your organization is connected to Miro, which a maintainer does under **Settings** > **Integrations**. See [Connect integrations](/organization/integrations/). Without it, the dialog says the organization has no Miro access token and tells you to ask a maintainer. * The token must be allowed to write boards, not only read them. * You have the link of an empty or existing Miro board to draw on. * The event model you want to draw is open on the board. ## Draw the board [Section titled “Draw the board”](#draw-the-board) 1. Select **Project options** at the top left, then **Export**, then **Draw on Miro**. 2. In **Draw on Miro**, paste the **Miro board link**. 3. Select **Draw on Miro**. It reads **Drawing…** while it works. 4. When it finishes, Studio tells you how many frames and notes it drew. Select **Open the Miro board** to look. app.cratis.studio ![The Draw on Miro dialog with the text that Studio adds a frame per slice, a notice that the organization has no Miro access token, the empty Miro board link box, and the Draw on Miro and Close buttons](/_astro/miro-draw-dialog.DpvoJfDG_10Fun9.webp) The dialog explains what will be drawn. Here the organization has no Miro access token yet, and the dialog says so: a maintainer adds one before Draw on Miro will work. The link box is where you paste your board's address. ## How it is drawn [Section titled “How it is drawn”](#how-it-is-drawn) Studio adds one frame for each slice, left to right in timeline order. Each element of the slice is a sticky note in its event modeling color. Event modeling uses orange for events, blue for commands and green for read models, and Studio reads the same colors when you [import a Miro board](/discover/import-a-model/). Nothing that is already on the Miro board is changed or removed. Studio only adds. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) Open the Miro board. You should see one frame for each slice of the event model, in order, with colored sticky notes inside. Recap You drew an event model on Miro. If the connection to Miro is not set up yet, a maintainer can do it in [Your organization](/organization/). Other ways to share: [a read-only snapshot link](/share/snapshot-link/) or [an export](/share/export/).

# Share a read-only snapshot link

> Give anyone a link to see your board as it is right now, without signing in, and take the link back when you are done.

Sometimes the person who needs to see the model is not in your organization: a customer, a manager, a reviewer. A snapshot link shows them your board as it is now, read-only, with no account. What you'll learn * create a link for the whole board or for one feature * choose how long it works * see the links you have shared, and revoke one ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) Open the event model you want to share. A snapshot is of one event model. ## Create a link [Section titled “Create a link”](#create-a-link) 1. Select **Project options** at the top left, then **Export**, then **Share snapshot link**. app.cratis.studio ![The Project options menu with the Export submenu open](/_astro/export-menu.Cr3REasF_Z1Hxy7E.webp) Share snapshot link is in the Export submenu. 2. In the **Share a snapshot** dialog, under **What to share**, choose **The whole board** or one feature. 3. Under **Link works for**, choose **Until revoked**, or 1, 7, 30 or 90 days. New links start at 30 days. 4. Select **Create link**. 5. Select **Copy** and send the link to the person. **Copy the link now - it is shown only this once.** Anyone with the link sees the board as it is right now, read-only, without signing in. Later changes to your board do not show up in a link you have already made. To share newer work, create a new link. If the board is too large to share, Studio says so and asks you to share one of its features instead. ## What the person sees [Section titled “What the person sees”](#what-the-person-sees) The link opens the snapshot in the [Viewer](/share/viewer/): the same board, with pan and zoom, and nothing to edit. ## See and revoke links [Section titled “See and revoke links”](#see-and-revoke-links) The same dialog lists **Shared snapshots** for this board, each with when it was shared and when it expires, or that it does not expire. A link that was revoked or has expired is marked **Revoked** or **Expired**. To take a link back, select **Revoke** next to it and confirm **Revoke this link?** Anyone who opens it afterwards sees that it no longer works. This cannot be undone: share again to get a new link. Caution Treat a link like a password to a read-only copy of your board. Anyone who has it can open it, and the link cannot be shown again after you close the dialog. Revoke links you no longer need. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) Open the copied link in a private browser window where you are not signed in. You should see the board. Then revoke it and reload: the page says the link no longer works. Recap You shared a read-only snapshot, chose how long it lasts, and learned how to revoke it. Other ways to hand a model to someone: [export it](/share/export/) as a file, or [show it with the Viewer](/share/viewer/).

# Show a model anywhere with the Viewer

> Open any public Screenplay file as a read-only board at view.cratis.studio, with pan and zoom and no sign-in.

The Viewer shows an event model that lives in a file. Give it the address of a public Screenplay (`.play`) file and it draws the model on the same board Studio uses, read-only, with pan and zoom. Nobody needs an account, so you can link to a model from a README, a blog post or an email. What you'll learn * open a public Screenplay file in the Viewer * understand what it shows and what it leaves out * know why a file may not open ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) You need a Screenplay file, or a model made of several, at a public web address. A file in a public repository works, as does any page that serves the file’s text. [Export the model](/share/export/) to get one. ## Open a file [Section titled “Open a file”](#open-a-file) 1. Take the address of the file, such as `https://example.com/models/shop.play`. 2. Encode it for a web address, so that characters like `:` and `/` are written as `%3A` and `%2F`. 3. Open `https://view.cratis.studio/?url=` followed by the encoded address. For the example above that is `https://view.cratis.studio/?url=https%3A%2F%2Fexample.com%2Fmodels%2Fshop.play`. The model appears with its modules, features, slices, commands, events, read models and specifications. Pan and zoom to move around. There is nothing to click into and nothing to edit. The page shows the name of the document and the host it came from, and a link to Cratis Studio. app.cratis.studio ![view.cratis.studio showing basket.play from raw.githubusercontent.com: the Basket feature with its slices and their specifications on the board, the buttons Show details, Fit to view and Open Cratis Studio at the top right, and the notice Some of the document is not shown (33) at the top left](/_astro/viewer-basket.CQo1Ey99_Z2dSBm4.webp) The name of the file and the host it came from are at the top left, with the Fit to view and Open Cratis Studio buttons at the right. The notice under the name, Some of the document is not shown, is explained below. ## What it leaves out [Section titled “What it leaves out”](#what-it-leaves-out) The board cannot hold everything a Screenplay file can say. When a file has parts it cannot draw, such as policies, personas, concepts, reactors or screens, the page lists them in a collapsed list above the board, and draws the rest. In the picture above, the notice reads **Some of the document is not shown** with a number, 33 for that file; **Show details** opens the list. If the file does not compile, the page lists the problems with the line and column of each. Fix the file and reload. ## Limits [Section titled “Limits”](#limits) The Viewer fetches the file for you, so it is deliberately strict about what it fetches: * the address must be an absolute `http` or `https` address; * the file must be reachable from the public internet. Addresses on private networks or on a machine of its own are refused, so a file on your laptop or behind a company network does not open; * a file can be at most 2 MB; * a slow or unreachable address is given up on after about 10 seconds. ## Check that it worked [Section titled “Check that it worked”](#check-that-it-worked) The board appears with the name of your document above it. If instead you see a message, it names the reason: the address is not valid, the host is not allowed, the file is too large, the address could not be reached, or the file did not compile. Recap You opened a public Screenplay file as a read-only board. Next, [share a snapshot](/share/snapshot-link/) from Studio, or read about [Screenplay files](/developers/screenplay-files/).

# Work with AI

> What the AI agents in Cratis Studio do for you, what they never do without you, and where your data goes when you use them.

Studio has a small team of AI agents that help you model. You can ask them to change the board, explain how a part of it works, suggest the next slice, or talk a flow through with you out loud. This section shows how. app.cratis.studio ![The chat bar at the bottom of an event model with an instruction typed but not sent](/_astro/chat-bar-instruction.UsgNEgvQ_Z2g8Fpw.webp) The chat bar sits at the bottom of every board. Type what you want, or say it. ## What AI does in Studio [Section titled “What AI does in Studio”](#what-ai-does-in-studio) * **Changes the board when you ask.** “Add an event called Author Registered” is an instruction, and Studio makes the change. * **Explains.** An agent can tell you how a domain, an event model, a module or a feature works. * **Generates.** An agent can draft a project, a domain, an event model, a feature or a slice from a description. * **Suggests.** Studio proposes slices, features or modules that fit what is already on the board. * **Talks with you.** You can type, dictate a line, or hold a spoken conversation with an agent. Every organization starts with a team of named agents: Atlas models, Merlin writes code, Quill writes documentation, Sherlock reviews, Echo carries the conversation, Hunch suggests what to model next, and Iris designs screens. Your organization can change them in [Agents and skills](/organization/agents-and-skills/). ## What it never does without you [Section titled “What it never does without you”](#what-it-never-does-without-you) * **Large or destructive changes are never made from speech alone.** Studio asks first, and you confirm by selecting **Yes** or typing yes. * **A thought is not an instruction.** When you say “Maybe we need something for cancellations”, Studio offers to do it and waits for your answer. * **A suggestion is only a suggestion.** It is added only when you add it, and you can preview it first. * **Everything is reported.** Every change made from the chat bar shows up in the board’s chat with the line that caused it. ## You choose the AI provider [Section titled “You choose the AI provider”](#you-choose-the-ai-provider) Studio does not come with a language model. Your organization connects its own AI provider with its own API key, and gives each agent a provider and a model. Until an agent is set up, the AI features stay switched off, and Studio says so. That choice is also a statement about your data. What an agent needs to do your request, such as your instruction and the part of the model it concerns, goes to the provider your organization chose, under the terms your organization has with that provider. Each guide in this section says what that means for the feature it describes. Keys are stored encrypted and are never shown again after they are saved. Setting up providers and agents is a maintainer’s job. See [Your organization](/organization/). ## The on and off switches [Section titled “The on and off switches”](#the-on-and-off-switches) AI can be switched off at several levels, so a team can use as much of it as it wants. | Where | What it controls | | ------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Organization | Which AI providers are connected, which provider and model each agent uses, and whether **Conversation mode** is allowed. | | Project | **Run autonomously**. | | Application | **Enable AI**, which is off until someone turns it on, and **Predictive modeling**. | Members who are not maintainers cannot turn AI on. When AI is not set up, Studio tells them to ask a maintainer. ## What you can do here [Section titled “What you can do here”](#what-you-can-do-here) [Talk to your board ](/working-with-ai/talk-to-your-board/)Tell Studio what to change from the chat bar, and review what it did. [Explain and generate ](/working-with-ai/explain-and-generate/)Ask how a part of the model works, or have a draft generated. [Suggestions and predictive modeling ](/working-with-ai/suggestions/)Preview a suggested slice, add it whole, or discuss it first. [Guided modeling and modeling by voice ](/working-with-ai/guided-modeling-and-voice/)Be walked to a checked model, or talk it onto the board. [Conversation mode ](/working-with-ai/conversation-mode/)Talk with agents out loud, with the microphone open. ## Hand a slice to an outside assistant [Section titled “Hand a slice to an outside assistant”](#hand-a-slice-to-an-outside-assistant) If you use a coding assistant outside Studio, give it the slice instead of retyping it. Open a slice’s menu, choose **Context for an agent**, and copy the slice as Markdown or download it. app.cratis.studio ![A slice's menu with the Context for an agent submenu open, offering copy as Markdown and downloads as Markdown, JSON, YAML and TOON](/_astro/context-for-an-agent-menu.3rGsft0r_10II3j.webp) Context for an agent turns a slice into text an outside assistant can read. More about this is in [the context for an agent](/developers/agent-context/). ## Related [Section titled “Related”](#related) * [Set up AI providers](/organization/ai-providers/) * [Agents and skills](/organization/agents-and-skills/) * [Conversation settings](/organization/conversation-settings/)

# Conversation mode

> Talk with Studio's agents out loud from the chat bar, with the microphone open, and control listening, voices and what is kept.

When you want to discuss a flow without typing every request, start a conversation from the chat bar. The microphone stays open while you work. Studio decides when you have finished a thought and whether you meant it for an agent, and the agent answers in its own voice. Typed and spoken lines are the same dialog. They are routed in the same way, whichever way you send them. ## Before you start [Section titled “Before you start”](#before-you-start) * AI must be set up. See [Work with AI](/working-with-ai/). * A maintainer can turn conversation mode off for the organization under **Settings** > **Conversation**. If you cannot find the conversation button, the chat bar explains why. * Your browser needs a microphone it may use. ## Start and end a conversation [Section titled “Start and end a conversation”](#start-and-end-a-conversation) 1. Select the conversation button next to the microphone in the chat bar, or press **⌘⇧.** (**Ctrl+Shift+.** on Windows and Linux). 2. Talk. A caption above the bar shows what an agent says. 3. To stop listening without ending, select the mute button. Select it again to continue. 4. To end, select the conversation button again. Studio pauses the conversation when you switch tabs, and resumes when you come back. It ends the conversation when you leave the board, or after the silence you set in your preferences. The bar always says why it stopped. Only one tab can hold a conversation at a time. Without a conversation running, hold **Shift+Option** to say a single line. ## How Studio decides what to do [Section titled “How Studio decides what to do”](#how-studio-decides-what-to-do) Studio waits for a short silence and for the sentence to sound finished. A longer silence ends the turn anyway. Say “go ahead” to hand over at once, or “scratch that” to drop what you were saying. | You say | What happens | | -------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | An instruction (“Add an event called Author Registered”) | Studio changes the board and says briefly what it did. | | An agent’s name first (“Merlin, can you…”) | That agent takes the line. | | A thought (“Maybe we need something for cancellations”) | Studio offers to do it and waits for your yes or no. | | “Yes” or “no” while an offer is shown | The offer is carried out or dropped. | | “Undo that”, or a correction | Studio reverts or corrects the last change. | | A question about the board | The agent answers in a sentence or two, out loud. | | A request for a review (“Is anything missing here?”) | Sherlock gives the few findings that matter most and offers to make the change. | | Something not meant for Studio | Nothing happens. The line shows faintly above the bar, and you can send it anyway or dismiss it. | Each line goes to the agent whose work it is: modeling to Atlas, code to Merlin, documentation to Quill, reviews to Sherlock, and conversation to Echo. While you are in an exchange with an agent, your next line stays with it unless you name another agent. When an agent cannot make a change without guessing something about your business, it asks one short question and waits. Say “never mind” to drop it. Large or destructive changes are never made from speech alone.
